# On the Three Members of Domestic Society
<meta>catchword: chapter</meta>

...are bound. And God willed this honor to be next to His own: a fact that even Phocylides <note>Phocylides was an ancient Greek poet famous for his moral maxims.</note> was not ignorant of.

->First honor God, and thereafter your parents.<- <note>original Greek: "Πρῶτα θεὸν τίμα, μετέπειτα δὲ σεῷ γονήασ"</note>
->*In the first place worship the Gods, and then your parents.*<- <note>original Latin: "In primis venerare Deos, ac deindè Parentes."</note>

Typotius, in *On Laws, folio 174*, states that there is no surer sign of impiety than the neglect of parents. See also Richter, *Economic Axioms 44 and following*. However, the love of parents with which they embrace their children is more burning than, conversely, the love of children toward their parents. Hence the Arabs have a proverb: *My heart is upon my son; but my son's heart is upon a stone.* <note>original: "Cor meum super filio est; at Cor filij mei super lapide," cited from the Arabic proverbs of the legendary sage Lokman, number 27.</note> And the elegant Robert Garnier in *Bradamante, Act 2, final scene*:

->*O God, how a child can martyr our spirits!*<- <note>original French: "Hè Dieu qu' un enfant peut nos esprits martyrifer."</note>

But should a son who has been established in a public Office <term>Magistratu</term> <note>a position of high civil authority</note> yield his place to a father who remains a private citizen? See Henning Arnisaeus, *Political Doctrine, chapter 5, folio 117*; Aegidius Strauchius, *Ethical Disputations, decade 10, final thesis*; and my own *Commentary on the title regarding Justice and Law, book 2, question 4*. Furthermore, Baldus and Ludovicus Romanus note on the law regarding summoning to court <note>original: "ad l. 8. de in Ius vocand."</note> that even if a son is adorned with an "Ecclesiastical Dictatorship"—that is, if he is elected Supreme Pontiff—he must nonetheless show reverence to his father. Matthew *chapter 22, verse 43* is relevant here. For this reason, the Doges of Venice carry a cross on their mitre, so that the father might not be considered to show [undue] honor to the son <note>The author suggests the father honors the cross on the son's head rather than the son himself, preserving the family hierarchy.</note>. Guazzo, *in Dialogues, folio 312*. Elsewhere, the love of parents is deservedly preferred over the reverence that a vassal owes to a lord. Consequently, a son is not compelled to aid his lord against his own father. See Kirchner, *Orations 7 and 8*, and my *Commentary on the Laws regarding Justice and Law, final question*. Hence also the curses of parents are rarely without effect. Richter, *Historical Axiom 291 and Economic Axiom 19*.

VI. A part of parental devotion <term>pietas</term> <note>the Roman and legal concept of duty and affection toward family and state</note> is that children must nourish parents worn out by old age—a duty that even animals lacking reason do not neglect. Hence Aristophanes in *The Birds*:

> *For us Birds, this is the ancient Law,*
> *Written in the tablets of laws which the Storks keep;*
> *For when the Father Stork has nourished the little storks, and they have now become fledglings,*
> *The offspring must return this favor, so that they nourish the old ones in turn.* <note>This refers to the "stork-law" (lex ciconiaria), a common classical metaphor for children caring for elderly parents.</note>

Rabbi Solomon <note>Refers to Rashi, the famous 11th-century Jewish commentator.</note> teaches how parents are to be honored. What is the honor that a son owes to a father and mother? He gives them food and drink, puts on their shoes, and leads them in and out. Regarding this, a Hebrew source says: We read, "Honor God from your wealth," and "Honor father and mother." God is to be honored if you have wealth; parents are to be honored whether you have it or not. For if you have nothing, you must beg from door to door for them, as reported by Jan Drusius in his notes on *Matthew*.

It emphasizes that filial duty often outweighs social or political rank, even in cases of sons becoming high-ranking officials or vassals.</summary>

chapter 15; and see the same author in *Hebrew Questions, book 2, chapter 63*, and on the passage in *Leviticus, chapter 64*. "He who does not nourish his parents is base," <note>original Latin: "Ignobilis est qui Parentes non nutrit"</note> according to Diogenes Laertius's life of Solon, folio 37. See also Enenckel, *On the Privileges of Parents, book 7, chapter 6*. Indeed, children could also once be sold by their parents: see Dominik Arumaeus, *Exercise 2, Thesis 2*, and Pierre Cavallo, volume 3, question 780. From these points it is also proved: that a statutory inheritance <term>Legitima</term> <note>a portion of an estate that the law requires to be left to specific heirs, regardless of the will's contents</note> is owed to parents (just as to children) even by Natural Law. See the *Sext*, chapter "If a father," under the title "On Testaments"; Bernhard Graeve, book 2, conclusion 119, consideration 1, at the end.

### CHAPTER IV.
### Concerning
### Paternal Authority.

The obedience owed to parents is asserted and kept intact by a certain command and power. Therefore, the legal interpreters—following Bartolus—have wrongly claimed that this paternal authority <term>Patria potestas</term> <note>The Roman legal concept of a father's nearly absolute power over his household</note> belongs solely to Civil Law <term>Jus Civile</term> <note>the specific laws of the Roman state, as opposed to the universal Law of Nature</note>. For indeed, a son is said to be in the power of his father by nature, according to the *Digest* 195, section "familia," under the title "On the Significance of Words"; Aristotle’s *Ethics*, book 8, chapter 2; Vacunius à Vacuna, *Declarations 8*; Bodin’s *On the Republic, book 1, chapter 4*, and *Method of History, chapter 6*; and Alberico Gentili, *On Marriage, book 4, parts 1 and 2*. While this right is indeed specific to Roman citizens—as noted in the *Institutes*, title "On Paternal Power," section 2, because those who are deported <note>those stripped of citizenship and exiled</note> do not enjoy it—it is not therefore unknown to other nations or to Nature itself. Wesenbeck relates the same regarding Adoption in his *Commentary on the Pandects*, title "On Adoptions," number 2; see however Andreas Gerhard, *Disputations on the Institutes 2, thesis 1*; Timon Faber, *Disputations on the Institutes 5, thesis 2*; and my own *Commentary on the Pandects, title 5 and 6, question 13*, and *Disputations on the Pandects 1, thesis 3*.

II. Our own doctors—specifically Dr. Bidembach, *On Matrimonial Causes 1, question 1*; Cothman, volume 1, counsel 1; Dr. Harprecht, in the preface to the *Institutes* on marriage, number 23; and Graeve, book 2, conclusion 95, consideration 1; and Besold, *On Family Pacts, folio 138*—all say that a marriage contracted while parents are protesting and unwilling is against rational Natural Law, and also against Civil Law. However, others dissent with plausible reasons, such as Antonio Contius, *Successive Readings 4*; Guibert Costanus, book 1, question chapter 12; and Bellarmine, *Controversies*, volume 1, "On Marriage," chapter 27. It is unjust that a person should be born as an heir to an unwilling father. <note>original Latin: "Iniquum est, ut Patri invito, suus hæres agnascatur."</note> This reasoning holds true today even for emancipated children <term>emancipatis</term> <note>those legally freed from paternal power</note>, because even a grandfather is bound to honor an emancipated grandson with a statutory inheritance share. Experience teaches that marriages not entered into according to the parents' wish are almost always unlucky. See Richter, *Economic Axioms 59*. The Canonists <note>experts in Catholic Church law</note> seem to wander too far into trifles when they say that such a marriage cannot be prohibited by any statute; as if without...

<summary>This page argues that children owe their parents financial support and a legal share of inheritance by natural law. It then asserts that paternal authority is rooted in nature rather than just Roman law, concluding that marriages made without parental consent are legally and naturally invalid.</summary>

-># On the Three Household Societies<-

<page-num>22</page-num>

Children should contract marriages with the **consent of their parents**. Graevaevus, *consideration 2 at the cited place*; Oswald Hilliger, *on Donellus book 13, chapter 20, letter C*. For even the **French law** <note>original: "Gallicana sanctio"</note> requires the consent of parents, according to Pasquier, *book 3 on epigrams, folio 37*; Papon, *book 22, title 6, on the Crime of Abduction*; Servin, *volume 3, plea 6, folio 127*; Thuanus, *volume 19, folio 404*; and Adrien Pulvaeus, *treatise on marriage*. Furthermore, the **older Canons** <term>Canon Law</term> <note>the ecclesiastical law governing the Catholic and some Protestant churches</note> almost always require this same consent: *chapter "otherwise," chapter "our people," cause 30, question 5, canon "not all," and the following canon, cause 32, question 3*. See also Alberico Gentili, *On Marriage, book 4, chapters 4 and 8*; Lord Bocerus, *class 1, dispute 9, thesis 8*; Arumaeus, *Justinian Exercises 2, thesis 9*; Corasius, *1 Miscellany 13, and on law 10 regarding the status of men*; Bronchorst, *2 Assertions 15*; and Rittershusius, *On the differences between Civil and Canon Law, chapter 2 and following*. 

Even if a betrothal <term>Sponsalia</term> <note>a formal promise to marry</note> has degenerated into a "quasi-marriage" through subsequent sexual intercourse, it seems that a parent who desires a separation <note>original: "distractionem," referring here to the dissolution of the union</note> should still be heard. *Digest book 12, section 3, on captives and the right of return; dissenting: chapter "when the cause," extra, on abduction*. On this most serious controversy, see Wesenbec, *in the Paratitla, law number 8 on the ritual of marriage*; **Philip Melanchthon** <note>A famous German Reformer and collaborator with Martin Luther</note>, *in the examination on the consent of parents*; Lord Doctor Felix Bidembach, *chapter 1, question 1, section "but if truly," folio 19*; Lord Doctor Harpprecht, *in the preface of the Institutes on marriage, number 70*; Alberico Gentili, *On Marriage, book 4, chapter 7*; Arumaeus, *Justinian Exercises 2, thesis 11*; and Wilhelm Valentin Forstner, *dispute on the Institutes 3, thesis 22*. 

For although a separation of this kind may be harsh and bitter, such audacity should not be left unpunished; yet it will happen never, or very rarely, that such severity must be used, according to Hilliger, *on Donellus book 13, chapter 20, letter V*. Indeed, unless we say this, all laws requiring the consent of parents would be meaningless <note>original: "elusoria," or illusory</note>. This is also consistent with French Law: Papon, *book 22, title 9, decree 9 near the end*; see also Lehman, *folio 112, column 2, and my own treatise on marriage subjoined to counsel 2*. One might also reasonably say that after the **father's death**, the **consent of the mother** is required by Civil Law for the marriage of children, especially for minor daughters: *Code book 1 and law 20, on marriage*. Borcholten, *treatise on degrees, page 52 etc.*, proves this from *Novella 115, chapter 3, section 11* <term>Novella</term> <note>a new law issued by Emperor Justinian after his initial legal code was completed</note>. See also Cypreus, *on betrothals 6, section 7*; and this is well-settled among the Theologians: Lord Bidembach, *cited question 1, section "finally"*; Antonius Matthaeus, *in notes on the Institutes, title on marriage, folio 32*; Papon, *cited title 6, decrees 9 and 10*. Indeed, in ancient times, the mother even bestowed names upon the sons: see Drusius, *in fragments on Genesis, folio 91*.

### III.
Furthermore, Pierre Ayrault <note>original: "Petr. Aerod."</note>, in his *unique treatise on Paternal Law*, attempts to prove that a son cannot rightly enter a **monastery** against the father's will. In that work, he lashes out quite bitterly against his **Jesuit son**, and brings to his side the text of **Matthew 15, verse 4** and following <note>Jesus's critique of the "Corban" tradition, where children dedicated money to God to avoid supporting their parents</note>, although some hold a different view based on *Code book 56, section 1, on bishops and clergy*. Nor is anyone freed from **paternal authority** <term>Patria potestas</term> <note>the legal power of a father over his household</note> simply because they have taken up a religious life, as some claim. See Hieronymus Cavallos, *Common opinions against common questions, 889*; and Heinrich Velsten, *centuries*.

<meta>This page continues a legal and philosophical discourse on the limits of parental authority. While the previous page addressed parental consent for marriage, this page explores the extent of obedience owed to parents—particularly in religious matters—and the history of the "right of life and death" in Roman law.</meta>

->### On the Types of Societies. Discourse I.<-

<page-num>23</page-num>

*Political Questions, Decade 2, Question 2.* Enenckel, *On the Privileges of Parents, book 1, chapter 9, number 10, etc., and number 21, etc.* Bellarmine <note>Robert Bellarmine (1542–1621), an influential Jesuit cardinal and scholar</note>, however, disagrees for major reasons in *On Monks, chapter 36.* The same author, in *the same treatise, chapter 21,* examines whether and to what extent a father must be obeyed in all things.

IV. According to ancient Roman custom, the **Right of life and death** <term>Jus vitae et necis</term> was granted to the father over his children, and this was even weightier than the power a master held over his slaves. Joachim Stephan, *On Jurisdiction, book 3, chapter 2, number 23, etc.*; Valentin Forster, *On Jurisdiction, folio 202*; Rodulphus Fornerus, *Daily Matters, book 1, chapter 2*; Ernest Cothman, *Disputations on the Institutes, 4, theses 6, 7, and 10*; Jacob Cramer, *Disputations on the Institutes, 3, thesis 3 and following*; Ayrault <note>original: "Ærod." referring to Pierre Ayrault, a French jurist</note>, *folio 486 and following, book 6, title 7, and folio 502.* This originated from the **Law of Romulus** <note>original: "Lex Romulea," the legendary foundational laws of Rome</note>: according to which, illustrious men who were addressing the people from the platforms <note>original: "pro rostris," the speaker's podium in the Roman Forum</note>, even while enjoying popular favor and stirring the Senate’s envy, were dragged down from the platform and led away by their parents to face punishment according to the father's own judgment. While they were being led through the forum, no one standing by could rescue them: not the Consuls, not the Tribunes, nor the crowd itself, etc. Dionysius of Halicarnassus, *book 2.* On the difference between the power of a Father and that of a Master, see Muretus in Gruter, *volume 2, folio 992.*

V. Pierre Charron <note>a French philosopher and theologian (1541–1603)</note> argues in *On Wisdom, book 1, chapter 47,* that the cutting away of this broad power is an evil that spreads infinitely. He says it is because of this loss of power that children refuse obedience and aid to their parents; they frequently engage in legal battles with them in the "forensic mill" <note>a metaphor for the exhausting and grinding nature of the law courts</note>, carrying out their business against them "hand-to-hand, from a distance, with slashing blows, and with thrusting blows" <note>original: "cominùs, eminùs, cæsim, punctimque," terms from fencing used here to describe aggressive litigation</note>. Jean Bodin <note>a French jurist and political philosopher (1530–1596)</note> agrees with this in *On the Republic, book 1, chapter 4.* Nevertheless, that harsher coercion of children—the power of life and death—was gradually abolished, first through disuse because of its very cruelty, and then by the decrees of Emperors. Thus, not without great reason, it has ceased and is now prohibited everywhere. The illustrious Enenckel, *On the Privileges of Parents, book 1, chapter 1, number 5, etc.*; Guyon, *volume 3, book 1, chapter 14*; Arnisaeus, *Politics, book 5, folio 118*; Kirchner, *Disputations on the Republic, 1, thesis 6*; Velsten, *Decade 2, thesis 6*; Rodulphus Fornerus, *Daily Matters, book 1, chapter 3*; Joachim Cluten, *on Paradoxes, thesis 3*; Zabelius in Arumaeus, *Final Discourse, thesis 4.* For according to the best customs, when all things were [governed by that law], fathers did not easily abuse that power. *Argument from the Digest, book 22, section 4, regarding adulterers.* This power was granted to them only in the hope that children, deterred by the fear of it, would better maintain their duties. Since we now live under different customs, it does not seem advisable to allow such unrestrained power to parents. Only one trace of the old paternal power to kill children remains: in the case of an **adulterous daughter**. *Digest book 20 and following, on the Julian Law regarding Adultery.* Forster, *On Roman Jurisdiction, folio 148, etc.* In other matters, if children do not recognize the piety owed to their parents, it is indeed not forbidden to punish them by right of paternal power; however, a harsher remedy...

<summary>This page examines the historical "right of life and death" held by Roman fathers and discusses the legal transition toward more limited parental authority. It highlights arguments from philosophers like Charron and Bodin who feared that weakening this power would lead to social disorder and litigious children.</summary>

# On the Powers of Domestic Society

<page-num>24</page-num>

<meta>The catchword "bantur" from the previous page completes the word "judicabantur" (were judged).</meta>

...judged. Intending to use a middle course <note>original: "medium usuri," meaning a moderate path between private execution and total leniency</note>, Fathers are ordered to present their children to the Governor of the Province, who is to pronounce the sentence that the Fathers themselves desired to be spoken. *Code of Justinian, On parental power, law 3.* Likewise, a question is raised: Can a son defend himself against his Father in any case? I believe that he can never resist a Father who is administering correction, but he may sometimes resist one who is attacking him unlawfully. *Code of Justinian, On those who cannot be accused, law 14.* See Cluten, *Paradoxes, thesis 4.* I have also addressed this in my *Commentary on the title of Justice and Law, at law 2, question 9.* See also Deuteronomy *21, verse 18.* <note>This biblical verse describes the procedure for a "stubborn and rebellious son."</note>

VI. There was also an ancient right of Parents: the **disowning** <term>abdicatio; the formal act of casting a son out of the family</term> of disobedient children. Plato, *On Laws, 11.* Forster, *On Jurisdiction, folio 205, etc.*, of which Servin writes beautifully in *volume 1, plea 9, folio 421, etc.* Once this was abolished by the law *abdicatio* in the *Code of Justinian, On parental power*, a milder Law was introduced, namely **Disinheritance** <term>Exheredatio; the legal exclusion from a will</term>: which Justinian, under the New Law, further restricted to certain specific causes. *Novell 115, chapter 3.* This power of Parents over ungrateful and less obedient children is founded in Genesis *chapter 49.* See August Callias, *Forest of Mysteries, 1, folio 49.*

->*So Reuben and Levi, and Simeon, by the open*<-
->*Pronouncement of their Father, lost the right of the firstborn.*<-

VII. It also followed from the Father's power over life and death that the Romans formerly possessed nothing of their own as long as their Fathers remained alive. From this, many laws were introduced, which Valentin Forster treats extensively in *On Roman Jurisdiction, folio 225, etc.* These laws were partly abolished by the Justinian Legislation itself, in the *titles of the Code on the goods of children and the goods of mothers.* Furthermore, by the customs of certain Nations, this matter was so arranged that as soon as a Mother departs from life, the child immediately falls under the power of guardians. Jacob Curtius, *Conjectures, volume 1, chapter 26.* This law, namely out of fear of **second vows** <note>original: "secundorum votorum," referring to a father's remarriage</note>, does not dare to trust the protective good faith of the Father.

VIII. It should be known, however, that today, according to the custom of all Germany, children are **emancipated** <term>Emancipatio; the release of a child from parental authority</term> by the very fact that their Parents permit them to have their own Household. Zasius, *on the law "utrùm turpem," 106, on Verbal Obligations.* Forster, *On Jurisdiction, folio 279, etc.* See Gilken, *on prescription, part 3, chapter 3, number 360, etc.* Ernest Cothman, *disputations on the Institutes, 4, crown 2.* Eberhard Speckhan, *century 1, question 28.* Hilliger, *on Donellus, 2, chapter 26, letter M.* Berlich, *part 2, conclusion 11.* This same custom flourishes in Spain: Castillo, *On Usufruct, chapter 3, number 77,* and likewise in Portugal: Molina, *disputation 220, number 3,* and it is found confirmed in Württemberg Law. It also seems to have a foundation in Genesis *2, verse 25; and add Guyon, 1, chapter 15.*

IX. And finally, so that the Person of the Parents would be all the more sacred to the Children, horrendous punishments were inflicted upon those who killed their Parents...

# On the Kinds [of Society]. Discourse I.

<page-num>25</page-num>

...were judged <note>original: "judicabantur"; this completes the sentence from the bottom of page 24</note>, according to Aerodius, book 8, *on parricide*; Alexander ab Alexandro, *Genial Days*, book 3, chapter 5; Magius, *Miscellany*, book 4, chapter 8; Zepper, *On Mosaic Laws*, book 4, chapter 12; Christian Coler, *Additions*, chapter 3; Kornmann, *On the Miracles of the Dead*, final part, chapter 29. These practices, because of their atrocity and the danger they posed to the soul, have ceased to be observed in most Kingdoms and Principalities. ### CHAPTER V.
### On
## Ancient Servants.

AND this concludes the discussion on Parental Society. Next follows the <term>despotikē</term> <note>original Greek: "δεσποτικη"; meaning the authority of a master over a household</note> society, which exists between Master and Servant. To serve and to be a "servant" is spoken of in two ways: Philosophically and Politically. For some men are considered born for freedom, and others for servitude. Keckerman, *Practical Disputes*, 28, problem 31; Velstenius, decade 3, theses 8 and 9. Thus Nature presides, and he is a Master by nature who prevails through the intelligence to provide foresight. But those who are as inferior to others as the Body is distant from the Soul, or a beast from a man (namely, those who are so constituted that their function is the use of the body) and who participate in reason only so far as to perceive it, but not to possess it: these are servants by nature. For such people, it is far better to be led than to lead, and it is best for them to be governed by this Command. Aristotle, *Politics*, books 1 and 3; Theodoret, *On Providence*, sermon 7; Aerodius, folio 967. Nature does nothing in vain: but it would be in vain if everyone were fit for commanding, since everyone could not command. And just as some stars are more illustrious than others, and as various differences show themselves in bodies, so too the splendor of each soul shines forth in a dissimilar way, and various discrepancies of minds reveal themselves. Just as the Angelic intelligences differ among themselves in order, dignity, duties, and ministries—following Saint Thomas [Aquinas] and Bellarmine in his treatise *On the Ascent of the Mind to God*, grade 2, folio 37—which the entire Hierarchy of Spirits demonstrates: so even after this life, by the testimony of Daniel <note>A reference to Daniel 12:3, "those who are wise shall shine like the brightness of the firmament"</note>, the blessed souls themselves will be more illustrious than one another. So perhaps even in the souls of men, a certain difference can be established, not without reason. Levinus Lemnius, book 1, *On...*

# On the Three [Kinds of] Domestic Society
<page-num>26</page-num>

<margin>*On the Hidden Miracles of Nature, chapter 13*</margin> Souls indeed, by their very nature, tend toward the good equally, but they do so through different means so that harmony may be produced: some by commanding, others by obeying. By this individual difference (not solely by number), souls appear to differ. The gifts of God are various; these certainly do not belong to the physical temperament <note>original: "complexioni"; referring to the four humors of the body</note>, but are fixed within the souls themselves: rendering one person fit for rightly commanding, and another for obeying. Epictetus, according to Arrian, *Book 3, final chapter*: In every kind of thing, it is absolutely necessary for the skilled to command the unskilled, etc. But the error of Origen <note>A 3rd-century theologian</note> is intolerable, who in *On First Principles, Book 1, final chapter*, ascribes this diversity of souls to merits and to sins committed before the creation of the world; Philo does the same in *Book 2, chapter 9, page 57 of his works*, where he "calvinizes" <note>An anachronistic term used by the author to suggest Philo's views on predestination align with those of John Calvin</note>. Conversely, William of Paris in his *Treatise on the Universe, folio 265*, tries to prove that souls do not differ in the way that Angels do. I leave this most serious question, which is great among the greatest, undecided.

II. Certain people are likewise servants by Law, and are said to serve contrary to Nature. For there is a certain agreement, or public consent, by which those who are overcome in War are said to belong to the Victors. *Aristotle, Politics, Book 1, chapter 4*; Marcus Lycklama, *Book 4, chapter 11*; Jakob Martini, *Treatise on the Supreme Good, dispute 8, questions 1 & 8*. And because others have maintained—both the founders of our Law in the *Institutes, section 2, on Natural Law* and the *Digest, book 4, on Justice and Law*, as well as those praised by Christopher Coler in his *Additions, final chapter*—that all are born free from the beginning, and that Freedom is contained within **Natural Law**, while Lordship <note>original: "Dominationem"</note> was introduced by the **Law of Nations** <term>Ius Gentium</term> <note>The body of laws shared by all civilized peoples, as opposed to natural law</note>; this should be understood not as that higher form of service, but as this servitude which descends from **Positive Law** <note>Man-made law</note> and suffocates the effect of natural liberty. Nevertheless, the name of this servitude is most ancient, and its origin was most antique, as Pignorius deduces on *folios 7 and 8*.

III. Among the Romans, there were many species of Servants. Some, to be sure, were called Servants because they were redeemed by bronze <note>purchased with money</note>, captured in War, or left by a Will; others were acquired by various means, such as the *Vernae* <term>home-born slaves</term> <note>original: "Vernæ"; slaves born to one's own female servants</note> and those born from our female servants: and there are other ways of bringing Servants under our mastery. *Digest, book 4 & 5, on the Status of Men*. All these were considered <term>Mancipia</term> <note>Slaves regarded as property or "chattel"</note>, and as belonging immediately to the Person and their heirs. The *Dedititii* <term>surrendered ones</term> were servants who, from a state of siege, gave themselves into the power of the Victor; some of these were called freedmen, others Foreigners. The *Censiti* <term>censused servants</term> were those registered and assigned to the soil; they served a Master along with the land, and were transferred or alienated together with the estates. How great the multitude of Servants once was is shown by Pignorius in *On Servants, folio 1, 2, etc.* (where he treats their duties and other matters at length) and also by Leonicus in *Various Histories, Book 3, chapter 4*. Likewise, concerning Servants, consult Robbigius, *Book 9, chapter 9, etc.*

IV. Although the Romans held the greatest power over Servants,

<meta>This page continues the discussion on the legal status of servants. The previous page ended by discussing the origins of servitude in the laws of war and how it contrasts with natural freedom. This page addresses the historical transition from the absolute power of masters in antiquity to the gradual abolition of slavery in Christian Europe.</meta>

### On the Types. Discourse I.
<sig>D 2</sig>
<meta>catchword: Marti-</meta>

[...continued from the previous page] greatest power, so much so that they—namely, those properly called Slaves, not surrendered subjects or those bound to the soil <note>original: "glebæ adscriptos"; these were serfs who could not be sold away from the land they farmed</note>—could be killed by their masters even for the slightest cause: Seneca, *On Anger*, book 3, chapter 40; Tacitus, book 14; Cicero, *For Roscius*. This was also a common practice among other nations: Arnisæus, chapter 4, folio 107. However, they could not do this without any cause at all: Ærod. folio 484, etc. And certainly, that cruelty, in a well-ordered Republic <note>The author uses "Republica" in the classical sense to mean any organized state or commonwealth</note>, is in no way to be tolerated. The Romans themselves tried to help the injuries of slaves through various means, such as the establishment of Sanctuaries <note>original: "Asylorum"; holy places where slaves could seek refuge from abusive masters</note>, the sanction of the Cornelian Law on Assassins <note>original: "Legis Cornel. de Sicarijs"; a law against murderers that was eventually extended to protect slaves</note>, and others: Law 1, *On the Office of the Prefect of the City*; Laws 1 and 2, *On those who are under their own or another's power*. Bodin, book 1, chapter 5; Arnisæus, said folio 107; Cothmann, *Disputations on the Institutes*, 4, conclusion 1; Jakob Cramer, *Disputation 2, Thesis 9*. Although slaves ought to earn their Masters' favor through obedience rather than provoke them with defiance, it was nowhere permitted for a slave to be obedient to a Master's command in the commission of crimes. If he had done this, he paid the penalties, and judgment was rendered against him even after he gained his freedom: Law 157, *On the Rules of Law*. Guillaume Fornerius, *Selected Works*, book 1, chapter 27; add Pacius, book 1, question 21. Thus, the power of the Republic was everywhere judged greater than that of the Master.

V. The laws of ancient servitude are today considered entirely abolished among Christians. For just as a greater humanity and equality soon followed when religion passed from Paganism to Christianity, even the harshest condition of servitude was mitigated step by step. Thus at last, around the Year of Christ 1250, it was completely removed, as Bodin notes in *On the Republic*, book 1, chapter 5, and Thomas Smith in *On the Republic of the English*, book 3, chapter 10; see Lycklama, book 4, selection 11. Indeed, in the Year of Christ 1200, it is clear that slaves still existed in Italy from the title of the *Decretals on the Marriage of Slaves* <note>original: "Decretal. de conjug. servor."; part of Church law dealing with the legalities of slaves marrying</note>. However, Bartolus, who flourished in the Year of Christ 1300, testifies that in his age, no slaves or people for sale existed at all: *On the law regarding the enemy, in the Digest on captives and the right of return*. Add especially Lorenzo Pignoria, *On Slaves*, folio 195; Louys Guyon, *Various Lessons*, book 1, chapter 6. And the laws of the Lombards and Visigoths still speak of slaves in various places; see Lindenbrog’s glossary, added to the ancient laws of the Germanic nations, under the word **slave** <note>original: "servus"</note>, where he also discusses their price. But nevertheless, the Most Christian Kings of the French so abhor servitude that whoever comes into France is immediately rendered free: Lord Lansius, *Oration for France*, folio 157; add Guillaume Paradin, *History of Lyon*, book 2, chapter 24.

VI. It seems to hold little controversy that Christians, with an uninjured conscience, could use the labor of even legal servants. St. Paul says so in the *Epistle to Philemon*, as noted by Scipio Gentilis, chapters 43 and following; Donellus, *Commentaries*, book 1, chapter 12, and there Oswald Hilliger; Bronchorst, *Miscellaneous Assertions*, book 1, chapter 4; Velsten, *Century of Political Questions*, decade 3, final thesis. Jakob...

It highlights the abolition of formal slavery in Europe by the 13th century and the legal principle that any person entering France becomes free.</summary>

<meta>This page continues the legal and ethical analysis of servitude. While the previous page focused on the absolute power of masters in antiquity, this page discusses the biblical limitations on slavery among the Jews and debates whether ancient slavery should be restored to solve modern social problems like vagrancy. It concludes by introducing the "modern" form of servitude: serfdom.</meta>

->De Tribus Domestica Societatis<-
->On the Three Types of Domestic Society<-

Martini, *Philosophical Collection 1, dispute 10, final inquiry* and the same author, *Ethical Dispute 8, question 9.* Yet a Jew could not hold another Jew in perpetual servitude. *Exodus 21, verse 2.* But this point is more controversial: whether it would be beneficial for the Commonwealth <note>The author uses "Republica" to mean any organized state or civil society</note> to restore the ancient Law of Servitude and the power of the master? This might perhaps be defended in part; see Bodin, *On the Republic*, book 1, chapter 5; Arnisæus, chapter 4, pages 106, 108, etc.; Kirchner, *Orations 3 & 4*; Jakob Martini, *Ethical Dispute 8, question 10.* Indeed, the condition of beggars today is more wretched than that of slaves in the past. And who would dare deny that it would be more to the public benefit if the healthy <note>original: "vegeti"; literally vigorous or lively</note> who are accustomed to wandering idly and begging—or those whom flogging and exile lead directly to the gallows—were seized into servitude and assigned to public works? It was also a very ancient institution to make slaves labor as a punishment. *Judges 16, verse 21.* Certainly, if we consider that for slaves, the household is a kind of Commonwealth, and the home is like a City—as Pliny says in *Epistle 8.16*—and if we treat them with Christian charity, the condition of servitude would bring more benefit than harm to the slave. Thus also in Arrian <note>Arrian was the disciple who recorded the teachings of the Stoic philosopher Epictetus</note>, *book 3, final chapter, page 357*, Epictetus says that freedmen yearn for their former servitude. [One might say:] "One person provided me with clothing, another with shoes, another fed me, and another cared for me in sickness; I served him in only a few matters," etc. However, Guyon <note>Louis de Guyon, a French physician and historian</note>, in the aforementioned book, chapter 7, does not approve of servitude because of the dangers of slave wars, such as the one that occurred long ago in Saxony. Girard <note>likely Bernard de Girard, a French chronicler</note>, page 476. But just as there was once such close kinship among the Greeks that if a Greek occasionally rose up against another Greek, the law of kinship was not considered abolished; it was not regarded as a true war, but only as a discord stirred up between them. Joachim Stephani, *On Jurisdiction*, book 2, chapter 6, number 7. In the same way, most rightly, no servitude follows the wars of Christians; instead, captives are released upon the payment of some ransom <note>original: "lytro"; from the Greek word for a ransom price</note>.

### CHAPTER VI.
### On
### *Modern Servants.*

Furthermore, because we can hardly do without servitude, it has not entirely fallen out of use even now; a new type of servant has been introduced, both because of the poverty of the common people and the great wealth of the rich. Krantz, *Politics*, book 1, chapter 4. This condition persists mostly among certain peasants and farmers, whom we commonly call <term>serfs</term> <note>original German: "Leibeygne Leuth"; literally "people owned in body"</note> or <term>subjects settled for daily service</term> <note>original German: "zur alltäglichen Diensten gesessne Underthonen"</note>. Legal practitioners call them "Proprietary persons" <note>original: "Proprios"</note>; the French call them <term>those of the dead hand</term> <note>original: "de main morte"; a legal term for those whose property rights were restricted</note> and <term>men and women of the body</term> <note>original: "hommes & femmes de corps"</note>.

<meta>This page continues the discussion on the legal classifications of servitude, shifting from the absolute slavery of the ancient world to the "modern" (early modern) systems of serfdom and feudal dependency. It explores whether these subjects are legally "slaves" or "free," and examines the historical origins of these classes in Germanic custom and Roman provincial law.</meta>

# On the Types [of Servitude]. Discourse I.

...Pasquier, in book 4 of *Researches*, chapter 5, where he calls the *adscriptitii* <note>original: "adscriptitios"; individuals legally bound to a specific piece of land</note> "landed serfs" <note>original: "serfs fonciers"</note>. There were also *Aldriones* <note>semi-free persons under Lombard law</note>, *mansarii* <note>tenants of a farmstead</note>, and *Fiscalini* <note>servants belonging to the state treasury</note>. See Fauchet, *Antiquities*, volume 1, book 4, chapter 5. Clearly, however, "owned men" <note>original: "homines proprii"; a term for serfs</note> differ from true slaves in many ways, since they enjoy various privileges of Civil Law just as free people do. See Zasius, *Unique Responses 3*; Sichard on the title of the Code *Concerning the Services of Freedmen*; Mynsing, *Observations*, book 2, observation 23; Arnisæus, chapter 3, page 105; Dauth, *On Testaments*, number 240, etc.; Dr. Majer, *Lectures on the Pandects*, volume 1, page 24; Liebenthal, *Political Lectures*, exercise 3, number 45 and following; and see Wehner under the word *Leibeygenschafft* <note>German: serfdom, literally "ownership of the body"</note>. 

And yet the condition of these people seems harsher than that of the ancient slaves. See Waremund in *Likelihoods*, chapter 4, number 17, page 53. In France, certainly, even today the common people are held almost in the place of slaves. Lansius, *Oration against the French*, page 334. Lords demand labor from them; moreover, if they change their original home without the Lord's permission, the Lords contend they are allowed to lead them back home by a halter. See Bodin, *On the Republic*, book 1, chapter 5. In France, there were slaves who were inhabitants of that kingdom conquered in war. There were "Captain Vassals," to whom these slaves were assigned. The free men were the soldiers, who by virtue of their condition and ethnicity were called "Franks" <note>original: "Franci"; the author notes the etymological link between the name of the Franks and the word for "free"</note>, as Pasquier notes somewhere in his treatise *Researches*. So too Michalo, in his fragment *On the Customs of the Tartars, Lithuanians, etc.*, fragment 6, criticizes the Lithuanians because they hold in perpetual servitude people of their own nation and religion—orphans, the poor, and those ensnared by marriage to female slaves—even though they were not acquired through war or purchase, nor are they foreigners.

II. The origin of these [serfs] comes either from the ancient custom of the Germans, who did not use slaves for specific household duties as the Romans did; rather, each slave had his own home and hearth, with the Master enjoining a certain amount of grain, cattle, or clothing. See Tacitus, *On the Customs of the Germans*, and Zasius in the cited location, number 76, etc. Or—as I am more inclined to believe—that servitude should be attributed to the *Adscriptitii* <note>those registered to the land</note> and the *Dedititii* <note>conquered peoples who surrendered</note> of the Romans. For just as the Emperor Probus, in letters sent to the Senate, boasted about himself that "all of Germany is subdued... all the Barbarians now plow for the Romans, and sow for the Romans." See Flavius Vopiscus. Thus also later the Franks, occupying the Gauls (acting as "apes of the Romans" in governing the State), made the conquered people into this kind of slave; they left the lands to be cultivated by them so that the Province would not be reduced to a wilderness, which would be fruitless even for the victors. See Estienne Pasquier, *Researches*, book 4, chapter 5; add *Jeremiah*, chapters 35 and the last. From this descend the "rural fiefs" <note>original: "Feuda rustica"; also "Bauren lehen / Schupflehen" in German, referring to peasant tenures</note>. See John Selden in *The Face of the English Janus*, page 47; add Albert of Stade, page 153b and following. By this reasoning, the condition of the *gentes* <note>meaning the "tribes" or Barbarians</note> was made better than that of the Romans. For which reason, even today in Italy, France, and Britain, the word "Gentile" <note>original: "Gentilis"; the root of "Gentleman"</note> is used for a noble. <unclear>Welser, Augustan Vindicant Affairs, book 2</unclear>

<summary>This page discusses the legal evolution of serfdom from ancient Roman and Germanic roots, arguing that medieval "owned men" (serfs) occupy a middle ground between ancient slaves and free citizens. It traces the origins of the European nobility (the "Franks" or "Gentiles") to the conquering tribes who spared the local populations to keep the land productive.</summary>

<meta>This page continues the legal analysis of social classes and servitude, specifically examining the historical origins of serfdom among the Germanic tribes and the Frankish Empire. It connects Roman legal precedents to the feudal systems of the Middle Ages, citing the Salic Law and the reigns of Clovis I, Henry the Fowler, and Charlemagne.</meta>

### On the Power of the Domestic Household
<meta>catchword: minibus</meta>

...originally. So it is also in the **Salic Laws**, title 34: a Roman commits a more serious offense against a Frank than vice versa. Who today are similar to the *adscriptitii* <note>original: "Adscriptitijs"; people legally bound to a specific plot of land</note>? Tuschus discusses this in *letter A, conclusion 217*. And regarding the various types of tenant farmers, see Valascus, *On Emphyteutic Law, question 37*.

III. Likewise, Clovis I, King of the Franks, in the year 499 AD, after defeating the Alamanni at Tolbiac <gloss>now Zülpich in the Cologne district</gloss> (Lehman, book 3, chapter 2), took away their weapons and delivered them into perpetual servitude. They were called *Fiscalini* <note>original: "Fiscalini"; servants or tenants belonging to the royal treasury or "fisc"</note>, according to the Lindenbrog Glossary under the word *Fiscalini*. Afterward, they were handed over to the nobles and even to the clergy along with the lands, being counted as "predial property" <note>property attached to the land</note>, following Stumpf and others (Andreas Knich, *On Investiture Contracts, chapter 3, number 8*). Lehman, *In the Speyer Chronicle, book 2, chapters 19 and 20, and book 4, chapter 22*. Concerning the servants of the English, see John Selden in *The English Janus, pages 69 to 106*. And concerning the servants of the Germans, Goldast has much to say in *Alamannic Matters, volume 2*. Also <insert>in the notes to the Ancient Admonitions, page 433 and following, and page 457</insert> where he discusses the <term>Hauptstahl</term> <note>German: a "death tax" or heriot, where a lord claimed the best head of livestock or piece of property upon a tenant's death</note>. Thus, even the inhabitants of the town named after Saint Denis in France were subjected to the yoke of servitude to the Church of that same Saint. (Author of *The Antiquities of Paris, chapter 9, at the beginning*). And since, as Tacitus testifies, there were no cities in ancient Germany, and later they were quite rare; Henry the Fowler transferred every ninth man from the fields into the towns—newly built and fortified here and there—making a distinction between the Urban and Rural commoners. He called the former <term>Burger</term> <note>German: "Burgers" or "Citizens"</note> from the *Burgs* <note>fortified castles or towns</note> which they were appointed to guard within the walls. The latter he called <term>Bawren</term> <note>German: "Peasants" or "Farmers"</note> from their farming <gloss>from "bawen," meaning to cultivate</gloss>. See Widukind, *Annals, book 1, page 11*; Sigebert under the year 925; and Spangenberg in the *Querfurt Chronicle*. Add also Lehman on the inhabitants of cities, *book 2, chapters 14 and 19*. Yet, even later there were servants in the cities; those in Speyer were granted freedom by Henry V (Lehman, *book 2, chapter 22*). Perhaps foreign servants also inhabited the cities, and there were those who are otherwise called <term>Pfalburgeri</term> <note>German: "Pale-burghers"; people who lived outside the city walls but enjoyed the rights of citizenship</note>. See Lehman, *page 353*, etc. Certainly, in Württemberg (and commonly elsewhere), those who are "owned men" <note>serfs</note> cannot obtain the rights of citizenship in the towns. <term>Provincial Ordinance, page 4</term>. Hence the old proverb: <term>No hen flies over the walls</term> <note>meaning a serf cannot escape their legal status simply by entering a city</note>.

IV. Charlemagne also afflicted the Saxons with a condition of servitude (Lehman, *book 2, chapter 20*). At the same time, when the Saxons waged war against the Obotrites and neighboring tribes to compel them to embrace the Christian religion, they used the same rights over the conquered as the victors of old were accustomed to: everything they left to those who surrendered remained in the power of the Lords, so much so that the Nobles and Prefects could even expel them and take the land back for themselves. (Friedrich Husanus, *Treatise on Men*).

It highlights how servitude was tied to the land and the military protection of fortified towns.</summary>

<meta>This page continues the legal and historical classification of various types of servitude, transitioning from the Frankish "Fiscalini" to the specific terms used in Saxon, Hebrew, and Germanic law. It explores the linguistic roots of social status and the evolution from ancient slavery to medieval serfdom.</meta>

# Types of Servitude. Discourse I.
<meta>catchword: Spaniards</meta>

...by their own names. *Chapter 2, number 28.* These servants, therefore, the Saxons still call today <term>Lassen</term> <note>original: "Lassen"; derived from the idea of being "left behind" or "released" into a specific status</note>, as if they were "those left behind" in the province, as seen in the *Saxon Mirror* (*Sachsenspiegel*), *book 3, article 44 at the end, where the German glossary is found.* See also Johann Rudinger, *Observations, century 3, observation 33*; Treutler, *Discussions on the Pandects, volume 1, discussion 2, thesis 4. b.*; and Selden in *The English Janus, page 41, etc.* 

The Hebrews called those who had surrendered <note>original: "Dedititios"</note> and were public servants <term>Netinim</term> <note>original: "netinim"; meaning "the given ones," traditionally Temple servants</note>, from the word <term>nathan</term> <note>original: "nathan"; Hebrew for "to give"</note>. See Buxtorf in his *Lexicon*. Likewise, Solomon made tributaries and servants out of the conquered peoples, while the Israelites themselves waged the wars. *1 Kings chapter 9, verse 22.* 

In the same way, Reineccius, in his *Commentary on the Origins of the Saxons*, distinguishes the ranks of citizens as follows: Nobles, Free Men, <term>Liti</term> <note>original: "Litos"; a class of semi-free peasants</note>, and Servants. He equates the *Liti* with the terms <term>Leuthe</term> (People) or <term>Arme Leuth</term> (Poor Folk), placing them in the category of freedmen; he says that in the Bavarian Laws, they are referred to as <term>Frilassos</term> (Freedmen). 

However, they are called <term>Arme Leuth</term> <note>Poor Folk</note> not because of poverty, but from the word for "arm" or "branch"—that is, from the "hand" or power. For this reason, the French use the term <term>de main morte</term> <note>original: "of the dead hand"; referring to property that cannot be alienated or inherited because it belongs to a lord or the church</note>. See Lehman, *page 355*, and Wehner on the definition of the words *Arm* and *Armfrey*. Even the Turks have their own "Lassos," called <term>Budovvez</term>, as noted in the *Chronicles, page 169*. 

Regarding the servants of the Germans, one should certainly consult—after the others praised there—Reineccius in his notes on the *Anonymous Poet concerning the deeds of Charlemagne, page 59. b.*, and Goldast in his *Notes on the Ancient Admonitions, page 433, etc.* Add to these Isaac Pontanus, *Origins of the Franks, book 6, chapter 15*, and Mr. Mager in his most complete work, *On Armed Patronage, chapter 6, number 929, etc.* 

On the difference between <term>Leibeygen</term> (Serfdom/Ownership of the body) and <term>Knechtschafft</term> (Servitude/Bondage), see Sebastian Franck’s *Emperor's Chronicle, page 241 and 238*, where he discusses the Peasants’ War. Regarding the emancipation of German servants, consult Lehman, *chapter 20, pages 99 and 101, column 1*, where he notes that even freedmen were obligated to certain burdens, such as <term>Leibhennen</term> <note>original: "Leibhennen"; literally "body-hens," a tribute of poultry paid annually to a lord as a sign of recognition of his authority</note>. 

It seems that our "bondmen" are not truly "slaves" in the ancient sense. That old servitude introduced by the Franks (like the ancient Roman version) has vanished; yet it left behind a right that passes to heirs. Furthermore, those who are bound to <term>alltäglichen Diensten</term> (daily services) are very nearly true servants. Uranius, in his *Legal Opinions, volume 2, opinion 50*, calls the <term>Leibeygne</term> (serfs) "the commended ones" (*recommendatitios*). 

Mr. Lehman also notes on *page 719* that in the time of Albert I, servants were called <term>Bnoben</term>. Likewise, Georgius Fabricius, in his *Supplement to the Meissen Chronicle for the year 1286*, mentions a certain class of servants called <term>Gasti</term> (Guests), who were forced to perform any service for the Church without refusal at the Church’s whim, though they sometimes redeemed themselves for a small sum of silver. 

Today, there are two types of servants of a freed condition: some pay a "body-hen" and a <term>Hauptfall</term> <note>original: "Hauptfall"; a death tax or heriot, where the lord claimed the best animal or possession upon a tenant's death</note> and can even live outside the Lord's jurisdiction. Others are settled for "daily services" and are, so to speak, "bound to the territory." Formerly, the <term>Hauptfall</term> was given to Lay Lords, while the <term>Burttheil</term> <note>original: "Burttheil"; a specific share of an estate</note> was given to the Clergy. See Lehman, *page 352, 356, and also book 5, chapter 64*. However, now the Germans, French, Italians, and Spaniards all eagerly call any servant a "Slave" (*Sclavum*)...

<summary>This page analyzes the legal terminology of servitude across Saxon, Hebrew, and German traditions, distinguishing between ancient slavery, medieval serfdom, and specific feudal taxes like the "death tax" and "tribute hens." It emphasizes that the term "Poor Folk" (Arme Leuth) originally referred to power and jurisdiction rather than financial poverty.</summary>
<keywords>serfdom, Lassen, Sachsenspiegel, Netinim, Liti, Leibeygen, Hauptfall, Leibhennen, feudal law, social classes, etymology of power</keywords>

<meta>This page continues the discussion on the origins and terminology of servitude across different cultures, following the previous discussion on Saxon and Hebrew terms. It transitions from the etymology of the word "slave" to the specific types of labor and rents required of peasants in the author's own time.</meta>

### Concerning the Three Parts of Domestic Society
<meta>catchword: Eren-</meta>

...Spanish; *Slave* <note>original: "Sclav, Ecclave, Schiavo, Esclavo"</note>; the name comes from the people [the Slavs] who were the last to be brought into servitude by Christians. This is just as the ancients used to name their servants after the nations they had conquered, such as the Getae, Dacians, Syrians, and so on. See Meursius, *in his glossary under the word Sclav*. Strictly speaking, however, <term>slavvny</term> <note>original: "slavvny"; a Slavic root</note> signifies "noble," a name that the nation itself claimed; see Theodor Bibliander, *Treatise on the Common Nature of Languages, page 13*. The Poles also have servants whom they call <term>Kmethones</term> <note>original: "Kmethones"; a term for Polish peasants or serfs</note>; regarding them, see the *Polish Statutes* under the word *Kmetho*. For the servants of the Portuguese, see Clenardus *in his letters, page 20*. For the servants of the Cypriots before their Turkish enslavement, see Feurer’s *Travels, page 107* and the *History of Cyprus*. François Estienne, in *The Lusignans, chapter 16, page 68 of the French version*, notes they were called <term>Pariques</term> <note>original: "Pariques"; a class of serfs in medieval Cyprus</note> there. On the Helots of the Spartans, see Leonicus, *Various Histories, book 1, chapter 52*.

V. In our own time, servants exist in many different forms, varying according to the customs of each region. Some pay a census or tax from the estates they cultivate, either in a few coins or groschen—called <term>Silver-rent</term> <note>original: "Silberzinß"</note>—or in several measures of grain, known as <term>Fixed-rent</term> <note>original: "Gült"</note> or <term>Lease-grain</term> <note>original: "Pachtgetreide"</note>. In other places, they provide chickens or geese, known as <term>Smoke-chickens</term> <note>original: "Rauch-Hüner"</note> or <term>Rent-chickens</term> <note>original: "Zinß-Hüner"</note>. Even more frequently, they owe labor services, such as <term>Ploughing and manual labor</term> <note>original: "Pflug und Handtdienste"</note>. See Treutler, *Dispute 2, thesis 4. b* and Henelius, *in Silesiographia, page 79*. Experience will explain these more clearly. However, there is a general legal rule: labor should be demanded from the peasants—who in some places hold a servile status—only in the manner that was agreed upon or observed from ancient times. *Code: In which cases Colonists etc., law 1; Code: On farmers and taxpayers, law 3, section 1.* See also Gail, *Observations, book 2, observation 62* and *Treatise on Arrests, chapter 8, number 12.* 

Wesner uses the terms <term>The Quota</term> <note>original: "Die quottel"</note> and <term>Socage labor</term> <note>original: "Frohndienste"</note>. Forsterus derives the latter from an old Saxon word, <term>Frohn</term>, which in Saxon Law is <term>called "Holy"</term>; see his *Treatise on Roman Jurisdiction, part 2, page 467*. I, however, prefer to say that <term>Frohn</term> means the same as "public" or "common." For example: <term>Common Fast</term> <note>original: "Frohnfast"</note>; <term>Public Messenger</term> <note>original: "Frohnbott"</note>; <term>Common Labor</term> <note>original: "Frohndienst"</note>; <term>Holy Body</term> <note>original: "Frohnleichnam" or Corpus Christi</note>—that most holy body which was given for all; and <term>Public Prison</term> <note>original: "Frohn Vest"</note>. Elsewhere, these labors are called <term>Robota</term> <note>original: "Robota"; the Slavic root for "work" and the origin of the word "robot"</note> in the Polish tongue, and among the French, <term>Corvées</term>. Regarding the origin of this word, see Guy de la Pape, *Decision 472*, and Cujas, who says it is bodily labor and adds the German word <term>Wee</term> <note>original: "Wee"; meaning woe or pain, suggesting labor is a hardship</note>. They are also called *Charrois* [cartage] and *manocueres* [hand-labor]. See Papon, *Book 13 of Arrests, title 6.* I note in passing here that compulsory services are called <term>Common Fasts</term> <note>original: "Frohnfasten"</note> because to perform compulsory service is, in fact, <term>to serve the public</term> <note>original: "Frohnen"</note>.

VI. Indefinite services—that is, <term>unmeasured or daily labor</term> <note>original: "ungemeßne / alltägliche Dienst"</note>—can be determined by the office of a judge or the judgment of a fair man, and reduced to a fixed number. *Based on the law: If a freedman, 30, on the labor of freedmen; Law 22, section 1, on the Rules of Law.* See also the precedents of the Imperial Chamber...

<meta>This page continues the legal discussion of the types of servitude and peasant obligations. It moves from general definitions to specific legal precedents and the limits of labor services (corvée) in the Holy Roman Empire, particularly within Saxony and Austria.</meta>

### On the Species [of Servitude]. Discourse I.
(Signature E)
<meta>catchword: Book</meta>

Erenberg, *Treatise on Subsidies, chapter 4, number 10, etc.* Jean Papon, *in French Rulings, book 13, title 3, ruling 1.* This also pertains to the titles *Lest services be demanded from taxpayers* <note>original Latin: "ne operæ à collator. exigantur"; referencing Book 10 of the Code of Justinian</note> and *Lest peasants be called away for any service* <note>original Latin: "ne rusticani ad ullum obseq. devocentur"; referencing Book 11 of the Code of Justinian</note>. The Elector of Saxony reserves the right to make this determination within his own lands. *New Statutes of Elector August, part 2, chapter 52,* where Daniel Moller provides a *commentary*. The pious man Henry Suso also rebukes the immoderate extraction of services in *The Nine Rocks, chapter 16, folio 380*, as does Zehner in his *Sacred Proverbs*. When the bricks are doubled, Moses comes <note>A proverb derived from Exodus 5: when the burdens imposed by a ruler become unbearable, a liberator or divine relief is imminent</note>. From the fact that labor services have been performed a few times, a <term>prescription</term> <note>The legal acquisition of a right or the fixing of an obligation through long-term continuous practice</note> is not always established; this is especially true if it can be proven that certain acts were performed not out of legal obligation, but as a favor to the lordship (**as a favor to the lordship**) <note>original German: "Der Herrschafft zu gefallen"</note>. See Rutger Ruland, *Decisions on Controversial Law, question 39*. Likewise, services that are undue or unusual are presumed to have been extorted through fear. Ruland, *at the same location, question 40*; Daniel Moller, *Semestria, book 4, chapter 38*. Regarding the length of time required for these to be prescribed, see Modestin Pistor, *volume 1, question 38*; and what the law says if these services have long been paid in money, consult Berlich, *part 2, conclusion 6*.

It has been disputed that when there are two lords of the same place—one holding <term>civil jurisdiction</term> <note>The power to judge non-criminal matters like contracts and property</note> and the other <term>criminal jurisdiction</term> <note>The power to prosecute crimes and impose physical punishment</note>—to which one should the labor services be rendered? Forster argues in favor of the one with criminal jurisdiction, *Treatise on Jurisdiction, folio 473*. I would conclude in favor of the one with civil jurisdiction, unless the territory itself belongs by right to the criminal judge; this follows the argument handed down by Thomas Michael, *Dispute on Jurisdiction, thesis 56, letter D*. Thus, he who holds the "low" jurisdiction is the subject of the one who holds the "high" jurisdiction. Hence, in Austria and Meissen, the Nobles (who are all **landed vassals**) <note>original Latin/German: "Landfassij"; referring to nobles who held their land directly from the territorial prince</note> are bound to redeem the services they demand from their subjects from the Prince (**to the Prince of the land**) <note>original German: "Dem Landesfürsten"</note> with a certain sum of money. 

Can labor or services be ceded by a Lord to another person? Can they be converted into a monetary payment? Can they be divided among multiple heirs? Must they be performed outside the territory? Can someone obligated to daily labor also be forced into night watches or guard duty? Can subjects be forced to perform unusual labor for the building of a castle (**beyond the established service**) <note>original German: "über die gesetzte Dienst"</note>? See Forster, *Treatise on Jurisdiction, folio 470 and many following pages*. Where subjects are bound to the Nobles (**for the construction of the knightly seat**) <note>original German: "zum Bau führen der Rittersitz"</note>, and a certain Noble leaves behind several sons, the peasants cannot be forced to build entirely new houses for each. Daniel Moller, *Semestria, book 2, chapters 32 and 33*. See also Thomas Maulum, who discusses this matter abundantly in *Treatise on Homage, chapter 4, throughout*, and Caspar Ziegler, *under the section on nobles, conclusion 1, number 50, etc.* Waremund notes that such people should be treated with clemency, *on subsidies, folio 54*. There exists an elegant dispute concerning the labor of peasants in *volume 6 of the Basel Disputes*, and singular treatises on servants were written by Bonacossa and Husanus, as well as by Johann Hermann Stamm in the treatise he titled *On Personal Servitude*.

<summary>This page discusses the legal limits of labor services (corvée) owed by peasants to their lords, emphasizing that occasional acts of service do not necessarily create a permanent legal obligation and warning against the abuse of power. It addresses jurisdictional conflicts between different levels of lordship and cites numerous legal authorities on the rights of "landed vassals" and their subjects.</summary>
<keywords>labor services, corvée, legal prescription, civil jurisdiction, criminal jurisdiction, Elector of Saxony, peasant rights, landed vassals, feudal law, servitude</keywords>

# SECOND DISSERTATION
## On the
### Law and Classes of Citizens.

#### CHAPTER 1.
#### What a Citizen is.

<image-desc>Decorative woodcut initial 'I' featuring floral and vine motifs within a square frame.</image-desc>

Ruling is not only a matter of dignity, but it is also an art; indeed, it is the art of arts and the highest of all arts. As the Blessed Chrysostom <note>John Chrysostom (c. 347–407), an influential Early Church Father known for his eloquence</note> writes in his *15th sermon on the Epistle to the Corinthians*: all those placed in a position of dignity, or those who wish to assist those shining with dignity through their counsel, ought to be skilled in the art of ruling—that is, in <term>Politics</term> and <term>Jurisprudence</term>. As Pliny elegantly writes in *book 3, epistle 30*: "Sometimes one must speak of the State; the rarer the occasion for such subject matter, the less it should be omitted. Let our studies contain something that is not humble or base, nor confined merely to private affairs." Furthermore, those disciplines are not light, worn-out, or obvious, but seem to consist in the hidden recesses of nature, the customs of nations, and Civil Law. Hence the Blessed Nazianzenus <note>Gregory of Nazianzus (c. 329–390), a 4th-century Archbishop of Constantinople and theologian</note> says in his *Apologetic*: "It truly seems to me to be the art of arts and the discipline of disciplines to rule a human being, who is certainly, among all living creatures, the most varied in character and diverse in will."

That art consists in the knowledge of the Law, both the law held by those who command and the law held by those who are subjects. Concerning the former, I have already discussed it elsewhere, following the work of others. Now I shall treat the law and condition of subjects, and their various classes. I will not do so as the jurists <note>original: "Icc." for Jurisconsulti</note> commonly do in the section of the <term>Digest</term> <note>The *Pandects* or *Digest*, a major part of the Body of Civil Law (Corpus Juris Civilis) compiled under Emperor Justinian</note> regarding the status of men or in the *Institutes* regarding the Law of Persons, but in a way that joins Politics and Public Law together with Private Law.

Now, human beings—as we consider them here (that is, insofar as they are in some way subject to a Sovereign Power)—are either Citizens or Non-citizens. However, what a "Citizen" actually is remains difficult to define; for often, one who is a citizen in an <term>Oligarchy</term> <note>original: "potentiâ paucorum," literally "the power of the few"</note> is not considered a citizen in a <term>Democracy</term> <note>original: "Populari Statu," literally "a popular state"</note>, as Aristotle writes in *book 3, chapter 1* of his *Politics*. For he...

<summary>This page introduces the "Second Dissertation," shifting from specific legalities of servitude to a philosophical and legal definition of citizenship. The author argues that governing is the "art of arts" and requires a deep understanding of both public and private law.</summary>
<keywords>citizenship, art of ruling, Politics, Jurisprudence, John Chrysostom, Gregory of Nazianzus, Aristotle, Public Law, Private Law</keywords>

<meta>This page continues the "Second Dissertation" regarding the legal definition of citizenship. The previous page established that ruling is an art requiring knowledge of politics and law; this page examines specific historical and legal definitions of who qualifies as a "citizen" across different types of government.</meta>

Dissertation II.

E 2
<meta>catchword: definition</meta>

The same author <note>Aristotle, in his *Politics*</note> in the same place defines a Citizen as: *A participant in Power and Judgment.* In this way, anyone—even those of the smallest means—is considered a citizen in a popular state <note>A "popular state" refers to a democracy</note> where the Republic is shared; however, this is not the case in an oligarchy <note>original: "paucorum potentiâ," meaning the power of the few</note>. Therefore, a person is a citizen in the former, but in the latter, he is not considered one. Since the law and custom of each Republic determine who is a citizen, it is not at all surprising why many have felt differently about the nature of citizens, adapting themselves to the manners and customs of their own Republic. Laurentius Grimaldus <note>Wawrzyniec Grimalda Goślicki (1530–1607), a Polish bishop and political philosopher</note> in his *Treatise on the Optimal Senator, book 1, page 60*, says: some have called every human gathering in a single city by the name of "citizens"; some only those who are descended from free-born and noble citizens; others believe those are called citizens who are born of at least one free-born parent; while some trace the origin back to grandfathers and great-grandfathers, and so on.

Certainly, Aristotle’s definition mentioned above, while it provided many with a reason for error, does not seem to fit all states. For if a citizen were only someone who has access to Power, then in a hereditary Monarchy, only the Prince and his family would enjoy the Right of the City. Nor does this definition fit every Democracy: for there is a type called a <term>Timocracy</term> <note>A state where only those with a certain amount of property or honor are allowed to participate in government</note>, where only the Nobles administer the Offices and Commands, *as I said in the discourse on Democracy, chapter 1, at the end.* Even in the city of Athens itself, where the Republic is said to have been the most popular of all, the fourth class of the lowest poor—that is, the largest part of the People by far—was barred from the Senate and the lottery for Magistrates by the law of Solon <note>Solon (c. 630–560 BCE), the Athenian lawmaker famous for his reforms</note>, as Plutarch testifies. 

And if only those were citizens who possessed the power to give an opinion or to dispense justice, then those who are prohibited from honors, judgments, and the public council, and cannot aspire to them in their own city, would be exiles and foreigners. The Emperor Antoninus <note>Antoninus Pius or Caracalla, referencing the *Constitutio Antoniniana* which granted citizenship to all free men in the Roman Empire</note> in the law *In the World* (regarding the status of men) and Justinian <note>Byzantine Emperor and legal reformer</note> in *Novel 78* would not have rightly decreed that all who are within the Roman world are Roman citizens. Nor would anyone be a citizen in the City of Venice except for the Nobles. In a Monarchy, those who are not citizens, nor absolutely subject to the Prince, are often admitted to judgments and the greatest deliberations: such as the Nobles of Swabia, Franconia, etc., who do not acknowledge the Forum of the Prince at whose court they hold the highest dignities, <note>original: "vnnd dörffen allein recht nemmen vnnd geben / was jhr Ampt anberühret / oder Sie in loco Domicilij contrahiret"</note> and they are permitted only to receive and dispense justice in matters that concern their office, or where they enter into contracts in their place of residence, etc.

II. It remains, therefore, that we propose such a definition or description of a citizen that seems to fit every form of Republic. Namely, if we say that a citizen is a Person bound by the supreme power of another, participating in the Law of the city or in the communion of civil affairs. This description almost agrees with the definition by Heinricus Velstenius—

<summary>This page critiques Aristotle's narrow definition of citizenship (as active participation in rule) by showing it does not apply to monarchies, oligarchies, or even historical democracies like Athens. The author begins to propose a broader definition where citizenship is defined by being subject to a common supreme power and sharing in civil laws.</summary>

<meta>This page continues the discussion from page 35 regarding various scholars' definitions of "citizen." The text transitions from theoretical definitions (like Aristotle's) to more practical, early modern legal definitions, eventually moving into the specific legal act of swearing "Homage."</meta>

# On the Law and the Classes of Citizens.

...definition: who, in his *Centuries of Political Questions, decade 4, question 7*, says a Citizen is: A companion enrolled in a specific city with his family, who enjoys the prerogative of the city. Likewise, Clemens Timpler <note>Clemens Timpler (1563–1624) was a German philosopher and educator known for his systematic textbooks</note>, in *Politics, book 1, chapter 5, in the precepts*, defines a Citizen as: A person bound by the laws of the city and a participant in its privileges. He adds other similar definitions in the same place at the end of question 2. Hoenon has another in *Political Disputations 1, thesis 8*. Jean Bodin <note>Jean Bodin (1530–1596) was a French jurist and political philosopher who pioneered the concept of sovereignty</note>, in *On the Republic, book 1, chapter 6*, says a Citizen is: A free person bound to the supreme power of another. Velstenius says in the same place that this definition wanders too broadly, since it extends to any subject. 

And rightly, Master Ulrich Wolff—my friend, to be cherished with brotherly love—warns in *Political Disputation 12, thesis 17*: that "citizen" is understood in a narrower sense than "subject." For a citizen is indeed subject to the jurisdiction of his magistrate, but there can be a subject who is not a citizen—for instance, a resident <note>original: "Incola"</note> who merely has a home there. Most ingeniously, Bruning, in *On Homage, thesis 2, question 7*, distinguishes the "Subject" from the "Citizen" in this way: the name "subject" designates the actual subjection owed to the Republic; whereas one is called a "citizen" insofar as he is a member of the city before the political structure or command is applied. 

But regarding Aristotle’s and Bodin's definition of a citizen, Canonhiero has more in *The Introduction to Politics, book 1, chapter 4, from the beginning*. Timpler discusses this above in the mentioned question 7. Concerning citizens, residents, and townsmen, not only does Canonhiero have much in the cited place, but also Poncet in *Treatise on Municipal Law*; Tuscus under the word *Citizen*; Reiger in his *Treasury* under the heading *City*; Bodin in book 1, chapter 6; Ayrault in *On Decided Matters* under the title *On the Municipal Law*, page 923; and Master Griphiander in his *Disputation on Subjects and Vassals*.

### CHAPTER II.
### On
### Homage.

Since today, in almost all places, those are considered subjects and citizens who have performed Homage <note>Homage was a formal ritual where a person acknowledged their subservience to a lord or state</note> or the Oath of Subjection—<term>those who have taken or rendered the citizen's oath, the hereditary homage, or the duty to the land</term> <note>original German: "die den Burger Aidt / Erbhuldigung / Landtspflicht / abgelegt oder erstattet"</note>: Maulius, in chapter 1, says because this material is elegant and of the greatest use, I shall hand down some points. Whoever desires a broader explanation, I refer them to Thomas Maulius, Master Johann Bruning, and Ritter, who have performed excellent work on this subject. Master Reinkingk also has something in book 1, class 5, chapter 3; and one may see <term>the Brunswick Homage Acts, and the Hessian and Marburg Acts, in the Volume of Documents, page 34</term> <note>These "Acta" refer to official legal records of swearing-in ceremonies in specific German regions</note>. Also Lehman, book 2, page 89, and page 272, column 2, book 4, chapter 10, toward the end.

<summary>This page compares various legal definitions of citizenship, distinguishing a "citizen" (a privileged member of a community) from a "subject" (anyone under a ruler's power). It also introduces the concept of Homage as the legal mechanism by which these statuses are formally established.</summary>
