<meta>This page continues the legal and etymological discussion regarding the relationship between a subject and a ruler. The previous page concluded by defining a "citizen" as a member of a city or commonwealth focused on a specific goal. This page moves into the specific ritual and legal history of "Homage."</meta>

# Dissertation II.

...goal. I shall, however, first inquire into the origin of the term. The word **Homage** <note>original: "Homagium"</note> was therefore coined, as if it were "man-service" <note>original: "hominium"</note>. For the word **Man** <note>original: "Homo"</note>—much like the German *a Man* <note>original: "ein Mann"</note>, as I stated in my *Treatise on Barons*—is used to refer to a subject, or even a servant. 

There are traces of this usage in the *Glossary* of Lindenbrog <note>Friedrich Lindenbrog (1573–1648) was a legal scholar known for his collections of ancient laws</note>, as well as in the Code of Justinian, book 4, regarding dignities, and the final law regarding village inhabitants. It is called **Homage** because the person performing this oath appears to become the **Man**—that is, the subject—of the Lord to whom it is performed. This is supported by Hotoman <note>François Hotman (1524–1590), a prominent French legal humanist</note> in his *Feudal Lexicon* under the words *Man*, *Homage*, and *Agreement*; also by Meursius in his *Glossary*, page 381; and likewise by Krantz <note>Albert Krantz (1448–1517), a German historian</note> in his *History of the Vandals*, book 3, chapter 36. 

Krantz writes that, "Before the Emperor, he purchased his safety with an immense amount of gold as restitution and by performing 'man-service,' which they now call **Homage**." The same Krantz, in his *History of the Saxons*, book 6, chapter 37, records the following from a letter of Manuel, the Emperor of the Greeks <note>Manuel I Komnenos (reigned 1143–1180)</note>: "The Sultan surrendered himself to our Empire and, having sent ambassadors, pleaded for mercy; he performed 'man-service' and an oath to our Empire, promising to serve us against every man, such that he would be a friend to our friends and an enemy to our enemies." 

The Germans call this by the most appropriate word: **Huldigung** <note>The formal German act of swearing fealty</note>, which is essentially a pact of fidelity—**an agreement to be loyal and true.** Furthermore, John Selden <note>John Selden (1584–1654) was an English jurist and polymath</note> reveals in *The Face of English Law* <note>original: "Iano Anglor."</note>, book 2, page 106, that Homage is elsewhere called **Baronage** <note>original: "Baronagium"</note>. This is used in the same sense I mentioned, because it makes the person performing it the "man" of his Lord. For a **Baron** is simply a **Man**, as I noted in the aforementioned *Treatise on Barons*. Because of this, the French formerly used the term **Great Barony** to mean **a great troop of men** or **a great nobility.** However, since a "Baron" is not just any man, but one of the most eminent, the term "Baronage" is said to be performed only by the high orders of the Kingdom; it perhaps differs from "Homage" in that the latter is sworn by all citizens.

II. Although the word **Homage** is relatively new, the practice itself is ancient, contrary to what some believe. For instance, the author of the *Speculator* <note>Guillaume Durand (c. 1230–1296), a medieval canonist nicknamed "The Speculator"</note>, in the section on Feuds, attributes it to customary law. Yet traces of this custom exist in the Holy Scriptures: Joshua 1, verse 17; 2 Samuel 5, verse 2; 2 Chronicles 23, verse 16; and 1 Maccabees 14, verse 47. 

According to Dio <note>Cassius Dio, a Roman historian</note>, the Roman citizens swore an oath every year on the first of January to the acts of the Emperors. This is beautifully described by Jules César Boulenger <note>A 16th-century Jesuit scholar</note> in book 2 of *On the Roman Empire*, chapter 15. He writes that in Rome, they swore by the laws and words of the Emperors at the Great Altar; in the military camps, they swore at the standards and at the headquarters where the standards were kept. An oath was even sworn under the reign of Tiberius to the words of Sejanus <note>The ambitious commander of the Praetorian Guard</note>, who sought to seize the Empire. After the death of Sejanus, this was forbidden; Dio notes that the Senate decreed that no one should swear an oath in the name of anyone but the Emperor. Sometimes, soldiers performing the oath would hold swords to their own necks to show that they would face death before their loyalty to the sacred oath <note>original: "Sacramentum"</note> was abandoned. See Ammianus, book 21.

E 3

<meta>catchword: Iussi-</meta>

<summary>This page explores the etymology of "Homage," linking it to the concept of becoming a "man" or subject of a lord, and argues that while the term is medieval, the practice of swearing loyalty to a ruler is an ancient tradition found in both the Bible and Roman history.</summary>

<meta>This page continues the legal discussion of the "Oath of Fidelity" or "Homage." While the previous page defined the etymology of "Homage" as "man-service," this page examines who is required to take the oath (vassals, citizens, and even high-ranking princes) and who is exempt (women, children, and often the clergy). The catchword "widows" (Latin: viduæ) at the bottom indicates the next page will likely discuss the status of women without husbands.</meta>

### Concerning the Law and the Ranks of Citizens.

And they were all ordered to swear solemnly in his name, with swords held to their necks, under dire curses; they swore in prescribed formulas <note>original: *verbis conceptis*, a Roman legal term for a formal, set oath</note> that they would endure all outcomes for him (Julian) <note>Julian the Apostate (reigned 361–363 AD), the last pagan Roman Emperor</note>, even to the point of sacrificing their lives if necessity demanded it. In this same way, the Jews swore to Herod, as Baronius reports for the year 2 AD <note>Cesare Baronius (1538–1607) was a Cardinal and a central figure in Catholic church history writing</note>.

III. This Oath is performed by all **Subjects**. (Bruning, *Theses*, 290). Hence, it is considered to prove subjection and to be a consequence of a superior’s authority. (Heinrich Bruning, *Disputation on Universities*, thesis 33, volume 4 of the *Basel Disputations*.) This is true, although it also has its limitations. For instance, Cologne, Speyer, and other cities perform an oath to their Bishops, and Hamburg to the Duke of Holstein, while preserving their liberty (which is discussed elsewhere). Regularly, however, all subjects are compelled to swear in this manner. This is expressly held in the **Peace of Constance** <note>A 1183 treaty between Emperor Frederick Barbarossa and the Italian cities of the Lombard League</note>, in the section regarding our vassals in the *Usage of Feuds*, stating that **Vassals** and **Citizens** from the age of sixteen up to seventy must offer the Sacrament of Fidelity—unless they are such persons to whom it should and can be remitted without fraud. And where **Land-Tenure** <term>Lansassitatus: a Germanic legal concept where a person is settled on the land of a lord, owing him certain duties</term> is in use, such as in Saxony, Bavaria, Austria, etc., both the **Nobles** and the **Ignoble** (or commoners) swear alike. (Maulius, *On Homage*, chapter 1, number 4 and following.) Indeed, even Counts, Princes, and Marquises swear if they recognize a superior—such as the Princes of Silesia to the King of Bohemia, and so on. They even say that a brother of the Lord is bound to it if he has a home and property in the place. (Moditius, on the *Plebiscitum*, question 84, number 1.)

IV. **Women** are exempted: for it is a "manly duty" <note>original: *officium virile*, a legal principle suggesting certain public duties were reserved for men</note>; yet they are no less citizens for that reason. So too are **Children**: Kirchner proves that they are also citizens by reason of law, against Aristotle who denied this and labeled them "imperfect citizens." (Kirchner, *Disputation on the Republic*, 7, thesis 2, title C; see also Joachim Perion, *Treatise on Roman and Greek Magistrates*, page 310.) For immaturity of age does not suspend the rights of citizenship any more than it suspends the rights of liberty; for these rights immediately take up those who have been conceived and not yet brought forth, let alone those already born. (Legal Code, *On the Status of Men*, law 7.) Indeed, birth, not age, makes one a citizen. (Legal Code, *To the Municipalities*, law 1.) In certain places, such as Strasbourg <note>original: *Argentinæ*, the Latin name for Strasbourg</note>, young men who are still under their father's legal power also perform the oath of subjection. (Bruning, *Theses*, 322.) Otherwise, **Homage** performed by a husband is understood to have been performed by the wife as well, and that performed by a father also binds the sons. (Bruning, *Theses*, 327.) Since the father is the head of the family and represents it. (Bellarmine, on Psalm 1, verse 1.)

V. **Clergy**, or Ministers of the Church, likewise do not perform this oath in many places, and yet they hold the rights of citizens and are considered subjects. And certainly in this Duchy, by virtue of the Ecclesiastical Ordinance, <term>The Great Church Ordinance</term> <note>original German: *der grossen Kirchen Ordnung*</note>, the Ministers of the Church have their rights...

<summary>This page details the legal requirements of the Oath of Fidelity, noting that while almost all subjects (including high nobility) are required to swear it, women, children, and clergy are often exempted despite maintaining their status as citizens.</summary>
<keywords>Oath of Fidelity, Homage, Citizenship, Peace of Constance, Legal Status of Women, Legal Status of Children, Clerical Immunity, Vassalage</keywords>

--- Page 41 ---
<meta>This page continues the legal discussion of the "Oath of Fidelity" or "Homage." While the previous page established that all subjects are generally required to swear an oath to their sovereign, this page examines specific exceptions and variations in citizenship rights among the clergy, university professors, and public officials.</meta>

### Dissertation II.

Widows and children possess the rights of citizenship where they are under the Ministry <note>original: *in Ministerio*; likely referring to the ecclesiastical or administrative service of the territory</note>. Thus, in this very City, although academic citizens and even Professors do not enjoy the Privileges of the town citizens—for they cannot buy immovable property <note>real estate</note>, they cannot sell wine, nor use the communal pastures as others do—nevertheless, the Professors of Theology who preach and distribute the Holy Sacraments enjoy the full Right of citizens. In certain places, even clerics are compelled to swear oaths, although it is prohibited in the Decretals <note>original: *c. nimis extr. de Iurejurand. & in c. solita d. major. & obed.*—these are references to Canon Law (the Decretals of Gregory IX) which argued for the independence of the church from secular oversight</note> that clerics, who do not hold temporal lands from temporal Lords, should be bound to perform homage to them. Indeed, a few years ago in England, a great dispute took place against the English, made famous by the writings of the most Illustrious Bellarmine <note>Robert Bellarmine (1542–1621) was a Jesuit Cardinal and a major figure in the Counter-Reformation; he debated King James I of England over the 1606 Oath of Allegiance</note>: whether Clerics and other Catholics are bound by law to the Oath of Fidelity? It is likewise argued most fiercely whether Clerics are subjects. This question was also raised between the Supreme Pontiff Paul V and the Venetians <note>Referring to the Venetian Interdict of 1606–1607, a diplomatic struggle between the Papacy and the Republic of Venice over secular vs. religious jurisdiction</note>, as I have discussed elsewhere in this treatise in the dissertation on the *Rights of Majesty* and the dissertation on *Information*, where I deal with Religious Superiors. I do not make this dispute my own now. Let it suffice to indicate that our own people affirm this, based on the argument of **1 Peter 2, verse 17**, where it is held: "be subject to every human ordinance," <note>original: *subditi estote omni humanæ ordinationi*</note> and there Winckelmann <note>Johannes Winckelmann (1551–1626), a Lutheran theologian</note> busies himself with teaching this at length.

VI. It comes into inquiry: whether an Official in any place holds the Right of citizenship? This is discussed by Paris de Puteo <note>A 15th-century Neapolitan jurist famous for his work on legal trials and duels</note> in the *Treatise on Syndicates*, in the section on the "citizenship of the official," through the whole page 260. There he teaches that "The Power" (for that is what the Italians call the Official) ought to be treated as a citizen, and is like a citizen with his family for the duration of his office. For he ought to be punished with the penalty of citizens for a crime committed in office, where the penalty would be different for a foreigner: because he is the head of the citizens. And therefore, he ought also to enjoy the same Privilege: according to the law *regarding the status of military wills* <note>original: *l. quòd ad statum. ff. de Testam. Militar.*, a reference to the Digest of Roman Law</note>. So too, a statute of the city speaking of a "citizen" applies to officials as well. The *Gloss on the Law of Citizens* <note>original: *Gloss. in l. cives. C. de Incol. lib. 11.*</note> says the same. They also say that a lecturing Doctor <note>A university professor</note> enjoys the Privileges of the city and all favors, according to Benius <note>Paolo Beni (1552–1625), a professor and humanist scholar</note>, though this is not practiced among us.

VII. Here another doubt arises: whether one who has not performed homage, but nevertheless enjoys the right of the city and conducts himself as a citizen, is bound by all the statutes written specifically for a citizen? For example: He who is admitted into the registry of students <note>original: *album studiosorum*</note> is compelled to swear that even after he has departed from here, he will appear if summoned by the Rector. Suppose someone was a student for a long time, or was the son of a Professor, and enjoyed the privileges of a student; but by some unknown wrong, or by singular cleverness, he did not perform the Oath, nor did the Rector demand it as should have been done. The question is: is he bound to appear if summoned? It might seem that he is not, since one who [has not taken] the Oath...

It highlights historical conflicts between church and state, such as the disputes involving Cardinal Bellarmine and the Republic of Venice.</summary>

<meta>This page continues the legal analysis of the Oath of Fidelity, moving from the specific status of clergy and professors to the general obligations of those who act as citizens, as well as the legal timeline (prescription) for demanding such oaths.</meta>

# On Law and the Ranks of Citizens.
<meta>catchword: IX. Laws-</meta>

...did not perform it, protected by this unique clause, nor can it be said that he is so specifically bound. But it is better that we say the contrary: since, according to the wording of the Oath, every academic citizen—and whoever is considered a citizen and behaves as a citizen—is enrolled. For the Oath does not introduce a new obligation, but only makes the old one firmer. And Dauth <note>Tobias Dauth (1564–1621), a German jurist</note> teaches in his *repetition on the chapter 'quamvis,' folio 414*, that the Oath is considered to be performed by the act itself if someone accepts any "sworn office" <note>original: *officium jurabile*; a position that by its nature requires an oath of office</note> (as he calls it). Thus, in this case also, he who behaves as a citizen and is recognized as such is judged to have sworn in actual fact. Servants and **journeymen craftsmen** <note>original German: *Handwercks Gesellen*</note> are also compelled to swear to certain articles in some places, such as Strasbourg. According to Bruning <note>thes. 327</note>, this also occurs in this Duchy by virtue of the **Land Ordinance** <note>original German: *Landsordnung*; the territorial constitution or code of laws</note>, even though they are not full citizens.

VIII. Now I shall say in a few words who can demand this Oath. Here it must first be noted that it can be demanded even if it has not been performed for a long time, and no **prescription** <term>prescription: a legal principle (similar to a statute of limitations) where a right is gained or lost due to the passage of time</term> stands in the way. See Cravetta, *cons. 803.* Indeed, it does not help subjects if they argue they are not bound to this kind of Oath of Fidelity because it was never performed by them or their ancestors. They cannot claim to have acquired any prescription from this unless it was clear that, having been requested, they had refused to perform it on the grounds that they were free and exempt from that jurisdiction, and the time for prescription had subsequently passed. This is noted by Baldus <note>Baldus de Ubaldis (1327–1400), a highly influential jurist of civil law</note> *on the chapter 'licet,' in the 2nd note*, where also Alvarotus *in the 2nd note on 'if there were a controversy over a fief'*, and Alphonso Moditio *in the section on the 'Plebiscitum,' question 84, number 2*. For in those matters which are of **mere faculty** <term>mere faculty: legal acts that a person is free to perform at their own discretion; rights that do not expire simply because one chooses not to use them</term> and depend upon our will, prescription does not run through non-use <note>original: *gloss in l. qui luminibus. ff. de servit. Urb. prædior.*</note>. And although our learned Doctors anxiously investigate this question—whether liberty can be prescribed against this kind of Oath—as can be seen in Master Bruning's *On Homage, thesis 341 and following*, where the arguments of both sides are proposed with laudable zeal, and Heinrich Bruning takes the affirmative side in his *dissertation on the Laws of Universities, thesis 33*. Nevertheless, I think a distinction should be established between those who still admit they are subjects, and those who claim a full exemption. Those who still exist as subjects are compelled to swear, unless it has been waived for them by a contract or a unique privilege. For subjection can exist even without a sworn promise, just as a **Fief** <note>original: *Feudum*; land held on condition of feudal service</note> can exist without an oath of loyalty. See Cravetta *consil. 452, num. 11.* For otherwise, since it is a matter of "mere faculty," non-use does not seem to result in the oath not being owed; since it pertains to the **Right of Sovereignty** <note>original: *Iure Majestatis*; the inherent rights and powers of a supreme ruler</note>, as I said in my *Treatise on the Rights of Political Sovereignty*. Whether indeed full liberty can be obtained through prescription is a matter for another place, and I have dealt with that concern in my *Treatise on Appeals*.

<summary>This page discusses how the obligation to an oath of loyalty is inherent to citizenship and subjection, regardless of whether the physical oath was taken. It also argues that a ruler's right to demand this oath does not expire over time (prescription) unless a subject has explicitly and successfully claimed exemption.</summary>

# Dissertation II.

F

<meta>catchword: XI. Homa-</meta>

IX. The Emperor can demand this oath, and all the Estates perform it for him: thus the Princes, and even the Electors themselves, as I have stated elsewhere, exist as liege vassals <note>original: *Vasalli Ligij*; vassals who owe their primary and supreme allegiance to a specific lord</note> and true subjects of the Emperor and the Empire. Furthermore, the Princes and other Estates of the Empire typically claim this demanding of Homage <note>original: *Homagium*; a formal public acknowledgment of feudal allegiance</note> from their own subjects as a symbol and badge of their unique preeminence. Knichen, *on territory, chapter 3, number 245.* Vultejus, *volume 3, Marburg counsels, counsel 35, number 30 and following.* Although sometimes it is also performed for municipalities, and it is called a **Citizen's Oath** <term>original: *BurgerAidt*; an oath sworn to a city to obtain its privileges, immunities, or the rights of a citizen</term>. <insert>This consists of civic liberty and duties: such as the rights of community, brewing, and pouring; residency, pasture, the driving and grazing of cattle, and other common uses and profits.</insert> Gylman, *volume 5, symphorem. relat. 10, folio 47, number 1 and following.* And so, it must be investigated here: Can someone, by the very fact that they hold high and low jurisdiction <note>original: *merum mixtumque Imperium*; the full power of the law, including the authority to punish crimes</note> and simple jurisdiction through a grant of a fief or other title, demand Homage? On this matter, one may look to Bruning, *here, thesis 294, etc.*, who denies it. For Homage implies sovereignty <note>original: *superioritatem*</note> and has nothing in common with mere civil or criminal jurisdiction. However, all these things depend on custom and usage. And sometimes, those who possess High Jurisdiction, or the **Zent** <term>original: *Zent*; a historical term for a high criminal court district</term>, are accustomed to having such an oath performed to them in that name. Otherwise, since the oath is one of sovereignty or immediate jurisdiction—not of high jurisdiction alone—those Nobles who are free and immediate <note>original: *immediati*; those holding their land directly from the Emperor without any middle-man lord</note> can also demand it, even if someone else holds high jurisdiction there and only the **Bailiwick's Authority** <term>original: *Vogteitliche Oberkeit*; the administrative or protective authority of a bailiff over a territory</term> belongs to them, provided it is not attached to the right of the territory. Furthermore, Homage is performed to both <insert>the hereditary lord and the pledge-holders, if the city has been pawned under a pact of redemption.</insert> Wehner, *under the word Landtshuldigung.*

X. Homage is personal, and it cannot be demanded from one who is a simple possessor of property: for in the name of these things, no one performs an absolute oath unless they also "keep their hearth" <note>original: *foveat larem*; a classical expression meaning to maintain a permanent home or residence</note> there. Menoch, *counsel 943, number 27.* Property alone does not usually make a person a subject. *law: libertus, section: sola, on Municipal law.* Vultejus, *on Fiefs, chapter 6, number 1, book 1.* But sometimes the oath of subjection is "real" <note>legal term meaning the obligation is attached to the "thing" (the land) rather than the person</note>: Hence we see many in the Roman Empire who, by reason of their property, perform Homage to a Prince; yet, by reason of their own person, they remain entirely free and subject to no one but the Emperor. Wehner, *under the word Erbhuldigung.* Bruning, *on universities, thesis 34.* That oath is most aptly called an **Oath of Assurance** <term>original: *Assecurationis juramentum*; a guarantee that the land-holder will not act against the lord's interests</term>. Bruning, *on homage, thesis 305.* For it makes the lord secure, ensuring that the possessor of such property will attempt nothing against the state, and will recognize and fulfill the duties imposed upon those properties.

<summary>This page discusses who has the legal right to demand an oath of homage, distinguishing between sovereignty and mere judicial authority, and explains how some people owe homage based on the land they own even if they are personally free.</summary>

<meta>This page continues the legal discussion regarding the Oath of Fidelity, moving from who may demand the oath to the specific wording and legal implications of the "Homage" ceremony itself. ### On the Law and the Ranks of Citizens.

XI. The form of **Homage** <note>original: *Homagij*; a formal public acknowledgment of feudal allegiance</note> is prescribed in Title 5 of the second book of Feudal Laws. According to our customs, individual magistrates prescribe the form. Forster, *On Jurisdiction, part 2, folio 463, number 15.* The common form is as follows: <note>original: *das er wolle underthänig/ gehorsamb/ hold/ trew unnd gewertig sein/ seines Herren frommen fördern/ unnd schaden wenden/ auch in allen sachen/ was ein getrewer Landtsäß und Underthan/ seinem rechten Erbherren und Landtsfürsten zuthun schuldig ist/ getrewlich thun soll unnd wolle/ alle arge list unnd gefehrde außgeschlossen.*</note> "that he intends to be humble, obedient, favorable, faithful, and diligent; to promote his Lord's benefit and avert harm; and also in all things that a faithful resident and subject is bound to do for his rightful hereditary Lord and Prince, he shall and will do faithfully, all wicked cunning and danger excluded." Wehner, *in the same place.* These words almost entirely agree with the ancient formula of subjection by which vassals in France were also bound. Claude Fauchet, *book 2, On the Origin of Dignities, chapter 6.*

Similarly, Wehner writes aptly at the end of that section: what law a <term>Prince of the Land</term> <note>original: *Landtsfürst*; a sovereign ruler of a specific territory within the Empire</note> or another to whom such homage was performed holds over those who swear depends on both custom and the specific facts of the case, citing the *Speculator* there, *title on Feuds, section 2, number 33.* Nevertheless, it seems the Lord has a well-founded intention to be able to demand from such subjects all those things which follow subjection by the disposition of law and are deemed to belong to the right of command—unless the contrary is proven by the subjects. And for the most part, in Oaths of Homage, something is usually reserved for the subjects, or the subjection is limited. Indeed, in many places, the Princes themselves have in turn sworn to the Estates; on which matter Bruning, *on Homage*, treats exactly and excellently throughout. 

Wehner also rightly says in the same place: a distinction is made between <term>temperate homage</term> <note>original: *gemäßigte Huldigung*; a limited oath of loyalty often restricted by specific conditions or privileges</note> and the common <term>homage of the land</term> <note>original: *Landshuldigung*; a general, standard oath of subjection to a territorial ruler</note>. From this, it appears that the form of Homage pertains to the fundamental law: and its various phrasings result in citizens being regarded either as absolute subjects or as non-absolute subjects. Those who are absolute subjects cannot misuse even their own property, let alone themselves; a prohibition is made against them transferring their home elsewhere, or traveling abroad without permission, much less taking up the citizenship of another state without permission and the payment of a fee. Camillo Borelli, *on Magistrates, book 4, chapter 9.* Bodin, *on the Republic, book 1, chapter 6, number 58.* 

For the most part, a free faculty of leaving is granted to subjects; but the Treasury retains a certain portion of their goods, which is usually a tenth, commonly called the <term>departure tax</term> <note>original: *Abzug*; a tax paid to a lord or city when a subject moved their wealth out of the jurisdiction</note>; on this matter, see Jacob Curtius, *volume 1, conjectures, book 3, chapter 36 and following.* Others have "the justice of free passage," meaning that one may move out of their citizenship at will, with their belongings and goods, without the payment of any departure tax. The citizens of Württemberg hold such a right by virtue of the <term>Treaty of Tübingen</term> <note>original: *Tübingischen Vertrags*; a landmark 1514 constitutional agreement between Duke Ulrich and the estates of Württemberg, often called the first written constitution in Germany</note>, which is the fundamental law of this Duchy; and in it, its primary privileges are contained.

XII. In this view, the oath is not simple, but is either respective or conditional. Bruning, *here, thesis 43 and following.* A respective oath is that which is made by subjects who, in certain matters, owe obedience to the magistrate to whom they offer it:

It highlights the Treaty of Tübingen as a key legal instrument providing Württemberg citizens with the rare right of free movement without exit taxes.</summary>

Dissertatio II.

F 2

<meta>catchword: then [tum],</meta>

...in certain cases, however, they are **immediately subject to the Empire**: just as in the case of **regal rights** <note>original: *regalium*; the specific rights and powers belonging to a sovereign, such as minting coins or collecting tolls</note> and other rights that depend immediately on the Empire. Such is also the case for those who are free by reason of their person, but subject by reason of their property. See the *Acts of Ortenburg against Bavaria*. Likewise, such is the case with what Cologne, Speyer, and other cities provide to the Bishop. See my treatise *On the Rights of Imperial Cities*. Subjection is **conditional** where it is limited, or made under the condition <note>original: *das sie ihrem Herren Trew vnd Hold sein wollen / solang sie von jhme / bey ihren Gnaden / Gerechtigkeiten / Freyheiten vnd allen hergebrachten gewohnheiten gelassen werden.*</note> “that they wish to be true and loyal to their Lord, as long as they are left by him in their graces, rights, liberties, and all traditional customs.” And such an oath is especially a **contract**, binding on both sides and reciprocal. Bruning, *theses 152 and 398*; and the principle applies there: **a faithful Lord, a faithful servant** <note>original: *fidelis Dominus, fidelis servus*; implying that if the lord is not faithful to his obligations, the servant's obligation is also affected</note>. Sometimes it is even agreed between the Prince and the subjects that before **Homage** <note>original: *Homagium*</note> is performed, the magistrate and the subjects must peacefully settle all controversies that have arisen until now between the predecessors of the magistrates and the subjects. Bruning, *extensively in theses 331 and following*.

### XIII.

And it follows from what has been argued thus far that the ancient and old form of the oath must be preserved: lest through a new form, a new burden might perhaps be introduced, and thereby something be extorted through **harassment** <note>original: *concussionem*; a legal term for the extortion of money or services by an official through misuse of their authority</note>. *Code: on those things done through force or fear.* Jacobus de Sancto Georgio, *on homage, noting one point.* Forster, *On Roman Jurisdiction, part 2, page 463, number 151.* Maulius, *On Homage, chapter 1, number 18.* And if the accustomed form of the oath of obedience is not proposed, the subjects are able to refuse it. *Decretals: on majority and obedience; on the excesses of Prelates; on custom.* Speculator, *title on Feuds, section "since," and note that it ought not.* Schrader, *part 10, section 5, number 98 and counsel 21, number 182.* Nor is that oath valid if it was performed under compulsion against the liberty or public utility of the community's law. Rutger Ruland, *Decisions on Controversial Law, question 21.* For even a confession made against liberty is not valid—much less when someone has given their word under compulsion that they are not free. *Code: on liberal causes*, where the **Gloss** <note>Standard marginal commentary on legal texts</note> and Baldus note this. Ruland reports in the same place that it was concluded in the **Imperial Chamber Court** <note>original: *Camerâ*; the *Reichskammergericht*, one of the two highest judicial institutions in the Holy Roman Empire</note> in the case of the **Town of Wangen against all the Bakers of Labiß**, etc., that such an oath is void by the law itself. And likewise, an oath of fidelity and obedience performed outside of or against any standard form is presumed to have been extorted by fear: and it cannot prejudice either the person themselves or their successors. Baldus, *book 1, number 1, and there Afflictis, number 16, on how a Vassal ought to swear.* Schrader, *in the said counsel, number 183, and section 5, number 99*, where he says: that subjects are able to take action against the lord and claim that they took that oath through fear,

It emphasizes that the wording of the oath must follow ancient customs and that any oath extorted through fear or force is legally void.</summary>

# On the Law and Ranks of Citizens.

<meta>catchword: until [donec]</meta>

...then, that an impression or error was made: and therefore they petition that this oath, insofar as it exceeds the customary form, be either retracted or declared that they themselves are not bound by this oath, and that they and their descendants are not held to perform that oath.

XIV. Unless something else has been introduced by custom, agreement, or prescription, **homage** <term>homagium: a formal public acknowledgment of feudal allegiance</term> must be performed by means of a **physical oath** <note>original: *corporali iuramento*; an oath sworn while physically touching a sacred object, such as a Bible or relics</note>. *c. 1. & ibi Andr. de Isern. Iacob. d. Belvis. Bald. Alvarot. Præpos. Matth. d. Afflict. & Dd. qualiter iur. Vasall. Dom. fidel. c. 1. §. Vasalli nostri. & ibi Bald. de Pac. constant.* Furthermore, homage is to be performed by subjects whenever the person of the Lord has changed, whether by death or in any other manner. *arg. c. 1 d. cap. cons.* Likewise, whenever anyone becomes a new subject of any lord *arg. d. c. 1.* In that case, however, when the person of the lord is changed, the subjects are not bound to go to the lord and offer the performance of homage; rather, they wait for the Lord's demand, and it is sufficient if they do not refuse when the lord demands it. 

The penalty for a subject who refuses to perform this oath to the lord without a just cause is the loss of all property. *Schrader. consil. 21. num. 167. & seq. ac facit c. 1. §. qui Pacem de pace iuram. firm.* For a subject who refuses to do that which he is bound to do by law is judged to be living rebelliously: and every rebel deserves to lose both **feudal property** <note>land held from a superior lord in exchange for service</note> and **allodial property** <note>original: *bona allodialia*; land owned outright without any superior lord</note>; *Maulius. cap. 1. num. 24.* This loss of property occurs by the law itself, and thus from the day the crime was committed, the guilty party loses ownership of his things by the law itself, as in cases of **treason** <note>original: *læsæ Majestat. crimen*; an offense against the dignity or safety of the sovereign</note>. *l. fin. C. ad L. Iul. Majest. l. 2 ff. d. accusat. Clarus. §. sentent. §. læsæ Majestat. crimen. n. 9.* However, the lord cannot take away property from his subject who refuses to perform this oath on his own authority: rather, there is a need for a summons and a declaratory sentence. *Ant. Gabriel. lib. 7. commun. conclus. 12. Gail. de pac. public. lib. 2. cap. 3. num. 10.* 

Subjects can be compelled to perform this oath by the office of a judge, or by custom, condition, or by law. *l. litibus 9. & l. si colon. de agricol. & censit. lib. 11. Cod.* There are also **interdicts** <note>legal prohibitions or court orders to restore rights</note> granted to the lord if he is in possession and the subjects refuse to swear; as discussed in similar passages by Eguinar. Baro, *4. de benefic. cap. 7.* Rosenthal. *cap. 12. conclus. 12. num. 51.* Similarly, **penal mandates** <note>original: *Mandata pœnalia*; court orders that carry a specific penalty if ignored</note> can be obtained in the Imperial Chamber <note>The supreme court of the Holy Roman Empire</note> against subjects unwilling to swear, **or to render hereditary homage** <note>original: *oder die Erbhuldigung erstatten*</note>, if the lord is immediately subject to the Empire. See the *Author on questions in controversial law, or the precedents of the Imperial Chamber, under the word "subject" there: subjects unwilling to swear.* For proceedings can be taken against subjects who refuse homage without a just cause through the arrest of property and persons; their property can be seized, their persons committed to prison, the fruits of the land blocked, the distraint of livestock carried out, and rural estates with all their equipment essentially sequestered, for as long as, until

See the notes on Cravetta, *counsel 869.* This principle was cruelly neglected in the Palatinate <note>A region in southwestern Germany that suffered immense devastation during the Thirty Years' War</note>, which brought this once most flourishing region to utter ruin.

### CAP. III.

### *In which various divisions of citizens are proposed.*

The **Right of Citizenship** <term>Ius civitatis: the legal status and privileges belonging to a member of a political community</term> is twofold: universal, which is common to the whole Republic (in this sense, all Germans are citizens of the Roman Empire), and particular. This particular right is likewise considered in two ways: namely, as belonging to a certain Province, or to a specific town or village—for example, a citizen of Württemberg, a citizen of Tübingen, or a citizen of Derendingen. Indeed, all persons living within the **Holy Roman Empire** <note>original: *Imperio Romano-Germanico*</note> and its borders are subject to the Empire, with no exceptions, even if they are **mediated** <note>original: *mit mittel*; a legal term meaning they are subjects of a local lord who is in turn a subject of the Emperor, rather than being "immediate" subjects of the Emperor himself</note>. And although these mediated subjects perform homage and an oath of subjection to their local lord, the authority of the Emperor and the Empire as the superior power is always understood to be reserved in every oath and obligation. *Chapter "Venientes," and the gloss there on "Oaths"; Octavianus Cacheranus, "Piedmontese Decisions," 78, number 10; Modestinus Pistoris, "Counsel" 24, number 26, volume 1; Prosper Farinaccius, "On Crimes," book 1, title question 17, number 16,* and those cited there. *Imperial Recess of the Year 1555, the section "and those shall."*

II. Furthermore, the right of citizenship and its privileges are again twofold in another way: namely, the **full citizen** and the **partial citizen** <note>original: *Civis pleni & non pleni*</note>. I believe this distinction must be defined and determined according to the judgment, custom, law, or practice of each individual city. Certainly, **Aristotle** <note>The Greek philosopher (384–322 BC) whose "Politics" defined a citizen as one who shares in the administration of justice and in the holding of office</note> seems to refer to full citizenship when he defines a citizen—as I mentioned above—as one capable of holding magisterial office and participating in public deliberations and judgments. Those who say that in Venice only the patricians are citizens, or that in Poland only the Nobles are citizens, point toward this same definition. Thus **Keckermann** <note>Bartholomäus Keckermann (1572–1609), a prominent German scholar of political science</note>, in his *Final Practical Disputation, second to last problem*, says that the Venetian Republic is not an Aristocracy, but a Democracy; this is because he considers only those who are admitted to the Great Council to be citizens. He relies on the authority of **Contarini** <note>Gasparo Contarini (1483–1542), a Venetian diplomat whose works praised the Venetian constitution</note>, who writes: "It was rightly provided by our Ancestors that the common people should not be admitted to the assembly of citizens." Lest the people complain of being excluded, Contarini says, the definition of a citizen was made with no less prudence based on nobility of lineage rather than wealth—

<meta>This page continues the investigation into the legal definition of citizenship. It shifts from the general classification of subjects in the Empire to the specific scholarly debates regarding who truly qualifies as a "citizen," critiquing rigid Classical definitions in favor of a more nuanced hierarchy that includes both "full" and "lesser" citizens.</meta>

# On the Right and Orders of Citizens.

...assessed by the census of their estates. But if this were the rule, then surely very few would be counted as Venetian citizens. Furthermore, in a hereditary Monarchy, no one except the Prince and his family could be called citizens. Indeed, both Gasparo Contarini <note>original: "Contarenus"; a Venetian diplomat and cardinal who wrote extensively on the Venetian constitution</note> and Bartholomew Keckermann <note>A prominent German Reformed theologian and philosopher</note> were equally deceived by Aristotle’s definition <note>Aristotle famously defined a citizen as one who shares in the administration of justice and in offices, which effectively excluded the majority of people in larger states</note>. This difficulty is best remedied by those who distinguish between **full citizens** and **citizens with limited rights** <term>cives plenos & minus plenos: a legal distinction between those who hold political office and those who only enjoy the city's protection and civil rights</term>. Similarly, Keckermann, in the *final problem of his political disputations*, argues that since all and only Nobles in Poland have access to the governance of the State, they alone are citizens; for this reason, he believes the Polish Republic has a Democratic, rather than an Aristocratic, character <note>This refers to the "Golden Liberty" of Poland, where the vast number of nobles—roughly 10% of the population—exercised political power, creating a "Nobles' Democracy"</note>. He has more reason for making the Polish Nobles the sole citizens than for doing the same with the Venetian patricians, since the number of the former is infinite, while the common people there are held in the place of servants. Yet, I still believe that in the cities of Poland, there are many who are both free and citizens.

III. Conversely, in view of this "lesser" right of citizenship, Philipp Hoenon <note>original: "Hoennon"; a jurist and professor at the Academy of Herborn</note> in his *political disputations* even includes servants and serfs in the count of citizens. Although under Roman Law, slaves were held as "nothings" and occupied the legal place of the dead, and although Aristotle in his *Politics* counts them as mere tools of the household and equates them almost with cattle, Jean Bodin <note>A famous French jurist and political philosopher</note> rightly judges in his *Six Books of the Commonwealth* that servants as well as free men should be granted citizenship. Clemens Timpler <note>A German philosopher</note> agrees with him on this. For it is the height of arrogance—indeed, it is impious—to treat a "divine animal" <note>A common Renaissance term for a human being, emphasizing that man is made in God's image</note> like a beast of the field. Even in the human body, there are some members so shameful that no one dares name them without a blush, yet they do not cease to be parts of the body. Furthermore, it does not align with right reason to cast down beneath the condition and dignity of humanity those servants who are subject to political authority and who usually assist and promote the Republic with their labor and ministry, depriving them of the use of all civil privileges and benefits. This is especially true since nature created all people equal and free, and fit for cultivating civil society with others. Ancient legislators seem to have excluded servants from the number of citizens only because they were under the absolute power of their masters, who could even abuse or kill them; however, even under Roman Law, this was gradually changed after the Christian religion was introduced, as I mentioned above in the section on servants. It is a privilege of certain Imperial cities that they may <note>original: "vnnd Amptleuth so nicht rechnung erstattet / zu Burger auffnemmen"; translated from the German source cited in the text</note> accept even servants and officials who have not yet rendered an account of their service as citizens. See Lehman, *last chapter, section 70 and following.*

IV. Furthermore, in many Imperial cities of Germany, there is a great difference between full and limited citizens. Thus, in some places, foreigners do not become full citizens except after a long period of residency. Joachim...

### Dissertation II.
<meta>catchword: tem-</meta>

Krantz, in the *Political Orchard, book 4, chapter 1, page 94*, notes that formerly citizens were classified as **Citizens**, **Free Citizens**, **Nobles**, **Clerks**, or **Decurions** <term>Decuriales: officials belonging to the city council</term>, who practiced no trade; others were mere residents or craftsmen. Lehmann, book 4, chapter 14, notes they were later divided into **Noble families** <note>original German: "Geschlechter"</note> and commoners, including the Minters and Household Associates <note>original: "Münzern/ Haußgenossen"; these were specific high-status guilds often associated with the patriciate</note>. The **craftsmen** were only inhabitants, not full citizens, and were mostly of a servile condition. When these craftsmen entered into marriages with free persons, various legal disputes and the burdens of servitude arose; therefore, Emperor Henry V freed the people of Speyer from this burden. (Lehmann, book 4, chapter 22, page 354, and throughout book 6).

Thus, as Kyllinger testifies in his *Discourse on Joint Inheritance*, discourse 3, number 105, it is a frequent occurrence in Augsburg that only the **patrician families** <note>original: "Geschlechter"</note> are called by the name of **Citizens**; this is done "by way of excellence" <note>original Greek: κατ’ ἐξοχὴν (kat’ exokhēn)</note> and because of their preeminence. From this, the public buildings there intended for celebrating the weddings of patricians and those associated with them through marriage to patrician daughters—whom they call "from the Greater Society" <note>original: "von der mehrern Gesellschaft"; the elite social circle of Augsburg</note>—and for other public business conducted among them, are commonly named the **Citizens' Rooms** <note>original: "Burgerstuben"</note> by universally accepted usage. For the merchants, however, buildings were erected for similar use, simply designated the **Merchants' Rooms** <note>original: "Kaufleuth stuben"</note>. Likewise, in the Frankfurt riots, the patricians alone claimed for themselves the full right of citizenship.

And those citizens, the "Burgers," formerly lived in towers, as can still be seen in Schwäbisch Hall, Esslingen, and other places where similar towers remain visible. Others were called **Resident-Aliens** <note>original: "Beyfessen," likely a transcription of "Beisassen"; residents who lacked full citizen rights but held more than mere residency</note> and, as I think, they held a somewhat "fatter" <note>more substantial</note> right than mere residency. Such towers also existed in Pisa, Italy, due to civil dissensions. Carlo Sigonio records, in his history of Bologna under Otto II, that every most noble man began to build private towers of remarkable height in the city for the sake of his own private power. Following this example, most cities of Lombardy and Tuscany fortified themselves with towers against any hostile attacks from their own citizens, should they ever arise.

The same author writes regarding the era of Henry IV: "The Bolognese, resting from foreign wars and still striving to build private towers against domestic enemies (as is the custom in free cities), constructed two towers remarkable for their wonderful but vastly different structures. These were built by the **Asinelli** and **Garisenda** families, whose houses were joined together, arising from a contest of dignity and power. While the Asinelli sought praise from magnificent height, the Garisenda family sought it from the enormous slant of the structure. Since these still stand while most others have been torn down, they are with great merit a cause of wonder to onlookers and no small ornament to the city itself." And under Henry V, he reports: "The Tower of the Alberici, which was at the Ravenna Gate, when it happened to collapse onto the nearby houses of the Asinelli, [caused] an enormous..."

# On the Law and Orders of Citizens

...[with a] terrible ruin, it brought about the miserable death of nearly forty people. Warned by this disaster, many either completely demolished their towers or certainly lowered them so they would not be subject to such a great fall.

V. I shall here mention something regarding the **Pfalburgers** (*von den Pfalburgers*), who are also mentioned in the *Golden Bull of Charles IV, title 16*. <note>The Golden Bull of 1356 was a fundamental law of the Holy Roman Empire.</note> The meaning of this word is certainly obscure. Wehner, under the term *Pfalburger*, thinks a **Pfalburger** is called "one who sits between his four poles" <note>German: *der zwischen seinen vier Pfâlen sitzet*</note>. That is, as one may perhaps guess, "one who lives not in a tower, but in a wooden house" <note>German: *der nicht in einem Thurn / sondern in einem hölzin Hauß wohnet*</note>, or one who lives in a suburb—specifically one enclosed by poles rather than stone walls. For in German, a pole is a *Pfal* <note>modern German: *Pfahl*</note>; and from this, such suburban fortifications are called **Palisades**. And what prevents *Faulxbourg* <note>modern French: *faubourg*</note>—which is a suburb to the French—from being derived from this, as if it were a kind of *Pfalburg*?

Marquard Freher thinks the term **Pfalburger** denotes a "false citizen"; this interpretation is supported by the text of the *Golden Bull*, where **Pfalburgers** are defined as those who, in order to defraud their superior, arrange to be enrolled in another city so they may enjoy its privileges and defense, yet remain in their original place of residence. Arumaeus, in his *Discourse on the Golden Bull 6, thesis 11*, gives the example of a subject of some Prince wishing to acquire the rights of a free city for the purpose of being safe from his own lord. This gave rise to many quarrels and contentions among the Estates; and thus many constitutions concerning **Pfalburgers** were issued—for instance, by Charlemagne, Frederick I, and Henry and his son—which are found in Goldast's volumes of constitutions.

It appears from the *Imperial Recess of the year 1512, section "Item als uns und die Ständt"*, that the decree of Charles IV was not sufficiently observed. It seems there was a similar situation in France regarding those who called themselves **King's Bourgeois** (*Bourgeois du Roy*) and thereby withdrew themselves from the jurisdiction of the lesser lords in whose territories they lived; Philip the Fair corrected this abuse (Estienne Pasquier, *Researches, book 4, chapter 5*). But in many places, **Pfalburgers** are those who do not have the full rights of citizenship, even though they usually live there. Among the French, there is a difference between **Bourgeois** and **Citizen** (*Citoyen*); the latter name brings a "fatter" <note>more substantial or complete</note> right and denotes one who enjoys the best and greatest law of the city. The former indicates one who has acquired the right of the city but does not fully enjoy all privileges (Louis Guyon, *Various Lessons, volume 3, book 3, chapter 17*).

VI. Regarding Roman antiquity, whoever held the right of an "Island" <note>term: *Insula*, a large apartment block or tenement</note> or a house in Rome was considered a citizen by the most perfect right. To these, the **Right of the Quirites** <note>term: *jus Quiritum*, the full legal rights of a Roman citizen</note> belonged by law. But those who were only in a tribe—although they had the right to vote in decreeing honors and magistracies—yet lacked a house in the city; they were called citizens by a "perfect" right at least, but not by the "most perfect" right (Joachim Stephan, *Treatise on Jurisdiction, book 3, chapter 1, final number*). So too is a **Resident** <note>original Greek: ἔνοικος (enoikos)</note>, who does not [live] in his own...

# Dissertation II.

G

<meta>catchword: could</meta>

He lived in a house that was his own, yet was considered a foreign one; in some places even today, such a person is usually distinguished from a full citizen. See Timpler, book 1, chapter 5, question 5, at the end. At Rome, the **Proletarians** were indeed citizens, but they belonged to no specific class, according to Theodore Marcilius *in his elegant interpretation of the Law of the Twelve Tables, chapters 5 and 19.* <note>The Law of the Twelve Tables was the foundation of Roman law, written c. 450 BCE.</note> For this reason, they were called **those counted by the head** <note>original: "capite censi"</note>; see Aulus Gellius, *book 16, chapter 10*, because only their persons, not their property, were assessed in the census. And indeed, the Proletarians in the *Law of the Twelve Tables* were contrasted with the **taxpaying citizens** <note>original: "assiduis"</note>; in clearly the same sense as in the imperial constitutions, "men of the Empire" or "property owners" are distinguished from those who are "merely men," as seen in *law 7, section 3 of the Digest regarding the property of the condemned.* Or even the **Shareholders** <note>original: "Portionarij"</note>; the taxpaying citizens or the wealthy are those who can bear the burdens of the State and assist it. And thus, those men were indeed "shareholders" in a sense, but they were not "men of the Empire," as they were not wealthy or useful to the State. For it is in the interest of the State—that is, the Prince and the Empire—that no one should use their own property badly. *Section 11 of the Institutes on those who are under their own or another’s power.* <note>The "Institutes" were a textbook for law students under Emperor Justinian.</note> This means it is in the interest of the State to have wealthy citizens. And so it says in the *preface of Novel 8*: "Both the Empire and the Treasury will be in good condition by employing citizens who are wealthy." <note>The "Novels" (Novellae) were new laws issued by Justinian after the Code was published.</note> Not dissimilar are the words of Theodosius and Valentinian in the *New Acts on the indulgence of arrears*: "To us," they say, "a possessor made poorer is a loss; one who is not burdened is a benefit to us." But later, under the Emperors, in the course of time, it happened that all free men who existed within the Roman Empire and shared in civil affairs, war, and peace, were called citizens. Zasius, *on law 2 of the Digest regarding the origin of law.* And all who are within the Roman world were made Roman citizens by a constitution of the Divine Hadrian, *law "those who are in the world" in the Digest regarding the status of men.* And finally, regarding the privileges of Roman citizens, see Alciatus, *book 1, Parerga, chapter 14*, and add the same author's *Disputations, book 2, chapter 21*; they were made citizens by origin, manumission <note>The formal act of freeing a slave.</note>, election, or adoption. *Law 7 of the Code regarding residents, book 10.*

VII. Furthermore, this must also be added: those who debate whether **craftsmen** <note>original: "opifices"</note> should be counted among citizens take the name "citizen" to mean a "full citizen"—that is, as citizens are regarded in a Democracy and defined by Aristotle: those who, by full right, are participants in honors, dignities, and voting. Whether craftsmen should be counted among citizens is investigated by Johann Gerhard in *Political Questions 9, decade 9*; see also Melchior Junius, *Political Question 60, section 4: "Whether they are among the citizens."* Certainly, among the Egyptians, artisans were not admitted to the magistracies. Diodorus Siculus, *book 1, chapter 74.* It is also written in *law 6 of the Code regarding dignity, book 12*: "No one from the lowest merchants, or minters, or lowly offices, or unseemly services, or stationary guards, and all the dregs of offices, and those fed by various shameful gains, should attempt to enjoy any dignity; and even if someone has earned it, he should be rejected; those rejected must also be returned to their own guilds." In Sparta, as Plutarch testifies *in his life of Lycurgus*, and at Rome, as Dionysius testifies in *book 2*, it was not permitted for a citizen to practice a trade. For no Roman citizen

# On the Law and the Ranks of Citizens.

<meta>catchword: [translated as "could"]</meta>

...could be a tailor, a shoemaker, or practice any other trade; instead, men were expected to be either at leisure or soldiers. Giphanius, <note>Hubert Giphanius (1534–1604) was a prominent German legal scholar and philosopher.</note> *commentary on book 1, chapter 2 of the [Aristotle's] Ethics, leaf 17.* Likewise today, a Nobleman does not engage in trade. For even now, that opinion holds sway among all nations, and most especially among the Germans: that a man of lower rank can attain true and genuine Nobility by no other means than through **military virtue**. Augustus Vischer, *Treatise on Duels, leaf 341.* Certainly, the ancient **Democracies** flourished more in the past because craftsmen did not enjoy the full right of citizenship at all. Similarly, in the ancient Imperial Cities, only the **Patricians** <note>The elite, land-owning families of Rome or the ruling class in medieval free cities.</note> were considered citizens, as I have just said above; Nauclerus calls them the "older citizens," *generation 43, leaf 231, back.* Now, on the contrary, citizens devoted to servile trades willingly obey Monarchs, or else they manage free Republics with too little wisdom. For it is impossible for those occupied with small and base matters to conceive of sublime and generous spirits; as Demosthenes says in his *Oration on Organizing and Confirming the Republic.* See also the Son of Sirach, <note>A reference to the biblical Book of Sirach (Ecclesiasticus) 38:25–34, which argues that craftsmen and laborers lack the leisure required for wisdom and governance.</note> *chapter 38, verse 25 and following.* 

Herodotus contributes to this in *book 2*. <note>original: "lib. 11" — Likely a citation error in the source, as Herodotus's Histories has only 9 books; the discussion of Egyptian social classes is in Book 2.</note> Whether the Greeks borrowed this from the Egyptians, I cannot judge for certain; yet I see that among the Thracians, Scythians, Persians, and Lydians—indeed, among almost all Barbarians—those who learn trades and their children are held to be ignoble citizens. Those who abstain from manual labor, and especially those called to military service, are considered more noble. Even in **Mixed Republics** <note>A "mixed" government combines elements of monarchy (the Prince), aristocracy (the Senate/Nobles), and democracy (the People).</note> where the Estates have some power, it is not the Peasants or others from the lowest common people who are admitted to the assemblies of the Ranks, but the leading citizens from the Cities. This is what Laurentius Grimalius <note>Wawrzyniec Grzymała Goślicki (1530–1607), a Polish bishop whose work "The Best Senator" influenced early modern political thought.</note> suggests in *The Best Senator, book 1, leaf 35*, when he says: "We wish the popular order in this our Republic (the mixed one, naturally) which participates in its governance, to be of such a kind that it consists of citizens outstanding in lineage, nobility, and virtue, etc. For we do not wish the popular order to be forged from the multitude, farmers, craftsmen, and the rest of the dregs of men." 

And all well-constituted Republics established ranks or grades of citizens, as Numa <note>Numa Pompilius, the legendary second king of Rome, credited with organizing the city's religious and social guilds.</note> did at Rome. See Plutarch, *in the Life [of Numa], leaf 134.* Hippodamus of Miletus, <note>An ancient Greek architect and political theorist.</note> describing the form of a Republic, did not distribute the city into craftsmen, farmers, and soldiers as Aristotle did; instead, he divided it into those who would govern the Republic by counsel, those who would defend it with arms, and those who would practice the necessary arts. Muretus, *Various Readings, book 1, chapter 14, and the last chapter of book 18.* The ranks of those living in Venice are designated by the author of *The Magnificence of Venice, leaf 37.*

# Dissertation II.

G 2

<meta>catchword: X. They are</meta>

...of [the people] or citizens, a distinction must necessarily be made: so that some are **ruling citizens**, others are **subject citizens**. I call "Ruling Citizens" those who hold the right of citizenship in a dominant Republic; I call "subject citizens" those in a Province which is subject to a dominant Republic. Thus, in the past, some were Roman citizens, while others were **Burghers of Municipalities** <note>Latin: *Municipes*. These were inhabitants of towns that were incorporated into the Roman state but often retained their own local laws and customs.</note>, as seen in the whole title of the *Digests* <note>The *Digests* (or *Pandects*) are a major component of the Corpus Juris Civilis, the body of Roman law issued by Justinian.</note> regarding Municipal Law, and there *Wesenbec in his summary*. These Burghers were Roman citizens from the Municipalities, using their own laws and rights, but only sharing in the duties of the Roman people. They had their own privilege, that is, the **Municipal Law**. <note>See Aulus Gellius, *Attic Nights*, book 16, chapter 12; Fornerius, *Selections*, book 2, chapter 13.</note> There were two kinds of these Burghers. One, to whom citizenship was given along with the **right to vote**. These enjoyed all the privileges of citizens, with one exception: they were not enrolled in the Roman wards. The other kind were those to whom citizenship was given **without the right to vote**; they could indeed hold honors, yet they were free from business and public burdens. The Senators of both kinds of Municipalities are called **Decurions** <note>Local council members or administrative officials in Roman municipal towns.</note>; regarding whom, see the whole title of the *Digests* and the *Code* on Decurions.

IX. The status of the **Italian Colonies** was of a lower legal condition than that of the Burghers. Even harsher than this was the law governing provinces and prefectures, for they were stripped of their own laws and magistrates. From this, Zacharias Fridenreich, in his *Politics*, chapter 29, page 196, rightly warns: the Roman Republic, with respect to its provinces and municipalities, was a **Monarchy**—or rather, it usurped monarchical right, insofar as they succeeded to the rights of the expelled Kings. Busius notes this in *On the Republic*, book 2, chapter 3, numbers 2 and 6, and Virgil implies the same in the first book of the *Aeneid*:

->*This people ruling far and wide.*<-
<note>Original Latin: *Hunc Populum latè Regem*. Virgil uses this to describe the Roman people's destiny as an imperial power.</note>

Later, however, the Emperors gifted all subjects with the **right of Roman citizenship**—a singular **political stratagem**. By this act, the old right of Roman citizens and their liberty was extinguished, and Romans were made equal to the provincials. <note>See Magius, *Miscellany*, book 4, chapter 5; and my own commentary on the *Digests*, book 5 and 6, question 21.</note> There were three types of Colonies: citizen, Latin, and Italian. <note>Torrentius on Suetonius’ *Life of Julius Caesar*, chapter 8.</note> What are the differences between Colonists and Burghers? Busius has many noteworthy things to say regarding their diverse rights in his *Political Disputations*, 2, thesis 6 and following, as well as in his *On the Republic*, book 1, chapter 7. See also Alberico Gentili <note>A famous Italian jurist (1552–1608) considered one of the founders of international law.</note> on law 228 regarding the meaning of words; Tileman, *Disputation 3*, thesis 25 and following, volume 1; Denais, *Assertion of Imperial Chamber Jurisdiction*, page 267 and following; and Franz Guillimann on the *Swiss*, chapter 10. This difference between citizens also appears in certain **mixed Republics** and Kingdoms that enjoy liberty more than others. Thus, the Chancellor of Poland, in Reinhold Heidenstein’s *On the Muscovite War*, book 2, persuades the Poles to acquire a province that may bear the burdens of the state, so that in the meantime they themselves may enjoy an **immune liberty**. <note>The Polish-Lithuanian Commonwealth was often described as a "Nobles' Democracy" where the ruling class enjoyed vast freedoms while subject territories or classes bore the tax and military burdens.</note>

It also applies these ancient legal categories to contemporary European states like Poland to explain how ruling classes maintain liberty by delegating state burdens to subject provinces.</summary>

# On the Law and the Ranks of Citizens.

<meta>catchword: domicilium (continued from "do-" at the end of the page)</meta>

X. Furthermore, there are citizens who are either **natural**, created by their origin, or **enrolled**; these are people who have come to the city from elsewhere and have obtained the **right of citizenship**. <note>Original: *jus civitatis*. This refers to the full set of legal rights and privileges held by a member of a city-state.</note> *Law 7 of the Code regarding Inhabitants; Master Brüning on Homage, thesis 262, etc.* For this reason, one fatherland is of the place, and another is of the law. As Cicero says in the second book of *On the Laws*: We speak of one fatherland as the place where we were born, and another as the place that has received us. Citizens are born either from both parents being citizens, or only one of the two (<note>original: *Hybridæ*. In this legal context, "hybrids" refers to children of mixed parentage where only one parent holds citizenship rights.</note> as they are called in some places; see Kirchner, *Disputation 15, corollary 1*; Wehner under the term **Citizenship Law** <note>German: *Burgerrecht*.</note>), according to what the laws of each city-state provide. Thus, a son follows the citizenship from which his father draws his natural origin, not the father's **domicile**. <note>Latin: *domicilium*. In law, "origin" is the place where one's family is legally rooted, whereas "domicile" is where one currently resides.</note> *Law 6, § 1 of the Digests on Municipalities.* 

One who is enrolled into a new city does not lose the old one which nature and location, or lineage and ancestors, gave to him. See Mejer, *Disputations on the Digests 5, thesis 206, etc.* From this it also follows that he can be called to **public duties and honors** <note>Latin: *munera & honores*. These are the obligations (like taxes or military service) and the privileges (like holding office) of a citizen.</note> in both places—that is, in the one that gave him birth, and in the one that received him. *Law on Rank, final §, on Municipalities.* However, by the customs of almost all nations, it has been accepted that no citizen should belong to more than one city; instead, he who no longer wishes to remain a citizen is reduced to the status of a foreigner. Jacob Curtius, *volume 1 of Conjectures, book 3, chapter 45* (where he also discusses whether children follow their father when changing citizenship). See Wehner under the term **Departure Tax**. <note>German: *Nachsteuer*. This was a tax paid when a citizen moved their property out of a jurisdiction to live elsewhere.</note> Arumæus, *On the Golden Bull, discourse 6, chapter 16, theses 11 and 12*; Master Rümmelin, *ibid, part 2, dissertation 5, thesis 4.* This is done because of the diversity of **jurisdictions**, which now attach to each territory individually. And can someone be prohibited from changing their city? See Clock, *On Taxes, thesis 63 b.* Just as in the old days, a person who was a citizen at Rome could not be a citizen elsewhere. Perionius, *On Greek and Roman Magistrates, book 1, page 309*; add Winzler, *On Collections, observation 10, number 8.* 

**Original citizens** living outside the city do not cease to be citizens, but enjoy the privilege of the city. Cephalus, *Counsel 741, number 439 and following, book 4.* For a native is only considered exempt from the jurisdiction of their place of origin when they have established a domicile elsewhere with the intent to return to their fatherland never again. Francesco Vivio, *Neapolitan Decisions 477, throughout*; Wesenbeck, *Counsel 1, number 57.* In cases of doubt, however, the intent to dwell elsewhere permanently is not presumed. Mascardus, *On Evidence, volume 1, conclusion 534, number 1*; Wesenbeck, *in the place cited*; Graevæus, *Practical Conclusions, book 2, conclusion 36, number 6, etc.* Likewise, a woman married elsewhere, although she has moved her domicile to the city of her husband, nevertheless retains the citizenship of her origin. Bartolus, *on Law: Emperors, § the same they wrote back, in the Digests on Municipalities*; Decio, *on Law: When a certain girl, number 6, in the Digests on Jurisdiction*; extensively in Cephalus, *Counsel 526, numbers 32 and 33*; Rolando a Valle, *Counsel 79, number 49, book 3*; and most recently, after many others, Giovanni Vincente Hondedei, *Counsel 5, number 12, especially numbers 45 and 46, book 1*; by the reasoning that she departs from the city of origin and [goes] to the do-

<summary>This page defines the legal distinction between natural-born and enrolled citizens, exploring the relationship between one's place of birth (origin) and place of residence (domicile). It addresses the historical transition from Roman dual citizenship to the modern practice of single citizenship necessitated by conflicting territorial jurisdictions.</summary>

### Dissertation II.
G 3
<meta>catchword: equal-</meta>

...the residence of a man out of necessity, as stated in the law *exigere* in the Digests on Judgments; so say Cephalus in the cited place, number 34 and following, and Hondedei in the cited counsel, number 47.

XI. It is convenient to ask here whether spurious or illegitimate children <note>The text uses "spurij" and "nothi," which were specific legal categories of children born outside of wedlock.</note> have the right of citizenship where their mother resides? I am more inclined to affirm this, though it is against the views of Aristotle and Case (Book 4 of *Politics*, chapter 6) and Melchior Junius (Political Question 60), along with Tiraqueau, Cardano, and others cited by Keckerman (Book 1 of *Politics*, chapter 8, folio 78, and *Practical Disputations* 30, problem 6). See also Master Molina in his treatise *On Justice and Law*, disputation 220, number 22; Heig, part 1, question 21; and Zepper in Book 3 of *On the Laws of Moses*, chapter 10. See also Law 1 at the end of the section *On Municipalities*, and the Wisdom of Solomon, chapter 3, verse 16. Consider also the treatise published in Augsburg some time ago titled: *A Christian Exhortation that for illegitimate children, for the sake of their physical sustenance, the mastery of trades, entry into guilds, and the right of citizenship have been unjustly withheld until now.* <note>Original German: *Christliche ermahnung / das den Unehelichen Kindern / zu ihrer Leibs Nahrung unbillicher weiß / biß hieher / Lehnung der Handwercken / einfommung der Zünfften / vnd Burgerrecht auffgehalten werden.*</note> Johann Gerhard, in the section *On the Magistrate*, number 107; see also Sigonius on Bologna, folio 473.

There are three types of illegitimate children. One type consists of those born to bachelors from concubines or mistresses taken into the home; Justinian calls these "natural" children <note>Latin: *naturales*.</note> in *Novel 89*, and he calls them to a sixth part of the inheritance with the mother. There are others conceived by a woman who mingles her body with many men, or even one who is joined to a man without the appearance of concubinage—that is, without the hope of marriage; these are called "spurious" <note>Latin: *spurij*.</note>. The third type consists of those begotten through adultery and incest. See Curtius, volume 1, *Conjectures*, book 3, chapter 8; Peregrinus, *On the Law of the Treasury*, book 3, title 18; and Schifordegher, book 3, treatise 25, throughout.

In the Jewish Republic, <note>Latin: *Respublica Iudaica*. The author treats the historical biblical state of Israel as a legal model.</note> spurious children were not tolerated because that Republic was the pattern for the Church in the New Testament, which ought to strive for the greatest possible purity of spirit. Nevertheless, Gospel equity does not allow the son to carry the iniquity of the parents. Spurious children do not become Cardinals; yet, where the law did not prevent it, Alexander VI—if Guicciardini is to be believed (Book 1, folio 56)—procured false testimonies so that he could grant the dignity of the Cardinalate to his illegitimate son. <note>Alexander VI was the Borgia Pope; he famously promoted his son Cesare Borgia within the church despite his illegitimacy.</note> Among us, the right of citizenship is generally not granted to anyone unless they have a "testimony of legitimate birth." <note>German: *Ein Zeugnuß ehelicher Geburt*.</note> However, Pasquier (Book 6 of *Researches*, chapter 35) proves that a large portion of the greatest empires were either founded or increased by the help of bastards. See also Paleotti's unique treatise *On Spurious Children*.

XII. Likewise, what should be thought concerning adopted citizens? Or should foreigners <note>Latin: *peregrini*.</note> be granted the right of citizenship? This is a disputed question discussed by many: Timpler (Book 1 of *Politics*, chapter 5, question 13) and others listed by Hoennon (Political Disputation 2, thesis 24). The Roman Kings from the beginning, by accepting any foreigners and granting them the rights of citizenship without any distinction of birth or status, increased the city with a great multitude of people; and Tullus <note>Tullus Hostilius, the third King of Rome.</note> even [admitted] the slaves themselves, who were granted freedom, into the rights...

It also begins exploring the historical Roman practice of granting citizenship to foreigners and freed slaves to increase a city's population.</summary>

# On the Law and the Classes of Citizens.

Dionysius of Halicarnassus <note>A Greek historian who wrote about Roman history to prove the Greeks and Romans were related.</note> attests in Book 4, number 2, that equality was received. See also Scipione Ammirato, Discourse 11, number 6. Regarding Romulus and his **Asylums** <note>Romulus famously established a sanctuary or "asylum" on the Capitoline Hill to attract inhabitants to the newly founded city of Rome, welcoming outcasts and refugees.</note>, see Lactantius, Book 2, chapter 7, folio 63. Likewise, the Emperors imitated this; they gradually granted the rights of the city to others as well. Hence, in Tacitus, Claudius argues that whatever is excellent anywhere should be transferred to Rome. See Guillaume Paradin’s *History of Lyon*, Book 1, chapter 9. Nothing is as necessary to a state that aspires to Empire and affects great things as a high frequency of men. Dido, in Virgil’s first book of the *Aeneid*, had this in mind when, founding her new city, she said:

> Whether Trojan or Tyrian, I shall treat them with no distinction. <note>original: "Tros, Tyriusque mihi, nullo discrimine agetur."</note>

The Turks also have foreigners, and their army consists almost entirely of slaves who have renounced the Christian faith. For the Turks are not accustomed to counting Persians and Tartars in the number of their slaves. Indeed, these honors are so great and so desirable that this reason alone is set against that immense danger mentioned by Soranzo in chapter 26; see also chapter 10. The Moors do the same: and certainly in the Portuguese War against King Sebastian, the Moorish leader had 10,000 men, almost all originating from Andalusia, as well as **Renegades** <note>In this context, "renegades" refers to Christians who converted to Islam to serve in the Ottoman or Moorish administrations.</note>. See Conestaggio, folio 65, book 2. And for this reason, the Court of the Turkish Emperor is called the **Porte** <note>The "Sublime Porte" or *Porta*, the central government of the Ottoman Empire.</note>, as if it were a kind of open asylum, standing open to all those who have subjected themselves to the kind protection of this Empire. See Tarducci, folio 242. Antoninus the Philosopher <note>The Emperor Marcus Aurelius.</note> settled infinite numbers of people from various nations on Roman soil, as Capitolinus notes in his *Life*. Charlemagne loved foreigners, according to Girard, folio 366. In Athens, Theseus established an asylum for all wanderers, according to Plutarch’s *Life*, folio 21; as did Solon, according to Plutarch, folio 173. So also, some years ago, a certain Prince in France promised that he would turn a refuge for thieves and robbers into an elegant city, as noted by Jodocus Sincerus in his *French Itinerary*, folio 100 and following. Elsewhere, foreigners are not admitted to the right of citizenship unless they have lived in the city for some time. Avenarius suggests that the "vile" should not be taken in, in his *Political Disputations* 13, folio 288. On receiving foreigners and Belgians, see Lather, Book 3, *On the Census*, 3. Conversely, Aristotle reports in Book 5 of the *Politics*, chapter 3, that those who have granted citizenship to **foreign residents** <term>inquilini</term> <note>Foreigners living in a city who do not yet have full civic rights.</note> and newcomers have for the most part been tossed about by seditions; and that diversity of stock is a cause of dissension until their spirits agree. Isocrates looks toward this when he says: That city alone is to be called happy which does not rashly collect many citizens from a promiscuous dregs of men, but which strives to preserve its most ancient and primary families above all. Truly, those who affect the expansion of Empire rather than tranquility still imitate the example of the Romans. On the con-
