## Dissertation II.

On the contrary, the Lycurgan rigor <note>Lycurgus was the legendary lawgiver of Sparta, who famously instituted "xenelasia," the practice of periodically expelling foreigners to prevent outside influence from corrupting Spartan customs.</note> against foreigners—which is imitated today by some who deprive outsiders of the right of citizenship and the right to vote—is least of all conducive to increasing the Republic. See Zerneman in *The Labors of Julius Caesar*, number 130, etc. But nevertheless, the Spartans also admitted foreigners who submitted themselves to their institutions, as Cragius testifies in *On the Republic of the Lacedaemonians*, book 3, table 2, instruction 4, folio 120. Similarly, the Jews had proselytes <note>original: "proselytos"; individuals from other nations who converted to Judaism and joined the Jewish community.</note>: nor was any nation so rigid that it completely excluded outsiders.

Therefore, the question concerns a great multitude, or those who do not merge into the citizenry, institutions, and religion. By the customs of almost all, he who marries a widow simultaneously gains the right of citizenship. See the *Würtemberg Territorial Ordinance*, folio 318 and following. Does a man lose the right of citizenship if he marries a foreign woman? And conversely, when a citizen's daughter marries out into foreign status, does she make her husband a citizen? Likewise, does a widow return to her former right after her husband's death? See Curtius, volume 1, *Conjectures*, book 3, chapter 36.

I would answer negatively to all of these. Although in some places it is provided by law that he who marries a wife abroad loses the right of citizenship. See Wehner in the *Treatise on the Significance of Words*, under the word "Custom" <note>original: "Gewohnheit"</note>, number 12. And concerning the Statute by virtue of which a woman married outside the territory is excluded from the succession of her parents, see Cravetta, *Counsel 609*. There are also some citizens who live in the countryside, and others in the city. See Pacius, *Antinomies*, final question 76.

Here it is also usefully disputed concerning the right to change citizenship: especially if someone elected to the Senate, or burdened by a new levy <note>A "collation" here refers to a tax or contribution required by the state.</note>, wishes to be reduced to foreign status—will this be granted to him? See Jacob Curtius, in the cited chapter 36, who denies it. I expand upon this.

### XIII. Honorary Citizens

An **honorary citizen** is one to whom the right of citizenship is granted for the sake of honor; yet he is not therefore held by any command, or necessary office, or burden. See Paul Busius, *Political Disputations 2*, thesis 18. Such is the right of Roman citizenship which is granted to certain people today, and which was granted to Michael de Montaigne <note>The famous French philosopher and author of the *Essays*.</note> in book 3 of his *Essays*, chapter 9; and likewise to Hubert Goltzius <note>A renowned 16th-century humanist, painter, and numismatist.</note>. See Melchior Adam in his *Lives*. It was also held by Melissus <note>Paul Schede Melissus, a humanist poet and librarian.</note> and others.

The Swiss cities also sometimes bestow this same right upon illustrious persons. Nor did Louis XI, King of France, disdain to accept it. See Philippe de Comines, book 6, chapter 4. So too did the Venetians make Ferdinand, King of Castile and Aragon, a citizen and Patrician. See *The Political Treasure*, part 1, *Report on Venice*, folio 82. And likewise the Medici family. See Bembo, *History*, book 12, folio 534. Thus the city of Besançon had illustrious citizens. See Chiflet, book 1, chapter 62. And William, Count of Holland, was a citizen of Utrecht so that he might be able to buy immovable property <note>i.e., real estate or land.</note> there. See Lehmann, book 5, chapter 90. There are also in the Swiss Republics not only...

### CHAPTER IV.

## On residents: especially concerning the Jews.

The right of stay is based on either domicile or hospitality: the former makes a person a resident, the latter a foreigner. Since both perceive certain benefits from the Republic, it is fair that they are, in turn, bound to certain burdens. *Code on residents, law 5.* Kirchner, *discourse 7, thesis 1.* A resident—whom the Germans call a *Saß* <note>original: "Saß"; a dweller or occupant.</note> / or *Pfalburger* <note>original: "Pfalburger" or "Pfahlbürger"; literally "pole-citizen," referring to those living outside the city walls but under its jurisdiction.</note> / *Beysiß* <note>original: "Beysiß" or "Beisasse"; a resident without full civic rights.</note> / *Kyllinger*. *On Common Heritage, folio 33.*—is called by the Greeks a *paroikon* <note>original: "$\pi\acute{\alpha}\rho o\iota\kappa o\nu$"; literally "neighboring inhabitant."</note>, that is, one living nearby; see *Acts of the Apostles, chapter 17, verse 21* and the commentary there by Beza. Establishing a domicile is what creates this status, *Digest on the meaning of words, law "pupillus" 239, § 2.* And in that same place, it is not doubted that individuals have a domicile where one has established their hearth <note>original: "larem"; the Roman household god, signifying the permanent home.</note> and the sum of their affairs and fortunes, from which they do not intend to depart unless something calls them away. *Code on residents, law 7.* Petrus Wesenbec, *legal counsel 1*. And this right of residency offers all the comforts of habitation and safety in the city: namely, the use of liberty, the City, the Forum, the Baths, Sacred Rites, and Theaters. Hence, a resident is also bound by the duties of the city, *Code on residents, law 5*. By common law, one can be a citizen of one place and a resident of another; and it is necessary to obey those Magistrates where one is a resident, while nonetheless remaining subject to those where one is a citizen by full right, so that one can be recalled. *Digest on Municipal Law, law "incola" 29.* And the discussion of *Pfalburgeri* <note>External citizens living in the suburbs or surrounding territory.</note> pertains to this; on which matter see Buxtorff, *On the Golden Bull, thesis 99, and also above in this treatise.* And certainly, those who were of servile condition were not given the right of citizenship in Imperial cities, but only the right of residency. Lehman, *folio 101.* Those who possess property in a foreign territory but do not have the seat of their domicile established there are considered "quasi-residents." They are not, however, bound to homage. Bruning, *discourse on corporations, thesis 34.* For the reasoning and conditions under which Nobles may obtain the right of residency in Speyer, see Lehman, *book 6, last chapter.*

II. It is not doubted that it is better to have a more frequent number of citizens than of residents. Kirchner, *discourse 17, thesis 1, letter c.* Hence, for such outsiders established outside the oath and the right of citizenship, so that the opportunity for staying may be precluded, it is found that by the accepted law of many places, they are denied or prohibited from holding immovable property <note>term: "bona immobilia"; real estate such as land or buildings, as opposed to "movable" goods like money or cattle.</note>, lest anyone who is a citizen elsewhere should possess immovable property in the territory of the Republic—

<summary>This page defines the legal distinctions between citizens, residents (incolae), and foreigners, detailing the rights and obligations of those who dwell in a city without full citizenship. It specifically introduces the concept of "external citizens" and the restrictions often placed on foreigners regarding land ownership.</summary>

<header>Dissertation II.</header>

...should possess: lest the number of those who are compelled to undergo personal (or even mixed) <term>burdens</term> <note>original: "munera"; these were compulsory public services, duties, or taxes tied to citizenship or residency.</note> should decrease. Thus, in the Duchy of Württemberg, if immovable goods <note>Real estate or land.</note> fall to foreigners, they are required to sell them within the space of a year. See the *Württemberg State Ordinance, folios 35 and 41* <note>original: "Würtemberg. Landtsordnung."</note>; the Statute of Padua agrees with this. Peregrinus, *decision 86*, and Master Bocer, *on taxes, chapter 9, number 19, and chapter 11, number 14, etc.*, teach that such statutes are valid. See also Caspar Klock, *on contributions, thesis 27.*

For this same purpose in France, the inheritances of foreign men cannot pass to the next of kin residing outside the kingdom; instead, they return to the treasury. They call this the <term>Right of Aubaine</term> <note>original: "jus Albinagij" or "le droict d’Aubaine"; a law where the crown inherited the property of a deceased alien.</note>. See Guidon, *on Finances, folio 54*, and Bechtius, *treatise on security, theses 83 and 85.* Jean Bacquet deals very extensively with this law and the neighboring questions arising from it in *Rights of the Domain, book 1.* Just as in ancient times, only Roman citizens could receive the inheritance of a citizen, and only they held the right of marriage among themselves.

A question for investigation arises here: whether the <term>Right of Retaliation</term> <note>original: "jus Talionis"; here referring to legal reciprocity.</note> takes place; so that a person may not be an heir in a land where a foreigner is not permitted to be an heir? I would affirm this. See Schultes, *practical questions, 6*, and the *Württemberg State Ordinance, folio 7.*

In some places, inhabitants are also prohibited from engaging in commerce or brewing beer, etc. From this, it is asked: whether a Nobleman received into residency <note>original: "incolatum"; the status of a resident alien.</note> under this law—that he may not brew beer—might still do so for the use of his own house? See Moller, *4. semestr. 17.* Regarding the evils introduced by Italians living in France, see *The Mirror of the French, folio 34, etc.*

¶ II. Especially, however, the assemblies or "colleges" of outsiders are to be prohibited. Kirchner, *dispute 14, thesis 1, toward the end.* Here this question occurs: whether in Christian Republics, Jewish synagogues are to be tolerated? Christopher Helvicus disputes this in various ways in *Refutation of the Jews, 8, throughout*; also Antonius Margaritha, *treatise on the whole Jewish Faith, throughout*; Velsten, *decade 4, final thesis*; Johann Gerhard, *century of political questions, decade 9, question 1, and on the Magistrate, number 223*; Julius Benedictus Crescentius in *German Counsel on the Privileges of the Jews*; Speckhan, *century 2, class 1, question 16*; add Lather, *book 3, chapter 23.*

I would approve the counsel of St. Paul in *Romans 11, verse 30*, who wills that through our mercy, the Jews might also obtain mercy: but not so that they may increase their private wealth in idleness to the detriment of Christians, or openly blaspheme. See the argument in the *Code on Jews, law 6, and the Code on Pagans*; join this with Moditius, *§ 2, question 165, and § on Princes, question 41*; Becht, *dispute on security, thesis 92, etc.*; and Balthasar Meisner, *on laws, folio 374.* All of these authors dispute for and against this matter, and most seem to be rather unfavorable toward the Jews. They are certainly to be tolerated (1) because of the promise and hope of their conversion, and (2) so that they may be witnesses to the historical truth of the New and Old Testaments. (For many, perhaps, would not believe that such men...)

...[that they were] men, unless we were to see them before our eyes.) They serve as a mirror of Divine wrath against those who unjustly subjected our Savior to punishment. See also Filesac, *Select Works, book 1, chapter 12.* Regarding the Jews, the Lord Mager deals with them legally *in his distinguished treatise on Advocacy*; so too Sixtus of Medici, *Treatise on the Jews, individual matters*; Kitzel, *Treatise on the Royal Rights of Holding Jews*; Cornman, *in his counsel on this matter*; Stamm, *On Personal Servitude, book 1, title 5*; Reinkingk, *class 2, chapter 23*; and Barthius, *Adversaria, book 2, chapter 21.* Urspergenſis and Aventinus testify that Pope Hildebrand <note>Pope Gregory VII (c. 1015–1085).</note> was the first who, around the year 1100 AD, opened the Christian world to the Jews. He was followed by Gregory IX, who in the year 1300—despite the unwillingness and reluctance of Christian Kings—granted them immunity (which they had purchased with much gold) and inundated France, England, Italy, and Germany with a gathering of Jews, as Platina and Bale have noted. (Though it appears from Lehmann that Jews were in Europe even before Hildebrand. *book 5, chapter 37.*) From that time, they have given many proofs of treachery and hatred toward Christians. See Dominicus Arumæus *on the Golden Bull, discourse 5, thesis 6*; Henricus Scotanus *in the Paratitla of the Code, at the title on Jews, folio 79*; Lehmann *in the Speyer Chronicle, book 5, chapters 37 and 52; also book 7, chapter 42.* Not infrequently, they have also experienced the cruelty and the unwise zeal of our own people. See Felix Faber, *Description of Swabia, folio 151 and following*; Stumpff, *Swiss History, volume 2, folios 53, 59, 60, 61, book 2, chapter 33; also folio 398b and 424b.* Their fortune in France is recorded by the author of *The Grand Almoner, folio 273 and many following*; Guyon, *volume 3, book 4, chapter 1*; Paradin, *History of Lyon, book 2, chapters 60 and 98*; and Girard, *volume 1, folio 750 and 790, and volume 2, folio 89.* Most detestable is the crime of Ivan Vasilyevich <note>Ivan the Terrible (1530–1584).</note>, Prince of the Muscovites, of whom the *French Mercury* writes thus on folio 31b:

> <note>original: "Ayant faict une ordonance, qu' il vouloit, qu' ils fuſſent baptiſez, il les fit amener de tous les quartiers de ſon Empire, ſur un pont, les fit reconcer à leur croyance, & puis pieds & mains liez, les fit tous jetter dans l'eau."</note> Having issued an ordinance that he wished them to be baptized, he had them brought from all parts of his Empire onto a bridge, forced them to renounce their belief, and then, with their hands and feet bound, had them all thrown into the water.

And truly, the Jews have always experienced the calumnies of other nations, which Josephus dilutes <note>refutes or explains away</note> in *Against Apion, throughout, and in book 2, folio 324*, where it is recorded that it was once imputed to them that they sacrificed a Greek man every year. So too, some believe that they cannot live without Christian blood. However, even in ancient times, the Jews were the cause of Christians being killed. See Eusebius, *History, book 4, chapters 15 and 18.* And in Alexandria, they crucified a child. See *Tripartite History, book 11, chapter 13.* Whether they use Christian blood is investigated by Langius in *Medical Letters, book 1, letter 71.* Certainly, the apostate monks burned at Bern confessed that they had used Jewish blood <note>original: "sanguine Judaico"</note> for their magical arts. See Stumpff, *volume 2, folio 458b.* Godfrey of Bouillon <note>A leader of the First Crusade.</note> also persecuted the Jews. See the Abbot of Stade, *folio 142.*

# Dissertation II

[Signature mark H 2]

...they are held as servants and, in the condition of slaves, are subject to the Imperial Treasury <note>original: "Fisco Cæsareo". Under the medieval and early modern legal concept of *Servitudo camerae* (Chamber Serfdom), Jews were considered direct subjects of the Emperor's treasury in exchange for protection.</note>. Marquard Freher <note>A German jurist and statesman (1565–1614).</note> in his commentary on Peter of Andlau, *folio 186, b.* And in that place, the power of receiving Jews was granted to those possessing Royal Rights <term>Regalia: the exclusive rights and privileges belonging to a sovereign</term>. Thomas Michael, *On Jurisdiction, conclusion 48, letter b.* Graevaeus, *Practical Observations, 2, observation 5, in the Supplement, number 4.* 

But can they be expelled at will and without the Emperor's consent? I would almost affirm this. See Buxtorf <note>Johannes Buxtorf (1564–1629), a celebrated Hebrew scholar.</note> on the *Golden Bull*, thesis 9, letter C; though Marquardus de Susanis <note>An Italian jurist (1500–1578) known for his treatise on the legal status of Jews.</note>, part 1, chapter 7, disagrees. By the privileges of certain places, even Princes are hindered from being able to admit Jews. Ulrich Zasius <note>A prominent German legal scholar (1461–1535).</note> strives to prove in a singular treatise that even their children cannot be baptized against the parents' will. Still less should their children be burned; a fate which Reuchlin <note>Johannes Reuchlin (1455–1522), a humanist who famously defended Jewish books against the inquisitorial efforts of Jacob van Hoogstraten to have them seized and burned.</note> formerly prevented against the efforts of Hoogstraten. Sleidanus, *Commentaries, book 1, from the beginning.* J. Bismarc, *Lives of the Wittenberg Theologians, where he discusses Reuchlin, E. 2. etc.* Melchior Adam, *Lives of the Germans, folio 39. etc.* See also *Law 16 of the Code on Heretics.* 

A Jew, however, is not excused from prior crimes through the profession of Christianity, though the punishment in this case should be mitigated. Speckhan, *century 2, class 1, question 17.* Andreas Gerhard, *final dispute on the Institutes, in the Supplement.* And their legal cases are to be decided according to common law <term>jus commune: the general legal system of the Empire based on Roman law</term>. Speckhan, *class 1, question 18.* Marquardus de Susanis, *extensively in part 2.* Coras, *Miscellany 5, chapter 16.* And regarding the punishment that should be inflicted on a Jew who seduces a Christian and is the cause of their defecting from our religion, Pius Felix of Augsburg recently published a *notable counsel*. 

Regarding the condition of Jews living in Turkey <note>The Ottoman Empire.</note>, see Pierre Belon, *Observations, book 1, chapters 57, 76, and book 2, chapters 26, 87, 91, 97 and book 3, chapters 13, 29, 46, 49.* Also *book 1, chapters 10 and 55.* The same Belon writes: There is such a great abundance of Jews in Turkey, expelled from Spain, that the Spanish language is like a mother tongue to them there. Jews were also always hated by the Roman Pagans, and hence they are sharply criticized by Horace (Satires 1.4 and 9) and others. This was due to frequent seditions. For even under Hadrian, they moved to war because they were forbidden to mutilate their genitals <note>A reference to the Roman ban on circumcision, which was a primary cause of the Bar Kokhba revolt (132–136 AD).</note>. Spartianus, *in the Life [of Hadrian].* 

The travelogue of Benjamin of Tudela <note>A 12th-century Jewish traveler from Navarre who visited Europe, Asia, and Africa.</note> is indeed criticized by the most industrious Lord Barth, *Animadversions 50, chapter 4.* Nevertheless, I cannot omit adding certain transcriptions here from the same author, who says: In Narbonne there are wise men, both great and respected, especially **Kolonymos**, son of the great and respected Theodore, of blessed memory, from the seed of David, in a direct lineage. 

<margin>
Descendants of David in our age.
</margin>

Asher the Pharisee <note>In this context, "Pharisee" (Perushim) refers to Jewish ascetics or "separatists" rather than the ancient sect.</note> (in Lunel), who, separated from the eyes of the world, devotes himself to books day and night, fasting and perpetually abstaining from the eating of meat. There are Pharisees.

Heretics
</margin>

But these people profane the evening of the Sabbath and observe the evening of the first day <note>Sunday</note>.

### On the Law and the Orders of Citizens.

...the wives, whom they commonly call Samaritans <note>The text refers to the Samaritans, an ethno-religious group related to the Jews but who recognize only the first five books of the Bible (the Pentateuch) as scripture.</note>, are observers of the Mosaic Law alone. They have priests from the lineage of Aaron the priest (may he rest in peace), who do not enter into marriage with any others except for the women or men of their own family, and so forth. Soon after, it is noted: these people lack three letters: <term>ה (He)</term>, <term>ח (Heth)</term>, and <term>ע (Ayin)</term>. The letter <term>ה</term> is from the name of Abraham; <term>ח</term> from the name of Isaac <note>original: "Iishhac"</note>; and <term>ע</term> from the name of Jacob <note>original: "Iaghacob"</note>. In place of these, they substitute an *Aleph*, which is to say, a soft breath <note>original: "spiritum tenuem." The author is noting a linguistic shift where the Samaritans lost the guttural pronunciations of certain Hebrew letters, which the author uses as proof that they are not of the original "seed of Israel."</note>. By this manifest sign they are proven not to be from the posterity and seed of Israel.

<margin>
Literalists.
Sectarian disputes.
Posterity of Samuel.
The Devoted Ones.
Posterity of David.
Head of the Captivity.
Where it once was.
Where Jews rule and are subject to no one.
Pharisees.
</margin>

Literalist scholars—that is, those practiced in the simple meaning of the sacred books—maintain great peace and harmony among all their groups (both the literalist Cuthites <note>A traditional term for Samaritans, referencing their origins in the city of Cuthah.</note> and the remaining Jews of Damascus); however, these diverse factions do not intermarry. Eliezer Ben Thata in Baghdad is the head of an order that traces its origin from the Prophet Samuel; he and his brothers play skillfully upon musical instruments. All these are called the "Vacant Ones" <note>original: "vacantes," meaning those at leisure from worldly work.</note>, because they devote themselves to no other matter besides the administration of their society.

However, the supreme leader of all these is Daniel, called the son of Hastai <note>original: "Hhastai"</note>, who is the Head of the Captivity <note>The Exilarch, or *Resh Galuta*, the political leader of the Jews in the Babylonian diaspora.</note>. He possesses a book of genealogy traced all the way back to David. He is called "Our Lord" and "Head of the Captivity" by the Jews. Likewise, he is called *Sidna Bon David* <note>A Latinized version of the Arabic/Hebrew for "Our Lord, Son of David."</note> by the Ishmaelites <note>A common historical term for Muslims.</note>, and he holds great authority over all the congregations of the Israelites under the hand of the *Amir Almonin* <note>The Commander of the Faithful, the title of the Caliph.</note>, the Lord of the Ishmaelites. Indeed, at the time when a man of this family is appointed as the Head of the Captivity, he offers a great weight of money as a gift first to the King himself, and then to the Princes and Magistrates.

*Suria* is the place which the ancients called *Matha Mahhasia* <note>Likely referring to Mata Mehasya, a suburb of the city of Sura in Babylonia, famous for its rabbinic academy.</note>, where the Heads of the Captivity and the leaders of the Sanhedrins were initially located. Great men are buried there, including several of the Heads of the Captivity, Princes of the house of David, and leaders of the Sanhedrins who stayed there before the "excision" <note>A reference to a historical calamity or the decline of the academies.</note>.

And in this region live those Jews who are called the sons of Rechab <note>The Rechabites, a group mentioned in the Bible known for their asceticism.</note>, also known by the name *Theima*. For *Theima* is the start of their jurisdiction, which they now govern under Hanan the Prince <note>original: "Hanan Hanassi".</note>. The city of *Theima* itself is large and populous; their region extends among the mountains called the "Northern Mountains" for a journey of sixteen days. It is cultivated with large and very well-fortified cities, and it is subject to no external yoke of any nation. From there, the inhabitants go out and plunder all the neighboring and even remote nations, as far as the Arabs, who have a treaty with them. And those Jews whom we just indicated cultivate fields and pastures, and they possess herds and cattle, occupying a very wide and vast region. From all their produce they give tithes for the use of the disciples of the wise, who are perpetually devoted to doctrine and preaching, and for the Pharisees <note>Used here in the sense of "ascetics" or "separate ones."</note> who mourn for Zion and weep for Jerusalem, perpetually abstaining from meat and wine...

### Dissertation II.

...[mour]ning, and always dressed in mourning clothes <note>original: "pullatis vestibus." Wearing black or dark clothing was a traditional sign of mourning, specifically for the destruction of the Temple in Jerusalem.</note>. Among these (the Jewish men mentioned above) is Solomon the Prince <note>original: "Selomoh Hanaßi." The title Nasi (Prince) was used for high-ranking communal leaders, often claiming Davidic descent.</note>, that is, the chief, and his brother Hanan the Prince, from the lineage of King David; a family scroll traced through the centuries indicates this. Now, all these men walk with torn garments and fast for forty days on behalf of all the Jews living in captivity. That province contains about 40 cities, 200 towns, and 100 fortresses. The metropolis and capital is Thenai, and the total number of Jews living in all those cities comprises nearly three hundred thousand people.

They say these people belong to the tribes of Reuben and Gad and the half-tribe of Manasseh, who were taken captive by Shalmaneser, King of the Assyrians, and sent here. They built very large cities and, emerging from those places, they have waged and continue to wage war with all the neighboring kings. No man can reach them because of the vast, most difficult deserts requiring an 18-day journey.

It is now the twelfth year since a certain man named David Alroy <note>David Alroy was a famous 12th-century Jewish messianic leader in Persia who led an uprising against the Seljuk Sultanate.</note> arose from the city of Amadiya <note>original: "Ghamaria," likely Amadiya in modern-day Iraq.</note>. He had been a disciple of the <term>Head of the Captivity <note>The Exilarch, or Resh Galuta, the political leader of the Jewish community in Babylon.</note></term> Hasdai and of the head of the Sanhedrin, Rabbi Jacob the Dignified, in the metropolis of Baghdad. He had become most learned in the Law of Moses, in the books of doctrine, in all external wisdom, in the Arabic language and script <note>original: "Ismaelitica linguâ atque scripturâ."</note>, and even in the books of the magicians and enchanters.

He therefore took it into his head to take up arms against the King of the Persians. Having gathered those Jews who lived in the Haphton mountains, he intended to wage war against all nations and seek to conquer even Jerusalem. To persuade the Jews of this, he used false signs, claiming he was sent by God to conquer Jerusalem and free them from the yoke of the nations; so much so that he gained the trust of many Jews and obtained the name of their Messiah.

<margin>
The presumed Messiah.
</margin>

When the King of the Persians heard the rumor of this matter, he summoned him to a meeting. Alroy went there completely undaunted, and when asked if he were the King of the Jews, he steadfastly answered that it was so. Immediately, he was ordered by the King to be seized and thrust into the prison where those held by royal sentence are kept for life.

After three days, however, a council of princes and ministers was gathered by the King to deliberate and take action concerning the revolutionary plot initiated by the Jews. Suddenly, that David appeared in their midst, having been released from prison of his own accord without any man's knowledge. When the King saw him, he asked in wonder: "Who brought you here or freed you from prison?" To which he replied, "My wisdom and my industry."

<margin>
The Head of the Captivity and the leaders of the Sanhedrin [sent] letters of this kind...
</margin>

Finally, a certain King of the Turkomans <note>original: "Tagarmim," referring to Togarmah, a traditional name for Turkic peoples.</note> arose, named Zain al-Din <note>original: "Zinaldin."</note>, who obeyed the King of the Persians. He sent ten thousand gold coins to the father-in-law of David Alroy to bring an end to this evil by secretly killing his son-in-law...

# On Law and the Orders of Citizens.
<sig>H</sig>

<margin>They wrote to David: "We want you to know that the time of our liberation has not yet come, nor have our signs yet been seen. For a man is not made strong by the wind [mere words]. Therefore..."</margin>...persuaded [the governor] to kill him. Once he had undertaken to do so, he stabbed David with a sword while he was lying in bed in his own home; such was the end of his plans and that empty cleverness. They say truly that in those mountains of Nishapur <note>original: "Nisbor," a city in Khorasan, modern-day Iran</note> three tribes of Israelites dwell, who were led away in the first captivity by Shalmaneser, King of the Assyrians—namely Dan, Zebulun, Asher, and Naphtali. As it is written: *And he led them away to Halah and Habor by the mountains of Gozan, the mountains of Media.* <note>Referencing 2 Kings 17:6</note>. The region of these people extends for a twenty-day journey and is filled with many cities and fortresses. It is almost entirely mountainous, washed on the other side by the Gozan River. These inhabitants are subject to the rule of no other nation; instead, they are governed by a certain Prefect, whose name is now Joseph Amarkala the Levite <note>original: "Ioseph Amarc̣ala Levites." 'Amarkal' is a Hebrew title for a high-ranking Temple or communal administrator</note>. Among them are disciples of the Sages; they cultivate the fields and wage war with the neighboring "sons of Cush" <note>original: "Chus," referring to the peoples of Ethiopia or Nubia</note>.

The Jews in Zeila <note>An ancient port city in modern-day Somaliland, then associated with the Ethiopian sphere</note> are observers of the commandments, keep the Law of Moses, and read the Prophets. They are not entirely without doctrinal books and traditional customs. In that region (Ethiopia), there are very large and high mountains where many Israelites live, subject to the rule of no other nation. They have cities and towers built upon the ridges of the mountains; descending from there with weapons in hand, they harass the region of Hamaghtam <note>Likely referring to the central Ethiopian highlands</note> with war. This is the same as Libya, under the jurisdiction of Edom <note>original: "Edom," a traditional Jewish term for Rome and the Christian world</note>. Having often plundered and despoiled it, the Israelites retreat back to their mountains, where they fear no war from any mortal.

<margin>Differences in the books.</margin>A different custom and rite is observed by various groups in the distribution of the annual [Torah] readings. Indeed, the Babylonians are accustomed to read a single portion <note>original: "Parassas," from the Hebrew *Parashah*</note> every week, following the same method that is formal across all of Spain; thus, they complete the entire Law in a single year. The Israelites [of the Land of Israel], however, divide each portion into three parts, and thus they read through the Law over a period of three years <note>This refers to the historic Triennial Cycle of Torah reading, which was eventually superseded by the Babylonian Annual Cycle</note>. Both groups, however, join together twice a year in a solemn rite to offer prayers: on the Day of the Joy of the Law <note>Simchat Torah</note> and on the festival of the giving of the Law <note>Shavuot</note>. Among all these people, Nathanael holds the first place—the greatest of the Princes and head of the Sanhedrin <note>The supreme Jewish legal assembly</note>—who presides over all the communities of Egypt and appoints Masters and overseers. He is among the first and most intimate ministers of the Great King. 

<margin>Status.</margin>Thus, all the Israelites remain dispersed throughout every region. Whoever hinders the gathering of Israel shall see no good sign, nor live with Israel. At the time when God shall visit our captivity and exalt the horn of His Anointed One <note>original: "Christi sui," using the Latin word for 'Messiah'</note>, then everyone shall say: "I will lead the Jews out, and I will gather them myself." Rejoice, our brothers, for Divine Salvation will be as swift as the blink of an eye. For if we, out of fear, did not doubt that the end had not yet arrived, we would surely have been gathered together by now. But as of yet, we cannot...

### CHAPTER V.

### *Containing conjectures regarding the conversion of the Hebrews to Christ our Savior.*

**M**any do not merely harbor a suspicion, but also earnestly assert that the Jews are to be converted to Christ (our common Savior) before the end of the World. This position is championed above all by those who strive to investigate the mystical and <term>anagogical</term> <note>The "anagogical" sense is a method of Biblical interpretation that seeks to find spiritual meanings relating to the end of the world or the afterlife</note> sense of Holy Scripture. Out of many such writers, it is pleasing to review several. In addition to those I shall cite sporadically below, this is the opinion of St. Bernard in his *Sermon 79 on the Song of Songs*. Also Johannes Ferus <note>The Latinized name of Johann Wild (1495–1554), a popular Catholic preacher and commentator</note>, a theologian of great authority in my eyes, in his learned explanation of Genesis, *at chapter 21, on my page 395; likewise at the end of chapter 27, page 482; and chapter 38, page 594.* Also Seraphinus Firmanus, an excellent contemplative, in his *Exposition of the Apocalypse, chapters 3, 7, 10, 14, and 19*, where he believes the Gentiles are likewise to be converted. Also Remaclus de Vaulx, in his *Divine Harpocrates, part 1, under the fourth sign accompanying the Antichrist, page 339.* Johannes Viterbiensis, in his *Tract on the Future Triumphs of Christians, chapter 20.* Juan Luis Vives <note>A prominent Spanish humanist</note> in his commentary on St. Augustine’s *City of God, book 7, chapter 32—see his notes there.* Martinus Cellarius (whose true name was Borrhaus and who was born in Stuttgart; his life was included by Melchior Adam in his *Lives of German Theologians*) in his treatise *On the Works of God*, printed in Strasbourg in the year 1527 and commended with an excellent preface by Wolfgang Fabricius Capito—*from page 72 until the end of the book.* Caelius Secundus Curio, formerly a Professor at Basel, in his treatise *On the Amplitude of the Blessed Kingdom of God*, recently reprinted in Gouda and likewise in Frankfurt, *book 1, throughout.* Petrus Cunaeus, in that elegant work filled with every kind of wisdom, in which he describes the *Republic of the Hebrews, book 1, chapter 18.* <note>Cunaeus's "De Republica Hebraeorum" was a highly influential work that analyzed the ancient Jewish state as a model for modern governments</note> Justus Heurnius (son of the very famous physician Johannes Heurnius) also follows this argument at great length and with much effort in his very pious writing, *On undertaking an Evangelical Mission to the Indians, chapter 4, throughout.* The anonymous author of a booklet joins in...

# Concerning Law and the Orders of Citizens

<page-num>64</page-num>
<header>Concerning Law and the orders of citizens.</header>

...of the booklet, printed at Heidelberg in the year 1608, which bears the title: *Humble Considerations on the Final Signs of the World's End, page 84 and following, last chapter.* <note>original German: "Ohn-vorgreiffliche betrachtung von den letzten Merckzeichen der Welt Ende"</note> Schadæus, in his *Special Treatise on the Conversion of the Jews*, <note>original: "tract. sing. von bekehrung der Juden"</note> at section E. 2., adds the Letter of Saint Bernard in support of this view. Georg Rostius also hopes for the conversion of the Jews in his *Theological Prognosis on the Last Day, book 1*, <note>original: "theolog. prognoft. Vom Jüngsten Tag"</note> where he also brings forward the authorities of others. Likewise, the treatises of Jacobus Brocardus <note>An Italian Protestant visionary known for his mystical interpretations of prophecy</note> concerning the first and second coming of the Messiah to the Hebrews contribute to this; there he tries to prove that the Jews turned their minds and eyes away from the first coming of Christ and looked only toward the second, fleeing from the afflictions that characterized the first coming and embracing only the good that was proposed in the second. Regarding this same matter, he also treats it in the preface to his *Interpretation of the Bible*, which is prefixed to his *Explanation of Mystical and Prophetic Genesis, page 4*; and likewise, mention of the conversion of the Jews is made in the same work in chapter 45 and following.

2. It is indeed for Theologians to dispute this most weighty theme; yet I think nothing prevents me, in the manner of <term>Philologists</term> <note>In this context, a "philologist" is a scholar of classical texts and history, rather than a systematic divine or clergyman</note>, from treating it now. Indeed, I shall clearly perform the duties of a Collector, or a mere reporter. Judge and evaluate this piously, dear Reader. But do not be prejudiced against this at the very threshold because today many learned, pious, and wise men—who wish all people to obtain the knowledge of truth and salvation—nevertheless have little hope for the conversion of Jews and other infidels to Christ. 

For even the Apostles admitted into their minds only with great difficulty that the Gentiles—who, having utterly cast aside and trampled upon the religion of the Fathers, worshipped Idols instead of the living God—also belonged to the fellowship of the Messiah. And Saint Peter himself did not depart from that error until he was called back from it by a stupendous <term>oracle</term> <note>Refers to the vision of the sheet in Acts 10, which taught Peter that no person should be called "unclean"</note> from heaven. <margin>Theodor Bibliander, *On the Fates of the Roman Monarchy, in the preface, section 1. b.*</margin> But here too one must attend to the Divine oracles, and not decide concerning the salvation of men by the blind judgment of human reason. Furthermore, those oracles are mostly concerning incredible things, which no one had hoped for before they happened. Clement, *Recognitions, page 16*. Come then, let us consult them!

3. The text which seems most clear on that matter is found in Saint Paul, in the *Epistle to the Romans, chapter 11, verse 25 and following*, where he writes thus: *I do not want you to be ignorant, brothers, of this Mystery; so that you are not wise in your own conceits (or lest you be of a haughty mind within yourselves) because blindness (or hardening) has happened in part to Israel, until the fullness of the Gentiles has entered in: and so all Israel shall be saved. As it is written: There shall come out of Zion one who delivers (a redeemer) and shall turn away ungodliness from Jacob. And this is my testament to them, when I shall have taken away their sins.* <note>The author provides a Latin translation of the Greek New Testament text, emphasizing the "mystery" of Israel's temporary rejection</note> From these words, light is shed upon the preceding 12th verse: *But if their fall...*

## Dissertation II.

<page-num>65</page-num>

...of them, are the riches of the world, and their reduction is the riches of the nations; how much more their fullness! <note>original Latin: "divitiæ sunt mundi, & diminutio eorum divitiæ gentium; quantò magis plenitudo eorum." This is a translation of Romans 11:12.</note> Likewise in verse 15: For if their loss (or rejection) is the reconciliation of the world, what will their acceptance be but life from the dead? Commentators almost universally interpret these passages as referring to the future conversion of the Hebrews. This includes Haymo, Bishop of Halberstadt, Zwingli, Hunnius, Balduinus, etc., and likewise Mentzer in his *Analysis of Chapter 11 of Romans, volume 1, of the Giessen Disputations.* Remacle de Vaulx, in the *previously cited work on leaf 344*, attests that the unanimous explanation of both ancient and modern writers leans toward this conclusion. Now, I shall illustrate the more difficult words using the explanations of others.

*It happens in part*) <note>original: "Ex parte contingit"</note> The hardening does not encompass all of Israel, but only a part of it. For since there have always been some who came to the faithful, even this blindness will not be perpetual; but a time is coming when not just a few Israelites, but "all Israel" (that is, the nation itself) shall be gathered into the Church of Christ by common consent. And this is indeed that prophetic secret, so often emphasized by the Prophets, which Paul does not want the Gentiles to be ignorant of, lest they despise the Jews as if they were rejected forever. *Beza in his annotations on the same passage.*

*The fullness of the Gentiles*) <note>original: "Plenitudo gentium"</note> This can be said to have arrived when the Gospel has been preached in the whole world. This interpretation pleases Caelius Secundus Curio in *book 1, leaf 71*, who writes: the Jews will remain in that blindness and calamity until the remaining nations of the world have flocked to the Gospel, the times of which are defined by God. Similarly, regarding what is predicted of Ephraim in *Genesis chapter 48, verse 19*—that his seed would become the "fullness of nations"—Heurnius <note>leaf 146 and following</note> connects this with the words of the Apostle. He refers it to the conversion of the pagans who still remain, rather than to a numerous biological offspring (in which the tribe of Judah excelled). This is especially so because the word <term>The Nations</term> <note>original Hebrew: "הגויים" (HaGoyim)</note> seems to him to be specific to the Gentiles. Likewise, our Savior in *Luke chapter 21, verse 24* calls that which Saint Paul here calls the "fullness of the nations" the "Times of the Nations"—namely, the time of the dominion of those who are not Jews. Just like the Hebrews, these nations will also be punished in their own turn when their iniquity is ripe.

*The Deliverer*) <note>original: "Vindex," or Avenger/Vindicator</note> When he says "the deliverer or vindicator shall come," there can be no doubt that he speaks of a future arrival. And when he says "that deliverer," whom else do you think he means but Jesus Christ—the sole and perpetual savior, preserver, and liberator? This is especially clear since he adds "out of Zion," that is, from the Jewish nation itself, to which this prophecy looks. Because a certain Divine man and...

# On Law, and the orders of citizens.
<header>On Law, and the orders of citizens.</header>

...it seems not at all foreign to God's manner that a great man is to be sent by God, who may awaken and restore a Religion now extinct and a Church nearly collapsed. For that greatest Father is accustomed, when all things seem almost hopeless, to raise up some Elijah, etc. *Curio, in the cited book 1, folio 27 and following.* <note>The author cites Caelius Secundus Curio (1503–1569), an Italian humanist and theologian known for his views on the wide extent of God's kingdom.</note>

*What will their acceptance be, if not life from the dead?* <note>original: "Quæ assumptio, nisi vita ex mortuis". This refers to Romans 11:15.</note> It shall come to pass that when the Jews have approached the Gospel, the world will, as it were, come back to life. For then the joy of the world will be full and overflowing; when the Jews themselves, having embraced Christ along with the Gentiles, shall enjoy that common happiness. For although reconciliation is already scattered throughout the world, yet since the nation of the Jews still remains in death, the world has not yet fully come back to life: *Theodore Beza, in his annotations here.* <note>Theodore Beza (1519–1605) was a major Protestant reformer and successor to John Calvin.</note> Caelius Secundus Curio holds nearly the same views on these matters, *folio 43*. All things smile at the presence of Jesus Christ and his saving word; in his absence, they are foul and neglected, and as the poet sang:

->*All things then smile, but if the fair Alexis*<-
->*should depart from these mountains, you would see even the rivers dry up, etc.*<-
<note>original: "Omnia tunc rident, at si formosus Alexis / Montibus his abeat, videas & flumina sicca." This is a quote from Virgil’s Eclogue VII, used here as a metaphor for the spiritual desolation felt in the absence of Christ.</note>

<margin>Heurnius, *On the Evangelical Mission to the Indians*, chapter 4, folio 144 and following.</margin> <note>Justus Heurnius (1587–1652) was a Dutch physician and missionary to the East Indies; his work often discussed the global spread of Christianity.</note>
4. And there are other sayings everywhere, almost innumerable, which are thought to lean toward this conclusion; some of which I shall set forth here in order. In Genesis *chapter 49*, Jacob forewarns his sons "what will happen to them in the latter days." <note>original: "novissimis diebus"</note> To Judah, he promises the Messiah. Regarding Simeon and Levi, he mystically instills unfavorable things because of the killing of the Messiah: *verse 6.* But to the ten tribes who are to be dispersed, he announces a gathering, and that at last they shall possess their possessors. Regarding their victory, that sigh in *verse 18*—"I have waited for thy salvation, O Lord!"—indicates enough that he is dealing with Dan. Likewise, the same was said of Gad; *verse 19*, that an army shall overcome him, but he shall overcome at last: what else is this, than that he shall be led into captivity by the Pagans <note>original: "ab Ethnicis"</note>, but at last he shall lead his captors captive, with the spiritual weapons of God and the Messiah? Perhaps Balaam looked toward this outcome when he said: *Numbers chapter 23, verse 23*, "According to the time it shall be said to Jacob and to Israel: What has God wrought?" So also the other blessings of Joseph, *in the cited chapter 49 of Genesis, verse 26*, are eternal; nor do they seem yet to have fully ceased. Hardly anything is found promised to anyone else in more pregnant or more emphatic words: "The blessings of your father" (these are the words of Jacob to Joseph) "have prevailed above the blessings of my progenitors; even to the boundary of the everlasting hills they shall be on the head of Joseph," etc. There is also a distinguished and clear passage in Deuteronomy *chapter 30, verse 3*: "And the Lord thy God will bring back thy captivity, and have mercy upon thee, and will return and gather thee from all the nations whither the Lord thy God hath scattered thee."

### Dissertation II.
I 2
<meta>catchword: and oc-</meta>

"Your God there," etc. This has certainly not yet happened, nor was it fully fulfilled in the return from the Babylonian captivity, but that return (perhaps) was only a type <note>In biblical interpretation, a "type" is a historical event or person that foreshadows a future reality.</note> of a future liberation still to come, as argued in *Nehemiah chapter 1.* This is proven, I think, by the aforementioned *Deuteronomy chapter 30, verse 6:* "And the Lord your <margin>Paul Fagius, in his notes on Onkelos’s Targum, Deuteronomy chapter 30.</margin> God will circumcise your heart, and the heart of your offspring" (or as the Aramaic of Onkelos <note>Onkelos was a famous 2nd-century scholar; his "Targum" is an authoritative Aramaic translation and commentary on the Hebrew Bible.</note> has it: "He will remove the foolishness of your heart, and the foolishness of the heart of your children") "to love the Lord your God with all your heart, and with all your soul, so that you may live." Likewise in *verse 8:* "And you will return, and obey the voice of the Lord, and do all his commandments," etc. No one can easily prove that this was accomplished in Israel after the Babylonian exile. Furthermore, what is said there regarding the circumcision of the heart clearly pertains to the Christian faith (without which no one’s heart is cleansed) and foretells the end of ceremonial circumcision. 

To this may be added Deuteronomy chapter 32, *from verse 19 to 43*, and the explanation given by Heurnius <note>Justus Heurnius (1587–1652) was a Dutch theologian and physician who wrote on the conversion of non-Christian peoples.</note> on *page 144.* Indeed, the promise found in *1 Samuel chapter 12, verse 22* is eternal. Psalm 74 must be understood as referring to the final captivity of the Jews. For in *verse 9* it says: "We do not see our signs, there is no longer a prophet," etc. These things do not seem to refer to the Babylonian captivity, for they had prophets then <note>Prophets like Daniel and Ezekiel were active during or immediately after the Babylonian exile, whereas the author argues the current Jewish "exile" is marked by a lack of such figures.</note>. And yet, help and an eventual liberation are promised there. Such a promise also appears in Psalm 80 <note>The original Latin text cites "Psalm 8," but the context and verse reference (v. 17 to the end) suggest Psalm 80, which speaks of the restoration of Israel.</note>; *see verse 17 to the end.*

§. The Prophets, especially, abound with such examples. In Isaiah *chapter 11*, after the Lord had said that a shoot would come forth from the root <margin>Justus Heurnius, in the cited work, page 163.</margin> of Jesse, who would be a banner for the peoples, and that the nations would seek him; he adds concerning the final calling of the Jews in *verse 11, etc.*: "And it shall come to pass in that day, the Lord will set his hand a SECOND time to possess the remnant of his people which shall be left from Assyria, and from Egypt, and from Pathros, and from Cush, and from Elam, and from Shinar, and from Hamath, and from the islands of the sea. And he will raise a banner for the nations, and will gather the outcasts of Israel, and will assemble the dispersed of Judah from the four corners of the earth." And in *verse 15 and following:* "And the Lord will utterly destroy the tongue of the Egyptian sea with a curse, and will <margin>Heurnius, page 175.</margin> wave his hand over the river with the strength of his Spirit; and he will strike it into seven streams, and make them walk over in their sandals. And there shall be a highway for the remnant of his people who are left from Assyria, just as there was for Israel in the day he came up from the land of Egypt." 

That chapter 27 of the same Isaiah treats this same theme is proven to me by the most certain evidence: that Saint Paul, in the previously praised 11th chapter to the Romans, *verse 27*, cites the 9th verse of that chapter. Therefore, the Prophet prophesies thus: "In that day the Lord will visit with his hard, and great, and strong sword upon Leviathan the fleeing serpent; and upon Leviathan the twisting serpent..."

# On Law and the Orders of Citizens

<meta>catchword: verse 10</meta>

...[without] a Prince, and without sacrifice, and without a statue <margin>(see Hosea 3:4)</margin> and without an Ephod <note>A ceremonial vestment worn by the High Priest</note> or Teraphim <note>Ancient household idols or tools for divination</note>. Afterward, the children of Israel shall return and seek the Lord their God and David their King: and they shall tremble before the Lord and His goodness in the last days. Jonathan, the son of Uzziel, the Chaldean Paraphrast <note>original: "Chaldæus Paraphrastes"; refers to the author of the Targum, an Aramaic translation and expansion of the Hebrew Bible</note>—whom Immanuel Tremellius <note>A prominent 16th-century Jewish convert to Christianity and Hebraist</note> rendered into Latin and praised in his dedicatory preface as the best and clearest interpreter—translates the passage this way: "For a long time the children of Israel will remain without a King from the descendants of David; nor will there be anyone to exercise power in Israel, or anyone to perform sacrifices acceptable to God in Jerusalem; nor will there be any statue in Samaria, nor any Ephod or prophet. Afterward, the Israelites will be converted and will be eager for the worship of the Lord their God, and they will obey Christ, the son of David, their King: they will submit themselves to the worship of the Lord, and His future goodness will be immense, which shall come upon them in the last days."

After Saint Jerome, all the ancient commentators understand this place to refer to the conversion of the Jews to the Christian faith at the end of the world. This is attested by Remaclus de Vaulx in his *The Divine Harpocrates* <note>original: "Harpocrate divino," a work by de Vaulx linking silence and mystical theology</note>, part 1, thesis 29, page 341. Haymo, Bishop of Halberstadt, understands this place in the same way in his commentary; and especially Wolfgang Fabricius Capito <note>A leading German reformer and Hebraist</note>, whose commentary it may perhaps not be regrettable to have seen. Add, if you please, Jacobus Brocardus <note>A 16th-century mystical interpreter of prophecy</note> in his work *On the Coming of Christ*, page 126 and following, and also page 135. Add also Origen in the first chapter of his *Philocalia*, page 56, etc.

The words found in Amos, in the last chapter, verses 14 and following, also seem to lean in this direction to some: "I will turn back the captivity of my people Israel, and they shall build the desolate cities and inhabit them... And I will plant them upon their own soil, and they shall never again be plucked up." <margin>BEYOND.</margin> Likewise, the words of Micah, chapter 2, verse 12: "In gathering I will gather Jacob, all of you; in gathering I will gather the remnant of Israel," etc. And in chapter 4, verse 6 and following: "In that day, says the Lord, I will gather the limping one, and I will gather her who was cast out and whom I have afflicted. And I will make the limping one into a remnant, and her who was driven far off into a robust nation," etc.

Furthermore, regarding what the Prophet adds there in verse 8: "And you, O tower of the flock, the high place of the daughter of Zion, unto you it shall come, even the first dominion shall come: the Kingdom to the daughter of Jerusalem." Jonathan the Paraphrast explains it thus: "But you, O Christ of Israel, who lie hidden in obscurity because of the sins of the congregation of Zion: to you the Kingdom shall come," etc. Behold, he does not say that *another* Messiah will come at that time (whom the Jews now wait for in vain), but that he who was hidden from them because of the sins of the Hebrews will then hold power. And perhaps there is an allusion here to that Jewish fable or allegory: that the Messiah stays in Rome in a lowly persona <note>Refers to the legend that the Messiah waits at the gates of Rome among the poor and the lepers</note>—see Helvicus in his *Refutation*, regarding the second general reason—and that he moves among the poor—see Ricius in *Talmudic Doctrine*, page 271 and following. So too what Zephaniah says in the last chapter, verse 10...

# Dissertation II.

*verse 10.* "From beyond the rivers of Ethiopia <note>original: 'Chus', the biblical name for the region of Ethiopia and the Upper Nile</note>, my suppliants, the daughter of my dispersed ones, shall bring my offering." The Chaldean <note>referring to the Targum, the Aramaic paraphrase of the Hebrew Bible</note> explains it this way: "From the regions beyond the rivers of India, through mercy the exiles shall return—my people, who had been led away—and they will bring them as offerings." Furthermore, is there not a sufficiently illustrious prophecy on this matter in Zechariah, chapter 12, <margin>Heurnius, folio 162 and following.</margin> *verse 10.* "I will pour out upon the house of David, and upon the inhabitant of Jerusalem, the spirit of grace and of mercies; and they shall look upon me whom they have pierced." Finally, I add the concluding summary <term>epiphonema: a striking concluding sentence or summary statement</term> of the prophecy of Malachi, in the final chapter, the penultimate and last verses: "Behold, I send you Elijah the prophet, before the great and terrible day of the Lord comes. And he will turn the heart of the fathers to the children, and the heart of the children to their fathers: lest perhaps I come and strike the earth with a curse <note>original: 'anathemate', a formal curse or ban</note>."

Regarding this, Saint Theodoret, Bishop of Cyrrhus, writes in his commentary: "He spoke of the day of the second coming; and he teaches what the great Elijah will do (the Chaldean made indefinite mention of the Prophet) when he comes; and shows the reason why he comes first. Lest, he says, having found you all in unbelief, I should banish you into perpetual punishments, Elijah will come first; and he will persuade your minds, O Jews, so that without hesitation you may be joined with the faithful of the nations <note>Gentiles</note>, and be brought back into my one Church." He calls the Jews "fathers" because they were called first, and the nations "sons" because they came after them. In this way, the other Holy Fathers have always understood Malachi; and they considered this passage from him to be a perfect twin to what our Savior says in Matthew, chapter 17, *verse 11:* "Elijah indeed is to come first, and he will restore all things." 

And specifically, Saint Augustine, in *On the Harmony of the Evangelists*, Book 1, Chapter 21, says: "He will restore all things; that is, he will solidify in the faith those who are disturbed by the persecution of the Antichrist." Add to this Remacle de Vaulx, part 1, in the fourth sign, folio 339, and thesis 29, folio 345. Then those who pierced Christ shall see Him. And regarding Enoch and Elijah, see Cardinal Bellarmine, extensively in Book 3 on the Roman Pontiff, chapter 6, and the treatise against James, King of England, folio 190, and 6. Riccius folio 272, Remacle de Vaulx, folio 282, and Ludovicus ab Alcazar of the Society of Jesus, on the Apocalypse <note>The Book of Revelation</note>, chapter 11, verse 3, note 65.

The fourth book of Ezra <note>Also known as 2 Esdras, an apocalyptic book found in many Bibles of the era but often categorized as apocryphal</note> commonly has a poor reputation; see Cardinal Robert Bellarmine on the Word of God, Book 1, final chapter; the notes of Tremellius and Junius on the Bible; Jacob Cappellus in his *Defense of Casaubon*, Book 2, chapter 5, also Book 3, chapter 1, folio 63. And now, in some places, anyone who believes it to be canonical <note>officially accepted as part of the inspired Bible</note> is considered a heretic. This was done, however, by Bibliander <note>Theodore Bibliander, a Swiss Reformer</note> in his treatise *On the Fates of the Roman Monarchy*, folio 4 and following. Nor did Saint Ambrose, a primary Doctor of the Church, hesitate to seek testimony from the 7th chapter of that book to establish the principal article of the Christian faith concerning the resurrection of the dead, in his book *On the Good of Death*. <margin>Heurnius <note>Justus Heurnius (1587–1652), a Dutch theologian and missionary known for his work on the conversion of non-Christians.</note>, folio 136 and following, and also folio 141 and following.</margin>
10. Long-lasting has been the blindness of the Jews, and stubborn their error; nor has anyone yet been able to use the reasoning of Scripture to make them hear what God speaks through the Prophets. This happens indeed by a singular judgment of God, one to be adored by human reason rather than scrutinized: but nevertheless, in the meantime, no one should doubt that their iniquities have thus far created a division between God and themselves: their sins have caused Him to hide His face from them, so that He might not hear them. That biting wickedness of usury <term>usuraria pravitas: the practice of lending money at interest, which was historically used as a primary legal and social grievance against Jewish communities</term>, for which they are infamous continually and even to this day, turns the kind face of God away from them. "Because of the iniquity of his greed I was angry, and I struck him while hiding myself," says the Lord in Isaiah *chapter 57, verse 17.* There is hardly a people more alienated from the virtues which our Savior commends to his followers. They do not love their enemies; in the greatest narrowness and hardships by which they are oppressed on all sides, they are not humble: they adore the letter, but are ignorant of the spirit, etc. The prophet Jeremiah says somewhere that their sin is written with a very hard diamond. And truly, they do not even today pray for the removal of their punishment, just as their fathers once did when they said: "His blood be upon us and upon our children." *Cunaeus <note>Petrus Cunaeus (1586–1638), author of "De Republica Hebraeorum," a foundational text on the Hebrew state.</note>, in the book "On the Hebrew Republic," chapter 18, folio 167 and following.*

11. But there are, furthermore, not a few things which make this hoped-for conversion of the Jews credible; and they easily persuade anyone weighing the matter more deeply that God, most Good and Great, is reserving them for some great and admirable work. Most wretched is the condition of the Jews. Dispersed, wandering, and exiled from both their climate and their soil, they roam through the world; without man, without God, without a King: as Tertullian says in *Apology, chapter 21.*

---

<margin>Aurelius Prudentius <note>A 4th-century Christian Latin poet.</note>, in "The Divinity of Christ" (Apotheosis), near the end.</margin>
> Your limbs, uprooted, are carried through all
> regions of the lands and the sea:
> The Jew wanders as a wandering exile, fluctuating here and there:
> after being torn from his ancestral home,
> he pays the penalty for murder, and
> sprinkled with the blood of the Christ he denied, he atones for the crimes committed:
> from which time the virtue of the ancient ancestors ebbed away.

But let us hear what Saint Jerome <note>A prominent Church Father and translator of the Vulgate Bible.</note> worthily declaims against the Jews *in his commentary on Zephaniah, chapter 1:* "To this present day," he says, "the faithless tenants, after the killing of the servants and finally the Son of God, are forbidden from entering Jerusalem, except to weep; and they buy at a price the permission to weep for the ruin of their city: so that those who once bought the blood of Christ now buy their own tears. You may see on the day when it was captured by the Romans and des[troyed]..."

<header>Dissertation II.</header>
<sig>K</sig>

...safe Jerusalem <note>original: "tuta Ierusalem"; this likely follows from the previous page's description of the people returning to the city</note> comes a mourning people: decrepit little women and old men, burdened by rags and years, flow together, demonstrating the wrath of God in both their bodies and their dress. A crowd of wretched people is gathered, and while the Lord's cross gleams and his Resurrection <note>original: "anastasi", from the Greek 'anastasis'</note> radiates, and from the Mount of Olives the shining banner of the cross also glows, they mourn the ruins of their Temple. They are a wretched people, and yet they are not to be pitied, etc. Regarding their vain attempts to rebuild the Temple, see Rufinus <note>Rufinus of Aquileia (c. 344–411), a monk and historian known for his Latin translation and continuation of Eusebius's Church History.</note>, *History, book 10, the second-to-last and last chapters.* But truly, the fact that they are preserved in this very most wretched misery, and not fundamentally rooted out, is surely not without a great miracle. It does not happen without mystery that they are like victims of public hatred, yet they are tolerated throughout the whole world. Among Christians, Saracens <note>A term commonly used in the early modern period to refer to Muslims.</note>, and even the idolaters themselves, they have a safe dwelling place; a privilege which no other sect seems to enjoy. <margin>*Marquardus de Susannis, Treatise on Jews and Usury, part 1, chapter 2;* see also *Justus Lipsius, in Monita, book 1, chapter 3, number 11, near the end.*</margin> They are certainly a very numerous people, who nevertheless propagate themselves even in the highest contempt. Through infinite persecutions, they still find a place to dwell among the nations, and this is undoubtedly by Divine disposition, which guides the hearts of Kings. And what stands as a stupendous miracle: they live by our labors, while they themselves remain idle. <note>This reflects a common 17th-century stereotype that viewed the Jewish population's focus on commerce and finance as "idleness" compared to manual or agricultural labor.</note> Therefore, the matter itself seems to cry out: this nation, even today, is part of Divine providence. By this providence it has happened that while all other nations have been mixed and confused with one another (so much so that very few families can trace their origin back a thousand years), the Jews alone remain, whose known history is as old as the world. Conquered by so many persecutions in the Christian world, they yet remain there. And indeed, Saint Paul also venerates them and extols them with magnificent praise, even while they err. For he says: "To them belong the adoption, the glory, the covenants, the giving of the law, the divine worship, and the promises; to them belong the Fathers, and from them, according to the flesh, is the Christ." <margin>*Mr. Johannes Kepler, Treatise on the New Star in the Serpent, chapter 30, folio 205 and following.*</margin> I admit, indeed, that today they are all of a servile and ungenerous character; nor, if you look at their lives or pursuits, would you find anything greatly worthy of great and upright minds. <margin>*Cunaeus, On the Hebrew Republic, last chapter, folio 170, etc.*</margin> But let us not wonder at the cause of this matter: for these things have a certain cycle, as it were; so that, just as the seasons turn, so do the turns of character. Certainly, the same day that took away from them the bright light of liberty and struck the empire from their hands also subdued their noble spirits, so that they now possess nothing more of lively vigor. And they were just the same under the Egyptian captivity: lowly, servile, and those who refused the liberty offered by God, nor did they grasp it manfully enough when it was thrust upon them. It is divine grace alone that adds spirit, reputation, and industry; when it withdraws, all those things fall; when it returns, they sprout anew. This custom the Lord preserves as if perpetual,

# Concerning Law and the Ranks of Citizens.

...vides, so that before He fully fulfills His promises, He permits all things to fall into ruin—the opposite of what was expected—for the sake of testing and proving our faith and declaring His power. Thus, nothing can be less hoped for than that which He promised would come to pass. *Martin Cellarius, On the Works of God, folio 78.* Certainly, this change will be sudden and unhoped for, as Heurnius <note>Johannes Heurnius (1543–1601), a Dutch physician and philosopher.</note> concludes from *Isaiah chapter 66, verse 6, folio 159:* "Who has heard of such a thing? Who has seen such things? Shall the earth be born in one day? Shall a nation be brought forth at once? For as soon as Zion travailed, she also brought forth her children."

11. Furthermore, it is considered a testimony and a covenant of this future illumination <note>Referring to the spiritual "enlightenment" or conversion of the Jews expected by many early modern theologians.</note> that in *Isaiah chapter 59, verse 21*, Jehovah says: "This is my covenant with them: My Spirit that is upon you, and my words which I have put in your mouth, shall not depart out of your mouth, nor out of the mouth of your seed, nor out of the mouth of your seed’s seed, from now and forever." Hence, the "Treasurers of the Christian Church" are not unskillfully so named by Augustine in *On Christian Doctrine* and *on Psalm 58*. And Saint Paul attests that the oracles of God <note>original: "eloquia Dei"</note> were entrusted to them. *Romans 3.* Saint Augustine, *on Psalm 41*, identifies them as our "book-bearers" <note>original: "capsarios"; specifically, slaves who carried boxes of books for students, implying that the Jews preserve the scriptures that Christians study.</note>, who carry the codices for us while we study. 

And thus, perhaps those who say they corrupted the Hebrew text of the sacred books should not be listened to; a point which Marcus Marinus of Brescia proves with many arguments in the *preface to Noah's Ark*, and to which Benedictus Arias Montanus <note>A Spanish Orientalist and editor of the Antwerp Polyglot Bible.</note> gives his consent in his *commentary on the various readings of the Hebrew books*. And indeed, the most learned Bellarmine <note>Robert Bellarmine (1542–1621), an influential Jesuit Cardinal.</note>, in *On the Word of God, book 2, chapter 2*, denies that the Jews studiously corrupted the Bible out of hatred for the Christian faith. However, he strives to prove that the "Hebrew source" is murky in some places; this is done, as I think, more out of hatred for those who rashly blame Jerome's translation <note>The Latin Vulgate translation of the Bible.</note> than because he seems to seriously intend it. See *Johannes Wower's Collection on the Interpretation of the Bible, chapter 12*, and on the integrity of the Hebrew text, see *Helvicus in Vindiciae Locorum*, and Sixtus Amama in his *Critique of the Vulgate Version, in the final folio of the introduction.*

<margin>
Petrus Cunaeus, Book 1 of *The Hebrew Republic*, last chapter, folio 173 and following. 
Johannes Buxtorf in his *History of the Masorah*, chapter 2, etc.
</margin>

With one voice, Christians deeply imbued with Hebrew learning confess: the diligence of the ancient Hebrews was admirable, their zeal for the preservation of the text of God’s word was most burning, and their devotion was inimitable and without example in any nation of the whole world. Nor is it possible, after that labor which the Masoretes <note>A group of Jewish scholar-scribes (roughly 6th–10th centuries CE) who standardized the text of the Hebrew Bible.</note> performed, that any change or corruption could have fallen or could ever fall upon any of the biblical books. This has not happened to any other nation. We see that this good fortune did not befall the books of the Chaldeans (Master Schickard in *Bechinat, folio 22*), nor the Greeks, nor the Romans, nor other peoples. Regarding the Masorah, see Master Schickard in *Bechinat, folio 4, 5, etc., also folio 50*, and Master Buxtorf in *Tiberias*. Nevertheless, Saint Justin <note>Justin Martyr (c. 100–165 CE).</note>, in his work *Against Trypho*, claims that the Jews falsified the scriptures. In this way, he relates certain things from *Isaiah* which now...

...are not held to be authentic. Bellarmine, *Book 4 on Christ, chapter 13.* The same **Saint Martyr** <note>original: "S. Martyr"; likely referring to Justin Martyr (c. 100–165 AD), who famously accused Jewish leaders of removing passages from the Bible that pointed to Jesus.</note> says that the Jews erased the passage concerning Christ's descent into hell. Bellarmine, *in the same book, chapter 14;* add also Serrarius of the Society of Jesus in his *Prolegomena to the Bible, folio 71 and following, in many places.* Tertullian in *Against the Jews*, also *Against Marcion, book 3*, and **Saint Augustine** in his commentary on *Psalm 95:* "The Lord reigned from the wood." <note>original: "Dominus regnavit à ligno"; a famous early Christian variant of Psalm 96:10 (numbered 95 in the Latin Vulgate). Early Christians argued the original text included "from the wood," referring to the Cross.</note> But here the church fathers erred, or some other mystery lies beneath the version; because neither Jerome nor our Greek version contains this passage. See Schindler’s *Lexicon* under the word <term>malak <note>original Hebrew: מלך; meaning "to reign" or "to be king."</note></term> and add Fuller, *book 3, chapter 13.* Bellarmine, in *Book 2 on the Word of God, chapter 13*, and *Grasser in his commentary on the Psalms* relate that many things in that place were changed through the transposition of letters; they argue that the translation of the Seventy Interpreters <note>The Septuagint, the ancient Greek translation of the Hebrew Bible.</note> is more intact, and that the Vulgate follows this because it is consistent with the Psalms. 12. There are also those who note the signs of the time in which this future conversion will occur. Namely, when the dominion of the Christian faith shall spread to the furthest ends of the world: God, as the nations flock to the banners of the Messiah, will show the Jews the glory of His power through the Gospel of Christ. In this way, He will provoke them to emulation <note>A reference to the Apostle Paul's idea in Romans 11:14 that the success of Christianity among non-Jews would make the Jewish people "jealous" and lead them back to God.</note> and open the eyes of the blinded, so that they may finally see the wonders of their Messiah, Jesus. And so (as some think), while labor is being spent on bringing the remnants of the Heathens to the true worship of God, the Jews will then be converted. It is certainly agreed among all that this happiness of the Jews is destined for the final times and the consummation of all things. For in Daniel, *the last chapter, verse 7*, the Angel swears that when the scattering of the power of the holy people is finished, all those things which were previously foretold will also be finished. Mr. Kepler <note>Johannes Kepler (1571–1630), the renowned astronomer, who also wrote on theology and chronology.</note>, in *On the New Star in the Foot of the Serpent, folio 206*, is of the opinion that all today's contentions and differences of worship among Christians generally tend toward this: that from their mutual ruin, an opportunity may arise for converting the Indians on one side, and the Jews and Turks on the other. The Jews rejected Christ and handed Him over to the nations; but when these nations, being no better than they, dared to pursue and even kill His body (namely, the true Church) while it was afflicted with insults, the Lord will rise again and reveal Himself specifically to the Jews, sending them out for the expansion of His Kingdom. That this time is now pressing is argued by the atheism prevalent in many people today.

# On Law, and the orders of citizens.

...[God] allows, so that before He absolutely fulfills His promises: so He permits everything to fall apart into the opposite, for the sake of testing and proving our faith, and for the sake of declaring His power; so that nothing less can be hoped for than that which He promised would be. *Martin Cellarius, On the Works of God, folio 78.* <note>Martin Cellarius (1499–1564), also known as Martin Borrhaus, was a Reformer and scholar who wrote on the mysterious ways God interacts with humanity.</note> Indeed, Heurnius gathers from *Isaiah chapter 66, verse 6, folio 159*, that this change will be sudden and unexpected: "Who has heard such a thing? Who has seen such things? Has a land been born in one day? Or shall a nation be born all at once? For Zion has travailed and also brought forth her children."

11. Furthermore, of this future illumination, it is considered a testimony and a covenant that in *Isaiah chapter 59, verse 21*, Jehovah says: "This is my covenant with them: My Spirit which is upon you, and my words which I have placed in your mouth, shall not depart from your mouth, nor from the mouth of your seed, nor from the mouth of your seed’s seed, from now and forever." <note>original Latin: "ex nunc, & usq; in seculū"</note> Hence, not unskillfully, the "Treasurers of the Christian Church" are spoken of by **Augustine** *in his book On Christian Doctrine, and on Psalm 58.* And Divine Paul, in *Romans 3*, attests that the oracles of God were entrusted to them. Saint Augustine, in his commentary *on Psalm 41*, calls them our **desk-bearers** <note>original: "capsarios"; a term for slaves or servants who carried the boxes (capsae) of books for students, implying the Jewish people preserved the scriptures for Christian use while not studying them in the Christian sense themselves.</note> who carry the books for us as we study. And thus, perhaps, we should not listen to those who say that they have corrupted the Hebrew text of the sacred books: a point which *Marcus Marinus Brixianus* proves with many arguments in the *Preface to Noah's Ark*, and to which *Benedictus Arias Montanus* gives his consent in his *Commentary on the various readings of the Hebrew books.* And although the most learned **Bellarmine**, *in On the Word of God, book 2, chapter 2*, denies that the Jews zealously corrupted the Bible out of hatred for the Christian faith, he still labors to prove that the "Hebrew fountain" <note>A common metaphor in this period comparing the "pure" Hebrew source to the "murky" Latin translations.</note> is murky in some places. This happens more, I think, out of hatred for those who rashly blame the Hieronymian version <note>The Vulgate translation produced by St. Jerome in the 4th century.</note> than because he seems to seriously desire it; see *Johann Wower’s Treatise on the Interpretation of the Bible, chapter 12*, and on the integrity of the Hebrew text, see *Helvicus*
