# On Law and the Orders of Citizens.

->### Dissertation II.<-
K 2

...there may be folds; followed in turn by Judah and Benjamin. Perhaps because of this, the Jews promise themselves great things from those brothers of theirs; regarding these, one can see Cunæus <note>Petrus Cunaeus (1586–1638), a Dutch jurist who wrote "The Hebrew Republic," a highly influential study of ancient Jewish government</note> *Book 1, on the Hebrew Republic, chapter 8, folio 66, etc., and also chapter 10.* Some conclude from the words of Saint Paul <note>Romans 11:15</note> that these same Israelites will even call the nations to the faith; when he says: the future acceptance of Israel will be life from the dead; that is, the living power of the Holy Spirit, bursting forth from the dead Jews; effective for converting the remaining Gentiles through the proclamation of the Kingdom of Heaven. To this they also bring forward *Isaiah chapter 66, verse 18:* "I (shall visit) their works and their thoughts, when I come to gather all nations and tongues," etc. Furthermore, Heurnius <note>Justus Heurnius (1587–1652), a Dutch missionary and theologian</note> conjectures *folio 176 and following* that the converted remnant of the Jews, united with the Church of the Gentiles, will resist the assaults of the enemies of God and will oppose the Antichrist (meaning all those who craftily profess the name of Christ but disdain to follow His law and standard of life). Regarding these Jews who have already become Christians, Seraphinus Firmanus <note>An Italian commentator on the Book of Revelation</note> writes, *on the Apocalypse, folio 666:* "These will be most faithful and most chaste above all others, endowed with a singular privilege: that just as God chose from their blood the foundation of His Church, so too from the same he may place the crown upon it and perfect the structure." Concerning this matter, Heurnius also writes, *folio 171 (see also folio 172 and following):* "Just as in the first calling of the nations, the greater glory of the Church was in Europe and the coastal regions of Asia facing it (that is, those parts of the world which the ancient Jews counted among the islands and maritime places) rather than in Assyria, Persia, or Arabia, which were called the continuous and interior land properly speaking: so now a far more beautiful bride of Christ is promised in those places which we are forced to approach by ships as islands, than that which we have in these places neighborly or contiguous to us." And for that reason, the glory of the Jews in that new Church, once they have joined, will be more illustrious; because while the Gentiles tread upon the outer court of the temple, they themselves will inhabit the innermost sanctuary <note>original: *sacrarij adyta* — referring to the Holy of Holies</note> and enjoy the knowledge of the highest mysteries, when the Ark of the Testament becomes visible in the temple of God.

<margin>
Heurnius, *folio 168 and following.*

Those who believe in a literal 1000-year earthly reign of Christ on earth</note>. *Eusebius, Ecclesiastical History 7, chapter 22*), or in the way the Sibylline oracles <note>A collection of oracular utterances written in Greek hexameters ascribed to the Sibyls</note> predict a restoration *folios 198, 201, 322*, or as Thomas Brightman <note>An English clergyman and commentator on the Apocalypse who predicted the restoration of the Jews</note> imagines regarding the Apocalypse *chapter 16, verse 12*. Indeed, the Jews and those like them expect all, or at least most things, to be carnal <note>meaning earthly, physical, or political rather than spiritual</note>, but the "false Israel" <note>likely referring here to the Jewish people in their current state, or the idea of an earthly nation</note> will, for its own good, recover all spiritual things according to its hope and opinion. And perhaps those who say much <insert>*</insert> about the Turk being routed <note>original: *fundi* — to be scattered or defeated in battle</note> by the Israelites in the land of Canaan are speaking in too human a fashion.

### CHAPTER VI.
## On Strangers and Foreigners.

The Greeks call a **Stranger** or **Foreigner** <term>advena/peregrinus</term> in a city an *apoikos* <note>Greek: ἄποικον</note>, that is, one who has set out from home, or an arriving colonist. *Law 239, section 4, regarding the meaning of words.* They hold only the privilege of passage and lodging in the city; and the more of these people there are in a city, the less that city deserves to be called a "common assembly" or community. For this reason, Cicero elegantly calls that arriving multitude of idle men the **"dregs of the city"** <note>Latin: *fecem civitatis*</note>, as they benefit no one but burden many. See Keckermann <note>Bartholomäus Keckermann (1572–1609), a German Calvinist philosopher and jurist</note>, *Practical Disputation 33, problem 1.* 

The unjust oppression of these people is forbidden everywhere in the Holy Scriptures. See Drusius <note>Johannes Drusius (1550–1616), a Flemish Protestant divine and Hebraist</note> on the passage in *Leviticus, chapter 68.* Canonhiero <note>Pietro Giacomo Canonhiero, a 17th-century Italian physician and political theorist</note>, *2nd introduction, chapter 7.* And so Lot preferred that his own daughters be violated rather than his guests. *Genesis 19, verse 8.* Furthermore, the **law of hospitality** <note>Latin: *hospitij jus*</note> was once considered sacred among our German people. Lord Lansius <note>Thomas Lansius (1577–1657), a German jurist and professor</note>, *in his consultations, folio 71.* Indeed, it is contrary to the human kinship that exists among all men that...

# On Law, and the orders of citizens.

Once, the Romans regarded a foreigner as an enemy <note>Latin: *peregrinum pro hoste sumebant*</note>: as noted by Varro and Cicero in *On Duties, Book 1*. This was so much the case that even a person captured when there was no active war became a slave (according to the law *On Captives and Postliminium* <note><term>Postliminium</term>: A legal right where a person who had been captured in war returned to their previous status and property upon returning to their own country.</note>), unless that person belonged to a federated nation; in which case they were then called a "Guest" <note>Latin: *Hospitem*</note>. Now, rightly by the common practice of the humanity of nations, only those are considered "enemies" <note>Latin: *hostes*</note> who were formerly called "public traitors" <note>Latin: *perduelles*</note>: namely, those against whom war has been publicly declared.

II. Regarding wandering beggars <note>Latin: *Mendicis vagis*</note> and exiles, what should be thought of them, and whether they are to be tolerated in the Republic, is a matter debated not without merit. Truly, it seems impious if beggars—even if they are able-bodied and idle (or even reduced to beggary by their own fault, as Menochius notes in *Arbitrary Cases, chapter 182, number 42*)—are indifferently expelled or neglected by private citizens, especially if they are already being neglected by the Magistrate. The text of Solomon in *Ecclesiastes, chapter 11*, at the beginning, supports this view. For just as it is not enough for certain punishments to be established for crimes, the Statesman <note>Latin: *Politicus*</note> ought to take care that men do not sin, or are not driven to sin. That wisest of fools <note>A playful reference to Sir Thomas More (1478–1535)</note> wisely warns of this in his *Utopia*. Thus, for this evil of ours, it does not seem to be a suitable medicine if we wish to restrain such men by hunger; rather, care must be taken lest they reach the point of beggary. For those who cannot provide for themselves, provision must be made through the foresight of the Prince.

On this matter, one may consult the following in detail: Heigius, *part 2, question 27*; Hoennonius, *Political Disputations 6, thesis 18, d.*; Keckermann, *Practical Disputations 30, problem 12*; Timpler, *Politics, book 1, chapter 5, question 16*; Isaac Pontanus in the *History of the City of Amsterdam, book 2, chapter 1 and following*; Joannes Ludovicus Vives, *Treatise on the Relief of the Poor, or on Human Necessities*; August Thuanus, *History, book 3, folio 59* on the "Chimera or Phantom of Beggars." See also Drusius on *Deuteronomy chapter 13*; Lather, *On the Census, folio 984*; Gerhard in the section *On the Magistrate, number 228*; Camerarius, *Century 2, chapter 74*; Montaigne, *Essays, folio 181*; Zepper, *On the Laws of Moses, book 4, final chapter*; Forster, *Disputations on the Decalogue, decade 3, problem 3*; Megelin, *On the Civil State, part 1, chapter 3* <note>Original German title: "vom Burgerlichen Standt"</note>; Cassander, *Letter 41*; Master Georg Rollhagen in the *Funeral Sermon of the Rich Man* <note>Original German: "in der Leichpredigt deß Reichen Manns"</note>; and *The Grand Almoner of France* <note>Original French: "Le Grand Aulmosnier de France"</note>. 

Certainly, the poor are not to be cross-examined, nor are they to be prohibited. See Diego de Estella <note>Latin: Didacus Stella</note>, *On Contempt of the World, book 1, chapter 52 and following*; and Grasser in *Mystical Theology, folio 111*. It appears from the *Odyssey, book 17*, that there were beggars going from door to door even in Homer's time. Therefore, I cannot approve of Master Dieterich’s opinion, which is far too rigid, put forth in his sermon *On Street Begging* <note>Original German: "Vom Gassen Bettel"</note>. I am more pleased by the opinion of Oecolampadius in a certain letter on this specific matter, which, if I am not mistaken, is inserted among the letters of Zwingli. Nobly did Rembert, Bishop of Bremen, say: "One must not hesitate to help all the poor; because we do not know in whom Christ may be, or when He comes to us." See Albert Krantz, *Metropolis, book 2*.

# Dissertation II.

<margin>Propol. 11. in the *Tripartite History*, book 12, chapter 2.</margin> A certain Atticus <note>Atticus was the Patriarch of Constantinople in the early 5th century, known for his charity to the poor during a famine.</note> considers those to be most worthy of alms who do not dare to ask for them. On the necessity and effects of Almsgiving, some beautiful things are also written by *Louis of Granada in his "Flowers," part 2, chapter 4, folio 115.* <note>Louis of Granada (1504–1588) was a famous Spanish Dominican friar and mystic writer.</note> See also Lactantius, book 6, chapter 6, section 11; Eccard *in his edition of the German Tauler, folio 288, etc.*; and Botero in *The Sayings, folio 146b, etc.* See also the Oration of Gregory of Nyssa <note>A 4th-century Church Father and Bishop of Nyssa.</note> *on loving the poor, in the Orthodox volume 2, folio 1781.* Boccaccio, in *The Decameron, Day 1, Novel 6*, falsely mocks those who give something to the poor only perfunctorily, just so they do not die. # On the Law and the Ranks of Citizens.

In the past, the Romans treated a foreigner as an enemy <note>original: "peregrinum pro hoste sumebant"</note>: according to Varro and Cicero in *On Duties*, so much so that even someone captured when there was no war became a slave (*Law 5, section 1, on captives and postliminy*), unless they were from an allied nation, whom they then called a Guest <note>original: "Hospitem"</note>. Now, rightly, by the common custom of human kindness among nations, only those are considered enemies <note>original: "hostes"</note> who were once called public enemies <note>original: "perduelles"</note>: those, namely, against whom war has been publicly declared (*Law 234, on the meaning of words*).

II. Regarding wandering beggars <note>original: "Mendicis vagis"</note> and exiles: what should be thought of them, and should they be tolerated in the Commonwealth? This is a matter of no small debate. Indeed, to drive away or ignore beggars indiscriminately—even if they are able-bodied and idle (or even reduced to begging by their own fault, as Menochius notes in *Arbitrary Cases, chapter 182, number 42*)—seems impious, especially if they are neglected by the Magistrate <term>Magistrate: a civil officer or local administrator</term>. This is supported by the text of Solomon in Ecclesiastes, *chapter 11, from the beginning*. For just as it is not enough to set certain punishments for crimes, the Statesman <term>Politicus: here referring to a ruler or political philosopher</term> must ensure that people do not sin, or are not driven to sin—as that wisest of fools <note>A reference to Thomas More, author of *Utopia*</note> wisely warns in his *Utopia*. Thus, for this current evil of ours, it does not seem to be a fitting medicine if we wish to control such men through hunger; rather, we must ensure they do not fall into beggary. Those who cannot provide for themselves must be looked after by the providence of the Prince. On this matter, one may consult the discourse of Heigius, *part 2, question 27*; Hoennonius, *political dispute 6, thesis 18, d.*; Keckermann, *practical dispute 30, problem 12*; Timpler, *Politics book 1, chapter 5, question 16*; Isaac Pontanus, *in the History of the City of Amsterdam, book 2, chapter 1 and following*; Juan Luis Vives, *Treatise on the Relief of the Poor, or On Human Necessities*; Augustus Thuanus, *History, book 3, folio 59*; *The Chimera or Phantom of Beggars*; Drusius, *on Deuteronomy chapter 15*; Lather, *on Taxes, folio 984*; Gerhard, *on the Magistrate, number 228*; Camerarius, *century 2, chapter 74*; Montaigne, *Essays, folio 181*; Zepper, *On Mosaic Laws, book 4, final chapter*; Forster, *Disputation on the Decalogue, decade 3, problem 3*; Negelin, *On the Civil State, part 1, chapter 3*; Cassander, *letter 41*; M. Georg Rollhagen, *in the Funeral Sermon of the Rich Man*; and the Great Almoner of France <note>original: "Le Grand Aulmosnier de France"</note>. Certainly, the poor are not to be interrogated, nor prohibited. Diego de Estella, *On the Contempt of the World, book 1, chapter 52 and following*; Grasser, *in Mystical Theology, folio 111*. Even in Homer’s time, there were those begging from door to door, as is clear from *Odyssey, book 17*. Therefore, I cannot approve of the overly rigid opinion of Mr. Dietericus, which he puts forward in his sermon *On Street Begging* <note>original: "Vom Gassen Bettel"</note>. I prefer the view of Oecolampadius <note>Johannes Oecolampadius (1482–1531), a German Reformer</note> in a certain letter on this subject, which, if I am not mistaken, is included in the letters of Zwingli. Rembert, Bishop of Bremen <note>A 9th-century missionary and archbishop known for his charity</note>, said it excellently: "We must not hesitate," he said, "to help all the poor, because we do not know in which of them Christ resides, or when He comes to us." Albert Crantzius, *Metropolis, book 2*.

## Dissertation II.

<margin>Sozomen, book 3, chapter 14.</margin> In the *Tripartite History*, book 12, chapter 2, a certain Atticus <note>Atticus was the Bishop of Constantinople in the early 5th century</note> considers those most worthy of almsgiving <term>almsgiving: the practice of giving money or food to the poor</term> who do not dare to ask for it. # On the Law and the Social Orders of Citizens.

<meta>catchword: Metropolis</meta>

In ancient times, the Romans treated the stranger <note>original: *peregrinum*</note> as an enemy <note>original: *hoste*</note>: as noted by Varro and Cicero in his work *On Duties* (*De Officiis*); so much so that even a person captured when there was no war became a slave (according to the law on captives and the right of return <note>original: *postliminium* — the legal right of a person to return to their original status after being a prisoner of war</note>), unless they were from a treaty-bound nation, in which case they were called a Guest <note>original: *Hospitem*</note>. Today, by the common and humane practice of nations, only those are considered "enemies" who were once called public foes <note>original: *perduelles*</note>: namely, those against whom war has been publicly declared.

II. Regarding wandering beggars and outcasts <note>original: *extorribus*</note>, what should be thought of them and whether they should be tolerated in a Republic is a matter of no small debate. Truly, it seems impious to indifferently expel or neglect beggars—even if they are able-bodied and idle, or even if they were reduced to begging through their own fault (as Menochius argues in his *Arbitrary Judgments*)—especially if they are neglected by the Magistrate. The text of Solomon in Ecclesiastes, chapter 11, supports this view. Indeed, just as it is not enough for specific punishments to be established for crimes, the Politician <term>Politician: here meaning a statesman or civic administrator</term> ought to ensure that men do not sin, or are not driven toward sinning. This is wisely advised by that "wisest of fools" <note>likely a reference to Thomas More</note> in his *Utopia*. Thus, it does not seem to be a suitable medicine for our current social ill if we wish to coerce such men through hunger; rather, care should be taken so that they do not reach the point of begging in the first place. For those who cannot look after themselves, provision must be made through the Prince’s providence.

On this matter, one may consult the discourse of Hegius; Hoennonius in his *Political Disputations*; Keckermann in his *Practical Disputations*; Timpler’s *Politics*; Isaac Pontanus in his *History of the City of Amsterdam*; Johannes Ludovicus Vives <note>a prominent Spanish humanist</note> in his treatise *On the Relief of the Poor, or Human Necessities*; Jacques Auguste de Thou in his *History*; the work titled *The Chimera or Phantom of Beggars*; Drusius on Deuteronomy; Lather *On the Census*; Gerhard on the *Magistracy*; Camerarius; Montaigne in his *Essays*; Zepper on *Mosaic Laws*; and Forster's *Disputations on the Decalogue*. See also Negelin's *On the Civil Estate* <note>original German: *vom Burgerlichen Standt*</note>; Cassander’s *Letters*; and Master Georg Rollhagen in his *Funeral Sermon for the Rich Man* <note>original German: *Leichpredigt deß Reichen Manns*</note>; as well as the office of the *Grand Almoner of France*.

Certainly, the poor are not to be interrogated or prohibited. So says Diego de Estella <note>original: *Didac. Stell.*</note> in his *Contempt for the World*, and Grasser in his *Mystical Theology*. It is also apparent from the *Odyssey*, book 17, that there were people begging door-to-door even in Homer's time. Therefore, I cannot approve of the overly rigid opinion of Master Dieterich, which he sets forth in his sermon *On Street Begging*. I am more pleased by the opinion of Oecolampadius <note>a Protestant Reformer in Basel</note> in a certain letter on this subject, which, if I am not mistaken, is included in the *Letters of Zwingli*. As Rembert, Bishop of Bremen, most excellently said: "One must not hesitate to help all the poor, because we do not know in whom Christ resides or when He comes to us." This is cited by Albert Krantz in his work *Metropolis*, book 2.

<summary>This page discusses the legal evolution of the status of foreigners from "enemies" to "guests" and debates the state's responsibility toward beggars, arguing that poverty should be prevented by the state rather than punished by hunger.</summary>
<keywords>foreigners, beggars, social welfare, Thomas More, almsgiving, Roman law, citizenship, poverty prevention</keywords>

<meta>catchword: Sebastia-</meta>

<margin>Propol. 11. In the Tripartite History <note>A standard medieval church history textbook combining the works of Socrates, Sozomen, and Theodoret</note>, book 12, chapter 2.</margin> A certain Atticus considers those most worthy of alms <note>original: *eleemosynâ*</note> who do not dare to ask for them. Regarding the necessity and effects of almsgiving, several beautiful points are also found in Luis de Granada *in Flowers, part 2, chapter 4, folio 115*; Lactantius 6. 6. 11; Eckhart *in the German Tauler, folio 288, etc.*; Botero *on Sayings, folio 146. b. etc.*; and see the orations of Gregory of Nyssa *on loving the poor, in the Orthodox library, volume 2, folio 1781.* Boccaccio, in his *Decameron* (*day 1, novella 6*), mockingly laughs at those who give something to the poor only perfunctorily, just so they do not die. A certain Bishop of Kraków once gave more to the poor than he received in income, and yet he lived comfortably enough (Guyon, *volume 3, book 2, chapter 22*). There is also truly "real" almsgiving, which is not done by the distribution of small and superfluous sums of money, but by visiting prisoners and the sick—something that even the poorest person can give. When the poor ask something from us, they are by no means asking for that which is ours, but for that which we owe to them; see Paracelsus *on Good and Bad Fortune, volume 2, folio 209*, where he teaches that riches are given to us for the specific purpose of being given to the poor. Hence, in the *Chapters of the Fathers* <note>The *Pirkei Avot*, a compilation of ethical teachings from the Mishnaic period</note>, *folio 52*, Rabbi Eleazar says: "Give to Him from His own, because you and whatever you have are His." And whoever confers a benefit upon one of the least of these, does it for Christ himself. Give therefore to the poor so that you may receive; sow so that you may reap; scatter so that you may gather:

->*Whatever is given to the needy is beyond the reach of Fortune:*<-
->*The only wealth you will always possess is that which you have given away.*<-

On the origin of Hospitals, see Guyon, *book 2, chapter 16*. Regarding the <term>Gazophylacio: originally the treasury of the Temple in Jerusalem, here used to mean a church's charity box</term>, Saint Paulinus writes beautifully in *Epistle 34*. However, able-bodied beggars <note>original: *validi mendicantes*</note> ought to be employed in suitable works by the Magistrate; see Muret, *Varia, book 13, chapter 4*; and regarding able-bodied beggars, see Wolff, *volume 1, folio 244*, citing Ansegisus. Certainly, the care of the poor is not neglected among the Saracens <note>A term used in the Middle Ages and Renaissance to refer to Muslims</note>. In the city of Baghdad, the Caliph built great houses and marketplaces, and even <term>Xenodochia: guest-houses or hospitals for strangers and the poor</term> suitable for the sick poor. Nearly sixty medical apothecaries are counted there, all abundantly supplied with all necessary kinds of spices, medicines, and other useful things brought from the Royal house. Thus, all the sick who are brought there are provided, at royal expense, with whatever is deemed suitable for medicine, food, and a complete cure, until they happen to recover. Furthermore, in that same place is a palace called *Paralmaraphthan* <note>A corruption of the Arabic *Dar al-Maristan*, meaning a hospital or asylum</note>, which is the House of Mercy, intended for confining, binding, and curing all the insane found in the region. All these things were established by that King for the sake of almsgiving and benefit, to be conferred commonly upon all coming there who labor under insanity or another disease. *The Itinerary of Benjamin* <note>Benjamin of Tudela, a 12th-century Jewish traveler</note>. In Turkey, there are no inns where travelers may stay, but there are large hospitals (Belon, book 1, chapter 59). <note>original French: *Les Turcs estiment grande aumosne, & merite, de mettre de l'eau sur les grands chemins pour abbreuver les passants.*</note> *The Turks consider it a great alms and a merit to place water on the main roads to provide drink for passersby.* (The same author, book 2, chapter 25). Regarding the generosity toward the poor of Amadeus IX, Duke of Savoy, see Vanderburch *in his life*, and Stumpff, book 11, final chapter. <note>original French: *Secours d'un Prince, nommé Heridice, envers les pauvres, au temps de la famine.*</note> *The aid of a Prince, named Heridice, toward the poor in a time of famine.* (Paradin, book 2, chapters 3 and following, *History of Lyon*). <note>original French: *Puis que nous avons commun, avec les Pauvres le naistre, & le mourir, le boire & le manger nous doit estre commun aussi avec eux.*</note> *Since we have birth and death in common with the poor, eating and drinking ought to be common to us with them as well.* (The same author, book 2, chapter 97). <note>original French: *Institution de l'ausmosne generale, faicte en la Cité de Lyon.*</note> *The establishment of the general alms, created in the city of Lyon.* (See the same Paradin, book 3, chapter 18). Regarding John the Almsgiver, consult Stumpff (*volume 1, folio 86 b*). Charlemagne daily provided food for a number of poor people (see Girard, *folio 271*). Finally, regarding the very severe laws of the Icelanders against vagabond beggars, see Arngrimur Jónsson, *On Icelandic Affairs*, book 1, chapter 7, folio 67, etc. Regarding a certain French ordinance concerning beggars, see the *French Mercury*, *volume 2, folio 504*. III. The condition of the Gypsies <term>Zigarorum/Attiganorum: historical terms for the Romani people</term> is different; they call themselves "Egyptians" <note>This was a common historical misconception that gave rise to the English word "Gypsy."</note>. These people are nothing other than a pack of thieves and a dreg of idle and fraudulent men, gathered from various nations that are not so remote. (Camerarius, *first century of historical meditations, chapter 17; book 2, chapter 75*; Delrio, *Against Mages, book 4, folio 538, etc.*; Pasquier, *Researches, 17*; Guyon, *volume 2, book 3, chapter 7*; Gerhard, *On the Magistrate, number 227*; Keckermann, *disputation 33, problem 2*; Michael Heberer, in the tract titled *Egyptian Servitude*; Pierre Belon, book 2, chapter 41; Johannes Stumpff, *in the Swiss Chronicle, final book, chapter 10*; Sebastian Münster, *in the Cosmography, book 3, chapter 5*). These people are not tolerated in the Duchy of Württemberg, because they once betrayed Duke Eberhard to the Sultan (Crusius, *part 3, folio 426*), and they were expelled from France a few years ago (see the *French Mercury, volume 2, folio 318*).

IV. Although some also grant that Turks, Saracens, and the like should have free entry for the sake of commerce—and that ports and roads ought to be open to them—certain precautions must nevertheless be applied here, regarding which see Keckermann, *disputation 31, problem 7*. For this reason, the Hanseatic League <note>A powerful medieval and early modern commercial and defensive confederation of merchant guilds and market towns in Northwestern and Central Europe</note> had colleges in London in England, Bergen in Norway, Bruges in Flanders, and Novgorod. (Chyträus, *Saxon Chronicle, book 23*).

<header>Dissertation II.</header>

<meta>catchword: dac.</meta>

And in the year [15]82, the Turks requested from the people of Antwerp that they might be permitted to establish a guild of eighteen merchants. <note>original: *Chytr. lib. 25.* referring to David Chytraeus, a Lutheran reformer and historian</note> Likewise, the Venetians and the French maintain Consuls <note>original: *Consules*; here meaning diplomatic representatives who protected the interests of their nation's merchants abroad</note> throughout the Turkish Empire. <note>original: *Cotovicus. in Itinerar. fol. 414.* referring to Jan Kootwyck's travels</note> Such matters must be judged according to the specific needs and conditions of each Republic. For a city devoted to trade must take the greatest care regarding hospitality, ensuring that justice is administered with an equal balance to both citizens and foreigners alike. On this account, Frankfurt is wonderfully commended by Henri Estienne <note>original: *Henrico Stephano*, a famous 16th-century scholar-printer</note> in his *Praise of the Frankfurt Fairs*. <note>original: *in encom. Nundinar. Francofurtens fol. 9.*</note> This stands in contrast to the laws against vagabonds and those without a master. <note>original French: *Contre les gens, sans adveu, & vagabonds.* See Paradin, *History of Lyon*, book 3, chapter 41.</note>

V. In the same way, however, foreigners are subject to the Republic in which they reside in many ways: specifically, they are bound to obey the statutes and laws enacted regarding foreigners (as Kirchner rightly defends against Bodin <note>referring to a legal debate between political theorists Hermann Kirchner and Jean Bodin</note>). For example, they must not investigate the secrets of a foreign kingdom; <note>original: *l. 4. C. d. commerc.* referring to the Code of Justinian regarding trade secrets</note> they must depart the city at a designated time; <note>original: *Selden, in Iano Anglor. fol. 116.* referring to John Selden’s work on English law</note> and they must declare their name and the location of their lodging, along with similar requirements. Here, a question may be asked: can the ships of foreigners be seized to repel the force of enemies? This is practiced today by the Spaniards and the English, and was done in the past by the Burgundians. <note>original: *Cominæus, lib. 3. cap. 5.* referring to Philippe de Commines</note> To this also pertains the incident where certain foreigners refused to kneel while standing to greet the Queen of France. <note>original: *Pierre Matthieu, lib 3. fol. m. 675.*</note> Likewise, if someone should die in a foreign territory, the inheritance does not follow the law of the place where they died, but the law of the place where they had their permanent home. <note>original: *Schultes, quæst. practic. 24. & 48. lib. 1.*</note> I remember it being asked whether someone can be prohibited from staying in a certain place? <note>original: *Ruland. decis. Iur. controv. quæst. 27.*</note> I believe such general statutes are valid, provided they are not introduced out of hatred for a specific person. Just as in many places, it is the accepted custom <gloss>German: that a guest must eat at the inn and is not allowed to have any smoke of his own.</gloss> <note>This German proverb implies that travelers were required to pay for the innkeeper's services and were forbidden from maintaining their own private hearth or cooking for themselves while staying in a city.</note> Regarding the marriage of a foreigner, see Sanchez on marriage, volume 3.

### CHAPTER VII.
## How Foreigners are Subject to Jurisdiction by Reason of the Place Where They Stay.

It is likewise well established that by reason of a crime, a person is subject to the jurisdiction <note>original: *forum*; the legal place where a case is heard</note> of the place where the offense was committed, so that they may be punished there, even if they have their home elsewhere. Nor can a defendant decline the jurisdiction of a judge in whose territory they have broken the law. <note>This refers to the legal concept of *forum delicti*, where the site of the crime determines the court.</note>

### C H A P T E R VII.
### How Foreigners are Assigned a Legal Forum based on the Place Where They Reside.

It is likewise well-established regarding the matter of a crime: that anyone is assigned a legal forum <term>forum sortiri: to become subject to the jurisdiction of a specific court</term> in the place where a crime was committed, so that he may be punished there, even if he has his permanent residence elsewhere. Nor can the accused decline the jurisdiction of the Judge in whose territory he offended. <note>original: citing the Code of Justinian and Hermann Vultejus on the jurisdiction of the place of the crime (*locus delicti*)</note> Didac. <note>Likely referring to Didacus Covarruvias, a Spanish jurist</note>

<meta>This page continues the discussion on the legal status of foreigners and the jurisdictional rules governing crimes committed by those outside their home territory. It specifically addresses the process of "remission" (extradition) and the authority of local judges over foreign offenders.</meta>

### On Law and the Classes of Citizens

... [man]date. <note>original: "dac.", likely completing a word like "mandate" or a citation from the previous page</note> *Covarruvias, Practical Questions, 11, number 3.* Hence it is established by law that if it is known that an offender is living in a certain province, the Judge of the location where the crime was committed must send letters to the Judge of the territory where the offender has their home (or wherever they are found; because a fugitive is considered to have a home anywhere) so that, in support of the law, the local judge may either warn the person to appear, or seize the criminal and send them back to those where the crime was committed, so that they may be subjected to lawful punishments. <note>original: *Novell. 134. cap. 5. Panormitan. c. fin. num. 6. extr. d for. competent. Rauchbar. 1. quæst. ult. Peguera decis. 2. & 66.*</note> This is done so that those who saw him commit the crime may also see him punished.

II. However, although today these "remissions" <term>remissio: the legal process of returning a prisoner to the jurisdiction where their crime was committed; a precursor to modern extradition</term> do not occur by general custom—since every city has its own boundaries and is considered to constitute a separate Republic (a reason correctly weighed by *Castrensis* and approved by *Vultejus, Obrecht,* and others)—nevertheless in Germany and other provinces, it is still the common practice for more heinous crimes that extradition is granted out of a sense of friendship and neighborliness. This is done so that an example may be set at the place of the crime. Such a transfer occurs under a "reversal" or acknowledgment <note>original German: "auff einen Revers oder beſtandtnuſs"</note>—that is, through letters of reversal <term>reversālibus: a legal document ensuring that the act of handing over a prisoner does not harm the surrendering judge's future jurisdiction or rights</term> from the Magistrate or Lord who requests the extradition. This ensures that the extradition will in no way harm the jurisdiction of the official who is handing the prisoner over. <note>original: *Gail. 1. de Pac. Public. cap. 16. num. 30.* and others</note> Regarding whether an Elector <term>Elector: one of the high-ranking princes of the Holy Roman Empire who had the right to elect the Emperor</term> can, by a special privilege, call back his own subjects who have committed crimes elsewhere, see *Arumæus on the Golden Bull, page 225.*

III. The Judge of the place of the crime <term>locus delicti: the location where a crime was committed</term> has jurisdiction for all legal purposes over an offender caught in his territory. This means his authority extends not only to the person of the offender, but also to the offender's goods located elsewhere. <note>original: *c. postulasti. ubi Canonistæ. extr. de for. competent.*</note> Indeed, if a person can alienate or transfer their property through a contract made in another territory, why should they not also do so through a crime? Consequently, the loss of property follows the crime. Therefore, **the accessory follows the principal.** <note>original Latin: *accessorium sequitur principale*. This is a legal maxim meaning that a secondary matter (like property) is governed by the status of the primary matter (the crime/person).</note> Thus, the Judge acquires the right of punishing the offender for the crime committed, even if the offender holds a high rank or is of greater status than the judge of the place of the crime. For even a superior, when committing a crime outside his own province, is treated as a private citizen...

<summary>This page discusses the legal mechanisms for extraditing criminals between different jurisdictions, particularly within the Holy Roman Empire, and establishes that a judge where a crime occurred has authority over both the person and their external property.</summary>

<meta>This page continues the discussion on whether legal status and privileges (such as those of a Senator or a Noble) follow a person when they commit a crime in a different territory. ### Dissertation II.

...is considered. <note>original: "cenfetur"; completing the sentence from the previous page regarding how a person's status is legally viewed.</note> *Digest, book 3, on the office of the President,* as noted by Panormitanus *in the final chapter, number 6, of the extra titles mentioned above.* However, see the *title of the Code on where Senators or the Illustrious [are tried], and also law 14 on Jurisdiction.* Yet, this latter law does not speak of jurisdiction inherent to a territory, but of that which is exercised in the name of the Prince, rather than by one's own right. Regarding the former, I say that Roman Senators and the Illustrious do not possess [this status outside their home]. Therefore, qualities do not exist for a non-entity.

Does a Count or Baron, for example, who possesses the privilege of the <term>Austregæ</term> <note>original: *Austregæ*; a special court of arbitration within the Holy Roman Empire. It allowed members of the Imperial Estates to have their disputes settled by their peers rather than by the Emperor's ordinary courts.</note>, still enjoy that privilege if he commits a crime in another's territory? Ummius affirms this immediately in his *disputation on process 4, number 9 at the end, and number 51.* Yet, the general reasoning of our thesis still seems to hold. See Cothman on this question, *volume 5, council 2, from the beginning.* 

But you might argue that the *Austregæ* pertain to criminal cases; see Arumæus *on the Golden Bull, discourse 5, thesis 12.* I respond: First, this is uncertain—for the Law of the *Austregæ* was included in the Cameral Ordinance, which does not pertain to criminal cases, and the Austregal Judges are essentially arbitrators who do not possess <term>merum Imperium</term> <note>original: *Imperium merum*; the "pure authority" or high justice that grants a judge the power of life and death over a peer.</note> over an equal. Even if it were so, this privilege fails in the case where the offender is caught in a different territory. See Vultejus *on the title of the Code: where Senators or the Illustrious [are tried], at the end;* Petrus Denaisius, *in his assertion on Cameral Jurisdiction, page 18;* Caspar Ziegler, *section Austregæ, conclusion 3, in the limitation, page 81;* and Zanger, *on Exceptions, title on the incompetence of the forum.* 

Likewise, the <term>Austregæ</term> cease to apply if a State holds property in the territory of another State and is sued in the name of those goods. A very similar question is: can an exempt person who commits a crime in the territory of an ordinary judge be punished? This is treated by Chokier, *on exemptions, last part, question 33.*

IV. The arguments deduced so far suggest the reason why the legal objection of Mary, Queen of Scots, was once rejected. Although she plotted many things in England to the prejudice of public peace (as her accusers claimed), she nevertheless wished to enjoy the same sovereign privilege in England that she enjoyed in her own Kingdom. On this matter, see Emanuel van Meteren, *book 13,* and Pierre Matthieu, *in the history of the last troubles, book 2, page 59.* Also *The Morning Alarm* <note>original: *Reveille Matin*; likely referring to the "Reveille-matin des François," a famous Huguenot political tract.</note>, *Dialogue 2;* *Memoirs of the League, volume 1, page 451;* and Christoph Forstner, *Notebooks, number 23.* 

However, captured Kings, even in an unjust war, are not to be killed. See Mornay, *in The Mystery of Iniquity,* and Pandolfo Collenuccio, *in the History of Naples, book 4, at the end,* where they discuss Conradin, Duke of Swabia <note>The last legitimate male heir of the Hohenstaufen dynasty, executed in 1268, a move that shocked many legal scholars of the era.</note>. I also discussed this in my *treatise on war.* Add also the singular oration of Cisner regarding this Conradin, inserted in his *Philological* works; Guyon, *volume 2, of various lessons, book 4, chapter 18;* Pasquier, *book 5 of Researches, chapter 14;* and Crusius, *in Swabian history.* Some of these writers excuse Conradin, while others accuse him; some even make the process and the jurisdiction a matter of controversy. But certainly, the Empress Constantina <note>original: "Constantina"; likely referring to Empress Constance of Sicily or a related figure in the Hohenstaufen/Neapolitan history.</note> acted more correctly...

-># On the Law and the ranks of citizens.<-
<meta>catchword: of things</meta>

...Constance, Queen of Sicily, who released the captive young King in honor of the Lord’s Passion. <note>Costanza d'Altavilla (1154–1198) was Queen of Sicily; the "young King" likely refers to a political captive released as an act of religious devotion.</note> Collenutius, *folio 298.*

V. From the same source, this also follows: that Ambassadors, although they are considered to have distinguished privileges above other guests, nonetheless, if they have plotted anything to the prejudice of the State <note>original: *Reipublicæ*; literally "the Republic," but referring to the Commonwealth or the sovereign power of the host country.</note> in whose territory they are staying, and act more as a spy than an Ambassador, they immediately lose all such rights; a day for a trial for high treason <note>original: *perduellionis*; a specific Roman law term for treason against the state.</note> can be set for them, and other penal actions can be brought against them. Charles Paschalius, *in The Ambassador, chapter 74 and following.* Kirchner, *on The Ambassador, book 2, chapter 1, number 14, etc.* Keckerman, *practical disputation 33, problem 11 and following,* after Gregory of Toulouse; Velsten, *decade 10, thesis 6;* and a memorable and recent example of this matter is found in the *French Mercury, volume 1, folio 14.* Add Arumæus, *discourse on Public Law 12,* as I have also stated in my own treatise *On the Ambassador.*

VI. It is also accepted for the best of reasons that a foreigner <note>original: *Forensem*; someone outside their own jurisdiction.</note> can be punished by the Judge of the place where the crime was committed, according to municipal laws—even those inflicting a heavier punishment than common law <note>original: *jus commune*; the general Roman-based legal system used across Europe as a standard.</note>—as per the *authority in the Province of the Code, where it is appropriate to act regarding crimes.* Covarruvias, *in the chapter 'Alma Mater,' section 10, number 8, on the sentence of excommunication.* Regarding the <term>Praetor of Foreigners</term> <note>original: *prætore peregrinorum*; a Roman magistrate who handled legal cases involving non-citizens.</note> who was at Rome, see Freher, *Parerga 2, chapter 17.* On the statute regarding the seizing of a foreigner’s goods, see Georg Everhard, *volume 1, counsel 10.* On whether one who commits a crime outside their territory can be punished in their place of residence, see Peguer, *decisions 2 and 66.* Cardinal Tuschus, *letter D, conclusion 181, etc.* Reiger, *in the treasury, under the word 'crime,' folio 640.* What the law is if someone flees into another territory with stolen property, see Berlichius, *volume 5, conclusion 45, number 42, etc.* Here also should be discussed the matter of the competent court by reason of contract: Tuschus, *letter F, conclusion 435.* Notes on the Counsels of Cravetta, 695.

### CHAPTER VIII.
## On Subjects of a higher status, primarily by reason of their office.

A new division of Subjects now follows, by which some are of a higher status and others of a lower one. For the logic of politics requires that there be a "harmonic" equity <note>original: *æquabilitas... harmonica*; a concept of justice where rewards and status are distributed proportionally based on merit and rank, rather than everyone being identical.</note> among the citizens of the same State; namely, so that to each citizen, according to the logic and distinction of their dignity and office, honor, liberty, and their own right may be left and granted. Truly, "equality"—where every single citizen is made equal to one another in all privileges, in law, honors, and immunity—is the cause of all disorder <note>original: *ἀταξίας* (ataxias); Greek for "confusion" or "lack of order."</note>...

->## Dissertation II.<-

...and of the disturbance of things, it is a perennial fountain and source. <note>The author is likely citing Lambert Daneau (c. 1530–1595), a French Reformed theologian, regarding the origins of civil disorder.</note> Lambert Daneau, *Christian Politics,* book 2, chapter 6, page 118; Bodin, *On the Republic,* book 3, chapter 8, page 543; Timpler, book 1, chapter 5, question 1. Most correctly Alsted says, in *System of Mnemonics, book 2, chapter 17, at the beginning, page 576:* "Human society," he says, "is ruled by inequality." This inequality is nothing other than a kind of harmonious discord and a discordant concord. <note>original: *concors quædam discordia & concordia discors*; a classic philosophical paradox suggesting that the tension between different social ranks actually creates a stable whole.</note> If, therefore, God had made all men and each individual equal, this machine of the Universe could not have stood for even a moment. For nothing can stand without order, and where there is order, there is a "before" and an "after," a "lower" and a "higher," a "greater" and a "lesser." But for what purpose? Specifically, so that <term>charitas</term> <note>Christian love or social concern for others.</note> may be nourished, so that superiors may help their inferiors—presiding over them for their benefit—and so that inferiors may be subject to their superiors. He explains his method for this in *conclusions 6 and 7.* See also St. Augustine, *The City of God,* book 2, chapter 21. This very diversity of inequality commends this entire universe of things. Whence Du Bartas <note>Guillaume de Salluste Du Bartas (1544–1590), a famous French Huguenot poet.</note> in *The Columns, page 276:*

> And that which even makes this Universe more beautiful,
> Is that it contains high and low, infinitely diverse.

This matter has already been discussed in my treatise *On the Ecclesiastical Rights of Majesty.*

### CHAPTER VIII.
## On Subjects of higher condition, especially by reason of office.

Another division of Subjects now follows, by which some exist of a higher condition and others of a lower. Indeed, the nature of Politics <term>Politiarum</term> requires a "harmonic" fairness among the citizens of the same State; namely, that honor, liberty, and their own right be left and assigned to every citizen according to the reason and difference of their dignity and office. But "equality"—where each individual citizen is made equal to one another in all privileges, law, honors, and immunity—is the source of all disorder <note>original: <term>ἀταξίας</term> (*ataxias*); a Greek term for lack of order or chaos.</note> and of...

# Dissertation II.

...and is a perennial spring and source of the disturbance of affairs. <note>original Latin: *rerumque perturbationis*. The author is arguing that total equality leads to social chaos.</note> **Lambert Daneau, *Christian Politics,* book 2, chapter 6, folio 118; Jean Bodin, *On the Republic,* book 3, chapter 8, folio 543; Clemens Timpler, book 1, chapter 5, question 1.** Johann Heinrich Alsted says most correctly in his *System of Mnemonics, book 2, chapter 17, at the beginning, folio 576:* "Human society," he says, "is governed by inequality." This <term>inæqualitas</term> <note>social or political inequality</note> is nothing other than a certain harmonious discord, and a discordant harmony. If, therefore, God had made each and every human being equal, this machine of the Universe <note>a common Renaissance metaphor for the created world</note> could not have stood for even a moment; for nothing can exist without order, and where there is order, there is a before and an after, a lower and a higher, a greater and a lesser. But for what purpose? Namely, so that <term>charitas</term> <note>Christian love or charity</note> may be nourished, so that superiors may help their inferiors, presiding over them and benefiting them; and that inferiors may be subject to their superiors: so that the rich may assist the poor, and the latter may help the former to the best of their ability. And Savonarola elegantly teaches in *On Simplicity, book 3, conclusion 5,* that it is necessary for inequality, and a corresponding diversity in external matters, to exist: yet in its own way, which he explains in *conclusions 6 and 7. See also St. Augustine, City of God, book 2, chapter 21.* This very diversity of inequality commends the whole universe of things. Hence, Du Bartas writes in *The Columns, folio edition 276:*

> And what makes this very Universe more beautiful, 
> Is that it is infinitely diverse, with both high and low. 
> <note>original French: *Et ce qui mesme rend plus beau cest Univers, / C'est qu'il è haut & bas infiniment divers.* Du Bartas (1544–1590) was a famous French Huguenot poet.</note>

II. Subjects of a more eminent condition are either established in some dignity by reason of their office, or they ought to be preferred to others by their very nature. Of these, some are ecclesiastical <note>clergy</note> and others are secular. For, in general, the name of Nobility <term>Nobilitas</term> encompasses all orders, dignities, and the duties of magistrates: which are both noble by their own nature and are suitable for Noble and more illustrious men. Hence the Emperor himself is called "Most Noble." *See Bulenger, On the Roman Empire, book 2, chapter 10.* However, we must not dispute here about the duties of ecclesiastical magistrates, counselors, etc.; for that seems to belong not to the constitution, but to the other part of Politics—namely, the administration of the Commonwealth <term>Respublica</term>. <margin>based on the arguments handed down by Aristotle in *Politics, book 7, chapter 8.*</margin>

III. As far as the Ecclesiastics are concerned, among Roman Catholics they are almost entirely withdrawn from civil authority. But our people <note>likely referring to Protestant jurists</note> say openly that this contradicts the precept of Paul in *Romans 13,* which subjects every soul to the higher powers. Bernard of Clairvaux explains it thus in *Epistle 42:* "If every soul, then yours as well: Who excepted you from the whole? If anyone attempts to except you, he endeavors to deceive you." This matter has already been discussed in *On the Rights of Ecclesiastical Majesty.*

IV. Secular persons, who are more worthy than others by reason of their office, are either counselors or they manage the business of the Commonwealth in some other way. Those who serve the Commonwealth in a way other than by giving counsel, either with...

# On Law and the Ranks of Citizens.

They perform these duties either with <term>Imperium</term> <note>sovereign or command power</note> or without it. Those with power are the **Magistrates**. Those without power are those who provide some public utility in their function for the administration or the state; these include the **Procurators of the Treasury**, <term>Rentmaster</term> <note>German: *Rentmaister*; a financial official</note> / <term>Steward</term> <note>German: *Verwalter*</note> / <term>Tax Collector</term> <note>German: *Schösser*</note>, and others like them. There are also those who are employed privately as domestics solely for the utility and splendor of the court. Among these roles in the Republic, some carry a great deal of honor and dignity; others are devoid of honor and consist only of menial service. Of these services, some are considered more worthy than others, while some are clearly base and lowly.

**V.** The characteristics of the **Court** <note>the *Aula*, or royal household</note> are so varied and manifold that several authors have established a specific discipline concerning the Court and Courtly Governance. They have written entire books on this subject, among whom **Antonio de Guevara** stands out especially in his book titled *The Courtier’s Orator*. Then there is **Baldassare Castiglione**, who was highly commended by Sadoleto in his letters; **Hippolytus à Collibus** in his *The Palatine*; and **Duri de Pascolo** (also known as **Eberhard von Weihe**) in *The Political Courtier*. Other references include **Vernulæus** (book 2, *Politics*, chapter 10), **Barclay** in *The Image of Minds* (chapter 14), and **Hattron** in the treatise he titled *Leisure and the Court*. My friend, **Mr. Christian Herold**, also wrote a *Dissertation on the Prince's Household*. However, it is not within my current scope to discuss these at length. The only question regarding the courtly state is: in what place should it be held? Indeed, although many good men have spoken quite harshly about courtly life—calling courts the "suburbs of hell"—**Antonio Pérez** <note>a famous and controversial secretary to King Philip II of Spain</note> specifically calls them *Cortes arrabal de Infierno* <note>Spanish: "Courts are the outskirts of Hell."</note>.

---

->*Let him depart the court*<-
->*Who wishes to be pious.*<-
->*The court is a place where slippery Fortune*<-
->*plays her games.*<-
->*Courtly life is not life;*<-
->*Or if it is life, it is not so*<-
->*as a pious life is.*<-

---

**Richter** collected many other such sayings in *Political Axioms, 161*. Add to this the oration of **Lucian of Samosata** *On those who live for hire in the families of the wealthy*, and his *Apologetic Oration* (volume 1). See also the play *The Faithful Shepherd* (Act 5, Scene 1); **Drusius** in his *Apophthegms* (folio 75, number 169); and **Hadamar** in *The Instruction of a Prince* (folio 122). Indeed, even the Court of the **Supreme Pontiff** <note>The Pope</note> has many incorrigible inconveniences, as noted in **Boccalini’s** *Epitome of Parnassus* (century 3, chapter 15). Nevertheless, this status of the Court was established by **GOD** no less than the status of Kings and Princes, who certainly neither can nor should live in solitude. One must always accurately distinguish the courtly order itself from the behavior of modern courtiers. See **Althusius** in *Politics*; **Pontanus** in *Exercises* (volume 1); and **Casmann**, *Politics* (chapter 22, folio 96). Let the dialogue of William be added.

### Dissertation II.

The Island Menapians <note>original: *Infulani Menapij*; likely a reference to people from the Low Countries or a specific contemporary academic group</note>, where he responds to the accusations of **Enea Silvio** <note>Enea Silvio Piccolomini, later Pope Pius II, who wrote a famous critique of court life</note> and **Ulrich von Hutten**, with which they attack the Courts. The courtiers of **Solomon** are called blessed, according to the judgment of the **Queen of Sheba**. *1 Kings 10:8.* Therefore, a Court sometimes brings blessings. But which one? The one where there is a Wise Prince, and where the courtiers hear the wisdom of the King. Regarding the Court of **David**, see **Arndt** in his commentary *on Psalm 15 at the beginning, and on Psalm 101.* For the character of the courtiers of the ancient Christian Emperors before **Diocletian**, see **Eusebius**, *Book 8, Chapter 1*; the same author in *Chapter 6* has much to say about their constant martyrdom under Diocletian. On the trivial and superfluous aspects of Courts, see **Richter**, *Historical Axioms, 16.* But however that may be: while courtiers show off their golden chains, they betray that they are but slaves. **Helwig** in *The Origin of the German Language, under the word "Ketten" <note>chains</note>.*

**VI.** It is also pleasing to ask here: whether all those who serve the Prince are considered part of his "family" <note>the *familia* or household in a legal sense</note>? The determination of this matter can be of no small use in establishing the jurisdiction of the Chamber <note>the *Camera*, referring to the high court of the Empire</note>. Indeed, those who reside within the Court, whom we call **Court Servants** <note>original German: *Hoffdiener*</note>, are considered members of the household; others, who are commonly designated as **Servants from outside the house** <note>original German: *Diener von Hauß auß*</note>, are not. See the most eminent **Ruland**, *Decisions on Legal Controversies, Question 2.*

### CHAPTER IX.

## On the Origin and Nature of Nobility, which comes from birth.

Subjects who are considered more eminent for reasons other than their office—either by the prerogative of their lineage or some other quality—enjoy a status above the rest and are specifically called **Nobles**. **Tiraqueau** has written most extensively on this subject, as has **Cyriacus Spangenberger** in his *Mirror of Nobility* <note>original German: *Adelspiegel*</note>. Also **Petrus Frize** in **Arumæus**; **Joseph Nolden**; **Johann zur Nedden** in their *special treatises*; **Vechner** in his *Discourse on Nobility*; and **Reiner Reineccius** in *On the Descent of the Nobility* <note>original German: *von deß Adels Herkommen*</note>. **Cravetta** has much on this as shown in his Index, as do the **Statutes of Poland** under the word "Noble." A Noble <note>original: *Nobilis*</note> is named from "knowing" <note>original: *noscendo*</note>, as if to say "Knowable" <note>original: *Noscibilis*</note>; and thus, in a more general sense, a "Noble" is one who has become known to men because of some thing, whether good or bad. **Jacobus Curtius**, *Conjectures, Volume 1, Book 2, Chapter 29.* Conversely, one is called "ignoble" whose name is more obscure than others. See **Gothofredus** on *Law 7 of the Code on Pleading.* But in the specific sense, those who are the subject of our current discussion are Nobles: those whose name or family is considered more illustrious in the Republic, more recognized by all, and more "knowable." Among the **Hebrews**, Nobles were called the *Atilim* <note>Hebrew: אֲצִילִים</note>, meaning the "optimates," the select and principal ones among the People, as if set apart; likewise the *Chorim* <note>Hebrew: חֹרִים</note>, meaning the Illustrious, the free, and the well-born (from which come the **Heroes** or **Lords** <note>original German: *Herren*</note>), as if they were "Candidates" <note>referring to the Roman *candidati*, who wore white robes to show purity/status</note> or...

### Concerning Law and the Orders of Citizens.
<meta>catchword: Not all who...</meta>

**I.** They are called **candidates** <note>original: *candidati*</note>, as if they were dressed in white garments <note>from the Latin *candidus*, meaning shining white; Roman office-seekers whitened their togas with chalk</note>. And so among the later Romans, those of the more honorable order were called "candidates," while the common, mixed crowd were called the **dark-clad** <note>original: *pullati*; those wearing mourning or dirty, dark-colored wool typical of the lower classes</note>. These you might call *Hofchurim* <note>original: *Hofchurim*; likely a reference to the Hebrew *ḥurim* (nobles/free-born), though the author here curiously links it to being "obscure" or "shadowy characters"</note>. *Drusius in fragments of the ancient interpreters, page 56; and on Genesis chapter 4; and page 117.* Among the Germans, this rank of dignity is usually called **Nobility** <note>original: *der Adel*</note>; a word which very conveniently corresponds with the Greek **worthy** <note>original: *ἀξιος* (axios)</note>, which signifies one who is well-deserving or has been affected by a reward. *Andreas Helwig in The Origins of the German Language, under the word "Adel"; see also Georg Vechner's discourse on Nobility, chapter 1.* On the subject of Nobility, Canonhiero also has much to say in *Book 7 of the Introduction, throughout.* The Spaniards use the term **Hidalgo**, as if saying "son of someone" <note>from *hijo de algo*; meaning a son of a man of substance or repute</note>: that is, born from one who was of some name. *Pedro Mexía, Book 4, Chapter 3.* For why the Italians call a Noble a **gentleman** <note>original: *gentilhuomo*</note>, see *Benediger's Glory, page 71.*

**II.** The name and dignity of this Nobility does not arise from Natural Law or the primitive Law of Nations, but rather from **Civil Law** <note>Law created by a specific state for its own members</note>; and therefore it varies according to the customs and institutions of each state and nation. *Bodin, Book 3 of the Commonwealth, number 350; and at length, Matthias Stephani in his treatise on Political Nobility, chapter 1.* It follows from this that Nobility cannot be defined without respect to the Commonwealth; nor can a single definition ever be brought forth that is suitable for all peoples. *Heig, part 1, question 2, number 63.* From this, furthermore, it is quite easy to infer that Nobility contains within itself a certain eminence, and indeed, in our terms, a **political** one (that is, human and by reputation), not at all supernatural or natural, as Bartolus did not hesitate to distinguish. *Tileman, part 1, dispute 15, conclusions 1 and 2.*

**III.** Thus **Romulus** <note>the legendary founder of Rome</note>, right at the beginning, established two orders of citizens: so that some were **Patricians** and some **Plebeians**. They were called Patricians because they were the progeny of the "Fathers"—that is, they were descended from the "Fathers" or Senators. These were the men who could "call upon their fathers" <note>original: *Patres ciere*; a pun on *Patricius*, implying they had known, free-born ancestors of high rank</note> (in the sense that the Spaniards say *Hidalgo*). To these, he prescribed that they should care for sacred rites, hold magistracies, administer the Commonwealth with him, and attend to urban affairs. The **Plebeians**, however, were to cultivate the fields, tend livestock, and practice profitable crafts. This arrangement lasted for a long time in the Free Republic. Later, as the pride of the Patricians grew and they abused their power, barring the Plebeians from all administration of the Commonwealth, the indignant Plebs seized for themselves the power of the Courts, the Senate, the Magistracies, and the right of pronouncing the law. At that time, as the order of the old Patricians diminished, a man was considered **Noble** if he was descended from one who had first attained a **Curule Magistracy** <note>a high Roman office, such as Consul or Praetor, that entitled the holder to sit on a *sella curulis* (ivory folding chair)</note> in that family and had the "right of statues" <note>the right to display wax masks and statues of ancestors in the home</note>. *See Carlo Sigonio, Book 1 of his Emendations, chapter 48, volume 2 of Gruter’s Critical Treasury, page 245* (where he discusses whether Patricians were also "new men") *and the same author in his emendations of Robortello, Book 2, chapter 55, page 464 of the same work, where he discusses Bologna. Baron Streinius, on the families of the Romans, where he discusses the Plebeians, E. 4.* Not all who...

<summary>This page explores the etymology and legal origins of nobility across Roman, German, Spanish, and Italian cultures, arguing that nobility is a product of civil law rather than natural law. It details the early Roman social division between Patricians and Plebeians and how the definition of a 'noble' evolved with political changes.</summary>

### Dissertation II.
**M**
<meta>catchword: From</meta>

Those who were Nobles and of a distinguished lineage among the Romans passed into the **Patriciate**; and thus our own Nobles can be more correctly likened to the Roman Patricians than to the [general class of] Roman Nobles. *Jacobus Curtius, Conjectures, volume 1, book 2, chapter 19; add also Calvin in the Juridical Lexicon, under the word "Patricians," etc.* Likewise, the **Roman Knights** <note>original: *Equites Romani*</note> were not Nobles but Plebeians; they were first chosen for the duty of judging as members of the panels of Judges; then, by Cicero during his consulship, they were distinguished by the honor of the Equestrian Order, and they received seats in the fourteen rows of the theaters separate from the Common People. And this was not by lineage or merit, but by wealth alone <note>original: *solo censu*; the Roman census required a property qualification of 400,000 sesterces for a man to be enrolled as a knight</note>. *Busius, Political Disputations 2, thesis 26; Alexander ab Alexandro, Genial Days, book 2, chapter 29.* Similarly among the **Swiss** <note>original: *Helvetios*</note>, after the old Nobles were for the most part expelled, those now considered Nobles are the leading men from among the People, and those whose ancestors held the highest magistracies.

**IV.** Among the **Greeks**, Nobility followed the lineage of Kings and Heroes. Just as also among the **Hebrews**, it followed the descendants of Aaron <note>the priestly class</note> or the Kings. *Bodin, On the Republic, book 3, chapter 8; and Reiner Reineccius deals with the institutions of other nations regarding this matter in the treatise "On the Origin of Nobility" <note>original: "von deß Adels Herkommen"</note>. Grasser in the Itinerary, folio 265; add Cluverius, On Ancient Germany, book 1, chapter 15; Guyon, volume 2, book 4, chapter 20.* Among the **Germans**, those were formerly called Nobles who had never served in slavery. *Heigius, question 1, number 61 and following; Stumpff, book 4, chapters 29 and 50, book 7, folio 201 b, book 10, chapter 1; and Münster, book 3, chapter 21.* Also Herzog, *In the Alsatian Chronicle, book 6, chapter 1; Wolfgang Hunger, Against Bouillium, under the word "Tournament" <note>original: "Tournoy"</note>.* On the **Dutch** <note>original: *Batavica*</note> Nobility, see Hadrianus Junius in *Batavian History, chapter 19.*

**V.** Now, whether nobility that descends by lineage should be approved in a well-ordered or Christian Republic, or whether it ought to be expelled as contrary to natural equity, is debated in various ways by various authors. *See Daneau, Christian Politics, book 2, chapter 6.* For there are those to whom that Nobility seems much more pleasing which arises from **generosity**, or a good temperament of mind and body; by which men are uniquely inclined toward great virtues, especially those of war. Hence also that saying of the Poet <note>Juvenal, Satire 8</note>:

> Though old waxen masks <note>original: *ceræ*; the wax ancestral portraits displayed in Roman homes</note> decorate the halls on all sides,
> Virtue is the only and unique nobility.

Who does not see how empty and futile the name of Nobility is? "If it refers to renown, it belongs to someone else," says **Boethius** in *The Consolation of Philosophy, book 3, prose 6.* He also says in the same book, poem 6:

> Why do you make a noise about lineage and ancestors?
> If you look at your beginnings
> and God your creator,
> no one is born base;
> unless, cherishing worse things through vices,
> he abandons his own origin.

<summary>The author compares the historical definitions of nobility across Roman, Swiss, German, and ancient cultures, noting that rank often derived from wealth or freedom from slavery rather than blood. The text concludes by debating whether hereditary nobility is compatible with Christian equity, citing Boethius to argue that true nobility lies in virtue, not ancestry.</summary>

### On the Law and the Orders of Citizens.
**M**

From the internal organs, it cannot be known who is noble or who is common. <note>original: "Ex intestinis cognosci nequit, quis nobilis vel plebejus existat." This physiological argument was a common trope among Renaissance humanists to argue for the fundamental equality of all people.</note> See Trajano Boccalini, *Advices from Parnassus*, book 2, chapter 8; and see more on this matter in Philo, *On Nobility*; Seneca, *Epistle 44*; Horace, *Satires*, book 1, satire 6; Juvenal, *Satire 8*; Rodrigo de Zamora in *The Mirror of Human Life*, book 1, chapters 7 and 8; Heinrich Cornelius Agrippa, *The Vanity of the Sciences*, chapter 80; Erasmus, in the Colloquies *The Knight without a Horse* and *The Marriage that is no Marriage* <note>original: "ἱππεὺς, ἄνιππος: γάμος, ἄγαμος"</note>; Nicodemus Frischlin in his *Oration on Rural Life*; Scribanius, *Christian Philosophy*, chapter 5; and Ens, *Morosophia* [Foolish-Wisdom], book 2, chapter 16.

Nor do only Philosophers and Poets criticize this [hereditary] nobility in their writings; even Theologians do not differ from them. Among them, Lactantius says in book 5, chapter 15: "God, who generates and inspires humans, willed all to be equal. He brought all forth for wisdom, and promised immortality to all. No one is excluded from His heavenly benefits." Prudentius, in his *Crowns of Martyrdom* <note>original: "Peristephanon," a collection of hymns by the late Roman poet Prudentius</note>, writes:

> If you look back at our first origin 
> In our cradles, through the weaver's thread of lineage:
> We began from the mouth of God our Parent,
> And whoever serves Him, he is truly Noble.

Michael ab Isselt, in his preface to the *Flowers of Luis de Granada*: "Those Nobles who boast of nothing but ancient wealth and the glory of ancestors do nothing other than illustrate their race with blood and plunder."

**VI.** I am pleased by the distinction made by those who differentiate between **virtue** and the **outward signs of virtue**. Nobility, specifically so called, should not be confused with virtue itself; rather, it exists so that there may be a distinction of families within the human race for the sake of order in the **Republic**. <note>The author argues that nobility is a political tool for social organization rather than an inherent moral quality.</note> For this reason, Cicero says in his speech *For Sestius*: "All of us good men always favor Nobility, both because it is useful to the Republic for there to be noble men worthy of their ancestors, and because the memory of famous men who have served the Republic well ought to carry weight with us, even after they are dead." See Keckerman, *Introduction to the System of Ethics*, folio 31 and following, and *Practical Disputations 21, problem 11*; Timpler, *Politics*, book 1, question 6 and following; add also Castiglione’s *The Courtier*, book 2, folio 12, etc.

Just as Riches often fall to the unworthy yet are not immediately taken from them and given to those more worthy, so it is with Nobility. The **Turks** are said to be the only ones among nations who value Nobility based on each person's own merit and virtue alone. *Pierre Belon, book 2, chapter 95.* Ogier Ghiselin de Busbecq also reports this in his first *Turkish Embassy Letter*, while simultaneously complaining that in **Germany** everything is granted to Birth; the approaches to honor are occupied by the reputation of Birth, and no place is left for Virtue.

But I believe this is a <term>arcanum dominationis: a "secret of rule" or political strategy used by monarchs to consolidate power</term>: that all should depend on the Prince alone, and no one becomes great or Noble except through the Prince, so that they might be more deeply indebted to the Prince. Tacitus reports this same thing regarding the **Swedes** <note>original: "Sueonibus"</note> in his *On the Customs of the Germans*. See also Boccalini, *Advices from Parnassus*, 2.91. Indeed, even the Turks have Nobles...

<summary>This page argues that nobility is a social and political construct rather than an inherent biological or moral trait, citing numerous classical and contemporary authors to favor virtue over hereditary rank. It contrasts the German system of hereditary honor with the Turkish system of meritocracy, which it interprets as a strategic tool for absolute monarchy.</summary>

## Dissertation II.
### On the Law and the Orders of Citizens.

...those of noble lineage, whom they call the *Tzelebi* <note>original: "Tzlebios," referring to the Turkish Çelebi, a title for a gentleman or a person of royal blood</note>; but they honor them very little. Busbequius, *in the cited place, folio 16b*; Bellonius, *book 3, chapter 17*. For the Sultans, fearing them, raise them to no high dignities, but prefer Mamluks <note>original: "Mammelucos," elite slave-soldiers who rose to high office</note> over them; because they can cast these men out again as often as they please without danger of rebellion. For these men, just as they are highly elevated, so are they exposed to the greatest storms of the envious, the ambitious, and the greedy. Often slandered for the slightest reason, they are either cast out or killed, even without being heard—while the Emperors offer more than open ears to informants (as Tyrants usually do). And this custom of the Turks is promoted as a "secret of state" <note>original: "arcano status"</note>: *The Political Treasury*, *part 1, "Report on the Turkish Empire," folio 136*; and it is commended by Trajano Boccalini in *Parnassus, report 2, chapter 43, folio 224 and following*, who notes elsewhere that a powerful nobility is hated by absolute kings. *Parnassus, report 2, chapter 43.*

VII. Certainly, one ought to have an order of Nobles as a "seminary" <note>In the sense of a nursery or training ground</note> both for offices and duties, and for the robed magistrates, but especially for the sake of the military. Heigius, *book 1, question 2, number 65.* For this reason, the name "Soldier" is used in feudal customs to mean "Noble," because it is proper for Nobles to serve in the military for the Fatherland. *See the entire title on the Military Vassal*, and the feudalists there. And before two hundred years ago or thereabouts, only Nobles served as soldiers in Germany, France, and Italy; almost the entire army consisted of cavalry. Hence Nobles, both then and now, are called Knights, or the <term>Ritterschafft</term> <note>German: Knighthood or the collective body of knights</note>. In tombs and ancient documents, "Soldier" signifies a Knight and a Noble. Chiflet, *On Besançon, part 1, chapter 26, folio 93.* And they are called "Shield-bearers" <note>original: "Scutiferi"</note>, who among us are called <term>Edel Knecht</term> <note>German: "Noble Servant," equivalent to a Squire</note>. Hence a noble fief, or one belonging to shield-bearers (mentioned in the *Extraordinary Titles*), is a <term>Schildt Lehen</term> <note>German: "Shield Fief"</note>; it is nothing other than a free, noble, and ancestral fief. In France, the nobles are immune from other burdens and only provide service to the King in war. *Political Treasury, part 1, "Report on France," folio 172 and following.* They held "Free Fiefs," which were not burdened except by military expeditions. But while the Crusades <note>original: "bellis sacris," holy wars</note> were raging, especially in Palestine, these fiefs passed into the hands of Commoners, who paid money for that permission. Guyon, *volume 2, book 1, chapter 6.* The order of Nobles is held in certain places as a nursery for the principal offices because, being liberally educated, they are considered wiser; and because, according to the old proverb, Jupiter is thought to have "taken away half the mind from slaves." <note>A reference to a Homeric sentiment that slavery degrades the human character</note> Rittershusius, *on the Novellae, folio 524.* Thus also the Emperor Alexander Severus did not admit freedmen into the Equestrian order, saying that the Equestrian rank is the nursery of Senators. Lampridius. Monarchs also generally favor the Nobility, because Nobles are devoted to Monarchs and prefer to endure a Prince rather than equality among all. Meteren, *History of the Netherlands, book 2, folio 49.*
