### On the Law and the Orders of Citizens.

VIII. To ensure that men can lead these duties—so necessary to the Republic—with dignity, and so that they may be liberally educated, Fiefs <term>Feuda: land or property granted by a lord to a vassal in exchange for service</term> were anciently given to the Nobility, and many immunities were granted. My brother, of pious memory, writes in his *Speech on the Origin of Fiefs* <note>original: "in Oration. de Orig. feudor."</note>, that this was done so that they could devote themselves to public offices with greater zeal, and not be easily pulled away while seeking the means to sustain their lives from other sources. See Kirchner, *Disputation 12 on the Republic, thesis 1*; and Keckermann, *Disputation 31, problem 1*. For, as Aristotle says in the beginning of *Politics, Book 2, Chapter 7*, the proper management of a State requires leisure from necessary labors. Nobility without wealth is ridiculous, and soon vanishes. As Thomas Costo says in *The Pastime* <note>original: "Fuggilozio d. Tomaso Costo," a collection of stories and maxims</note>, *page 477 and 482*:

->*That Nobility I value little,*<-
->*And virtue even less, if wealth is not also there.*<-
<note>original: "Che Nobilità poco li prezzo, / E men vertu, se non v' è anchora ricchezza."</note>

And wretched are those Nobles who drive a donkey laden with wood to the market, and yet swear by their "noble honor" that they previously sold such a load for a high price. Guazzo, *On Civil Conversation, book 2, page 297.*

IX. Meanwhile, all men of whatever condition ought to strive with the greatest zeal to acquire **Natural Nobility**, which is rightly defined as: *A disposition suited to virtue.* Therefore, whether that disposition is innate or implanted by education, only those who possess it are noble by nature. Lipsius, *Manuduction to Stoic Philosophy, book 3, dissertation 17.* This Nobility belongs to everyone, because it is a gift from God; and *God is no respecter of persons.* But truly, as Lucan sings in the *Panegyric of Piso*:

> *All nobility perishes in that man*
> *Whose praise lies only in his origin.*

Such men are by nature the most ignoble; they are proud in their iniquity and shall receive the reward of injustice from God. Augustus Vischer argues excellently against such people in his *Treatise on the Law of Duels, page 344.* I wish to add here what was proposed by that most noble and well-born youth, Lord Carolus Christoph Schallenberg of Biberstein and Leonbach, an Austrian Knight, in a Disputation on Nobility (held in 1615 under my presidency). I include this both in memory of our old friendship and because it is worth knowing. These are his words, verbatim: "I am pleased to affirm here this most true Paradox: That only the <term>Poor in Spirit</term> <note>referring to the Beatitude "Blessed are the poor in spirit" (Matthew 5:3)</note> is truly—that is, naturally—Noble. For Poverty is the Likeness of **GOD**: and since the poor man depends on none of the creatures which are beneath him, but rests only in Him who is exalted above all things. And this is the very Nobility of Poverty: that it depends on the Supreme Good alone, and looks down upon all inferior things. It befits a man to be so Poor that he is stripped of all worldly Wisdom, Love, Will, Desire, and such passions." However, this must be understood in the sense that nothing is fitting for a man...

<summary>The text argues that while hereditary nobles require wealth and fiefs to perform state duties, "True Nobility" is a natural disposition toward virtue and spiritual poverty, available to all regardless of birth.</summary>

### Dissertation II.

<margin>M 3</margin>

to either know or desire anything, except what God Himself knows or wills in him. Indeed, the more a person considers all knowledge as foreign to themselves, the more perfect that knowledge will be. Therefore, **Poverty of Spirit** <term>Paupertas Spiritus: a spiritual state of humility and detachment from worldly possessions and ego-driven desires</term> is to be embraced, so that a person—free from all creatures and stripped of self—may nakedly embrace the **One Good** <note>original: "Unum Bonum," referring to God as the ultimate source of goodness</note>. And this is what it means to leave everything for the sake of Christ and to deny oneself. From this abandonment of all things, one must enter into **Union**, and go out of oneself into God: so that the internal man remains unmoved, even while the external man is moved in various ways; although this state of "indolence" <note>original: "indolentia," here meaning a state of being unaffected by external suffering or passion</note> cannot perfectly befall a person in this life. 

For no creature possesses anything of its own, but everything it has is from the Creator. Therefore, those who are destitute of all created things are the ones whom Christ, in Matthew 5, calls "Blessed" under the name of the **Poor in Spirit**. Christ taught this in words and fulfilled it in His life. And finally, that spiritual poverty is unceasingly accompanied by true **Christian Freedom**, the most noble of all nobilities. For as **Augustine** says in *On True Religion*: <margin>Augustine, *On True Religion*.</margin> "Whoever delights in true Liberty should desire to be free from the love of mutable things; and whoever delights in reigning should be subject to God, the one Ruler of All, loving Him more than himself and all things. For in whatever condition of service, he is free who is not captured by love, not held by the chains of greed, and not bound by the fear of crime; who waits for the present with security and is not terrified by the future." 

Hence that most beautiful saying of **Seneca**, in *Epistle 31*: <margin>Seneca, *Epistle 31*.</margin> "For what, I ask, is Nobility, if not Freedom? And what is Freedom? To be a slave to nothing: to no necessity, to no accidents; to bring fortune down to an equal level." Since everyone can possess this freedom, why not Nobility as well? For in God's sight, the one who is more important is not the one recommended by noble birth or worldly dignity, but the one recommended by devotion of faith and a Holy Life. And these, although they are under the yoke of Christ, nevertheless acquire true freedom and dominion of the Spirit. 

Therefore, God is most good when He gives rest to His own in this exile and among such great tribulations. For one small drop of spiritual sweetness is more delightful than all worldly consolations. <margin>See Reverend Father Friar Diego de Estella, *On Contemning the Vanities of the World*, book 3, chapter 22.</margin> From these things, finally, those greatest and best effects are derived, such as: **Perfect Wisdom and Knowledge**, which is drawn from that hidden treasure in the human soul. <margin>Johann Arndt, *On True Christianity*, book 4.</margin> Hence it happens that we no longer have a need to drink in the knowledge of things from those heaps of books. But, because as the Apostle says, we are the **Temple of God**; God dwells in us if we acknowledge Him. Therefore, we possess all the treasures of wisdom with Him. It is fitting that we stir these up. **This is the task, this is the toil.** <note>original: "Hoc opus, hic labor est," a quote from Virgil’s Aeneid (VI.129) regarding the difficulty of returning to the upper world after descending to the underworld—here used to describe the effort of spiritual realization.</note> From which it happens that very many and almost the most keen-witted—

# On Law and the Orders of Citizens

### VIII.
For these duties, so necessary to the Commonwealth, to be presided over with dignity and for the officials to be liberally educated, **fiefs** <term>Feuda: land grants given by a sovereign in exchange for military or political service</term> were formerly given to the Nobility, and many immunities were granted. My brother (of pious memory) says in his *Oration on the Origin of Fiefs*, that this was done so that they might apply themselves to public duties with even greater zeal, and not be easily called away from them while seeking the means to sustain their lives from other sources. See Kirchner, *Disputation 12 on the Commonwealth, thesis 1*; and Keckerman, *Disputation 31, problem 1*. For, as **Aristotle** says in the *Politics, Book 2, chapter 7 at the beginning*, the correct governing of a Commonwealth requires leisure <note>original: "vacationem," referring to the Greek concept of *schole*—freedom from manual labor to pursue intellectual or civic life</note> from necessary labors. Nobility without riches is ridiculous and soon vanishes. As Fuggilozio says in the work of Tomaso Costo, *pages 477 and 482*:

->*That nobility I value little,*<-
->*and virtue even less, if wealth is not also there.*<-
<note>original Italian: "Che Nobilità poco li prezzo, / E men vertu, senon v' è anchora ricchezza."</note>

And miserable are those Nobles who drive a little donkey loaded with wood to the market, and swear by their noble word that they have never sold such a load before. See Guazzo, *On Civil Conversation, Book 2, page 297*.

### IX.
Meanwhile, all people of whatever condition ought to strive with the greatest zeal to acquire **Natural Nobility**, which is rightly defined as: **A disposition suited to virtue.** Therefore, whether that disposition is innate or implanted by education, only those are noble by nature who possess it. See Lipsius, *3rd Handbook to Stoic Philosophy, dissertation 17*. This nobility is available to everyone because it is a gift from God, and God is no respecter of persons. But indeed, as **Lucan** sings in the *Panegyric to Piso*:

> *All nobility perishes in that man*
> *whose only claim to praise is his ancestry.*
<note>original: "Perit omnis in illo / Nobilitas, cui laus est in origine solâ."</note>

Such people are by nature the most ignoble; they pride themselves on iniquity and shall receive from God the reward of injustice. Augustus Vischer argues excellently against such people in his *Treatise on the Law of Duels, page 344*. I wish to add here what a most noble and high-born youth, Mr. Carolus Christophorus Schallenberg of Biberstein and Leonbach, an Austrian Knight, proposed in a *Disputation on Nobility* (held in 1615 under my presidency). I do so both in memory of our old friendship and because it is worth knowing. His words, translated exactly, are as follows: "I am pleased to affirm here that most true paradox: **Only the Poor in Spirit is truly—that is, naturally—Noble.** Since Poverty is a likeness of GOD; and since the poor man depends on none of the creatures that are beneath him, but rests only in Him who is exalted above all. And this is the very nobility of Poverty: that it depends on the Supreme Good alone and looks down upon all lower things. It is fitting for a person to be so Poor that they are destitute even of all Wisdom, Love, Will, Desire, and similar affections—though this must be understood in the sense that it is not fitting for a person..."

## Dissertation II.

<sig>M 3</sig>
<meta>catchword: most acute—</meta>

...to know or to desire anything, except what **God** Himself either knows or wills within him. Indeed, the more a man judges all knowledge to be foreign to himself, the more perfect his knowledge will be. Therefore, Poverty of Spirit <term>Paupertas Spiritus: a mystical state of emptying the self to be filled with God</term> must be embraced, so that a man—free from all created things and stripped of himself—may nakedly embrace the One Good. This is what it means to desert all things for the sake of Christ and to deny oneself. From this abandonment of all things, one must enter into Union, and go out of oneself into **God**: so that the internal man remains unmoved, even while the external man is moved in various ways; although this state of detachment <note>original: "indolentia," referring to the Stoic-like state of being unaffected by external suffering or passion</note> cannot perfectly fall upon a man in this life. For no creature possesses anything of its own, but everything it has is from the Creator. Therefore, those who are destitute of all created things are the ones whom Christ—in Matthew 25 <note>The author likely intended to cite the Beatitudes in Matthew 5:3</note> under the name of the "Poor in Spirit"—proclaims as Blessed. Christ taught this through his words and fulfilled it through his life. Finally, this spiritual poverty is unceasingly accompanied by true Christian liberty, the "Most Noble of Nobilities." For as Augustine says in *On True Religion*: 

> He who delights in true Liberty should desire to be free from the love of changeable things; and he who delights in reigning should be subject to the one Ruler of All, **God**, by loving Him more than himself and all other things. For that man is free in any condition of service who is not captured by love, not held by the chains of greed, and not bound by the fear of crime; who waits for the present with a secure mind and is not terrified of the future.

Hence comes that most beautiful saying of Seneca, in *Letter 31*: 

> What, I ask, is Nobility, if not Liberty? But what is Liberty? To serve no thing, no necessity, no accidents; to bring fortune down to an equal level.

Since everyone can possess this liberty, why not Nobility as well? For in the eyes of **God**, the one who is more powerful is not the one commended by nobility of birth or the dignity of the age, but the one commended by the devotion of faith and a Holy Life. And these people, although they are under the yoke of Christ, nevertheless acquire true liberty and dominion of the Spirit. Therefore **God** is most good when, in this exile and amid such great tribulations, He gives rest to His own. For a single tiny drop of the Spirit’s sweetness is more delightful than all worldly consolations. <margin>See the Reverend Father Fray Diego de Estella, *On the Contempt of Worldly Vanities*, book 3, chapter 22.</margin> From these things, finally, those "Greatest and Best" effects are derived, such as: Perfect Wisdom and Knowledge, which is drawn from that hidden treasure within the human soul. <margin>Johann Arndt, *On True Christianity*, book 4.</margin> Hence it happens that we no longer have a need to draw our knowledge of things from those heaps of books. Truly, because as the Apostle says, we are the Temple of God; God dwells within us, if we acknowledge Him. Therefore, we possess all the treasures of Wisdom along with Him. It is fitting that we should stir these up. **This is the work, this is the labor.** <note>A famous quote from Virgil’s *Aeneid*: "Hoc opus, hic labor est," referring to the difficulty of the task.</note> From this, it has happened that many and almost the most acute—

<summary>This page defines true nobility as a spiritual state achieved through "Poverty of Spirit" and inner union with God. It argues that spiritual liberty and a holy life are superior to social rank and that divine wisdom is found within the soul rather than in external books.</summary>

<meta>This page continues the legal and social discussion on the orders of citizens, specifically focusing on the justification for the nobility's wealth and the higher concept of "Natural Nobility" vs. "Spiritual Poverty." It aligns with the previous page's conclusion regarding the mystical "Poverty of Spirit."</meta>

# On Law, and the orders of citizens.
<meta>catchword: or</meta>

VIII. To ensure that these duties, so necessary to the Republic, might be led with dignity and that the leaders might be educated in the liberal arts, Fiefs <term>Feuda: lands granted by a lord in exchange for service, providing the income necessary for public life</term> were long ago given to the Nobility, and many immunities were granted. My brother, of pious memory <note>original: "p.m." for "piae memoriae"</note>, in his *Oration on the Origin of Fiefs*, argues that this was done so that they might apply themselves to public offices with greater zeal and not be easily pulled away while searching elsewhere for the means to support their lives. This is supported by Kirchner in his *12th Disputation on the Republic, thesis 1*, and Keckerman in his *31st Disputation, problem 1*. For as Aristotle says in the beginning of the *2nd book of Politics, chapter 7*:

> The right order of a Republic requires leisure <note>vacationem: here meaning freedom or exemption from menial manual labor</note> from necessary labors. 

Nobility without wealth is ridiculous, and soon vanishes. As Tommaso Costo says in *Fuggilozio* (pages 477 and 482):

> *How little I prize Nobility,*
> *And even less virtue, if wealth is not also present.*
<note>original Italian: "Che Nobilità poco li prezzo / E men vertu, ſe non v' è anchora ricchezza."</note>

And wretched are those Nobles who drive a donkey laden with wood to the market, and swear "by the faith of their Nobility" that they have previously sold such a load for a certain price. (Guazzo, *On Civil Conversation, book 2, page 297*).

IX. Meanwhile, all men of whatever condition must strive with the greatest zeal to acquire Natural Nobility, which is rightly defined as: A disposition suited to virtue. Therefore, whether this disposition is innate or implanted by learning, only those who possess it are truly noble by nature. (Lipsius, *3rd Guide to Stoic Philosophy, dissertation 17*). This nobility is available to all because it is a gift of God; and God is no respecter of persons. But indeed, as Lucan sings in his *Panegyric to Piso*:

> *All nobility perishes in that man*
> *whose only praise lies in his ancestry.*
<note>original Latin: "Perit omnis in illo / Nobilitas, cui laus eſt in origine ſola."</note>

Such people are by nature the most ignoble; they pride themselves in their iniquity and will receive the reward of injustice from God. Augustus Vischer argues excellently against such people in his *Treatise on the Law of Duels, page 344*. I am pleased to add here what a most noble and generous young man, Mr. Karl Christoph Schallenberg of Biberstein and Leonbach, an Austrian Knight, proposed in a *Disputation on Nobility* (held in 1615 with myself presiding). I include this both in memory of our old friendship and because it is worth knowing. These are his words, quoted verbatim:

> I wish to affirm here this most true paradox: That only the Poor in Spirit <term>Spiritu Pauperem: a reference to the Beatitudes, implying a state of internal emptiness and humility</term> is truly—that is, naturally—Noble. Since Poverty is the Likeness of GOD; and since the poor man depends on none of the creatures that are beneath him, but rests only in Him who is exalted above all. And this is the very nobility of Poverty: that it depends only on the Highest Good and despises all inferior things. It is fitting for a man to be so Poor that he is even destitute of all wisdom, love, will, desire, and similar passions—though this must be understood in the sense that nothing is fitting for a man...

<summary>This page discusses the practical necessity of wealth for the nobility to serve the state, while contrasting inherited status with "Natural Nobility" and the spiritual ideal of "Poverty of Spirit."</summary>
<keywords>Nobility, Fiefs, Wealth, Aristotle, Natural Nobility, Poverty of Spirit, Karl Christoph Schallenberg, Virtue, Social Class</keywords>

<meta>This page continues the discussion from the previous page regarding the nature of nobility. While the previous section touched upon the necessity of wealth for secular nobility, this page transitions into a mystical and spiritual definition of "true" nobility, drawing on the tripartite nature of man and the teachings of Johannes Tauler, before beginning a formal classification of noble ranks.</meta>

# On Law, and the orders of citizens.
<meta>catchword: others</meta>

...the most sharp-minded [men] have not emerged as learned from the books of literature and philosophers, but from their own contemplation; with God, their guest, suggesting grace, they have progressed so much that they have left the most learned of many centuries (who dared to call themselves Philosophers) far behind them. For man consists of Three: Soul, Body, and the Spirit placed in the middle. Therefore, whichever way the Spirit turns, such a man is made: which Johannes Tauler <note>Johannes Tauler (c. 1300–1361) was a famous German Dominican mystic and theologian. His "Sermons" emphasized the "ground of the soul" and the inner path to God.</note> also teaches in his Discourses. Wherefore, if a man turns himself toward inferior things, he will be (indeed, by that very act, necessarily is) earthly, and thus unfit for the Kingdom of God. Meanwhile, though distinguished by this honor of Nobility, he can and even ought to remain a commoner; nor should he desire Liberty or Secular Power beyond his legitimate calling. Nonetheless, he who raises himself above all things to the One Principle of All: has already by that very act attained the highest Nobility. For such a person is united with God, joined with the same: therefore, with Him, he possesses all things. Nobles of this kind are free from the blows of fortune, which they can firmly despise. For they know the two ambiguous faces and two foreheads of the Starry Sky, or of Fortune: the front with hair, that is, prosperous; and the back bald, that is, adverse <note>original: "capillatam anteriorem... posteriorem calvam." This refers to the classical image of Opportunity or Fortune, who must be seized by the forelock because she is bald behind.</note>. Since they have joined themselves not to the Sky, but to the Lord of the Heavens, and thus have been made Lords of the Sky, they do not tremble; for in these movements of fortune, only the external man is moved. While we all strive to pursue these things, fewer (as I think) will be made worldly Nobles, and the Knightly Order of Heavenly Beings will be increased. And so we shall remain, having stepped over all created things, rushing headlong out of love into the abyss; and we shall be one in the one, in whom all things are one. In this consists eternal life and the true assembly of Nobles.

### CHAPTER X.
## ->On the Grades of Nobles; and how Nobility is acquired?<-

There are certain grades of Nobles: some are Barons, others are Nobles properly so-called, others are Nobles at home, or Patricians <term>Patricij: members of the hereditary aristocratic class, originally the ruling families of ancient Rome, but here referring to the urban elite</term>; but among some nations there is only one grade of Nobles. Philippus Hoenonius, *Political Disputations 2, thesis 27, section b*. Among the Romans, some were of the greater clans, others of the lesser clans: all of which matters must be judged from the various institutions of the Peoples. Political Nobility is in itself one and the same; yet it is distinguished so strictly and variously by grade that its various types are considered to exist, just as there are grades of almost all dignities;

It also begins a legalistic classification of noble ranks, such as Barons and Patricians, noting that these definitions vary by nation.</summary>

## Dissertation II.
<meta>catchword: personal.</meta>

...some are greater, others lesser. See Matthaeus Stephanus, *On Political Nobility, chapter 3,* at the beginning; Pierre Grégoire of Toulouse, *On the Republic, book 4, chapter 2, section 5.* And those are foolish who say they are just as noble as the King himself: as if one Noble cannot exist who is more noble than another. See Stefano Guazzo, *book 2, page 288;* Trajano Boccalini, *Parnassus, report 43, page 224.* 

Formerly, "Noble" was a term used only for noble servants <note>original: "Edelfnecht" (Edelknecht). Historically, this referred to a well-born youth serving a knight, similar to a squire.</note> and for Barons and Counts. See Matthaeus Stephanus, *on nobility, page 24.* In Austria, the Nobles and the Barons <note>original: "die Edlen vnd Freyherren"</note> constitute two distinct estates. In Germany, Barons are numbered among the Counts in the Diets <note>The "Diet" (Reichstag) was the deliberative assembly of the Holy Roman Empire.</note>. In Denmark, there is no Baron or Count; instead, the leading men are content with the simple title of Nobility. Regarding the purchase of new titles, "where someone allows himself to be made a Lord or a Count for money," <note>original: "da einer sich vmb Gelt zu einem Herren oder Graffen machen last"</note> Tommaso Costo laughs gently at this in his *Fugilozio, page 556.* Indeed, some are prone to such insanity that they buy titles and sell their lands. See Catharinus Dulcis, *in his aphorisms, number 31.* Boccalini, with the same kindness he usually applies when judging morals, grants these people the legal benefits usually reserved for minors, spendthrifts, and the insane. *Report 49, page 124.*

By reason of their location, Nobles are distinguished into Urban and Rural, though this distinction taken from their place of residence adds or detracts little or nothing from their Nobility. See André Tiraqueau, *On Nobility, chapter 2, number 53.* Patricians are those who are considered Noble not by Imperial authority, but by the particular custom of a city. For this reason, they are called City Squires <note>original: "Stätt Junker"</note> and "Nobles at home." See Étienne Pasquier, *Researches, book 3, chapter 13, page 211.* They are such only in the place where that custom exists; elsewhere, they are not. See Matthaeus Stephanus, *chapter 4, number 22, etc.* On the subject of Patricians, however, see Becht, *disputation on safe conduct, thesis 125;* Ulrich Wolff, *Political Disputation 12, thesis 12.*

II. Since the fame of one's fatherland increases one's Nobility, the Nobles in the Holy Roman Empire are rightly preferred over the Nobles of all other Kingdoms and Principalities. (Likewise, all the nobility of the Christian world arose from the Germans, as Lord Lansius says in his *Oration for the Germans, page 115, etc.*, namely because the Germans conquered all of Europe and ruled there.) For their privileges proceed from the greatest Prince of the Christian world <note>The Holy Roman Emperor.</note>, and they are, I believe, greater and more extensive than all others—though among these, the Equestrian Order of the Swabian, Franconian, and Rhenan regions seem to excel in liberty, since they are "immediate" <term>immediate (immediatè): A technical term meaning these knights answered directly to the Emperor, bypasssing intermediate lords.</term> subjects of the Emperor and the Empire. See Boccalini’s *Parnassus, book 1, chapter 8.*

III. There is a noble legal dispute: Whether children born before their father received Nobility become Noble themselves? And thus, whether they enjoy the privileges of Nobility? Along with Charles Dumoulin, *on the Customs of Paris, section 13, glossary 1, at the end,* and Pierre Costalius, *on the Digest, book 5, on the status of men,* I would say no. See also *the Code of Justinian, book 6, on heirs.* For they were neither born noble, nor made noble later. For Nobility is a personal privilege <note>original: "Privilegium personale"</note>.

<summary>This page classifies the ranks of nobility, distinguishing between imperial, territorial, and urban "patrician" statuses while criticizing the sale of titles. It asserts the superiority of German nobility due to their direct relationship with the Emperor and concludes that nobility is a personal legal privilege that does not automatically apply to children born before a father's ennoblement.</summary>

# On the Law and the Orders of Citizens.

...the most sharp-minded individuals have emerged as learned, not from the books of literature and philosophers, but from their own contemplation. With God as their guest suggesting grace, they have progressed so much that they have left the most learned of many centuries (who dared to call themselves <term>Philosophers</term> <note>literally "lovers of wisdom"</note>) a long distance behind them. For man consists of three parts: the <term>Soul</term>, the <term>Body</term>, and the <term>Spirit</term> placed in the middle. Whichever way the Spirit turns itself, such a man is made; as Johannes Tauler <note>A 14th-century German mystic known for his sermons on the inner life and the "ground" of the soul.</note> teaches in his *Sermons*. Therefore, if a man turns himself toward lower things, he will be (indeed, by that very fact, necessarily is) earthly, and thus unfit for the Kingdom of God. Meanwhile, though distinguished by this honor of Nobility, he can and even ought to remain a commoner; nor should he seek Secular Liberty or Power beyond his legitimate calling.

Nevertheless, he who raises himself above all things to the One Principle of All: by that very fact, he has attained the highest Nobility. For such a person is united with God, joined with the same; therefore, with Him, he possesses all things. Nobles of this kind are free from the blows of fortune, which they can firmly despise. For they know the two ambiguous faces and two foreheads of the Star-bearing Heaven, or of Fortune: the hairy front (that is, prosperous) and the bald back (that is, adverse). <note>This refers to the classical personification of *Occasio* or Opportunity, who has hair hanging over her face to be grasped as she approaches, but is bald behind, meaning she cannot be caught once she has passed by.</note> Because they have joined themselves not to the Heaven, but to the Lord of the Heavens, and thus have been made Masters of the Heavens, they do not tremble; for by these movements of fortune, only the external man is moved. While we all strive to pursue these things, fewer (as I think) will be made worldly Nobles, but the Knightly Order of Heavenly beings will be increased. And so we shall remain, having surpassed all created things, plunging headlong out of love into the abyss; and we shall be one in the one, in whom all things are one. In this consists eternal life and the true congregation of Nobles.

### CHAPTER X.
## On the Ranks of Nobles; and how Nobility is acquired?

There are certain ranks of Nobles: some are <term>Barons</term>, some are Nobles properly so-called, others are domestic Nobles, or <term>Patricians</term> <note>Urban elites or members of the ruling class in free imperial cities.</note>; yet among some nations, there is but one rank of Nobles. Philipp Hoenonius, *Political Disputations 2, thesis 27, b.* Among the Romans, some were of the "greater families" and others were of the "lesser families" <note>original: "alij majorum, alij minorum fuerunt gentium"</note>: all of which must be judged according to the various institutions of different peoples. Political nobility is, in itself, indeed one and the same; yet it is distinguished by rank so narrowly and in such various ways that its types are thought to be diverse; just as there are ranks of almost all honors;

### Dissertation II.

...some are greater, others lesser. <note>See Matthias Stephanus, *On Political Nobility*, chapter 3, at the beginning; Tholosanus, *On the Republic*, book 4, chapter 2, section 5.</note> And they are fools who say they are just as noble as the King himself: as if one Noble could not be more noble than another. <note>Guazzo, book 2, page 288; Boccalini, *Advertisements from Parnassus*, report 43, page 224.</note> Formerly, only <term>Noble Squires</term> <note>original: *Edelknecht*</note>, Barons, and Counts were called Nobles. <note>Matthias Stephanus, *On Nobility*, page 24.</note> In Austria, the <term>Nobles</term> and the <term>Barons</term> <note>original: *die Edlen vnd Freyherren*</note> constitute two distinct <term>Estates</term>. In Germany, Barons are counted among the Counts in the Diets. In Denmark, there is no Baro or Count; rather, the leading men are content with the bare title of Nobility. And the purchase of new titles—<quote>where one allows himself to be made a Lord or a Count for money</quote> <note>original: *da einer sich vmb Gelt zu einem Herren oder Graffen machen last*</note>—is smoothly ridiculed by Tomaso Costo in *The Diversion* <note>original: *Fugilozio*, page 556</note>. Indeed, some are usually so driven by madness that they buy titles and sell their lands. <note>Catharinus Dulcis, in *Apophthegms*, number 31.</note> Boccalini, with that sharpness he usually employs in judging morals, grants them the legal benefits conceded to minors, spendthrifts, and the insane. <note>Report 49, page 124.</note> By reason of their location, Nobles are distinguished into Urban and Rural, though this distinction taken from their place of residence adds or detracts little or nothing from their Nobility. <note>Tiraquellus, *On Nobility*, chapter 2, number 53.</note> <term>Patricians</term> are those who are considered noble not by Imperial authority, but by the particular custom of a city. For this reason, they are called <term>City Junkers</term> <note>original: *Stätt Junchern*</note> and "Nobles at home": <note>Pasquier, *Research on France*, book 1, chapter 13, page 211.</note> because they are only such in the place where the custom holds; outside of it, they are not. <note>Matthias Stephanus, chapter 4, number 22, etc.</note> Regarding Patricians, however, see Becht, *Disputation on Safe Conduct*, thesis 125; Ulrich Wolff, *Political Disputations*, 12, thesis 12.

II. Since the fame of one’s Fatherland increases one’s Nobility, the Nobles in the German-Roman Empire are deservedly preferred over the Nobles of all other Kingdoms and Principalities. (Likewise, all the Nobility of the Christian world originated from the Germans: <note>Lord Lansius, *Oration for the Germans*, page 115, etc.</note> specifically because the Germans conquered all of Europe and ruled there.) For their Privileges proceed from the greatest Prince of the Christian world, and they exist as greater and more extensive than all others, as I believe—although among these, the <term>Equestrian Order</term> <note>the Knights</note> of the Swabian, Franconian, and Rhenish regions seem to excel in liberty, since they are <term>Immediate</term> <note>subject only to the Emperor without an intermediate lord</note> to the Emperor and the Empire. <note>See Boccalini’s *Parnassus*, book 1, chapter 8.</note>

III. There is a noble debate: whether children born before the father received his Nobility become Noble themselves? And thus, do they enjoy the Privileges of Nobility? Following Molinæus <note>Charles Dumoulin, a French jurist</note> and Costalius, I would deny this. <note>Molinæus on the *Customs of Paris*, section 13, gloss 1, at the end; Costalius on the *Digest*, book 5, "On the status of men".</note> This is supported by the Code, "On their own and legal heirs" <note>Book 6 of the Justinian Code</note>; for they were neither born noble nor made so. For Nobility is a per<meta>catchword: personal</meta> <note>The catchword "sonales" completes the word "personales" (personal) on the next page.</note>

It also addresses the legal question of whether ennoblement applies retroactively to children born before a title was granted.</summary>

<meta>This page continues the legal discussion regarding the transmission of noble status, specifically addressing how nobility relates to inheritance, the purchase of land, and judicial rulings.</meta>

## Concerning Law and the Orders of Citizens.

...personal, inherent to the person, and does not usually pass beyond them. However, a son born afterward is part of the paternal substance, and certainly in terms of nobility. It is therefore advised that in a **Privilege** <note>A legal grant of special status or rights.</note> mention should be made of children already born. In a **Fief** <note>Land held under the feudal system in exchange for service.</note>—even a new one—the son succeeds, even one who was born before the land was acquired; but in that case, respect is given to the time of the father's death, and he who is the son at that time is also the successor to the Fief. But **Nobility** follows the blood: one does not "succeed" to the right of Nobility as one does to a Fief or a Kingdom, for a son does not possess that right while his Father is still alive. Nor does law 5 of the *Digest* <note>original: "ff." — The *Digest*, a massive collection of Roman legal jurists' writings compiled under Emperor Justinian.</note> regarding Senators stand in the way. For that status is granted by a singular privilege to the sons of senators; Nobility, however, descends from birth or by formal declaration.

IV. The following question can also be treated here: Does a **Peasant** <note>original: *Rusticus* — referring to a commoner or someone of the non-noble agrarian class.</note> who buys a noble Fief (about which see *Tuschus, letter F, conclusion 142*) become Noble? This is denied, since things are added to people, not people to things. <note>See Hippolytus a Collibus in *On Nobility*, thesis 28; David Argente, *Select Notes*, folio 226b, etc.</note> All "Political Nobility" depends on the will and power of the one who ennobles. <note>Vultejus, book 1 *On Fiefs*, 8; Niellius, *Feudal Disputations* 1, thesis 7, letter C; see also Dr. Bocer’s treatise *On the Distribution of Fiefs*, chapter 3, and *On Investiture*, chapter 3, number 152, folio 339; Matthias Stephanus, *On Political Nobility*, chapter 4, number 16.</note> It is also cited in the single chapter, the verse "but the others," where Jacobus Cujas and others discuss Title 10, Book 2 of the *Fiefs*; and Marta’s treatise *On Clauses*, part 2, conclusion 30. Even an inferior Prince can grant a noble Fief. Thus, some believe that when someone receives a noble Fief from one who has the right to ennoble, they are ennobled by that very fact. There are certain estates, <term>Free Noble Estates</term> <note>original: *Freye Adeliche Güter*</note> in Swabia, which grant a privilege to the possessor as a consequence of ownership, giving them immunity and exemption from certain taxes, but not true nobility. Thus, a person who holds a Duchy is not always a Duke, Count, or Baron. Instead, they are called <term>Holders</term> <note>original: *Inhabere* — meaning they occupy the property without possessing the hereditary title associated with it.</note>. <note>Matthias Stephanus, part 2, book 1, chapter 6, number 32.</note>

V. But what if someone—without any secret collusion and in the presence of a proper legal opponent—is pronounced Noble by a court? Does that sentence create a right that applies to everyone? Niellius affirms this <note>*Feudal Disputations*, final, thesis 26, number 7</note>, as this is uniquely accepted in cases regarding a person's legal status. <note>*Digest*, law 25, *On the Status of Men*; laws 3 and 4, *On Detecting Collusion*.</note> But I do not see the same logic in both cases. Surely the "favor of liberty" <note>A legal principle where, in ambiguous cases, the law should lean toward a person being free rather than a slave.</note> is greater than the favor of Nobility. Furthermore, how can the sentence of a Judge make someone noble? Only the **Emperor** can confer nobility. Otherwise, they say that nobility is proven by common reputation. <note>Vmius, *Disputation* 18, number 13.</note>

VI. This is likewise disputed: Whether a common-born wife, marrying a Noble Man, acquires nobility—and whether this marriage takes away any of the privileges of nobility from the children born of it?

<summary>This page discusses the legal mechanisms of nobility, arguing that it is a matter of blood and Imperial grant rather than property ownership or judicial rulings. It specifically addresses whether commoners can become noble by purchasing noble lands or through marriage.</summary>

->### Dissertation II<-

I deny this latter point based on the reasoning of the law: <note>original: "Vaud. 2. quæst. ult. Papon. 5. tit. arrest. 3." — These are references to legal authorities Vaudus and Jean Papon.</note> I would offer a different answer if a Noble woman marries a man of common birth. <note>See Hippolytus a Collibus, thesis 36 and following; Jacob Cramer, *Disputations on the Institutes*, thesis 11; Matthias Stephanus, chapter 4, number 31; Pruckman, *On the Differences between the Sexes*, part 1, article 23; and others.</note> Although in some places "the Womb ennobles." <note>original: "nobilitet Venter" — A legal principle in certain regions where a child inherits noble status from the mother, contrary to the usual rule of paternal descent.</note> <note>See Papon in the place cited.</note> And even in France, the daughters of Kings were called Queens, even though they were married to Counts. <note>Girard, folio 681.</note> This applied even to Widows. <note>Folio 857.</note> Although otherwise, women are not usually accustomed to conferring titles. <note>John Owen, *Epigrams*, number 103.</note> 

However, it is very well known that into the higher canonical colleges <gloss>at the high religious foundations</gloss> no one is admitted unless they can prove their nobility from the maternal line as well, through a long series of ancestors. <gloss>Full-blooded knights who can prove their ancestry with shield and helmet.</gloss> <note>See also the *Book of Fiefs*, title 10 and 29; and Matthias Stephani, *On Political Nobility*, chapter 4, number 44; and Scheplitz's commentary on the customs of Brandenburg.</note> (Though one may doubt whether that custom is valid, since a rule is pronounced weak if it admits only Nobles. <note>Wehner, under the word "Custom" [Gewohnheit].</note> And even where such a custom exists, one decorated with the degree of **Doctor** <note>In this era, a doctoral degree often conferred a status equivalent to personal nobility.</note> ought to be admitted. <note>See Benius, book 2, chapter 30.</note>)

Nor is there any doubt that the nobility of sons is more famous and illustrious if, on the Mother’s side, distinguished nobility is also added. <note>Baldus on the code regarding fugitive slaves.</note> For every composite thing participates in both its matter and its form. <note>Tiraqueau, chapter 18, number 26.</note> So too in <gloss>Silesia as well, no one can exercise the Knight’s Law unless he possesses "Four Shields"</gloss> <note>original: "Vier Schildtig" — A requirement to prove four noble grandparents (paternal and maternal) to qualify for certain legal privileges.</note> <note>See the *Treatise on Knight's Law*, folios 1 and 40.</note> and many following pages. 

But concerning the legitimation of those who are born to a noble concubine, see Wesenbeck. <note>Legal Counsel 101.</note> And even **Justinian** <note>The Roman Emperor famous for codifying law.</note> arranged for a **pragmatic sanction** <note>A formal imperial decree.</note> to be promulgated by his predecessor, by which it was permitted for prominent men to join themselves in marriage to women of the stage: so that he himself could marry his **Theodora**, the lowest prostitute of the amphitheater—if **Procopius** does not deceive us in his *Secret History*. <note>A scandalous contemporary account of Justinian's reign that portrayed the Empress Theodora as a woman of extremely low moral character before her marriage.</note>

**VII.** A commoner adopted by a Noble does not become Noble for that reason, unless it is done by the special authority of the Prince. <note>Sigonius, folio 540; Camman, thesis 34; Vechner, number 22; and others.</note> And I have argued the same in my *Disputations on the Pandects*. Just as, conversely, a Noble giving himself in adoption to a Commoner does not cease to be Noble by that act. <note>Lord Bocer, *On Regalia*, chapter 2, number 44.</note> Furthermore, that literacy

It also discusses the legal impact of adoption on social status and mentions the controversial marriage of Emperor Justinian to Theodora.</summary>

# On the Law and the Ranks of Citizens.

That [legal] learning and the Doctoral degree, or the profession of law, should obscure nobility, no one but a barbarian would say. <note>original: "nemo nisi Barbarus dicet"</note> See the specific orations on this matter: namely by Mynsinger, added by Eberhard Speckhan to the *first century of his questions*; those by Wolfgang Gruning and Arnold von Reyger. *Also the inaugural oration of Marcus Lyclama à Nienholt, and Kirchner’s Oration on the Dukes and Knights of Germany, where he notes that 600 years ago, those promoted to Masters, Licentiates, and Doctors were considered noble. See Doctor Lans on Academies, folio 83; add to this* Bernhard Herzog, in the *Alsatian Chronicle*, *Book 6, Chapter 3.* To this point belongs what Spartianus relates regarding Didius Julianus: his great-grandfather was Salvius Julianus, who was twice Consul, Prefect of the City, and a Jurist <term>Jurist: A legal expert or scholar of law; in Rome, these were often of the highest social standing.</term>, which made him all the more noble. And in France, the status of the judiciary and the office of Advocate does not detract from Nobility. <note>Papon, Book 5, Title 4, at the end.</note> Indeed, the dignity of a Counselor there brings Nobility even to one of common birth. <note>Papon, in the same place, ruling 4.</note>

VIII. The order of Nobles, because of the fortune of war—in which it is most necessary for them to serve—is, more than others, subject to the risks of death. Sometimes, noble families are also extinguished by luxury and pride; for this reason, the rulers frequently grant the Privileges of Nobility to new lineages. <note>Kirchner, *Disputation on the Republic*, 12, thesis 3; Mr. Cluten, in his *Collection*, where he discusses those ennobled by the prince.</note> Examples of this occur everywhere in the histories of all nations and ages, especially concerning the Emperor Henry the Fowler. <note>Henry I (the Fowler), a 10th-century German king, is historically credited with expanding the knightly class to defend against invasions.</note> In the same way, Floris, Count of Holland, raised many to the height of the Nobility. <note>Vanderburch, in *Amadeus*, 4, folio 57.</note> Thus, the distinction of a Noble into "born" and "made" is not to be rejected. A **Born Noble** <note>original: "natus"</note> is one who has inherited the political nobility of his race from his parents through its creation. A **Made Noble** <note>original: "factus"</note> is said to be one who has acquired the same by the favor of a Prince. <note>Timpler, *Politics*, Book 1, Chapter 5, Question 9.</note> Thus the Roman Emperors, when almost all the orders of the Patricians <term>Patrician: The original aristocratic elite of ancient Rome.</term> had failed and the administration of the Republic had devolved to the commoners, and the Senate was chosen from them, they made Patricians out of the most prominent and worthy Senators. <note>Forster, *On Jurisdiction*, folio 41 and following; Casaubon on Suetonius’s *Life of Caesar*, folio 8, verse 3. Concerning the Patricians under the later Emperors, see Meursius's *Glossary*, folio 412.</note> And since it belongs to the Majesty of the Prince that all his household should be considered endowed with dignity: *The Fabian Code*, *Book 9, Title 78, Definition 4.* From this, in former times, almost all the officials and ministers of Emperors and Princes were distinguished with the Privileges of Nobility. Traces of this remain in the fact that some are still named today after such offices: for example, the **Marshal** <gloss>Marschalck</gloss>, the **Cup-bearer** <gloss>Schenck</gloss>, the **Seneschal** <gloss>Truckfäß</gloss>, and the **Steward** <gloss>Keller</gloss>. <note>Münster, *Cosmography*, Book 3, Chapter 21, at the end. These titles originally referred to household servants (like a stable-master or a wine-server) but evolved into high-ranking noble offices.</note> Indeed, they even say the Prince's cook is a Noble. Rodolphinus, *On the Dukes of Italy, number 31; who at number 68* extends this even to Princes of lesser rank than the Emperor.

<meta>catchword: IX. Thus</meta>

<summary>This page argues that high education and legal professions enhance rather than diminish nobility, while also explaining how rulers create new noble lines ("Made Nobles") to replace those lost to war or decadence.</summary>
<keywords>Nobilis natus, Nobilis factus, legal profession and nobility, Henry the Fowler, Roman Patricians, court offices, ennoblement, Didius Julianus</keywords>

->Dissertatio II.<-

### IX.
And so, one who is ennobled because of certain outstanding virtues is truly Noble. Indeed, Hippolytus, in his *Treasury of Nobility, thesis 59*, asserts that such a person is the source of true Nobility, but is not yet "truly" Noble himself: just as unity is not a number, but rather the principle of number. <note>This is a philosophical argument: the first person to be ennobled is the "starting point" of a noble line, while his descendants are the ones who fully inhabit the rank.</note> However, a **New Man** <term>New Man: Translated from the Latin *Homo novus*, a term used in ancient Rome for the first man in a family to serve in the Senate or hold high office.</term> who has legitimately acquired a reputation for Virtue and the name of Nobility is considered preferable to a lazy Noble. 

On this matter, Marius speaks excellently in the works of Sallust: *Compare me, a New Man, with their pride; the things they are accustomed to hear about or read, I have seen in part and performed myself. Now see how unfair they are; that which they claim for themselves based on the virtue of others, they do not grant to me based on my own,* etc. See Mr. Bocer, *On Regalities, chapter 2, number 46, etc.*; add also Arnisaeus, *Politics, book 1, chapter 12, folio 301*; Vechner, *Discourse on Nobility, chapter 3, number 17*; and Gilbert Cousin <note>original: "Gilbert Cognat."</note> in his *Adages* under "New Man." 

Similarly, a certain Moor responded to a Portuguese Noble: that the name the noble held, he had merely brought out of his mother’s womb; whereas he, though once unknown and of no name, had obtained his name, the favor of his Prince, and great dignities through his own brave deeds. *Botero, Memorable Sayings, folio 43.* It is not a virtue to be born Noble, but to make oneself Noble; and with this saying, Pope Urban IV used to excuse the lowliness of his own family. <note>Urban IV (r. 1261–1264) was the son of a cobbler and often faced disdain from the established Roman aristocracy.</note> *Botero, folio 60. b.* Pliny says in his *Panegyric*: *The condition of those who have earned the right to have noble descendants should not be worse than those who inherited it from their parents.* Indeed, Paul Busius, in his *Political Disputations, 2, thesis 23*, thinks a Nobleman outstanding in virtue is more "honorable," while a Commoner who is equally excellent is more "praiseworthy." 

In the meantime, however, the older a Nobility is, the more illustrious it is, as Matthias Stephani teaches in his *History, chapter 4, number 30.* Charles the Bald created "new nobles" because of the negligence of the old ones. *Fauchet, On Antiquities, book 9, chapter 14.* However, it is a most vain insanity for those who curiously inquire into genealogies—either their own or those of others—and claim ancestors from the Trojan Horse. <note>A satirical reference to families who invent mythical, ancient lineages to seem more prestigious.</note> *Selden, in the English Janus, folio 12 and 13; Boccalini, century 1, report 50.* 

It has always and everywhere been the custom that the poor and those of humble birth are ennobled for their good merits, and so Joseph, Daniel, and Esther were ennobled. *Richter, Economic Axioms, 124.* Divine grace is not bound to a single family or century, nor is nature so exhausted that she cannot still produce people similar to those who once brought nobility to their lineage. As I said in my *Treatise on the Rights of Majesty*, those born of humble parents who legitimately aspire to higher things are not to be blamed. *Mexia, 2, chapter 36.* But I say "legitimately and soberly": for unless this is the case, that which is made quickly perishes quickly. And we see new families of this sort vanish and lose their dignity soon after the death of the first person to acquire it. The French call them **mushroom nobles**. <note>original: "les Nobles de potirions." This is a pejorative term for families who rose to status too quickly, lacking deep "roots" in the land or history.</note>

### X.
That species of Nobility which arises from the splendor and magnifi-

It notes that while ancient lineage is traditionally more prestigious, rulers have always ennobled commoners for service, though "upstart" families often struggle to maintain their status after the first generation.</summary>

# On the Law and the Orders of Citizens

...is commonly judged by license, it is rightly not admitted. Jean Bodin, *On the Republic, book 3, chapter 8, folio 566*; Arnisaeus, *chapter 12, folio 298 and following*. For what is more absurd or more destructive than to measure dignity by profit, rank by money, or Nobility by wealth? For these things are often given and taken away by theft and robbery, or by the lavish handouts of Tyrants to the very worst of people, or by mere chance and the recklessness of fortune (from which, some think, "Fortunes" <note>original: "Fortunas"</note> derive their name). See Richter, *Economic Axioms, 132*. 

There is also that greatest of controversies: whether the practice of **Commerce** <term>Commerce: The act of buying and selling goods for profit, often called "mercatura."</term> offends the splendor of Nobility? Bodin affirms this in *On the Republic, book 3, chapter 8*; as does Zieritz in *Treatise on the Change of Republics, folio 24*; and Boccalini in *Reports from Parnassus, 2, report 39*. Jacob Curtius denies it in *Conjectures, volume 1, book 2, chapter 29, at the end*. The opinion of those who take into account the diversity of the Region and the Republic seems more worthy of approval. Keckerman, *Politics, book 1, chapter 14, folio 243, and Practical Disputes, 31, problem 8*; Matthaeus Stephan, *noted chapter 6, number 5, etc.*; see Camman, *Dispute 4, thesis 34 and following*; Zieritz, *Dissertation on the Change of Republics, folio 22, etc.*; Lather, *book 3, chapter 13*; Scheplitz, *part 4, title 4, paragraph 1*. 

Kirchner teaches that commerce ought to be left to the commoners, *Dispute 13, thesis 1*. In ancient times, soldiers were not allowed to trade, nor were Merchants allowed to serve as soldiers. Guillaume Forner, *Select Matters, book 2, chapter 3*. Ludovico Tubero, a sufficiently good writer of the events of his own time, throughout his works and in *Commentaries, book 7, toward the end*, most sharply attacks the Venetian Patricians who, having neglected military matters, practice Commerce. And yet, they possess nothing on the mainland <note>original: "in partibus continentis," referring to the *Terraferma*, the Italian mainland territories of the Venetian Republic.</note>. Grasser, *Travels, folio 450*. Therefore, they are compelled to practice Commerce. Add concerning the Venetian Nobility, *Venetian Glory* <note>original: "Venediger Herrligkeit"</note>, *folio 43 and following*; see also concerning the Venetian Nobility, Paruta, *book 2, chapter 1, folio 283*. 

However, it is shameful for a Noble to sell **at retail** <note>original: "en detail"</note>. Truly, agriculture does not offend Nobility. Guido Papa, *Decisions, 41*. Whether they may brew beer is examined by Schrader, *Counsels, 44, volume 2*. 

Natural, illegitimate sons born of a Noble father are not even legally considered the sons of Nobles, because they do not descend from a noble spring, but from tainted blood; even if the mother was also noble. For as the cause is, so is the effect: that union is considered shameful, and therefore so is the offspring. Indeed, not even "Bastards" who are legally legitimized attain the nobility of their parents. Matthaeus Stephan, *chapter 4, number 38*. For it is presumed that the Emperor always disapproves of such illicit unions, and consequently does not grant Nobility to those not legitimately born. 

Hence, they do not carry paternal coats of arms <note>original: "insignia"</note> unless another practice is observed by custom. In France and Britain, those born out of wedlock carry a transverse bar <note>the "bar sinister"</note> on the paternal arms, from the left side to the right; hence the German proverb, <term>a stroke through it</term> <note>original: "ein strich dardurch," implying something is crossed out or cancelled.</note>. See Vechner, *Discourse on Nobility, chapter 3, number 23*. "Barre" is a Gallo-French word and signifies a barrier or a bolt.

It also details the legal exclusion of illegitimate children from noble status and their distinct heraldic markings.</summary>

### Dissertation II.
N 3
<meta>catchword: For</meta>

The French use the term *Barra* <note>original: "Barra"</note> even when writing in Latin. It is the custom in France that the sons of Kings—whether they are natural sons <note>meaning born out of wedlock</note> or younger sons born after the first-born—place a transverse "Bar" across their insignia, <gloss>a beam or stripe through the coat of arms</gloss>. Thus the Prince of Condé <note>A cadet branch of the French royal house.</note> bears the French lilies <term>fleur-de-lis: the stylized lily symbol of the French monarchy</term>, but they are restricted by such a bolt or bar. It is for this reason that when people at a certain banquet cheered for him by shouting, "*Bar à bas!*" <note>meaning "Down with the bar!"</note>, it was treated as a crime of High Treason, as if he were being encouraged to aim for the Kingdom itself by removing the mark of his secondary status. *Barre*, however, is a Germanic word and signifies a "barrier" <note>German: *Schrancken*</note>; and from this comes the phrase "to run the bar" <note>German: *barr lauffen*</note>, as if running within the barriers of a contest. Nevertheless, in Tuscany, bastards are admitted to noble ranks. *Afflictis, On the Right of Protimisis, folio 73.*

XI. Finally, a warning is prescribed for the descendants of the Noble class: they must avoid these three things as if they were the treacherous rocks of Scylla: (1) **Idleness and laziness**, by which they fall away from the virtues of their ancestors. (2) **Tyranny and pride**, which Aristotle says are the particular vices of the Nobility <note>Aristotle, *Rhetoric*, book 2, chapter 15; and Sallust, *The Jugurthine War*</note>. These vices lead them to oppress their subjects and despise those of lower rank, which eventually incurs the hatred of God and all other social classes. (3) And finally, **mutual envy and hidden feuds**, by which they eventually destroy both themselves and their rivals, accelerating the ruin of their families. Keckermann <note>Bartholomaeus Keckermann (1572–1609), a German philosopher and theologian.</note> lists other reasons why the children of Nobles and great men are sometimes prone to harm. *Practical Disputes 21, problem 12, and Dispute 27, problem 18.* Generally, those whom we call Patricians <term>Patricians: the elite ruling class of ancient Rome or similar urban aristocracies</term> are in some way less kind than the rest of humanity. *Marcus Aurelius, To Himself, folio 168.* Others still use the saying of the most ancient comic poet: *Aristophanes, The Frogs, folio 269.*

->*For how could he not be noble,*<-
->*Who knows only how to womanize and drink?*<-

But truly, for those who lack excellence of character, the fame of their lineage is more of a disgrace than a praise. It serves only to ensure they cannot hide their failings; they become a laughingstock for the common people if they degenerate. Sallust <note>A Roman historian known for his critiques of political corruption.</note> teaches excellently why and for what purpose the portraits of ancestors should be kept before the eyes of children when he says: "I have often heard that Quintus Maximus and other illustrious men of our state were used to saying that when they looked at the portraits of their ancestors, their hearts were most vehemently fired with a passion for virtue. It was not the wax or the physical likeness that held such power, but the memory of great deeds that kindled a flame in the breasts of these excellent men, which could not be quenched until their own virtue had matched the fame and glory of their fathers." If, however, Nobles boast of their brave ancestors while possessing none of their virtue, then that happens which Palingenius <note>Marcellus Palingenius Stellatus, a 16th-century poet.</note> sang of the Romans:

<meta>This page continues the discussion on the decline of the nobility, shifting from moral failings to the historical origins of noble privileges and the symbolic meaning of heraldic coats of arms. It follows the warning that noble status is easily lost through vice and idleness.</meta>

# On Law and the Ranks of Citizens.

> *For as soon as sluggish descendants arose,*
> *The Empire being already won, and in more prosperous years,*
> *Having pursued pleasures, light games, and luxury,*
> *They began to degenerate from their fathers; and then*
> *Nobility, loathing such men, withdrew*
> *Straightway to the gods above: until, with the growing*
> *Luxury and vice of the descendants, the pride of Rome was destroyed.*

The same thing occurred in the Constantinopolitan Empire <note>The Byzantine Empire</note>. For Salomon Schweigger <note>A German traveler and clergyman who visited the Ottoman Empire in the late 16th century.</note> graphically describes the pride, luxury, and tyranny of the Greek nobles, and the oppression of their subjects, in book 3 of his *Travels*, chapter 43. He argues that this was the reason why the greater part of them is now oppressed by the foul yoke of Turkish servitude. Time will tell what remains for the rest, and what awaits those nobles who suffer from similar vices in other regions of the world. Concerning the degenerating French nobility, see *The Cabinet of France* <note>original: "le Cabinet de la Franc."</note>, part 2, from the beginning through many pages. To counter these "diseases" of the noble order, equestrian games or tournaments <gloss>German: *Turnier*</gloss> were once established. Vechner, in *On Nobility*, chapter 8, number 4, folio 184, etc., suggests that they should be brought back into use for that very purpose, as does Kirchner in his *Oration*. In Portugal <note>original: "Lusitaniâ"</note>, the nobles do not have jurisdiction. Conestaggio <note>An Italian historian of the wars in the Low Countries and Portugal.</note>, folio 406. And formerly, they were almost like subjects, clients, or dependents. Girard, folio 624, volume 1. Later, when they abused their power, rebellions often arose against them. Concerning the *Jacquerie* <note>A 1358 peasant revolt in France; the text spells it "tacquerie".</note> against the nobles in France and England, see Froissart, book 1, chapter 182, and book 2, chapter 74. Regarding the nobles of his own century, see Suso <note>Henry Suso, a 14th-century German mystic.</note> in *The Nine Rocks*, chapter 17, folio 380.

XII. The privileges of the nobility are specific rights granted to those who have served the Commonwealth well. Vechner, *On Nobility*, chapter 7, number 18. Matthaeus Stephani reviews these laboriously throughout chapter 5 of his *History*. Danaeus treats them more succinctly in *Politics*, book 2, chapter 6, folio 123. All these matters, however, must be judged according to local use and custom. Nobles also possess certain signs of honor that distinguish them from the common folk; these are like hieroglyphic letters, as Vechner notes from Tholosanus <note>Petrus Gregorius Tholosanus, a French jurist.</note> in chapter 7, number 19. For their origin, see Claude Fauchet’s *Treatise on the Origins of Knights*, etc., book 1, chapter 2; Pasquier, book 2, chapter 11, at the end of folio 220; Welser in *The History of Augsburg*, book 4, at the end; Tuschus, under the letter A, conclusion 516; and Thessaurus, decision 270. To these insignia I add the titles by which nobles are distinguished from the common people for the sake of proper recognition. Vechner, in the aforementioned chapter 7, number 20. Insignia are sometimes seen as magical or fateful. Campanella, in *The Spanish Monarchy*, chapter 30. Paracelsus <note>A famous Swiss physician and alchemist.</note>, in his *Prognostication*, says of the three French lilies <term>fleur-de-lis: the lily symbol of the French crown</term>: <note>original German: "die magica hat dich recht bezeichnet"</note> "Magic has truly marked you." Most frequently, these symbols denote some outstanding deed: thus it is said of the coat of arms of Austria. Guilliman, in *The Habsburgs*, folio 6. And as for why five shields are inscribed on the shield of Portugal <note>These are the *Quinas*, representing the five Moorish kings defeated by Afonso Henriques.</note>, see the author of the *Political Treasury*, part 1, folio...

## Dissertation II.

<margin>1. folio m. 86. Spanish relations.</margin> I wrote in my treatise *On Ambassadors* <note>original: "de Legatu"</note>, specifically in the section concerning Heralds. Coats of arms consist of Shields and Helmets: this is to indicate that Nobility has its origin in the exercise of arms. Why nobles have open helmets, while others have closed ones? This is not without some doubt. Certainly, closed helmets are not suited for war and are added only for the sake of appearance; in the past, open ones were only opened so that they could provide visibility <note>original: "lumen," literally "light"</note>; today, they are opened more fully for the sake of decoration.

<image-desc>Four woodcut illustrations of heraldic helmets (galeae) shown in profile and three-quarter views. The first two on the left are closed helmets with vertical and horizontal bars/slits; the third is a barred visor helmet; the fourth on the right is an open-faced helmet with decorative scrollwork.</image-desc>

XIII. There is also another type of Nobility, called "Temporary" because it does not pass to heirs. Thus, the **Counts Palatine** <term>Comites Palatini: high-ranking officials of the Holy Roman Empire with special legal privileges</term> are noble, as were those who once enjoyed the honor of the **Patriciate** <term>Patriciatus: a high rank in the late Roman and Byzantine Empires, later used for urban elites</term>. (.. which dignity was once called an honorary consulship; it was not a Magistracy, but the name of an order. *Law 3. Codex on consuls. Cassiodorus, Various Letters, book 6. Suidas in the Collection.*) Likewise, the Knights, who are commonly called **Golden** <note>original: "Aurati," referring to the gilded spurs worn by these knights, often translated as Knights Bachelor or Imperial Knights</note>. *Botero, On the Origin of Cities, book 3, chapter 31. Busius, political disputations 2. thesis 27.* regarding whom one may read extensively in Pietro Calefato, *Treatise on Equestrian Dignity*. Pasquier, *Researches*, book 2, chapter 13, folio 213. Fauchet, *Treatise on Knights*, and add the author of *Research on the Greatness of the Kings of France*, book 3, chapter 8. Martin Crusius, in the *Supplement to the Swabian Annals*, folio 89. see, however, Freher’s notes on Peter of Andlau, folio 183. b. at the end. The dignity of these knights was once great, such that even the most Illustrious Princes did not shrink from it. *Lord Lansius in the dedicatory preface (.. to the Most High Prince Louis Frederick, Duke of Württemberg) prefixed to his orations.* To this type of Nobility may be referred the fact that Doctors and retired Professors of Law are commonly said to acquire nobility. *Lord Doctor Halbritter, oration on the Privileges of Doctors. Matthäus Stephanus, on Political Nobility, book 4. number 12. Arithmaeus, trial 1, disputation 7. corollary 1.* In this matter, it is noted that the condition of Doctors of Law is better than that of Doctors of Medicine, as observed by Antoine Favre, book 9 of the Code, title 28, definition 9 and following. see, however, Matthäus Stephanus, in the cited chapter 4, number 10. Lord Lansius, *The Academy*, folio 79. In France, those who through Jurisprudence obtain the dignity of Senator and Royal Councilor become noble by that very fact; indeed, they even prepare nobility for their descendants, as is read in the decreed Rulings <note>original: "Arrestis," legal precedents or judgments</note> of the Parliaments. *Papon, title 11, ruling 12.* In certain Cathedral Churches, nobles and Doctors are admitted to **Canonries** <term>Canonicatus: administrative and religious offices within a cathedral chapter</term> with equal right.

<meta>catchword: Freher.</meta>

<summary>This page explains the heraldic symbolism of helmets as a mark of military origin and distinguishes between hereditary nobility and 'temporary' nobility granted to officials, knights, and university doctors.</summary>

-># On Law and the Orders of Citizens #<-

Freher’s notes on Peter of Andlau, folio 195, verso. I omit other privileges of Doctors; Dr. Halbritter in the aforementioned speech and Matthäus Stephanus in his special treatise *On Literary Nobility* <note>original: "d. Nobilitat. litterariâ"</note> list them at length. I also dealt with this matter in my third treatise on Academies. It is not the same for those who, devoted to the study of wisdom and speculative philosophy, leave the assembly of men and pursue leisure; although they may be filled with distinguished virtues, they are not easily counted among the Nobles. Hippolytus a Collibus, *On Nobility*, thesis 13; Matthäus Stephanus, *On Political Nobility*, chapter 4, number 9. For Nobility is a civil matter, and therefore one can only attain it through civil disciplines—namely, either the gown <note>original: "togatâ," referring to the legal or administrative profession</note> or the military.

XIV. Soldiers too, because of the extreme necessity of arms, are considered to be of a more eminent condition. Here it is asked: Can the soldiers of our time rightfully and deservedly use and enjoy the privileges granted to the soldiers of old? I affirm this along with the illustrious Enenckel, *On the Privileges of Soldiers*, chapter 3; Dr. Bocer, *On the Law of War*; Forster, *On Jurisdiction*, folio 253; Graevaeus, conclusion 118; and Ayala, book 3, chapter 7. But Juvenal, in his last Satire, falsely mocks the excessive license and privileges of Roman soldiers. Although soldiers once constituted a separate order, it is more beneficial to the State for all fit men to serve; otherwise, as happened in the Roman and Greek Empires, it accelerated their downfall. Regarding that privilege where soldiers were once permitted the faculty of contracting marriage without any formality—<term>A Landsknecht’s Marriage <note>original: "Ein Landtsknechts Ehe"</note>—a traditional term for an informal or common-law marriage associated with mercenary soldiers</term>—Accursius says this has rightly been abolished today and reduced to general law (see the law *from Caligatus* <note>original: "l. à Caligato," referring to a law named after the military boot, the caliga</note>). See however the New Constitutions <note>original: "Novell."</note> 117, chapter 4. Consult Lipsius’s commentary on Tacitus’s *Annals* 14, number 75, where he states that soldiers formerly did not have wives. Above all others, the privilege soldiers have in the making of a Will <note>original: "Teftamenti factione"</note> is illustrious; I extend this even to frontier soldiers <note>original: "milites limitaneos"</note>, against Gail, but with Cujas, Gothofred, and Dr. Bocer. Other military privileges are listed by the illustrious Enenckel, Claude Coterau in his treatise *On Military Law and Privileges*, and Arumaeus, volume 3, discourse 18. Whether the military order is to be preferred to the literary order is also called into controversy. I am not speaking here of the common soldier, but the political soldier; nor of schoolteachers, but of political scholars. I judge the dignity of both to be equal, unless a present necessity makes the condition of one more noble: for then, the one whom present circumstances more urgently require is to be preferred. See the most famous Dr. Lansius, in his consultation on the contest of prerogatives between soldiers and scholars; Camerarius’s *Meditations*, book 3, chapter 68; add Guazzo’s *Dialogues*, 6. Although others argue that the virtue of the gown is by nature superior to military virtue, [it is...]

### CHAPTER XI.

## On Subjects of a Lower Condition.

Concerning subjects of a more eminent condition, enough has been said thus far. But since it is impossible for a Republic to consist only of Nobles, the learned, and their like—as Tholosan says in *On the Republic, book 1, chapter 1, number 17*—it is necessary that Plebeians be present, who, partly through manual labor and partly through the industry of trade, provide nourishment and make the State <term>self-sufficient<gloss>original: αὐτάρκη (autarkē), a Greek term referring to a community that can meet all its own needs without outside help</gloss></term>. Therefore, a few points must next be touched upon regarding the inferior subjects. A manifold difference is found among them. The wisdom of lawmakers has always taken no less account of the Plebeians than of the Nobility in establishing a Republic. Even in the body, those members that seem viler should not be neglected, unless one wishes to risk the loss of health. For this reason, Romulus, Theseus, and Lycurgus divided their cities into Patricians and Plebeians, as Plutarch testifies. The German state, being composed of both Plebeians and Nobility (Tacitus, *On the Customs of the Germans*), was better instituted than that of the ancient Gauls, who had the orders of Druids and Knights—the former devoted to religion, the latter to military service—with no account taken of the Plebeians, who were held in the place of slaves. Caesar, *Commentaries on the Gallic War, book 6.*

II. Merchants are subjects who live by trade. Here, it is first asked: should trade be considered a just and legitimate way of sustaining life? This should be affirmed, following Junius, *Political Question 61*, and Keckerman, *Practical Discourse 28, problem 44*. Provided that monopoly is guarded against by cautious laws and their diligent execution. Lather, *book 3, chapter 12.* Trade is useful and honest in itself, as nature substituted it for the purpose of compensating for the scarcity of things. *Law 1, Digest, on contracting.*

# On Nobility

<margin>*Treatises on Buying and Selling*</margin> "Not every land produces all things" <note>original: "Non omnis fert omnia tellus." A quote from Virgil's *Georgics*, used here to justify the necessity of international trade.</note>; and this is so for the sake of greater harmony and communication between nations. Trade <term>Mercatura: the practice of commerce and merchant trade</term> fills this deficiency. However, since this class of men is prone to making gain shamefully—*Law 44 of the Digest, on the Edict of the Aediles*—good laws are needed here. This is especially true against those who are the first to sell goods which they have intercepted in the port; or as Persius says, they are "the first to snatch the pepper from the thirsty camel" <note>original: "Primi tollunt piper à sitiente camelo." From Persius, *Satires* 5.136. It refers to aggressive merchants who rush to meet caravans to establish a monopoly.</note> so that they may be the first to sell.

III. The Republic likewise cannot do without artisans <term>Artifices: skilled craftsmen and makers</term>. Tholosan, *On the Republic, Book 4, chapter 9. Arnisaeus, chapter 12, page 321.* And the more "liberal" <term>Liberal Arts: skills considered worthy of a free person, involving the mind rather than just manual labor</term> these arts are, the higher they are ranked: such as painters, goldsmiths, architects, and sculptors. Those dedicated to meaner or "crasser" trades are considered lower than these; for where a more liberal intellect is required, the art is greater and closer to the study of letters. Nevertheless, some privileges arise from necessity, and some from the excellence of the craft. Walchius considers the dignity of crafts differently in his *Commentary on the Small Farmer*, page 292. Wigel, in part 3 of *Know Thyself*, page 21, says that some crafts descend from the heavens and some from the earth, and that the former are more excellent. But of all subjects, the most useful and necessary are the farmers <term>Agricolæ</term> and shepherds <term>Pastores</term>. Pliny, *Book 18, chapter 2. Tholosan, Book 4, chapter 8.* Therefore, they are to be cherished and protected above all, and defended with studious care against all force and injury. Thus, in ancient times, scepters were shaped in the form of a plow. Pierius, *On Hieroglyphics, page 345b.* and also **the miners** and **the small farmer** <note>original German: "die Bergleuth / Kleinbaur."</note>. Hoennon recounts many more ranks in his *Political Disputations, 2, thesis 48, etc.* And should soldiers be preferred over farmers? Maximus Tyrius discusses this in *Dissertation 13 and following.* Citizens are likewise divided into tribes, classes, and centuries. Bulenger, *On Taxes, page 13.*

IV. From the number of citizens, most people exclude pantomimes, actors, stage-performers, and other such administrators of pleasures and corruptions. See, however, Alberico Gentili, *Disputation on Stage Actors.* Finally, it is also debated by political writers: Should executioners <term>Carnifex: the public executioner or headsman</term> be mixed with honorable citizens? Arnisaeus denies this, *chapter 12, page 303 etc.* Philipp Hoennon, *Political Disputations, 2, thesis 51.* See Hieronymus Magius, *Miscellany 4, chapter 16. Arithmaeus 1, disputation 8, in the corollary. Herodotus, Book 2, page 106.* Generally, the Christian world also considers it a dreadful thing to shed blood so frequently; and therefore, not only are the executioners themselves considered infamous, but also their children: nor can anyone marry their daughters without the mark of shame. But Gerhard disagrees, *Section on the Magistrate, number 308.* Add Kornmann, *On the Miracles of the Dead, chapter 19, throughout.* There was no professional executioner among the Israelites; instead, the people stoned those condemned, or the one closest to the King would kill the one guilty of death—
