## On Nobility

Because of the similarity of the subject matter, I shall add a chapter treating **Nobility**, taken from the lectures of my late, pious brother, **Johannes Georgius Besoldus**, <note>Johannes Georgius Besoldus (1580–1625) was a noted jurist and brother to the author, Christoph Besold.</note> which he once gave regarding **Royal Prerogatives** <term>Regalia: the rights, powers, and privileges belonging to a sovereign or the nobility</term> at the Illustrious College in **Tübingen**. This will perhaps be useful for a more abundant explanation of that illustrious subject. Some are recorded in the number of the Nobles, even others of a more eminent order, so much so that formerly the title of "Noble" was attributed only to Dukes, Counts, and Barons. *Chasseneuz, in Catalog of the World’s Glory, page 8, consideration 46. Paurmeister, Book 2, On Jurisdiction, chapter 11, number 12. Lehmann, in the Speyer Chronicle, Book 2, chapters 14 and 19.* Others, however, from the Nobility were bound to the Princes, and their labor was used in wars; they were called **Adelscalk** <term>Adelscalk: a German term for a noble servant or "noble thrall," though here implying a high-ranking ministerial</term> or **Edelknecht** <term>Edelknecht: literally "noble servant," referring to an esquire or a member of the lower nobility who served a higher lord</term>, that is, Ministerials or Feudatories. *Lindenbrog, in the Lexicon under the word Adelscalk. Krantz, in Metropolis, Book 3, chapter 11; Book 8, chapter 14; Book 9, chapter 4. Matthias Stephanus, Treatise on Nobility, chapter 4, number 10. Josua Nolden, On the Status of Nobles, chapter 1, conclusion 1, number 4.* We must deal rightly with this specifically so-called Nobility, which stands at the lowest grade with respect to Dukes, Counts, and Barons, and the privileges by which it excels others. *See Einsiedel on Royal Prerogatives, page 253.*

For the order of Nobility, by which Nobles or patricians are separated from the commoners, is very necessary in the society of human life and in a well-constituted Republic <term>Respublica: the state or commonwealth</term>, since it removes destructive confusion and joins the citizens to each other and to the Republic with a fitting logic and a decent bond. Indeed, there exists almost no people among whom some distinction and order of citizens is not found. *Bodin, On the Republic, Book 3, chapter 8.* Hence, "all good men" (says that greatest of orators, in his speech *For Sestius*) "always favor the Nobility, both because it is useful to the Republic that noble men be worthy of their ancestors, and because the memory of famous men who have served the Republic well ought to be valued by us, even when they are dead." <note>original: "omnes boni... semper Nobilitati favemus." A quote from Cicero's Pro Sestio, 9.21.</note> Nobility is truly the offspring of glory, the summit of human happiness, the pillar of honors, the fountain and ornament of dignities; it is not inelegantly described by **Peter Friz**, *Discourse on Nobility, at the beginning, volume 1, in the discourses of Arumaeus.* And certainly, Nobility rouses descendants to virtue by its own example: it lifts and inflames the dejected mind; it leads one toward the imitation of great men and a singular pursuit of virtue. "Nothing else is to be sought in the living nobility of one's lineage," (says **Jerome** in his letter) "except that Nobles are bound by a certain necessity not to [stray] from their ancestors..."

...they degenerate from uprightness. And although there are many who, endowed with no virtue of their own, rely solely on the dignities of their ancestors and a multitude of smoky images <note>original: "imaginumque fumosarum." This refers to the wax funeral masks of ancestors kept in the atriums of Roman noble houses, which became blackened by the smoke of hearth fires over generations; here it implies a hollow reliance on ancient family history.</note>, and boast of a vain nobility of birth; whose ancestors, the more illustrious they were, the more these descendants are devoted to sloth and full of arrogance, while they admire only the name of Nobility and look down upon others with a high brow. Yet, if the truth be told, we more often see that those born of distinguished lineage show an excellent specimen of Nobility from their tenderest years—from their "tender fingernails" <note>original: "à teneris... unguiculis." A Latin idiom meaning "from earliest childhood."</note>, as they say—and display the blood and splendor of their ancestors' lives. They show that the virtue received from their forebears has been passed down as if from hand to hand. Even if they do not keep that virtue entirely whole and untainted, they nonetheless retain certain most brilliant sparks of ancient sincerity, constancy, and fortitude, and sometimes even eloquence. To them, that passage from **Cassiodorus** <note>Flavius Magnus Aurelius Cassiodorus (c. 485 – c. 585 AD), a Roman statesman and writer who served under the Ostrogothic kings.</note> in his **3rd Book of Epistles** can be fitly applied: *The present vein of virtues has taught the praises of the ancients.* <note>original: "Præconia veterum, præsens docuit vena virtutum."</note> Hence **Plato** rightly said that the best and most magnificent treasure for children is the virtue and glory of their parents. Therefore, on the testimony of the same Cassiodorus in the **3rd Book of his Variae**, it is better recognized to have *chosen* a Noble than to have *made* a man fortunate; because the former guards himself through the deeds of the ancients, whereas the latter has no example to follow except what he himself has done.

II. And since the excellence of Nobility is so great that it does not perish with the man, but having passed the boundaries of mortality, it always blooms more beautifully, gains strength as it goes, and the further it progresses, the greater the reputation for dignity and venerable antiquity it acquires. To its pupils—unless they fail themselves, or are corrupted by a wicked education, or are driven in a different direction by the wind of vain arrogance—it provides an easy path to the immense advantages of life and the chief offices of the **State** <term>Respublica: literally "the public thing," referring here to the organized political community or Commonwealth</term>. Therefore, Nobles ought to strive to give the impulse of their minds toward better things and despise what is humble and base; lest, puffed up by pride or corrupted by luxury, they renounce the praises of their ancient line. Nor should they ever, on account of the eminence of their lineage, sinisterly prefer themselves to others whom **Virtue** and conspicuous character render noteworthy. They should take the utmost care lest they be a disgrace to their ancestors, and lest—though famous in lineage—they become ignoble in mind. Rather, let them think that a man is truly Noble and sublime only if he deems his Nobility complete when he disdains to serve vices; and displays the Nobility of his parents not by title alone, but most especially by virtue.

III. Furthermore, we ascribe the origin of Nobility to the **Law of Nations** <term>Jus Gentium: a concept of law in the Roman legal tradition, referring to laws and customs thought to be shared by all human beings across different cultures</term>. For indeed, nothing is found even in nature that does not either surpass others in nobility or is surpassed by another kind of thing. Thus, among various nations, there are various conditions and orders of men, while some are judged to be Nobles and others ignoble or plebeians. But according to each Regio- <meta>catchword: Regional-</meta>

## Dissertation II.

->O 3<-
<meta>catchword: about Sa-</meta>

According to the customs and opinions of different regions, Nobility is valued in various ways. Most people in the East, especially the **Turks**, attribute everything to virtue and the type of office they hold. <note>original: "Auger. Busbequ. in epistol. 1. Legat. Turcic." Referring to Ogier Ghiselin de Busbecq (1522–1592), an imperial ambassador to the Ottoman Empire whose "Turkish Letters" were a primary source for European knowledge of the region.</note> **Europeans**, however, today give much weight to blood and judge everyone by their births. Among the **Romans** of old, those were called Noble whose ancestors had been honored with the dignity of the **Curule Magistracies** <note>High offices in ancient Rome, such as Consul or Praetor, which granted the right to sit on a "curule chair," a symbol of magisterial power.</note>. <note>Citing Jean Bodin’s *Six Books of the Republic*, Johannes Rosinus’s *Roman Antiquities*, and Robert Glover’s *Treatise on Political Nobility*.</note> These offices included the Consulship, the Censorship, the Praetorship, and the Aedileship. Among the **ancient Germans** as well, nobility existed in a threefold distinction: they had the **Adelingi**, the **Frilingi**, and the **Lassi**—namely, the Nobles, the Free, and the Freedmen. <note>These terms represent the early Germanic social hierarchy: the *Adelingi* (ancestors of the word 'edeling' or 'noble'), the *Frilingi* (free men), and the *Lassi* (semi-free or servile class).</note> This is shown from **Tacitus**, **Adam of Bremen**, and others by my brother, **Christoph Besold**, in his *Discourse on the Equestrian Order in the Roman Empire*.

IV. But it is consistent with reason that few of these ancient German nobles survive. Their origin is mostly to be assigned to the **Romans** and **Franks**, who established Leaders and Soldiers in their camps and castles to govern provinces won in war and to defend them against enemies and the former inhabitants. <note>The author is tracing the "knightly" class to military administrators (Duces and Milites) stationed in late Roman and Frankish fortifications.</note> In subsequent times, they retained those fortresses by right of grant or lordship. Hence almost all of our local nobles are named after their castles; and they are addressed almost exclusively as "Soldiers," or **those of the Knighthood** <note>original: "die von der Ritterschafft"</note>. On this point, one should consult the cited discourse of my brother, section 4.

<margin>Decianus, *Responses* 66, Vol. 3, at the beginning, says that Nobility is a quality bestowed by a prince, through which someone is shown to be accepted above honest commoners.</margin>

V. I believe this **Nobility** should be described in this way: it is a dignity by which someone—through the authority of either a Prince or the Law, and because of their own virtue or the excellence of their blood—shines above other men of common condition. Others have proposed different definitions of Nobility, as appears from **Matthias Stephanus’s** *Treatise on Nobility* and the discourse of **Johann von Nolden** *On the Civil State of Nobles*. But whether my description better fits the subject at hand, let others judge.

VI. The author <note>In this legal sense, the "author" is the source or creator of the status.</note> of Nobility is the one who holds the highest rank in any Republic and acknowledges no superior. <note>Citing Petrus Antonius de Petra, Decianus, Gutierez, and Andreas Knichen.</note> For this role, in the **Roman Empire**, we recognize the **Roman Emperor**. As the Law says: *those who have been decorated with honorary codicils without administrative duties* <note>original: "qui sine administratione honorarijs condecorati fuerint Codicillis." Citing the Code of Justinian, this refers to nobility granted as a title of honor rather than as an active government post.</note> and elsewhere: *it shall be permitted, whenever it pleases our Majesty, to obtain the Patrician dignity.* <note>original: "liceat, quando huc nostra sederit Majestati, Patritiam consequi dignitatem."</note> Indeed, from him, as from a most clear spring, all streams of all dignities flow. <note>Citing Andreas Gaill and Knichen.</note>

<meta>This page continues the legal analysis of nobility, shifting from historical definitions to the legal source of noble status and its recognition across international borders, specifically focusing on the Holy Roman Empire.</meta>

### On Nobility

...*on the Saxon [Privilege of] Not Being Summoned, on the Right of the Word of Dukes, chapter 1, number 2.* <note>original: "de Saxon. non provocand. Iur. verb. Ducum." This refers to a legal privilege where subjects of certain princes could not be summoned to external imperial courts.</note> And from there, the bestowing of Nobility is to be referred to the **Royal Prerogatives** <term>Regalia: The exclusive rights and powers belonging to a sovereign, such as minting coins, waging war, or granting titles.</term> of Majesty. Rosenthal, *on fiefs, chapter 5, conclusion 2.* Sixtin, *on Royal Prerogatives, chapter 2, number 36, book 1.* While we attribute the power of ennobling to the supreme head of the Republic <note>In this context, "Republic" refers to the State or the Holy Roman Empire at large.</note>, we do not exclude law or custom, in which the presumed will of the Rulers resides. Otherwise, things would go very badly for those who excel others in ancient lineage but cannot produce any certain proof regarding the specific author of their dignity.

VII. And this Nobility, since it draws its origin from the customs of Nations <note>original: "ex Gentium moribus." Referring to the *Ius Gentium* or Law of Nations, a concept of law thought to be common to all human beings.</note> and owes its form only to **Civil Law** <term>Ius Civile: The specific laws of a particular state or city, as opposed to natural law or the law of nations.</term>, is extended even outside the territory of the one granting it. Thus, by the mutual consent of peoples, the dignity of Nobility is valid everywhere and is not limited by the borders of a single Province; rather, German Nobles in Italy, France, Spain, and Denmark, and the Nobles of these Provinces in turn in Germany, may obtain the privileges of Nobility. Anton Faber, *on the Code, book 9, title 28, definition 12, number 9.* Nolden, *in his discourse on Nobles, chapter 1, at the end.* Nolden, however, along with almost everyone else, asserts that Nobility belongs to Civil Law and is unknown to the Law of Nations; from this, he argues that if one were to act by strict law, it could be said that he who holds a dignity by the privilege of his own supreme Prince is not a Noble outside the territory of that grantor. <note>original: "arg. l. ult. ff. de Iurisdiction..." The author is citing various sections of the *Digest* (the 'ff' mark), a massive compendium of Roman law, to support these jurisdictional arguments.</note>

VIII. Whether the **Electors** <note>The high-ranking princes of the Holy Roman Empire who had the right to elect the Emperor.</note>, Dukes, and other Princes of Germany possess the right of conferring Nobility within their own Principalities is a matter of no small debate. This is affirmed by Schrader, *on Fiefs, part 10, section 1, number 34*; Rosenthal, *chapter 2, question 2, number 9*; Knichen, *on the Saxon privilege... chapter 1, numbers 36 and 63*; and Nolden, *discourse on Nobles, chapter 2, number 18*. This view is held both because of the sublime and royal law of territorial jurisdiction and the very extensive power belonging to the Princes of Germany in their own territories (which is almost equal to Imperial majesty), and because the faculty of making laws belongs to them. Furthermore, these Princes are considered to have acquired these rights—originally reserved to the Emperor—through very ancient custom.

Those who deny this include Bodin, *on the Republic, book 3, chapter 8*, and Sixtin, *on Royal Prerogatives, book 1, chapter 5, number 123*. Their reasons are: first, that the Royal Prerogatives of Majesty require a special grant; <note>Citing the legal principle that sovereign rights must be explicitly handed down rather than assumed.</note> and second, because certain rights are specifically granted to the Electors by His Imperial Majesty in the **Golden Bull**. <note>original: "Aurea Bulla." The decree of 1356 which served as a constitutional document for the Holy Roman Empire, detailing the rights of Electors.</note> Sixtin, *number 125.* I, however, although I would wish nothing to be taken away from the dignity and grandeur of the most Illustrious Princes of Germany, nevertheless think that they cannot easily claim for themselves the rights of conferring Nobility; especially since their power in their own territories is from the [Emperor]...

<meta>catchword: Impe- [likely "Imperatore," meaning Emperor]</meta>

It specifically examines the controversial right of German Princes and Electors to grant nobility independently of the Holy Roman Emperor.</summary>

## Dissertation II.

...[the power of a local Prince] may seem to differ greatly from the Imperial loftiness in the Empire, and it admits various restrictions; it does not encompass all things reserved for the **Caesarean height** <note>original: "Cæsareo reservata culmini." This refers to the "Imperial Reserve," powers held exclusively by the Holy Roman Emperor.</note>: since they can neither create **Counts Palatine** <term>Comites Palatini: High-ranking officials with the authority to act on behalf of the Emperor in specific legal matters, such as legitimizing children or appointing notaries.</term> nor appoint Scriveners or Notaries, nor erect Universities or public schools, nor confer the titles of Doctor, nor grant the poetic laurel to anyone. Ferrar. Montan *treatise on fiefs 1. chapter 6. verse 3. fief.* Furthermore, they are less able to impede the course of an appeal to the **Imperial Chamber** <note>original: "Imperij Cameram." This is the Reichskammergericht, the highest judicial body in the Holy Roman Empire.</note> by a local statute, unless they have been fortified by a specific privilege. Gail. *1. observation 135. number 12.* Mynsing *century 1. observation 14.* Nor can they even decree the commissions of cases, or appoint delegated Judges with a clause removing the right of further appeal, as Sixtinus relates, *said chapter 5. number 126. argument from law 1. of the Digest on those from whom it is not permitted to appeal.*

IX. And even if someone were to obtain the insignia of Nobility from a Prince, I would indeed consider that he could use the privileges of Nobles within the territory of the one granting them: however, I would hardly consider him to be a fully-fledged Noble. Rosenthal. *on fiefs chapter 2. number 9.* Matth. Stephan. *treatise on Nobility chapter 5. number 5.* For since a Prince of the Empire cannot ensure that someone enjoys the dignity of Nobility throughout the entire **Roman Empire**, it is certainly rightly said that he is not a true Noble even at home. *based on the argument of those things related by* Johann. Gars. *on Nobility conclusion 6. number 19.* Gutierez. *practical questions books 3. & 4 question 13. number 95. see however* Matth. Stephan. *on Nobility chapter 5. number 42.* Only the **Most Serene Archdukes of Austria** enjoy this prerogative: they can create not only Nobles, but also Counts and Barons (whose grandeur is prominent throughout the entire Empire), and can even establish new tolls by their own authority. This is apparent from the Privilege which Münster inserted into his *Cosmography book 3. chapter 406*, and Joachim Cluten reports the same *in his collection of daily matters, conclusion 25. at the end.*

X. Furthermore, the rights of Nobility are to be granted to those whom the ornaments of virtue prove worthy, and whom the insignia of honors decorate. *Chapter "Since in all things" on Elections; Law "And of virtues" 4. Code on status and images; Law "That gradually" section 1. Digest on duties and honors.* These are those who have earned these distinguished rewards either by arms and military virtue, or by letters and political prudence. For unless someone has ascended to the peak of Nobility through the ladder of a **military or civilian life** <note>original: "sagatæ vel togatæ vitæ." Literally the "cloaked" (military) or "gowned" (legal/civil) life.</note>; but instead has earned these insignia only by riches and money, or by outrages, or even by treachery or cruelty <margin>Matth. Stephan. *treatise on Nobility chapter 5. number 14.*</margin>—I do not consider him worthy of Nobility. Indeed, dignity granted to the wicked and the unworthy is not dignity. Sallust *in the Jugurthine War chapter 4.* And I judge those to be impious and the most abject of all who wish to be held as Nobles because of their crimes.

XI. Moreover, even if someone is conspicuous for their distinguished virtue, they cannot attribute the rights of Nobility to themselves by their own authority, so that not even...

<meta>catchword: so that not even</meta>

<summary>This page explores the legal limits of local Princes in granting noble titles compared to the Emperor, noting that only the Archdukes of Austria possess the unique privilege to create titles recognized empire-wide. It also argues that true nobility must be earned through military or civil service and virtue, rather than through wealth or crime.</summary>

# On Nobility

...so that neither the glory of military service nor long experience in warfare confers the rank of nobility, as Matthias Stephanus teaches in his work *On Nobility*, chapter 5, number 15 and following. On the contrary, whoever is led by blind ambition to pretend and boast that he is a Noble, and usurps the privileges of Nobles, incurs the **crime of forgery** <note>original: "falsi incurrit crimen." In Roman and Civil law, this refers to the crime of fraudulent misrepresentation or counterfeiting of status.</note>. This is supported by the law *eos* in the section *qui se pro Milite gessit* in the Digest *on forgery*; Johannes Plat in law 1, at the verse *item alii* in the Code *on dignities*, book 12; Guido Papa, decision 89, number 2; Thomingius, counsel 47, number 11, volume 1; Nicolaus Everhard the Younger, counsel 54, number 4, volume 1.

XII. Some, however, are made Nobles, while others are born so. <margin>Paragraph 4, Institutes *on the law of persons*.</margin> Stephanus, *on nobility*, chapter 5, numbers 1 and 46. Men are made Nobles when they obtain Nobility from the **Imperial eminence** <note>The supreme authority of the Holy Roman Emperor.</note>, either expressly or tacitly: Expressly, when the Emperor bestows the favor of his Majesty either by the pronunciation of solemn words or by the granting of diplomas. Tacitly, however, Nobility is conferred by "silent vote" when the Emperor's will is not expressed in distinct words, but is made manifest either by law, by custom, or by certain other signs.

XIII. The custom of conferring Nobility through **Imperial Warrants** <term>Codicillos: Official documents or letters patent issued by the Emperor's court to grant titles or privileges.</term> in the Caesarean Court is indeed very ancient; it was in force over a thousand years ago even under the Roman Empire, while the Emperor Zeno held the helm of the Republic. This is supported by the law *quoties*, in the section *viros* of the Code *where a Senator or most distinguished person*, in which mention is made of Nobility that is conferred on certain people through honorary warrants without the requirement of holding an administrative office or arriving at the dignity of one's ancestors. Eguinarus Baro, *on nobility*, book 3, chapter 2; Paurmeister, *on jurisdiction*, book 1, chapter 29.

XIV. Furthermore, nothing prevents the Emperor—or anyone who does not recognize a superior—from being able to create Nobles even outside the boundaries of his own Empire: since an act of **voluntary jurisdiction** <term>Iurisdictionis voluntariæ: Legal authority exercised in matters that do not involve a dispute between parties, such as manumissions, adoptions, or the granting of honors, which can often be performed outside one's home territory.</term> may be rightly exercised anywhere. This is argued from law 2 of the Digest *on the office of Proconsul*; law 7 of the Code *on emancipation*; Tiraqueau, *on Nobility*, chapter 6, number 26.

XV. Furthermore, Imperial grace is usually accustomed to confer, along with Nobility, honorary signs and arms, distinguished from the common people, which signify an open nobility. Cassanaeus in the *Catalog of the Glory of the World*, part 1, consideration 38, conclusion 27. Regarding the origin of these insignias, Welser treats this in his *History of Augsburg*, book 3, near the end. Otherwise, these insignias themselves (which are sometimes accustomed to denote some remarkable deed, and from which various outcomes are often expected by many) do not make a person Noble by themselves, since they are also granted to other men of lower condition. Gylman in the *Symphorema*, volume 1, part 1, book 30, number 79 and following. And these insignias are transmitted to all descendants; however, upon the death of the last member of a family, according to the custom received by Nobles, they are usually broken and buried with him. Rudinger, *observations*, century 5, observation 46. And the preservation of the family and a certain qualification is presumed where a testator mandates his arms and insignias...

<meta>The catchword "po-" suggests the next page begins with a word like "possit" or "posteros."</meta>

<summary>This page defines how nobility is acquired—either by birth or Imperial grant—and establishes that claiming noble status falsely is a crime. It also discusses the ancient tradition of Imperial warrants and the use of hereditary coats of arms, noting that these insignias are often symbolically destroyed when a family line ends.</summary>

# Dissertation II

...to be carried. Stephanus Gratianus, *book 2, forensic disputes, chapter 288, number 26.* Nolden, *discourse on Nobility, chapter 15, number 136.*

XVI. However, the **open helmet** is a hallmark of Nobility, and it distinguishes their rank from the insignia of others, to which closed helmets are attached. From this, it is easy to infer that those to whom the open helmet is granted—which is used in War and Equestrian games—have the right to participate in **tilting** <note>original: "hastiludio." This refers to jousting or combat exercises with a lance.</note>; others, however, who use a closed helmet that is useless for battle, cannot take part in these Equestrian exercises.

XVII. Furthermore, the **Crown**, with which a helmet is often adorned, was once worn only by the most prominent of the Noble order; today it is seen even in the insignia of men of a lower sort. Among the Romans, these crowns were given to brave soldiers and leaders, some of which were made of gold, some of leaves, and some were even called "Equestrian." Indeed, even in this regard, the magnificence of ancient Kings was observed when they brought forth Crowned Knights in their processions. Athenaeus testifies to this in *book 5*, describing the wealth of King Antiochus, who produced in a certain assembly three thousand Cilicians wearing golden crowns, besides a thousand Pisidian Knights and three thousand others, most of whom had golden crests on their helmets and golden crowns. Carolus Paschalius provides more details on these crowns in his work *On Crowns, book 9, throughout.*

<margin>On nobility purchased with money, see Einsidel, page 258.</margin> XVIII. Furthermore, these **New** or "artificial" Nobles are by no means to be despised, especially if they have prepared their way to this higher rank not through foul flattery or the spending of money, but through true virtue. They cannot seem unworthy whom the Prince has honored and to whom he has attested to have granted a benefit on account of their merits. Jason, *counsel 277, number 38, volume 2.* Menochius, *counsel 1003, number 77 and following.* Schneidewin, *on Fiefs, part 3, number 53.* Hence, I believe we should not listen to Hippolytus de Collibus, who says in his work *The Noble, number 59*, that being ennobled is the beginning of true nobility, but that such a person is not "truly" Noble. See Einsidel, *on Regalities, chapter 2, number 137, page 258.* Indeed, Marius in the works of **Sallust** <note>Gaius Sallustius Crispus, a Roman historian. This refers to a famous speech by Gaius Marius against the decadent aristocracy.</note> excellently rebukes the pride of those Nobles who adorn themselves only with the deeds of their parents, as if with borrowed feathers, when he says: *See how unjust they are; what they claim for themselves from the virtue of others, they do not concede to me from my own.* Hence, "New" Nobles are also admitted as Assessors to the **Imperial Chamber Court** <term>Cameræ Imperialis: The highest judicial body in the Holy Roman Empire, which heard cases involving the various states and imperial subjects.</term>, provided they otherwise conform to the regulations, as Nolden confirms by precedent in his *discourse on Nobility, chapter 6, number 49.*

XIX. Nobility is conferred **tacitly** when someone obtains the privileges of Nobility by the law itself and through a necessary consequence, even without an express declaration being added. Lord Bocer, *on Regalities, 6, 2, number 8.* Matthias Stephanus, *treatise on Nobility, chapter 5, number 21.*

XX. Of this sort is he who [is granted] by the Emperor, or by another [possessing] the rights of ennobling...

# On Nobility

<margin>See Von Einsiedel’s *On Royal Rights*, chapter 1, number 339, page 269 and following.</margin>...[having the power of ennobling], if someone is invested with a **Noble Fief** <term>Feudum Nobile; a land holding historically restricted to the aristocracy</term> with jurisdiction attached, they are judged to have simultaneously acquired true Nobility based on the presumed will of the grantor. André Tiraqueau, *On Nobility, chapter 7, whether a fief ennobles, number 14 and following.* Matthaeus Wesenbeck, *part 3, legal opinion 135, number 70.* Tapia, *in the chapter On the Constitutions of the Prince, chapter 4, number 120, at the end.* Matthaeus Stephanus, *Treatise on Nobility, chapter 5, number 28.* However, if this investiture was performed by a Duke or a Count, or if a noble fief was purchased from a commoner, it does not ennoble the possessor. Nolden, *chapter 5, numbers 44 and 45.* Unless a specific custom of that particular region applies. Nicolo Intrigliolo, *Century of Fiefs 1, question 31, number 25.*

XXI. What if the Emperor calls someone "Noble" in a **Rescript** <note>original: "Rescripto"; a formal written reply or decree from the Emperor</note> or in another manner? Does that mean they are considered part of the number of Nobles by that very act? I hardly think so, unless it is established by other arguments of certain knowledge and the Emperor’s earnest will. Since, in case of doubt, it is not presumed that the Prince wishes to overturn the disposition of the law in a single moment. *Law: "si quando" in the preface, Code on Inofficious Wills.* This opinion is approved by Tiraqueau, *On Nobility, chapter 6, number 24.* Gylman, *in the Symphorema, vote 30, number 102.* Although I do not deny that Nobility is proven by this assertion of the Prince, even if the **Patent** <note>original: "Diplomate"; the official certificate conferring rank</note> (which would otherwise be necessary to obtain that dignity) is not present. For it is one thing for someone to *become* noble, and another for their Nobility to be *proven*; and Nobility is more easily proven than granted, as Antonius Faber says, *in the Code, book 9, title 28, definition 15.* So much so that the Nobility of a lineage is demonstrated from ancient family documents, in which the title of "Noble" is attributed to some grandfather or great-grandfather. This is especially true if these documents were produced and presented before the Prince or his officials. For it should not be thought that anyone would arrogate to themselves a dignity in a public document that does not belong to them. Antonius Faber, *in the Code, book 4, title 24, definition 27.*

XXII. But although armed military service does not in itself make a man Noble—*based on the arguments of the law: "two" on Military Wills; the law "of works" in the Digest on the works of freedmen; and the law "if anyone," section "void," in the Digest on unjust, broken, or voided wills*—unless a Soldier has served in camp for 10 or 15 years, as Paris de Puteo suggests (who is cited by Lancellottus in *The Temple of All Judges, chapter 1, book 1, section 1, question 2, number 15*); nevertheless, if someone is appointed by the Prince to lead an army, Nobility is considered to have been tacitly conferred upon him, says Tiraqueau, *On Nobility, chapter 8, number 15,* and after him Matthaeus Stephanus, *On Nobility, chapter 5, number 25.*

XXIII. Likewise, if someone is honored with **Equestrian dignity** <note>original: "Equestri dignitate"; knighthood or the rank of a knight</note> by His Imperial Majesty for an outstanding display of bravery or the brilliance of other virtues, he simultaneously attains the rights of Nobility. This is passed down by Matthaeus Stephanus, *said chapter 5, number 26,* and **my Brother**, *in Politics, chapter 11, number 64,* based on the law *unique, Code on Equestrian Dignity.* For the understanding of this law, consult Lancellottus, *in The Temple of All Judges, title on Duels, part 3, conclusion 77.* The same is proven from the Imperial...

## Dissertation II.

<sig>P 2</sig>

...in those Codicils [letters patent] by which the Imperial Majesty, usually with the following words, uses in bestowing Equestrian splendor: "We, by our present Edict, and out of the fullness of our Imperial power, make and create you a soldier, or a **Golden Knight** <term>Eques Auratus; a knight of the Golden Spur, a title of honor usually bestowed by the Emperor, often carrying personal nobility</term>, and we decorate you with the military title and dignity, and we admit you to the Military State and order. We also distinguish you with the beauty of the military Belt and baldric, and with the garlands and coat of arms of the golden knighthood, and we gird you with the sword of courage, and we mercifully enroll and gather you into the number and company of other Soldiers. We decree that hereafter you shall be held and honored everywhere as a true Soldier, and you may and should, for the adornment of the dignity thus received, enjoy the use of gold chains, swords, spurs, garments, trappings or golden horse-ornaments, and all and singular other privileges, graces, and dignities which true and brave Soldiers, made and created by Us with a drawn sword, are accustomed to use." Further writings on this Equestrian dignity were left by Petrus Calefatus, Jurist, *in his unique treatise written on this subject.*

XXIV. Furthermore, it is beyond controversy that one established in the Episcopal dignity is Noble, because such a person is seen to possess the pinnacle of dignity and excellence, is compared to Dukes, and is called a counselor of the Prince. *Tiraqueau, On Nobility, chapter 14, number 4, at the end; Gratianus, Forensic Disputations, chapter 590, number 15 and following.* Indeed, it is consistent that the chief ecclesiastical figures of our reformed Religion, who have succeeded to the place of Bishops, though they may lack the title of "noble," are not deprived of the same privileges.

XXV. Moreover, by a certain necessary consequence, a ray of Nobility arises in those whom the **Imperial Household** <note>original: "Imperialis Comitatus"; here referring to the retinue or administrative staff surrounding the Emperor</note> illuminates, or whom the Prince allows to remain at his side, and who are privy to his secrets. *Law 1, Code on Domestics and Protectors; single law, Code on the Superintendent of Labors; Decianus, Response 58, number 23, volume 5; Tiraqueau, On Nobility, chapter 6, number 10.* Such are the Emperor's chamberlains, physicians, secretaries, and all those who perform honorable duties in the Imperial Court. *According to the law "nemo præfectus," Code on Dignities; Tiraqueau, On Nobility, chapter 37.*

XXVI. Among these, the Emperor's Councilors and the most distinguished Senators of the Court and the Imperial Chamber Court stand out—<term>the Imperial Court Council and the Assessors of the Imperial Chamber Court</term> <note>original German: "die Reichs Hoffrath / vnd Cammergerichts Beysitzer"</note>. *Based on the law "fœmina," 8, Digest on Senators; law "omnium," in the preface, Code on Testaments; law "Iudices," 12, Code on Dignities; Carlo Tapia, in his commentary on the rubric of the Digest on the Constitutions of the Prince, chapter 4, number 113 and following; Pruckman, Counsel 2, number 81 and following, book 1.* These men fulfill the roles of the ancient **Conscript Fathers** <note>original: "Patrum conscriptorum"; the traditional title for Roman Senators</note> and are the perpetual and robed Senators of the Empire. *Visitation Memorial, from the year 1570, section "dann obwohl."* For these indeed constitute one body with the Prince. *Law "quisquis," section [blank], Code on the Julian Law of Majesty; Law "Ius Senatorum," 8.*

<meta>This page continues the legal analysis of who qualifies as "Noble." The previous page concluded a section on the Equestrian order and knights created by Imperial decree. This page shifts focus to high-ranking officials, specifically legal counselors and Doctors of Law, debating whether their professional dignity confers the same social and legal status as traditional military nobility.</meta>

# On Nobility

*Chapter on dignity,* they shape his [the Prince's] mind and soul. *Chapter 'Felices', under penalties, in the 'Sext'* <note>The 'Sext' or Liber Sextus is a collection of Canon Law.</note> And they illuminate him like solar rays in the firmament of heaven, through both legal expertise and political wisdom. *Schrader, on fiefs, part 10, section 2, number 24.* For this reason, Conrad von Einsidel <note>Conrad von Einsiedel (1597–1668) was a German jurist and diplomat.</note> unfairly strips away the splendor of nobility from these Counselors. *Treatise on Royal Prerogatives, chapter 2, folio 147.* This is especially true since the notorious custom of the Imperial Court and daily experience argue against him; even in France, the King’s Counselors and Secretaries, as well as the most wise Senators of the Supreme Parliaments and their sons, are considered Noble and enjoy the privileges and immunities of the Nobility, as confirmed by legal precedents. *Papon, on judgments or arrests, book 5, title 4, arrest 4.*

XXVII. Doctors of Law <term>Iuris Doctores; those holding the highest academic degree in law, which carried significant social weight</term> are also enrolled in the class of Nobles by our legal commentators. *Menochius, on judicial discretion, Case 70, number 9.* *Tholosanus, Book 4 on the Republic, chapter 10, number 14.* *Petrus Fritze, on Nobility, conclusion 14.* This is because legal experts are called "Noble" in the law *book 2 at the end, Digest, on the excuse of Tutors,* and even "Most Noble" in *law 'providend' 7, Code, on pleading, and book 4, section 1 at the end, Digest, on the excuse of Tutors.* Because of this, they are admitted to the Senatorial dignity in the Imperial Chamber <note>The Reichskammergericht, the highest judicial body in the Holy Roman Empire</note> in the place of Nobles, especially if other suitable Nobles are not available. *Ordinance of the Chamber, part 1, title 3, paragraph 'desgleichen sollen'. Imperial Recess of Speyer, Year [15]57, paragraph 'weiter nach dem'.* Furthermore, today they are received into the Chapters of Cathedral Churches alongside Nobles. *Matthäus Stephanus, on Jurisdiction, book 3, part 2, chapter 14, number 90 and following.* Indeed, in the Metropolitan Church of Cologne, entry is open only to Princes, Counts, and Barons, or to eight Doctors of Theology or Law—with all other Nobles excluded. This is attested by *Nolden, in the cited discourse on Nobility, chapter 5, number 38.*

XXVIII. From this they infer that Doctors are permitted to display noble insignias <note>original: 'insignia'; heraldic coats of arms</note> even without a special permission from the Emperor. *Camman, on the Rights of Majesty, dispute 3, thesis 63 and following. Dr. Harpprecht, paragraph 'in servorum' 5, number 57, Institutes on the Law of Persons.*

XXIX. However, Conrad von Einsidel thinks that the texts cited earlier—which were primarily conceived regarding "nobility of the mind" <note>original: 'animi nobilitate'</note> and the excellence of their craft—cannot be conveniently applied to the legal Nobility being discussed here. *On Royal Prerogatives, chapter 2, number 140.* He does not accept the aforementioned assertion of the Doctors in their own cause, but states generally at *number 148* that in our age, Nobility is acquired only through an express privilege of the Prince or by prescription <note>long-standing custom/time</note>, but not by the granting of some office or other dignity. I, however, if the inquiry is regarding nobility of lineage and family, would easily agree with him. Yet, it is not only the splendor of arms, or the antiquity of families, or the express indulgence of the Emperor that brings about the privileges of Nobility; but additionally, it arises from the manifest decrees of our Law and the consensus of almost all the nations of Europe...

<summary>This page argues that Doctors of Law and high-ranking counselors should be legally classified as nobles, citing Imperial law and the exclusive customs of Cathedral Chapters like Cologne. It also presents the counter-argument of Conrad von Einsidel, who believes true nobility requires a specific royal grant rather than just professional merit.</summary>

<meta>This page continues Dissertation II, moving from the definition of academic "Doctors of Law" as nobles to the broader concept of "Literary Nobility." It addresses how nobility can be acquired through marriage and long-standing custom (prescription), while contrasting the legal standing of jurists with that of medical doctors.</meta>

## Dissertation II
->P 3<-

...there is a certain Nobility that arises not from Mars <note>original: "ex Marte"; referring to military service</note> but from art, not from arms but from letters, and this can be called "Nobility of the Gown" <term>Nobilitas togata; a social rank achieved through administrative, judicial, or academic service rather than through inherited titles or military conquest</term> or "Literary Nobility." From this, it is not unsuitably argued that those upon whom the highest honors in the science of letters have been publicly conferred ought to be counted among the order of Nobles—especially if they profess the art of Law, in which the safety of the Republic consists no less than in arms. (For certain authors, such as Tiraquellus in *On Nobility*, chapter 31, and Antonius Faber in *On the Code*, book 9, title 28, definition 9, exclude Doctors of Medicine from this status). This is also approved by Antonius Faber in the same title 28, definition 10, and Matthaeus Stephanus in his *Treatise on Nobility*, book 2, throughout.

XXX. Furthermore, Nobility is tacitly secured through Marriage, if a woman of common <note>original: "plebejä conditionis"; referring to the plebeian or non-noble class</note> status enters into marriage with a Noble man. *Law 'fœminæ' 8, Digest on Senators; laws 10 and the last law, Code on marriage; law 'Mulier', Code on dignity.* See also Vaudus, *book 2, final question*, and Antonius Faber, *Code book 9, title 28, definition 16*. For a wife is considered one flesh with her husband, joined with him in an inseparable habit of life; she shines with his brilliance and privileges. *Law 2, Code on Bishops and Clerics; final law, Code on injuries; Novel 105, chapter 2, in the preface.* Josias Nolden <note>A 17th-century German jurist</note> asserts in *chapter 6, number 53* that this ornament of Nobility even passes to a betrothed bride, though I doubt this, based on the arguments in *law 1, Digest on betrothals*. However, it is clear that the Nobility of a deceased man remains with his widow. *Law 'cùm te' 10, Code on marriage; law 'fœminæ' 8, Digest on Senators; chapter 'is qui', section 'Mulier', on burials, in the 'Sext'.* See also the *Milanese Decisions, decision 9, number 7, book 1*; Antonius Faber, *book 9, Code, title 29, definition 22*; and Vultejus, *Marburg Counsels, 17, number 49, volume 3*. Indeed, a widow, as long as she remains in her state of widowhood, retains the husband's domicile, his legal jurisdiction, his family, and all his privileges, unless they belonged to him solely by reason of a specific office. *Tiraquellus in the treatise 'When the cause ceases', number 108; and Zanger, on exceptions, part 2, chapter 1, number 57.*

XXXI. And in this regard, Faber <note>Antonius Faber (1557–1624) was a celebrated jurist known for his work on the Justinian Code.</note> considers the condition of Legal Counselors to be better than that of Physicians. *The same title 28, definitions 9 and 10.* For Doctors of Law obtain their privileges from common law by the prerogative of their dignity. Physicians, however—even those approved in cities by public decree, whether they teach or devote themselves to healing—enjoy only certain personal privileges by reason of their office (such as being immune from all public burdens). These privileges expire when they die and are not transmitted to their widows or children. *Gloss on law 'medicos' 6, on the word 'children', and Bartolus there, Code on Professors and Physicians.*

XXXII. Among the ways of tacitly acquiring Nobility, one may also count Prescription <term>Præscriptio; the legal acquisition of a status or right through long-continued, undisputed use over time</term>. This, however, only introduces Nobility if it has been established for a time exceeding human memory, as Tiraquellus explains more extensively in *On Nobility, chapter 14*, and Mascardus in *On Probations, conclusion 1094, number 2*. For immemorial time has the force of a public instrument...

It also highlights the legal superiority of jurists over physicians and mentions the acquisition of noble status through long-standing custom (prescription).</summary>

<meta>This page continues the discussion from Dissertation II regarding the acquisition and transmission of nobility. # On Nobility
->Page 118<-

...of the mind, and obtains the force of a title, privilege, and Imperial consensus. By the weight of time <note>original: "temporis suffragio"; referring to the legal weight granted by long-standing duration or custom</note>, not only Jurisdiction and the Fief <term>Feudum; a landed estate held on condition of feudal service and allegiance</term> are acquired, but even the regalia <term>regalia; the specific rights and powers belonging to a sovereign, such as minting coins or administering high justice</term> and all things which are otherwise not considered subject to prescription <note>prescription is the legal principle where a right or title is acquired by continuous use over a long period</note>, as Nolden teaches more broadly in his *Discourse*, chapter 14, number 128.

XXXIII. Thus far we have spoken regarding the Nobles who, though they were previously of the common <note>original: "plebeji"; referring to the non-noble or commoner class</note> rank, obtained the rights of Nobility by the silent or express approval of the Imperial Majesty. It follows that I should speak of that acquisition of Nobility which rests upon the brilliance of birth and is conferred upon children from their nativity. *Law 1, where Bartolus comments, Code on Dignity; Law 19, Digest on the Status of Men; Law 1, § 2, regarding Municipalities.* Menochius, *Counsel 1261, number 15, book 13.* This does not reject the Emperor as the clear author of the lineage; but it recognizes the immediate bestowal of this higher status primarily from nature itself. For just as the virtues of parents are often derived through offspring as if in a sprout—as Aristotle says in his *Politics*, book 3—and the vein of ancestral blood and virtue is presumed in the children. *By analogy to Law 'quasi' 31, § 21, Digest on the Aedilitian Edict.* Menochius, *On Presumptions, book 6, presumption 58, number 2.* Thus also Nobility, as if by hereditary right, is legitimately continued in all descendants for perpetual memory. *Cassan in 'Catalog of the Glory of the World', part 8, consideration 24.*

XXXIV. While I have said that Nobility is propagated and descends to children, it can easily be understood that it neither ascends to parents nor can it progress in any way to collateral relatives <note>siblings, uncles, or cousins who are not in the direct line of descent</note>. For just as a fief regularly does not pass to parents and other relatives of the side-line <note>original: "transversæ lineæ agnatos"; male-line relatives who do not descend directly from the title-holder</note>, *Book 2 of Fiefs, 20; Book 1 of Fiefs, 8; Vultejus, 9, number 101 and following, book 1*, so also the privileges of Nobility are not shared with parents or brothers. *Tiraquellus, On Nobility, chapter 17; Hippolytus a Collibus, In Nobility, conclusion 35; Conrad von Einsidel, On Regalia, chapter 2, number 157.* Although, by the dignity of the children, parents are greatly honored. *Novell 81, chapter 1, § 1; Novell 105, chapter 2, at the beginning.*

XXXV. In this propagation, however, only the dignity of the **Father, not the Mother**, is considered. *Law 'exemplo' 63, Code on Decurions; Law 1, Code on Dignity; Bodin, Six Books of the Republic, book 6, chapter 6.* This is because children, in matters pertaining to Civil Law, follow the rank of the father when they are legitimate. *Law 29, On the Status of Men; Law 'eos' 22; Law 'nullos' 36, Code on Decurions.* Just as the Mother herself follows the family, kin, and dignity of her Husband, and is elevated by his honor. *Law 13, Code on Dignity; Novell 22, chapter 36.* And leaving behind the kin of her origin, she is identified by the surname of her Husband. *Conrad von Einsidel, as cited above, number 158.* Therefore, children born from a father of common rank <note>original: "ignobili Patre"</note> but a noble Mother are commoners. *Bocer, On Regalia, chapter 2, number 24; Camman, On the Rights of Majesty, thesis 31 and following.* Even if this common status of the children is less obscure and less lowly because of the Mother’s nobility. *Tiraquellus, On Nobility, chapter 18, number 26.*

XXXVI. But what about children who, before the Father obtained Nobility—

<summary>This page explains how nobility is legally transmitted, emphasizing that status is inherited from the father rather than the mother and descends to children without extending "upwards" to parents or "sideways" to siblings.</summary>

### Dissertation II.
->Page 119<-

...attained that dignity and saw the light <note>original: "aspexêre lucem"; an idiom for being born</note>, should they become Noble and enjoy the privileges of Nobles? This is a point of great debate. Those who affirm this cite a very clear text of our Law, in the law *Senatoris*, section *ff. de Senator.* <note>referring to the Digest of Justinian, Book 1, Title 9</note>, in which the legal consultant Ulpian grants Senatorial eminence to one who was adopted by a Senatorial father before the father held that dignity. This is supported by *Tiraquellus, on Nobility, chapter 15, number 7; Anton. Faber in the Code, book 9, title 29, definition 24, number 3; Matth. Stephanus on Jurisdiction, book 2, part 1, chapter 7, m. 2, number 22; Camman on the Law of Majesty, dispute 4, theses 29 & 30.* In those works, Camman shows the most brilliant effect of this question in a very serious matter: whether a firstborn son is absolutely to be preferred in the right of succession to a princedom <note>original: "principatu"</note>. Besides *Covarruvias, book 2 of Various Resolutions, chapter 8, number 5; Hotman, Illustrious Questions 2; Knichen on the Saxon privilege of not appealing for Electors, chapter 3, throughout; Tholosanus, book 7 of the Republic, 9; and Arnisaeus in Politics, book 9, folio 222*, the recently cited Camman asserts this.

XXXVII. There are, however, those who judge that the dignity of Nobility should by no means be granted to a son born before the Father acquired the Rights of Nobility; these are cited by *Jos. Nolden in his Discourse on Nobility, chapter 8, number 67.* They are supported by a rescript of the Emperors Valens, Gratian, and Valentinian, in the law *Senator 11, Code on Dignity, book 12*, which treats these children as commoners <note>original: "privatorum loco"; literally "in the place of private citizens," meaning those without official rank</note>. Regarding this text, along with the cited law *Senatoris*, section *C. de Senator.*, Camman (following Cujacius) brings forward this method of reconciliation in the aforementioned thesis 29: that Law 11 is to be understood as concerning a certain *special* privilege of Senators, namely the immunity from "glebal" duties <term>glebalis functio; a land-tax or burden attached to the soil</term> or the pension paid to the treasury from possessions. Thus, sons born before the Father's dignity was obtained do indeed enjoy the same dignity (according to Law 5 of the Digest on Senators) regarding the avoidance of certain punishments, but not regarding the sustaining of public burdens. This is the opinion of *Lord Bocer, on Regalia, chapter 2, number 11*, following Cujacius. His opinion is also bolstered by the text in the laws *doctitij 63* and *neminem 64*, along with the second-to-last and last laws of the *Code on Decurions, book 10*. From these laws, it is evident that sons born before the father attained his dignity are not released from the condition of Curial duties <term>munera Curialia; compulsory public services or financial obligations required of members of a municipal council</term>. 

However, I believe that the privileges of these sons should be extended more broadly, especially considering our current customs, and should even stretch to include immunity from duties. This is especially true in the case where the son, at the time Nobility was conferred upon the Father, was still under the father’s power <term>in ejus sacris; literally "in his sacred rites," a Roman law term for being under the legal control of the head of the household</term>, within his home and family, and had not been emancipated or freed from the bond of paternal power <term>patria potestas</term> due to a marriage contract. For it seems harsh to begrudge sons their Father’s honors. Nor should we fear the objection of the said Law 11 of the Code on Dignity and other similar texts: for all of those were drafted concerning privileges granted by specific law and by reason of some office, which...

# On Nobility

...do not easily pass beyond the person <note>original: "non facilè Personam egrediuntur." In legal terms, a "personal" privilege applies only to the individual, whereas a "real" privilege attaches to their property or lineage.</note>. Knichen, *On the Saxon privilege of non-appeal*. But since the splendor of granted nobility is considered to brighten the blood itself, I do not see by what reasoning children born into the light before this dignity of the father should be separated from it. This is especially true because parents desire and prepare all rights of whatever kind for their children, whether they were born before those rights were acquired or only afterwards. *Digest 50, "Nothing matters"; Digest 37, title 2, "In writing."* Hence, if a <term>Duchy</term>, <term>Marquisate</term>, or <term>County</term> (<note>original: "Ducatus, Marchionatus, vel Comitatus"</note>—titles to which nobility is absolutely annexed as <term>Imperial fiefs</term>) are granted to someone by feudal right, no one would doubt that sons born before the formal <term>investiture</term> <note>The formal ceremony of transferring a title or land</note> have an equal hope of succeeding to the title as those born afterward. So too, the rights of exemptions and immunities attributed to someone and their descendants belong equally to children born at any time. *Digest 4, "On the Law of Immunities"; Digest 13, "On duties and honors"; Digest 6, "On religious matters and funeral expenses,"* which laws Hotman brings forward to support this sense in *Alleged Questions, 2.*

XXXVIII. Furthermore, this genuine and ancestral nobility of blood rejects the rotten offspring of illicit affairs <note>original: "putridam scortationis propaginem"</note>. Therefore, <term>spurious</term> and <term>base-born</term> children <note>original: "spurij ac nothi." In Roman law, "spurii" were children of unknown fathers or temporary unions, while "nothi" were children of a known father and a concubine.</note> who boast of noble fathers are not to be recorded in the rank of nobles, since they are considered to have no legal father. *Institutes, "If against," On Marriage.* For if a marriage was legitimate, the children follow the status of the father. A child born of common union follows the mother. <note>original: "Vulgò quæsitus Matrem sequitur." A central maxim of Roman law regarding status.</note> *Digest 19, "On the status of men."* On this account, they are strangers to the house or <term>agnation</term> <note>Descent through the male line</note> of the alleged father. *Digest 4, "Whereby cognates."* Gabriele Paleotti, *On the base-born and spurious, chapter 71.* Consequently, they are not at all fit to represent his nobility. Tiraqueau, *On Nobility, chapter 15, number 10.* Vultejus, *Marburg Consultations, volume 3, number 38,* even if their mother was noble. Matthaeus Stephanus, *On Nobility, chapter 4, number 38.* Nor can they enjoy any of the father's privileges, *Code, "From a free woman," On heirs,* nor can they show off his arms or insignia. Decianus, *Response 31, number 92, volume 1.* Antoine Favre, *On the Code, title 29, definition 27.* Melchior Goldast, *On Seniority, part 2, question 2, number 19.* However, in many regions outside of Germany, such as in Spain, France, and Savoy, it is an accepted custom that bastards claiming a noble father are not considered exempt from the condition of nobles. Tiraqueau, *chapter 15, number 25.* Azevedo, *On the Spanish Constitutions, book 5, title 8, law 10, number 51.* Antoine Favre, *Definition 27, number 12.* Josua Nolden, *Discourse on Nobility, number 69.*

XXXIX. Yet, I believe there should be no doubt that if these illegitimate sons are legitimized by an <term>Imperial rescript</term> <note>A formal decree or reply from the Emperor</note> at the request of the noble father, they simultaneously attain the rights of nobles. For these children are restored to nature, to their ancient birthright, and to legal rights, and are placed under the power of the father...

### Dissertation II.

...are born, and are inserted into his family, <term>agnation</term> <note>Kinship traced exclusively through the male line, which was the basis for inheriting titles and family property.</note>, and house, so that they differ in no way from children born in wedlock. *Novel 89, chapter 9.* Conrad von Einsiedel, *On Regalities, chapter 2, number 164.* From this, Matthias Stephanus seems wrongly to disagree in his *Treatise on Nobility, chapter 5, number 92*, as does Petrus Fritze in his *Discourse on Nobility, thesis 20, book g.*

XL. Consequently, it is correctly asserted that a subsequent marriage grants paternal nobility to children born of a common woman <note>original: "ignobili fœminâ," referring to a woman of non-noble status.</note>. Tiraquellus, *chapter 15, number 2*; Lord Bocer, *On Regalities, chapter 2, number 26 and following.* For by the favor of marriage, they achieve the fullest right of agnation and are compared in all things and through all things to those born legitimately. *Novel 12, last chapter; Novel 18, final chapter; Novel 89, chapter 8.* Decianus, *Response 9, number 64, volume 4.* Although Nolden says the opposite holds true in Germany (*On Nobility, chapter 8, number 70*), I do not know whether credit should be given to this assertion and practice—which is entirely unknown to me—unless we wish to understand it as a special rule for admission to honors where a statute requires nobles to demonstrate the fullest and most untainted nobility.

XLI. But what should be said regarding adoption? Does a <term>commoner</term> <note>original: "Plebejus"</note> adopted by a noble enjoy the privileges of the nobility? Menochius thinks so (*Presumption 4, number 89, 35*), as does Arumæus (*Exercise 2, thesis 21*). However, this not undeservedly requires deeper inquiry, since adoption does not bestow blood kinship. *Digest 2, "Whence cognates."* And although a different rule is established regarding adoption by a maternal grandfather (*Institutes on adoption, section 2*), it is nonetheless not consistent that nobility could be granted to a commoner by the will of a private man. Therefore, it is rightly said that an adopted child cannot enjoy the father's nobility unless the Emperor's authority intervenes. Cammann, *Dispute 4, thesis 34.*

1. Nobility acquired in the aforementioned ways admits various distinctions, degrees, and conditions. For some is attributed to letters, and some to arms; or, some is called the nobility of Art, and some of Mars <note>The Roman god of war, representing military service.</note>. And since there is no small dispute over precedence between soldiers and the learned, and it is certainly established that men conspicuous for their erudition and prudence bring the greatest benefit to the <term>Commonwealth</term> <note>original: "Rempublicam"</note>, and that they best know what is most suitable for peace and when war must be used, it would certainly be desirable that many should join the splendor of letters with their ancestral nobility or the glory of arms. For the highest protection of the State arises from the stem of two things: namely, arms and laws (*as Emperor Justinian bears witness in the preface to the Constitution confirming the Justinian Code*). There is no doubt that one adorned with this double glory is to be preferred above all others. Furthermore, if we speak of the knowledge of letters...

->## On Nobility<-

...and whether a separate inquiry should be established regarding nobility of lineage or skill in arms, it is consistent with our customs that in military matters, soldiers or <term>nobles</term> <note>The Latin "Milites" here refers to the military class or knights, often used interchangeably with the lower nobility of the sword.</note> hold the first place; in literary matters, the learned; and in common and indifferent matters, soldiers likewise hold the first rank—unless the splendor of a specific office grants a prerogative to the scholar. *Chassanæus, in the Catalog of the Glory of the World. Lord Lans, my honored colleague, on the struggle for precedence between soldiers and scholars, folio 73.*

2. One kind of nobility is said to be of lineage and blood; another of virtue; and a third is mixed, taking a part from each of the preceding types. *Reyger, in the Treasury, title: On Nobility, number 47.*

3. One kind arises from the excellence of a public office; another belongs to a person even without the administration of a public duty. *Martinus Laudensis, in the treatise on dignity, verse 44.*

4. Some nobility rests solely on wealth, while another exists by itself without wealth. Regarding this, Albertus teaches <note>original: "tradit Albert. in l. Imperatores"</note> that wealth makes a family noble; and Baldus <note>original: "Bald. in c. super. extr. de Test."</note> says that **he who is rich is noble enough.** But if the riches are ancient and joined with the virtue of ancestors, Tiberius Decianus does not reject this view. *Response 66, number 40, volume 3.* However, although wealth may ornament nobility—and nobility that is ambitiously acquired but not fortified by wealth may be ridiculous and soon vanish—it is nevertheless not safe to attribute the dignity of nobility to the splendor of riches alone.

5. Some nobles live in cities, and some in the countryside. However, this distinction drawn from location seems poorly suited to the subject. *Jason, on the section "item Serviana," number 25, at the end. Tiraquellus, On Nobility, chapter 2, number 53. Decianus, in the cited response, number 43.*

6. One type of nobility is said to exist by custom or statute; another by Law or the decree of a Prince. Hence the <term>Patricians</term> <note>High-ranking families who held hereditary seats on city councils, particularly in the Free Imperial Cities of the Holy Roman Empire.</note> in the illustrious cities of Germany, who are noble primarily within their own jurisdictions, acknowledge that they received their nobility from manners or custom. *Matthias Stephanus, treatise on Nobility, chapter 5, number 42. My brother, in Politics, part 1, chapter 11, number 48.*

7. Some nobility is new, and some is ancient; but it is rightly asked: which of these is more worthy? Indeed, common consent grants preeminence to the antiquity of the lineage, *Johannes Gutierez, practical questions, book 3, question 14, number 26,* unless virtue departs from that ancient stock. *Arnisaeus, Politics 1, chapter 12, folio 301. Camman, disputation 4, thesis 16. Einsiedel, chapter 2, number 174.* Since it is holier for someone to flourish through their own deeds and be the beginning of nobility and an example of virtue for their descendants, than to be destitute of virtue and rely only on the reputation of their ancestors, as under the name of Cicero... <note>The text cuts off here; the catchword "ronis" indicates the name Cicero continues on the next page.</note>

### Dissertation II.

... [unclear/fragment of a name], in his *Oration to Sallust*, as a certain declaimer writes. From this, it is clear to everyone that the path to all offices assigned to the Nobility lies open to them, unless a special statute dictates otherwise; see *Nolden, Discourse on Nobility, number 50*, where dissenting opinions are listed. Thus, today these new Nobles are received as Assessors of the Chamber, provided they conform to the Ordinances. *Nolden, same discourse on Nobility, thesis 49.* 

However, it does not escape me that by the customs of almost all peoples (with the exception of the Turks, who, in an opinion completely contrary to other nations, believe those who are entirely ignorant of their parents to be the most noble of all, and grant them the highest ranks of dignity, as witnessed by Pierre Belon <note>A 16th-century French explorer and naturalist</note> in *Observations, book 2, chapter 95*) and by the statutes of many places, it is accepted that the older the Nobility, the more it is considered adorned with greater prerogatives. Hence, *Baldus <note>A famous 14th-century jurist</note>, in the law 'nobiliores,' Code on Commerce and Merchants*, says that some nobility is beginning, some is growing, and some is perfect. And the same author, *in the preface on Fiefs* <note>A 'fief' or 'feud' refers to land held under the feudal system in exchange for service</note>, teaches that an ancient Fief carries the flavor of Nobility; not so a new one, because Nobility is not born in the blink of an eye. This is also supported by the *text in the title on Maintaining Peace and its violation, chapter 3, section 'miles,' 2. Feudal Law, title 27.* 

And *Pliny the Younger, in his 8th letter to his grandfather-in-law Fabatus*, boasts that he would not leave "sudden images" <note>original: "subitas imagines"; this refers to the Roman 'ius imaginum,' the right of noble families to display wax masks of their ancestors. Pliny means he won't leave a legacy that lacks deep ancestral roots</note> to his children. Hence, in France, it is observed, as *Tiraquellus <note>André Tiraqueau, an influential French jurist</note> says in 'On Nobility,' chapter 19, number 7, at the end*, that he who has been recently ennobled must perform all the duties of a commoner just as a commoner would. *Guillaume Budé* <note>A famous French humanist and legal scholar</note> *also left in writing in his notes on the Pandects, at the final law of the Digest regarding Senators*, that it is accepted by the customs of certain places that true Nobility is not recognized until the great-grandson and those thereafter of the man who was restored to his birthright—that is, gifted with nobility. And also by Saxon Law, no one enjoys the Privileges of Knights unless he was born to a Father and Grandfather who were Knights: <term>he has no knightly right unless his father and his great-grandfather <note>The Latin 'avo' (grandfather) and German 'Elter Vatter' (great-grandfather) differ slightly in specific generational count here, but the principle is ancestral proof</note> were knights or companions of knights.</term> <note>original German: "hat nit Ritterrecht / sein Vatter vnd sein Elter Vatter seyen dann Ritter / oder Rittersgenoßen gewesen."</note> *Feudal Law, chapter 2, book 1.*

8. There follows the eighth distinction of Nobility, by which one type is called full or perfect, and another less full or imperfect. To full and perfect Nobility, we can apply not only that which shines through a long and ancient line of ancestors (which was discussed in the previous consideration), but also that which is illustrated by the Nobility of both parents. For although the Nobility of a Mother does not confer the rights of Nobility upon children if the marriage is tainted by the father being a commoner, it should nonetheless not be doubted that the Nobility received from the Father is more illustrious, more splendid, and clearer on account of the brilliance of the Mother’s lineage. *Matthias Stephanus, following Tiraquellus on Nobility, chapter 5, number 66.* In the same way, in Silesia, no one can use the privileges of perfect Nobility <term>who is not "four-shielded"—that is, who cannot produce his four noble ancestors.</term> <note>original German: "welcher nit Vierschildig / id est, welcher seine vier Adenliche Ahnen nit beybringen mag."</note> *Treatise on*

# On Nobility

...whether an inquiry should be conducted separately into the nobility of one's lineage or one's skill in arms, it is consistent with our customs that in military actions, soldiers or nobles take precedence; in literary matters, the learned; and in common and indifferent matters, soldiers likewise hold the first place, unless the splendor of a public office grants a prerogative to the learned man <note>original: "nisi officij splendor Literato prærogativam concedat"</note>. Chassanæus, *in the Catalog of the Glory of the World*. My honored colleague, Dr. Lansius, *on the certainty of precedence between Soldiers and the Learned, page 73.*

2. One type of nobility is said to be of lineage and blood <note>original: "stirpis & sanguinis"</note>, another of virtue, and another is "mixed," taking a part from each of the preceding types. Reyger, *in the Treasury, title on Nobility, number 47.*

3. One type arises from the eminence of a public office, while another belongs to a person without the administration of a public duty. Martinus Laudensis, *in the treatise on dignity, verse 44.*

4. One type of nobility relies solely on wealth <note>original: "Divitijs"</note>, while another exists by itself without wealth. Regarding this, Albertus <note>Likely referring to a legal commentator on Roman law</note> teaches that wealth ennobles a family, *in the law 'Imperatores,' in the preface of the Digest on Decurions* <note>A 'decurion' was a member of a city council in the Roman Empire; these positions were often hereditary and tied to status</note>; and Baldus <note>A famous 14th-century jurist</note>, *in the chapter 'super,' of the extra-decretals on Wills*, says that whoever is rich is noble enough. If the riches are ancient and joined with the virtue of ancestors, Tiberius Decianus does not reject this, *response 66, number 40, volume 3.* Yet, even if wealth adorns nobility, and nobility acquired through ambition but not fortified by wealth might be ridiculous and soon vanish: nevertheless, it is not safe to attribute the dignity of nobility to the splendor of riches alone.

5. Some nobles live in cities, while others live in the rural countryside. Yet this distinction based on location seems poorly suited to the topic. Jason de Mayno, *in the section 'item Serviana,' number 25, at the end of the Institutes on actions.* Tiraquellus, *on Nobility, chapter 2, number 53.* Decianus, *in the cited response, number 43.*

6. One type of nobility is said to exist by custom or statute; another by Law or the decree of a Prince. Hence the Patricians <note>original: "Patricij"; refers here to the high-ranking ruling families of free imperial cities</note> in the illustrious cities of Germany, who are noble primarily at home, owe their nobility to local manners or custom. Matthias Stephani, *treatise on Nobility, chapter 5, number 42.* My brother, *in Politics, part 1, chapter 11, number 48.*

7. One nobility is "new," and another is "ancient," but it is rightly asked: which of these is more worthy? Certainly, common consensus grants preeminence to the antiquity of the lineage <note>original: "generis antiquitati"</note>, Johannes Gutierrez, *practical questions, book 3, question 14, number 26*, unless virtue departs from the ancient family line. Arnifæus, *Politics, book 1, chapter 12, page 301.* Camman, *disputation 4, thesis 36.* Einsidel, *chapter 2, number 174.* Since it is holier for someone to flourish by their own deeds and to be the beginning of nobility and an example of virtue for their descendants, than to be destitute of virtue and rely only on the reputation of ancestors, as under the name of Cicero...

### Dissertation II.

...of Cicero, as a certain orator writes in his *Oration to Sallust*. From this, it is clear to everyone that access to all offices assigned to nobles is open to them, unless a specific statute dictates otherwise: Nolden, *Discourse on Nobility, no. 50*, where dissenting views are listed. Thus today, these new nobles are accepted as Assessors of the [Imperial] Chamber, provided they conform to the Ordinances. Nolden, *in the same discourse on Nobility, thesis 49*. 

However, I am not unaware that by the customs of almost all peoples (excepting the Turks, who, in a view plainly contrary to other nations, consider those to be the most noble—and grant the highest ranks of dignity to them—who are completely ignorant of their parents, according to the testimony of Pierre Belon, *Observations, Book 2, Chapter 95*), and by the statutes of many places, it is accepted that the older the nobility, the more extensive the prerogatives with which it is deemed to be adorned. Hence Baldus, *on the law 'nobiliores,' Codex on Commerce and Merchants*, says there is a nobility that is beginning, one that is growing, and one that is perfect. The same author, *in the preface to the Feuds*, teaches that an ancient fief "tastes" of nobility, but a new one does not, because nobility is not born in the blink of an eye. 

This is supported by the text *in the title on maintaining peace and its violation, chapter 3, paragraph 'soldier,' book 2 of the Feuds, title 27*. Also Pliny the Younger, *Epistles, book 8*, to his grandfather-in-law Fabatus, boasts that he would not leave "sudden ancestral masks" <note>original: "subitas imagines"; in Ancient Rome, noble families kept wax masks of their ancestors to display their lineage; a 'sudden' mask implies a newly-minted status without deep history</note> to his children. Hence in France, according to Tiraquellus, *on nobility, chapter 19, number 7, at the end*, it is observed that one who has been recently ennobled must perform all the duties of commoners just like any commoner. Guillaume Budé also left written *in his annotations on the Pandects, on the final law of the Digest regarding Senators*, that by the customs of certain places, true nobility is not recognized until the great-grandson and his descendants of the one who was restored to his birthright—that is, ennobled. And also under Saxon Law, no one enjoys the privileges of knights unless born of a father and grandfather who were knights: <term>he does not have the right of a knight unless his father and his grandfather were knights or companions of knights</term> <note>German: "hat nit Ritterrecht / sein Vatter vnd sein Elter Vatter seyen dann Ritter / oder Rittersgenoßen gewesen"</note>. *Feudal Law, chapter 2, book 1*.

8. The eighth distinction of nobility follows, by which one type is called full or perfect, and another less full or less perfect. To full and perfect nobility we can assign not only that which shines through an ancient and long series of ancestors (which was discussed in the previous section) but also that which is made illustrious by the nobility of both parents. For although the nobility of a mother may not confer the rights of nobility upon children if the marriage to a commoner father is "stained" <note>This refers to the legal principle that status usually followed the father; a noble woman marrying a commoner did not typically pass her status to her children</note>, there is no doubt that the nobility received by the sons from their father is made more illustrious, brighter, and more distinct by the splendor of the maternal lineage. Matthias Stephanus, following Tiraquellus *on Nobility, chapter 5, number 66*. In the same way, in Silesia, no one can use the privileges of perfect nobility <term>who is not "four-shielded"—that is, who cannot produce his four noble ancestors</term> <note>German: "welcher nit Vierschildig / id est, welcher seine vier Adenliche Ahnen nit beybringen mag." This refers to the *Ahnenprobe*, a requirement to prove noble descent from all four grandparents to enter certain knightly orders or chapters.</note>. Treatise...
