# On Nobility

...concerning Knightly Law, pages 1 and 40 with the following pages. Thus also, no one is admitted into the higher church colleges <note>canonical colleges: groups of clergy associated with a cathedral, often requiring noble proof for membership</note> unless they can demonstrate the Nobility of both parents through a long series of ancestors across eight generations—that is, *one who can prove his eight ancestors* <note>original German: welcher seine acht Ahnen beweisen kan</note>. Similarly, in the military game of tournaments, no place was granted to anyone unless they were born of noble parents and could prove their nobility through four grandparents, as Sebastian Münster in his *Cosmography* and Martin Crusius in his *Swabian Annals* show. And it is attested by Matthias Stephanus in *On Nobility, chapter 4, number 67*, that it has been received into German custom that eight generations of Nobles on both sides must be recorded without any defect in Nobility—*so that one can prove, from father and mother, and their grandfathers and grandmothers, and their fathers and mothers, the right to display a shield and helmet from of old, and at the very least can prove his eight ancestors.* <note>original German: das einer von Vatter vnd Mutter/ vnd derselben Großvatter vnnd Großmutter/ auch deroselben Vatter vnd Mutter/ von Alters hero Schildt vnd Helm auffzulegen/ vnd zum wenigsten Seine 8. ## Dissertation II.

Q 3

<meta>catchword: my,</meta>

The question arises: can the Emperor, by the force of his absolute power, make someone eligible for the **canonical colleges** <note>religious institutions for elite clergy that often required high-born status</note>, even if he cannot demonstrate four grandfathers and four grandmothers on both the paternal and maternal lines? Frederick Pruckman simply affirms this in *paragraph "soluta potestas," chapter 4, number 1, effect 4.* However, it seems this question should be resolved with a moderate distinction. If the original foundation of these colleges specifically excludes such "new men," I do not know if the Imperial power can easily be extended to destroy the express will of the founder—provided that others of full nobility are available and suitable. But if this "ancestral nobility" is required only by a specific statute or long-standing custom, then the Imperial authority is rightly considered. This is because the Prince can change matters of civil law that result in no one’s detriment—especially if someone relies on the "certain knowledge" <note>original: "certâ scientiâ," a legal term meaning the Emperor acted with full awareness of the facts, not just by routine</note> of the Emperor, and not merely on a patent of nobility, which are granted everywhere and indiscriminately in the best form. See Pruckman, *cited paragraph "soluta potestas," chapter 3.* This holds unless one believes that Imperial power should not be extended to the aforementioned regulation of ecclesiastical matters.

9. Add a ninth distinction: that some nobility is extended to posterity, while other types are extinguished along with the person. The nobility of a family <note>original: "gentis"</note> is perpetual. But that which is granted by reason of an office usually ends when the office ceases. See Antonius Faber, *Book 9 of the Code, title 28, definition 9, number 11, and definition 23 throughout.* However, the **senatorial dignity** is also acquired by one's children, although it is not extended beyond great-great-grandchildren. In France, the descendants of Senators also enjoy the privileges of nobility, as gathered from the Rulings of Papon.

10. A tenth distinction follows: some Nobles are called **immediate**, while others are **mediate**. The latter are subject to Princes and similar states of a higher Order; the former are bound by no law of inferior subjection but obey only the Emperor and the Empire directly. My brother says, in his *Discourse on the Knightly Order, folio 9*, that it is probable all Nobles—as "ministerial" servants of Princes, or <term>Edelknecht</term> <note>German: noble servant or squire</note> and Minor **Valvassors** <note>vassals of a higher lord</note>—were once subject to Princes. But today, the Nobles of Saxony and most German provinces remain in their ancient devotion to the local states, while only the Franconian, Swabian, and Rhenish Nobility, having cast off the bond of obedience to Princes, shine with a splendid liberty. Indeed, the illustrious Ummius, in his *Dispute on Judicial Process 14, number 55*, considers the followers of this Order to be numbered among the "illustrious."

Furthermore, my brother probably asserts that the Franconian and Swabian Nobility accrued to the Empire because of the extinction of the Dukes of Franconia and Swabia,

# On Nobility

<meta>catchword: Adenliche</meta>

my <note>Completing the sentence from the previous page, likely "my book" or "my discourse."</note> *alleged discourse on the Equestrian order, leaf 9 and following.* I do not doubt, however, that the Rhenish or Wetteravian class of Nobility (whose liberty has suffered a great insult in our memory) can demonstrate rights of sincere liberty and long-standing possession. This is supported by the **Imperial Recess of Augsburg** of the year 1500, under the title *how one should deal with the knighthood of Franconia, Swabia, and the Rhineland.*

However, we must examine whether the Nobles subject immediately to the Empire <note>Those who held their lands directly from the Emperor without an intermediary lord.</note> should be recorded among the **States of the Empire** <note>The constituent members of the Holy Roman Empire who held a seat and a vote in the Imperial Diet.</note>. Indeed, it is asserted in the *Chamber of Symphorema, under the word "Noble," leaf 234,* that these Nobles, considered collectively, should be included among the States of the Empire. Yet I fear that both experience and the **Imperial Matrix** <note>original: "matricula"; the official register listing the military and financial obligations of the Imperial estates</note>, in which they are not found, resist this; as does the order of the **Imperial Diets** <note>original: "Comitiorum Imperialium"; the general assembly of the Empire's estates</note>, which does not admit these Nobles nor grant them any right of voting. Some argue they appeared in the Diets in former times, citing the **Golden Bull** <note>The 1356 decree that served as the primary constitutional law of the Holy Roman Empire</note>, *title 1, section "we decree."* and the decree of King Frederick III established at Frankfurt am Main in the year 1442, which mentions: *Freemen, Lords, Servants, Knights, and Squires.* However, the author of the **Donauwörth Remonstrance** (a man truly most skilled in the state and affairs of Germany) argues on *leaf 169 and 173* that one cannot conclude with certainty that they held the rights of the States of the Empire, since they are mentioned there only as advisors—a likely conjecture which Josias Nolden applauds in his *discourse on Nobility, conclusion 149.*

Furthermore, some attempt to deduce from the fact that these immediate Nobles are not counted among the States of the Empire, that their dignity is not **Regal** <term>Regalia: Sovereign rights or privileges, such as the power to mint coins, collect certain taxes, or administer high justice, typically reserved for the Emperor or higher territorial princes.</term> as it is in the Princes and Counts of the Empire, and that the rights of territory and superiority cannot be attributed to them. Consequently, they argue that these nobles do not possess **Regalia** or **High Justice** <note>original: "merum Imperium"; the legal authority to exercise capital punishment, the "power of the sword"</note> except by feudal grant from the Emperor or long-standing possession; nor do they have the right to tax subjects or to collect the wine excise, known as **the beverage tax** <note>original: "das Vmbgelt"</note>.

Others do not hesitate to assert **territorial jurisdiction** for these immediate Nobles, granting them no less power in their own free castles and villages than the States of the Empire. From this, they infer that this supreme territorial jurisdiction which exists in a **territorial prince** <note>original: "Landtsfürsten"</note> or in a **count** <note>original: "Graven"</note>, also exists in the immediate noble...

### Dissertation II.

<margin>Noble Authority. Johann Bidembach in *Questions on the Nobility*, question 1, number 11. My brother, in his *Discourse on the Equestrian Order*, leaf 13.</margin>

And this opinion, formulated in favor of the territorial jurisdiction of the Nobles, does not seem improbable to me, provided that a fitting moderation is applied and these territorial rights are not extended to an infinite degree. For we know that political theorists, with matching votes, believe that Princes reflect and represent the "ray of the Emperor," like the most complete image of their own Sun. They also hold the same or similar jurisdiction over their subjects as the Emperor has over everyone, claiming for themselves the **Royal Rights** <note>original: "Regalia"; these were specific rights belonging to the crown, such as minting coins or collecting tolls</note> in their own territories—not only those expressed in the law *regarding which rights are royal*, but also any others held to be Royal Rights by accepted custom (while keeping the Majesty of the Emperor and his reserved rights entirely unharmed). These rights are exercised by Princes by virtue of their supreme territorial jurisdiction, or **High Territorial Sovereignty of a Prince** <term>Landtsfürstlicher hoher Obrigkeit</term>. If you wished to grant all these same powers to the Immediate Nobility by virtue of **Noble Authority** <term>Adelicher Obrigkeit</term>, you would create infinite "petty kings" and grant them a more at-will power than even the Princes themselves. This is because Princes hold their principalities under feudal law—as noted by Knichen in *On the Royal Territorial Right, chapter 1, number 16*—whereas, by contrast, there are many Nobles who possess their castles and villages by right of full ownership.

Nevertheless, these Nobles recognize no superior except the Imperial Majesty alone, just like the States of the Empire. They are in no way subject to the Princes in whose territories they possess noble and free estates (unless perhaps by the law of a vassal's duty), as Bidembach notes in the aforementioned first question, number 7. Instead, they are included in the constitutions speaking of those subject immediately to the Empire. They utilize the **Privilege of Arbitration** <note>original: "Privilegio Austragarum"; a legal right of the nobility to have disputes settled by peers rather than local courts</note> and enjoy the benefit of the **Peace of Religion** <note>The 1555 Peace of Augsburg, which allowed for the coexistence of Catholicism and Lutheranism</note>, according to the **Imperial Recess** <term>Reichs Abschiedt</term> of the year 1555, in the section "and in such peace." Paurmeister, in *On Jurisdiction, final chapter, number 46*, notes that they frequently enjoy simple jurisdiction, but more often possess **High Justice** <note>original: "mero Imperio"; the legal power to command and the authority to impose capital punishment</note>. They claim hunting rights for themselves and often obtain several Royal Rights by special privilege. For other Royal Rights not specifically acquired by them are considered to belong to the Emperor, not to the Princes in whose territories the Nobles hold their free villages. They contribute freely to the Empire, and their various ranks exercise the rights of the Archives, as Ruland notes in *On Commissions, book 2, chapter 4, number 51*. Finally, they enjoy such preeminence that they are quite rightly regarded not as private citizens, but as distinguished persons marked by illustrious dignity, according to Ulrich Ummius in his *Discourse on Process 14, number 55*.

As for the Immediate Nobility of the three illustrious provinces of Germany <note>The knightly circles of Franconia, Swabia, and the Rhineland</note> (which otherwise, like a body poorly put together, easily dissol-

<meta>This page continues the legal and political advice for the "Immediate Nobility"—knights who held their land directly from the Holy Roman Emperor. # On Nobility

...can be [strengthened] by force.) the nobility will both retain and increase its former authority if it applies its mind to the following points:

1. That these Immediate Nobles should adhere to the Emperor alone as their Head.

2. That they should not join themselves to more powerful neighbors, and that they should flee all those things from which any reason for subjection could ever be deduced.

3. That they should strive with the greatest effort so that their "Classes" or "Orders" <note>The Imperial Knights were organized into three "circles": Franconia, Swabia, and the Rhineland</note> might attain the rights of the **Estates of the Empire** <note>original: "Statuum Imperij"; this would give them a vote in the Imperial Diet, putting them on a similar legal footing to Princes</note> (under certain laws and agreements). For even if, in that way, they will perhaps be required to bear their share of the expenses of the **Imperial Chamber Court** <note>The *Reichskammergericht*, the highest judicial body in the Empire</note> and other burdens, nevertheless, by this remedy their liberty will be rendered more secure; furthermore, the ordinary taxes which they would contribute as Estates perhaps do not exceed the "charitable subsidies" they previously provided.

4. It is extremely necessary that this entire Nobility coalesce into one body and, as it were, a single Commonwealth.

5. From this, a "Greater Council" should be established, which ought to contain all three Classes, to which only matters of greater importance should be referred. Other matters, however, should be left to the three separate Assemblies in Franconia, Swabia, and along the Rhine.

6. Care must be taken that no discords or contentions arise in any way because of diverse religions.

7. In the higher canonical colleges, the **High Chapters** <term>Hohen Stifften</term>, care should be taken that the supreme ecclesiastical dignities are not conferred only upon the children of Princes and Counts, which would eventually exclude the Nobles.

8. That the Nobles should provide for themselves through "family compacts" <note>Legal agreements between family members to prevent the division of estates</note> so that their dignity may be preserved.

9. From this, they should also deliberate whether the rights of **primogeniture** <term>the right of the firstborn to inherit the entire estate</term> or even **majoratus** <term>the right of the eldest relative to succeed to the family property</term> are suitable for them.

10. It is also beneficial that, by a certain law, provision be made for male heirs, so that their daughters and sisters—even without a formal renunciation—are content with a fixed dowry, rather than setting their minds on the paternal and ancestral goods in which the splendor of the family consists.

11. That those Nobles committed to the **Augsburg Confession** <note>The primary confession of faith of the Lutheran Church</note> should ordain a specific ecclesiastical court, so that they do not ruinously take the law into their own hands due to the lack of a proper judge.

12. That the Council of each Class should appoint tutors and guardians for the children of the Nobles, govern their behavior, and inspect the accounts of the guardianships every year.

13. That from an annual collective fund, they should establish a common treasury, from which not only common expenses can be paid, but even the needy can be assisted.

### Dissertation II.

14. That the aforementioned Council should, through the strongest warnings and censures, bring back to thriftiness those who uselessly consume their wealth; and if it seems necessary, the Council may appoint **Curators** <note>legal guardians for those judged incapable of managing their own affairs</note> or, preferably, **Inspectors** over them, provided this is done without damaging their public reputation.

15. That care be taken so that no one transfers his free and **Noble estates** to another person who is not of this order; such a sale should either be void by law or at least subject to the **right of recovery** <note>original: "retractui"; a legal right allowing family or peers to buy back land sold to outsiders to keep it within the nobility</note>.

16. That Nobles should avoid the inextricable webs of lawsuits; instead, they should commit their controversies to **Arbitrators** chosen from among the Nobility.

17. That from the sentences of these Arbitrators, they may appeal to the aforementioned Council; however, they should entirely abstain from any further appeal beyond that. (This was also dictated by their ordinance established in the **Year 1561**, and confirmed by the Emperor Ferdinand I, though it has been poorly observed until now.)

18. That all Nobles who possess castles and villages should seek the exercise and execution of **criminal jurisdiction** <note>original: "Iurisdictionis criminalis"; the right to judge capital crimes, often called "high justice" or "blood justice"</note> from His Imperial Majesty, which they will easily obtain by virtue of the privilege granted to them in the **Year 1609**.

19. That they should flee from laziness and sloth, avoid pride, detest tyranny over their subjects, and finally, cast away envy and hidden animosities.

---

Hitherto we have spoken of the acquisition of Nobility, its propagation to posterity, and the various conditions of this order; now we must briefly address the **effect of Nobility**, which consists chiefly in the privileges granted to the same.

These privileges consist partly in the showing of reverence and honors, partly in the granting of immunity, and partly in the mitigation or remission of punishments.

Indeed, the dignity of Nobles flourishes most in the conferring and decreeing of honors and offices: they hold the first place in greetings, processions, and seating. <note>The author cites various sections of the *Codex Justinianus* (the Roman law code) regarding the seating of senators and high-ranking officials</note> For the same, there stands a greater presumption of both virtue and integrity, as well as loyalty. <note>References to legal scholars: Hondedei, Pruckmann, and Peter Fritze</note> This is to such an extent that the nobility and gravity of a person under investigation can strike down the proofs of a crime presented by the Treasury. <note>Reference to Hondedei</note> Also, in the bearing of testimony and in other acts generally, more credit is given to them than to the ignoble <note>commoners</note>. <note>References to Tiraquellus, Pruckmann, Farinaccius, and Cothmann</note> This is true provided that their testimony [is supported] by the taking of an oath (which, as it [pertains to] the substance of...

# On Nobility

<meta>catchword: vis</meta>

...the examination <note>original: "visitationis"; completing the word from the previous page's catchword</note> of witnesses, it cannot be omitted (as noted in the Law of Oaths, 9. Code of Justinian on witnesses, and the chapter *licet* in the Decretals on witnesses) that this is supported by established law (chapter *tuis* 39. Decretals on witnesses; Ruland on Commissioners, book 1, chapter 11, number 44 and following; Grævæus, conclusion 59, number 3). If, however, the controversy is to be decided within their own territory, then it will suffice if they transmit their testimony in writing alone, without the religious ritual of a formal oath: for in this case, they are trusted not merely as witnesses, but as **public persons** <note>individuals holding a recognized status or office that grants their word inherent legal weight</note>. (Meichsner, volume 1, Decisions of the Imperial Chamber, 30, number 1).

Furthermore, the **promises of Nobles** are held to be firmer and are not to be eluded by any evasion; but when made **upon Noble Honor, Faith, and Truth** <note>original German: "bey Adelichen Ehren/ Trew vnd Glauben"</note>, they are judged to have the force of an oath, as dictated by the general custom of Germany. (Gail, book 2, observation 59, number 2; Mynsing, century 1, observation 17; and Goeddaeus, Marburg Counsels, 27, volume 1; Sextinus, counsel 12, number 113, volume 2; Vultejus, counsel 34, number 9, volume 3; Schrader, counsel 1, number 456, volume 1). Indeed, Wesenbeck (counsel 52, number 22, part 2) and Cothman (counsel 30, number 281, part 1) record that a promise from a Noble is observed as if it were an oath. This applies especially if an oath of formal procedure is not required. (Fichard, counsel 57, number 7, volume 1; Wesenbeck, counsel 15, number 63; Modestinus Pistoris, counsel 19, numbers 50, 51, 56, volume 2). And although some attribute little weight to a promise of nobility unless the assertion is added "upon Christian faith," Pruckman (counsel 50, number 605, volume 1) says it is **wicked** if a Noble wishes to distinguish between a promise interposed with Christian faith and one which he made with a handshake and words suited for promising. In the same work (number 587), he also tries to prove that the legal remedy for rescinding a sale <note>the "L. 2. C. de rescind. vendit." refers to a law allowing a seller to cancel a sale if the price was less than half the value</note> does not apply to one born of a noble race; as Cothman adds (supporting this opinion in counsel 30, number 22, part 1), those who pursue and strive to repair a financial loss they have suffered might wound their own **reputation, modesty, and nobility**, and generally act against good morals. However, Martin Chemnitz (counsel 50, book 3 of Cothman’s Counsels, number 222) responds to this severely and not without emotion.

It has also been introduced as a favor to Nobles that even in criminal cases, they may both act and respond through a **Procurator** <note>a legal representative or attorney</note> (Law *fin.* Code on Injuries; Law *pen.* Code on Procurators; Gail, book 1, observation 111, number 44; Nicolaus Everhard the Younger, counsel 41, number 2). They ought also to be examined and bound by oath at their own home (though the Noble must provide the expenses, unless he was produced as a witness, concerning which see Nolden on Nobility, number 84). (Farinacius on witnesses, question 77; Grævæus, common conclusion 90, number 1 and following). Furthermore, they are not to be committed to **prison** even for a crime brought to judgment (unless the gravity of the offense demands it; Everhard the Younger, counsel 8, number 4, volume 2), but are to be released upon a **sworn guarantee** <term>juratoria cautio</term> <note>a "juratory caution" is a promise made under oath to return for trial, used instead of physical bail or imprisonment for persons of high status</note>. (Law *pen.* Code on Dignities; Novel 12; Novel 52).

<summary>This page details the legal privileges of the nobility in the Holy Roman Empire, emphasizing that a Noble's word of honor is legally equivalent to an oath and that they possess specific rights in criminal court, such as avoiding imprisonment through a sworn guarantee.</summary>
<keywords>noble honor, legal testimony, juratoria cautio, German custom, criminal jurisdiction, legal representation, witness examination, social status</keywords>

### Dissertation II.

...[the force of] sufficient security <note>original: *vis sufficiens satisdatio*</note> should be demanded from those closest to them <note>their relatives or kin</note>, as Julius Clarus testifies in his *Criminal Treatise*, final section, question 46, number 16; on which Nolden should be consulted at numbers 87 and 107. Nor should they be led to prison even for a civil debt, as noted by Covarruvias in *Various Resolutions*, chapter 1, number 4. Rather, they should only be sued to the extent that they are able to pay <note>this refers to the *beneficium competentiae*, a legal privilege allowing a person to retain enough assets for their own basic support</note>. This is supported by Coler in *On the Process of Execution*, chapter 6, number 146, and Hering in *On Sureties*, chapter 10, number 370. However, Gratianus, in *Forensic Disputations* 328, number 25, is of the opinion that a Noble can renounce this privilege.

Furthermore, the splendor of the Nobility requires that more generous maintenance <note>original: *alimenta*; the legal provision for the costs of living</note> be established for them, according to the dignity of their persons. (Surdus, *On Maintenance*, question 18, number 9, and question 24, number 10; Gylman, in the *Symphonia*, volume 5, report 32, number 16). Thus, a father should provide suitable maintenance for his son (even if the son could seek a living with his own hands), as per Gratianus, chapter 120, number 10. Likewise, for a daughter—so that she may acquire a husband worthy of the Family—assistance should be provided through a more generous dowry. This applies even if it exceeds the legal portion (Decianus, *Responses*, 78, number 16, volume 1) or if feudal property must be touched (Hartmann Pistor, second part, question 2, number 37; Rauchbar, question 38, number 43; Tiraquellus, chapter 20, number 163).

So also the preeminence of Nobles ensures that an injury <note>original: *injuria*; referring to both physical assault and insults to honor</note> inflicted upon them is considered more atrocious and is punished more severely than one committed against a commoner (based on the argument in the *Institutes*, section "Atrox," on injuries). Additionally, the damage caused to them receives a higher valuation when an action is brought under the Aquilian Law <note>original: *legis Aquiliæ*; a Roman law providing compensation for damage to property or person</note>. (Lord Harpprecht, *Criminal Treatise*, section "Illud," number 8, *Institutes* on the Aquilian Law). For this reason, Nobles are to be employed above others in conducting embassies and other arduous matters (based on the law in the *Code of Justinian*, book 10, "On the condition of public granaries"). They are also to be preferred over commoners in secular offices and Ecclesiastical benefices (Nolden, *On the Nobility*, number 77 and following).

The privileges of immunity consist in this: that by the long-standing custom of Germany, Nobles are not held to pay taxes <note>original: *collectas*; general tax collections</note> and public burdens, making no distinction between their own private property and lands held by their vassals. This is asserted by Johann Köpen (Decision 61, numbers 3 and 5), Friedrich Mindanus (in *Processes of the Imperial Chamber*, book 2, chapter 42, section 6), and Johann Bidembach (Question 18). I believe this custom flourishes among us in favor of the **Immediate Nobility** <note>nobles who held their land and titles directly from the Holy Roman Emperor, rather than through a middle-tier prince</note>. (Tiraquellus, following Guido Papæ, testifies that this was also accepted in France, *On Nobility*, chapter 20, number 168). However, if Nobles are viewed universally, it does not excuse them from the burden of paying taxes (Thomatus, *On Collections*, section "Exactis," through folio 321; Vincent de Franchis, Decision 128; Bodin, *On the Republic*, book 1, chapter 6). This is unless we wish to except the Nobility who possess fiefs. (Choppin, *On the French Domain*, book 1, chapter 14, number 124; Rosenthal, *On Fiefs*, chapter 5, conclusion 76; Surdus, Counsel 135, numbers 12 and following, book 1, and Counsel 235, number 28, book 2). Indeed, even the highest necessi-

# On Nobility

[Page 132]

...[immuni]ty, even for those exempted, imposes a subsidiary contribution <note>A secondary tax or emergency aid requested when regular treasury funds were exhausted.</note> according to the **Imperial Recess of the Year 1542, §. "likewise as now mentioned in the next Article."** <note>original: *Reichs Abschied*—the formal collection of laws passed by the Imperial Diet of the Holy Roman Empire.</note> See also Gail, book 2, observation 52; Mynsinger, book 4, observation 70; Wintzler, *On Contributions*, decision 6, number 13; and Heig, volume 1, question 18. Although unique privileges in a certain way free the "Immediate" Nobles <note>Nobles who held their land directly from the Emperor rather than through a middle lord.</note> from these extraordinary contributions; and therefore a fixed collection is not enjoined upon them as it is for the other Estates; they are instead only compelled to provide a benevolent and voluntary subsidy, **"for a compassionate aid, without any certain assessment."** <note>original: *umb ein mitleidenliche hilff/ ohn einigen gewissen Anschlag.* This refers to a voluntary payment given to the Emperor as a gesture of goodwill rather than a mandatory tax.</note> **Imperial Recess of the Year 1500, title: "how one should deal with the Knighthoods in Franconia, Swabia, and the Rhineland."** See also Caspar Klock, *Dissertation on Contributions*, conclusion 38. Therefore, when in previous years—in the aforementioned **Imperial Recess of the Year 1542**—a contribution was enjoined upon the Rhenish Nobility and other Nobles subject immediately to the Empire, Emperor Ferdinand later provided this guarantee to the Rhenish Nobility (who were anxious about their privileges of immunity) under the date of **the year 1542**: **"That the aforementioned Knighthood on the Rhine has been released from these and similar burdens from of old as free persons; also that their granted consent (which was certainly made by force of the aforementioned Imperial Recess) and their satisfaction and granting of the same aid shall be without prejudice to their exemption, freedoms, rights, and traditions."**

However, the immunity of knightly estates <note>original: *Equestrium bonorum*</note>, of which we have spoken, does not extend to estates purchased from commoners; these lands remain nonetheless subject to burdens and services, and they bind their new lords to the original obligations: **by the text of Law 8 of the Code on the Exacting of Tribute; Law 1 of the Code on Estates and Navicular Property; Laws 'next to last' and 'last' of the Code on the Purchase of Land Without Census; and the Law on These Burdens in the Digest on Contracting Purchases.** See also Modestinus Pistor, *Counsel 9, number 36, volume 2*; Papon, *book 5, title 11, arrest 39*; Andreas Knichen, *On Investiture Pacts, chapter 3, number 91 and following*; Köpen, *Illustrious Question 61, number 10*; Meisner, *volume 2, book 1, decision 8, number 46*; and after them, Vietor, *On Exemptions, conclusion 19.* For public welfare <note>original: *Publica Salus*</note> demands that this freedom of estates should not be extended too broadly, lest the burdens removed from these estates grow unfairly upon others, and a common burden for many eventually be imposed upon only a few. **Law 'Actores,' Code on the Exacting of Tribute, book 10.** This is especially true since, in the imposing of tributes and collections, the condition of the estates is considered rather than that of the persons; the quality inherent in the estate necessarily follows whoever the possessor may be. Modestinus Pistor, *cited counsel 9, number 48, volume 2.* Thus, even the Church, though enriched by the acquisition of secular lands, does not merit immunity [for those specific lands]. **Chapter 'If Tribute,' 11, question 1; and chapter 13, question 8.** Petrus de Jure, *Question, chapter 4, number 57*; Meichsner, *volume 2, book 2, decision 3, number 21.*

<summary>This page discusses the tax obligations of the Imperial Nobility, noting that while they are exempt from standard taxes, they are expected to give voluntary aid to the Emperor. It also clarifies that if a Noble buys land previously owned by a commoner, they must continue to pay the taxes associated with that land.</summary>

<header>Dissertation II.</header>

<meta>catchword: see</meta>

This is so true that even Nobles purchasing the estates of peasants in their own villages cannot avoid the prior burdens <note>Legal obligations or taxes attached to the land before the noble bought it.</note>, to the ruin of their subjects. Cravetta, *Consultations 642, number 21*; Andreas Knichen, *On the aforementioned chapter 3, number 113 and following*, where he asserts it has been so ruled. For not even a Prince ought to decline those duties which the prior owner sustained. <note>original: *per text. in l. 8. C. de exact. tribut...* — referring to the Code of Justinian regarding the collection of tributes and the duties of shipmasters.</note>

Furthermore, the aforementioned Privilege, which renders the Nobility immune from the collection of taxes <term>Collectae: assessments or direct taxes levied on property or persons</term>, also leaves them free from the exaction of tolls <term>Vectigalia: indirect taxes, such as customs duties, transit fees, or road tolls</term>, according to the text of the **Westphalian Reformation of Emperor Sigismund, chapter 2, number 4.** <note>Sigismund was Holy Roman Emperor in the early 15th century; his "Reformation" attempted to standardize imperial law.</note> Mindanus, *On Mandates, book 2, chapter 42, number 6*; Johann Köppen, *Decision 61, number 5*; and Master Bocer, *On Regalian Rights, number 198*, also pronounces these Nobles free from the burden of tolls by the authority of the Law. Others, however, ascribe this immunity only to custom. Thomatus, *On the aforementioned collection, §. unknowingly, number 20*; Mynsinger, *part 2, decision 15, response 1, numbers 34 & 16*; Arnisaeus, *On the Right of Majesty, chapter 7, number 2*; Heinrich Klock, *volume 3, Basel Disputations on the Law of Tolls, conclusion 31*, where he says that this custom has lost its force in many places. Nevertheless, this same immunity has remained secure for the "Immediate" Nobles <note>Nobles who reported directly to the Emperor rather than a local prince.</note>, and was declared and extended to those of the Rhineland (to say nothing of the Franks and Swabians) by **Emperor Charles in the Year 1547**, and by **Emperor Rudolph in the Year 1591.**

However, I know it is doubted whether, by the extension of this immunity, they might harm others who hold older rights to collect tolls. Klock affirms this, *cited conclusion 31, at the end*; he deems this should be doubted even less because the final declaration of the privilege of immunity obtained by the Nobles is fortified with a derogatory clause <note>A legal provision that nullifies any previous laws or privileges that contradict the new one.</note>, which utterly removes all other privileges that have already been granted or will be granted in the future.

Nobles are, moreover, immune from the burden of guardianship <term>Tutela: the legal duty to manage the affairs of a minor or "pupil"</term>, insofar as they are not compelled to undergo the guardianship of commoner pupils unless there is a shortage of available men. <note>original: *l. non tantum. 17. § 1. ff. de excusat. Tutor...* — references to the Digest of Justinian regarding excuses for declining guardianship.</note> Tiraquellus, *On Nobility, chapter 37, number 16*; Johann Köppen, *Decision 61, number 3.*

Finally, there follow the Privileges which consist of the tempering or mitigation of penalties. For just as in the awarding of prizes, so also in the establishing of punishments, it is not unreasonable that a distinction of dignities and ranks be made. Hence, Nobles are not easily to be punished with corporal punishment <term>Poena corporalis: physical punishment such as flogging, branding, or execution</term> (since among them, any corporal punishment is more severe—even the smallest—than any monetary fine. Menochius, *On Judicial Arbitrations, case 447, number 11.*). <note>original references to the Digest and Code: *l. aut facta 16... ff. de poen...* — various Roman laws establishing that high-ranking individuals should be treated differently in criminal sentencing.</note> On which matter, one speaking with no little skill...

It also establishes the legal principle that nobles should receive milder, non-physical punishments for crimes due to their social standing.</summary>

<meta>This page continues the legal analysis of noble privileges, specifically transitioning from tax exemptions to the specialized treatment of nobles within the criminal justice system. It examines how their rank influences both the severity of fines and the "honorableness" of corporal or capital punishments.</meta>

<page-num>134</page-num>
<header>On Nobility</header>

See Hermann Kirchner, *book 1, orations 1 and 2.* But in monetary matters, they are punished more severely than commoners. <note>original: "l. properandum. §. fin autem. C. de Iudic." — Referring to the Code of Justinian, under the section on Judges.</note> Covarruvias, *Diverse Resolutions, book 2, chapter 9, number 3.* Nevertheless, wise judges must take great care lest a "harvest of crimes" grows because of excessive leniency, or lest anything be determined more slackly than the safety of the Commonwealth demands. Hence, if the status of Nobility actually increases the gravity of the crime—as in cases of treason, the vindication of violated trust, the crime of Lese-majesty <term>Lese-majesty: an offense against the dignity or safety of a sovereign or state</term>, forgery, or other "excepted" and enormous crimes—then the protection of Nobility is not rightly sought. Farinaccius, *In Criminal Practice, question 98, throughout.* Consequently, the dignity of Nobles does not in the least remove the penalty of the **Cornelian Law on Assassins** <note>original: "Legis Corneliae de Sicariis" — an ancient Roman law regarding murder and public safety</note>, unless some other support is added. <note>original: "l. 3. §. patiatur. C. de Episcop. aud." — Referring to the Code of Justinian regarding Episcopal hearings.</note> Farinaccius, *In Criminal Practice, question 119, number 17.* Furthermore, even if Nobles are insulted by commoners or even by other Nobles, they are perhaps not as strictly bound to flee as the low-born. **Criminal Constitution of Charles V, articles 140 and 142.** <note>Known as the *Carolina*, the first body of German criminal law, issued in 1532.</note> Tiraqueau, *chapter 20, number 63*; Gail, *Observation 2, 100, number 7 and following*; Farinaccius, *Criminal Consultations, 141, number 20, book 2*; Pruckman, *Consultation 22, number 79.* For among such men, flight is considered more ignominious and shameful than for others. Vasquez, *Controversies, book 1, chapter 11, number 14.* Even those provoked to a Duel, who stain themselves with the blood of their adversary therein, are exempt from the ordinary penalty for homicide. Joseph Nolden, *On Nobility, number 121*; see however the dissertation of Master Harpprecht, *on the section: likewise the Cornelian Law on Assassins, number 124, Institutes on Public Judgments.*

And even in those crimes for which, setting Nobility aside, the punishment of death is dictated, a penalty that is too shameful or servile should not be easily applied. Hence the foulness of hanging (unless the disgrace of treason is charged. Farinaccius, *question 98, number 15*; Schrader, *On Feuds, part 9, chapter 3, number 66*) and the use of rods and whips should be kept far from the Noble body. <note>original: "arg. l. moris. 9. §. ista in ff. de paen." — Arguments based on the Digest of Justinian regarding customary punishments.</note> Farinaccius, *question 98, number 100*; Giuseppe Ludovici, *Decisions of Lucca, 1, number 23*; Nolden, *On Nobility, number 110.*

Furthermore, that Nobles are not to be subjected to torture <term>Tormenta: the use of physical pain to extract a confession during a trial</term>, except for "excepted" crimes, is argued by the text in the Digest, *book 3, section 1, on military matters* and the Code, *on interrogations.* Farinaccius, *question 41, number 74*; Zanger, *On Torture, chapter 5, number 30 and following.* So much so, that a custom of contrary usage is of no significance. <note>original: "per text. in alleg. l. omnes Iudices..." — citing the Code of Justinian to argue that illegal customs do not override written law.</note> Master Bocer, *On Torture, chapter 4, number 78.*

Finally, Nobles are not held to undergo the danger of a "shameful recantation" <note>A public, humiliating withdrawal of a statement, often used in defamation cases.</note> when commoners bring an action for injuries against them. <note>original: "arg. l. non debet. 11..." — referencing the Digest on the limits of liability for fraud or malice.</note> Covarruvias, *Diverse Resolutions, book 2, chapter 9, number 4*; Nolden, *On Nobility, number 111.* In all these matters, however, the wisdom of a discerning Judge and a fitting judgment must always carry great weight.

<summary>This page outlines the legal privileges of the nobility in criminal law, specifically their exemption from "shameful" punishments like hanging or flogging, and their general immunity from judicial torture. It emphasizes that while nobles face higher fines, their rank protects their physical "honor" unless they commit extreme crimes like treason.</summary>

### Dissertation II

It remains for me to finally add a few things regarding both the preservation and the loss of Nobility. Nobility is preserved through the same means by which it is acquired. Certainly, as Giovanni Botero testifies in *book 1 of The Reason of State* <note>original: "della ragion di Stato"</note>, the natural course of human affairs tends toward decline and ruin. Since Nobility is more often granted by the favor of Princes rather than by their judgment, and more often by the splendor of wealth than by the honor of eminent virtue, it seems almost more difficult to hold onto Nobility than to obtain it. Indeed, a man rightly preserves the prerogative of Nobility if he conducts himself in such a way that people do not form a low opinion of him, showing that his Nobility is supported not only by Imperial grace or the glory of his ancestors, but especially by the ornament of his own virtues. <margin>See Junius, *On Batavia*, page 318.</margin> He must stay far from pride and lust, luxury and laziness; he must devote himself to the arts of war and peace, demonstrate constancy in his words and deeds, and remain loyal to his Homeland and his Lord. He should believe that genuine Nobility is that which either virtue adorns as a companion of life, or glory honors as a companion of death—a quality that consists in virtuous action while life lasts, or is fulfilled in a glorious passing. Furthermore, he should practice a way of life that is not dishonorable, humble, or sordid, but worthy of Nobility. He should fittingly mix humble modesty with mature gravity, so that he can easily be distinguished and recognized from the commoners—not merely by family images or clothing, but by the splendor of his life; not by the size of his estates, but by the brilliance of his deeds. He must be both a guide and an authority for his descendants to enter upon the same path of virtue, providing them with both an example and an incentive to promote the welfare of the Commonwealth.

Otherwise, an action for injuries serves as the avenger of a Nobility that has been held in contempt. Prospero Farinacci, *in his criminal treatise, question 105, number 173.* Furthermore, against someone who insultingly denies another's Nobility, a legal remedy is available under the **Law regarding defamation** <note>original: "L. diffamari, C. de ingenuis & manumiß." — a law from the Code of Justinian used to protect one's status against slander</note>. Regarding the restoration of honor and the penalty included in the privilege, the slanderer is quite justly summoned before the **Imperial Chamber** <term>Imperial Chamber: The Reichskammergericht, the highest judicial court in the Holy Roman Empire</term>, according to the traditions of Joachim Mynsinger, *5th observation, 81, number 2*, and Andreas Gail, *1st observation, 10, number 2*. This is true to such an extent that even the Prosecutor of the Chamber may intervene on behalf of the treasury's rights. Gilman, *in the Symphony of Prejudices of the Chamber, book 3, title: privilege, page 275.*

But also, out of fear of future controversy, it is permitted to offer petitions to the Prince, asking him to declare that someone is Noble or that they draw their origin from a certain family. Giacomo Menochio, *counsel 1261, number 42, book 13*, and after him, Matthias Nolden, *On Nobility, number 115.*

However, when this Nobility is brought into legal judgment, since it is a sort of "adventitious" quality <note>In legal terms, an "adventitious" quality is one that is acquired or added, rather than being an inherent natural right, and thus requires external proof.</note>, it requires strong supporting evidence from which either a legitimate presumption or a sufficient proof results. André Tiraqueau, *On Nobility, chapter 10, number 13 and following*; Giuseppe Mascardi, *On Proofs, conclusion 1094, at the beginning.* Although, if the controversy regarding Nobility was only proposed incidentally, lighter...

<summary>This page discusses how nobility must be maintained through virtuous conduct and public service rather than just wealth. It also details the legal mechanisms for defending one's noble status against slander in the Imperial Chamber Court and the requirements for proving noble lineage.</summary>

### On Nobility

See Hermann Kirchner, *book 1, orations 1 and 2*. In monetary matters, however, Nobles are punished more heavily than commoners. *Law 'properandum', section 'fin autem', Code on Judges; final chapter on penalties.* Covarruvias, *Varied Resolutions, book 2, chapter 9, number 3.* Nevertheless, wise judges must take great care lest a "crop of crimes" grows due to excessive indulgence, nor should any sentence be settled more leniently than the safety of the State <note>original: "Reipublicæ"</note> demands. Hence, if Nobility actually makes the crime worse—such as in cases of treason, the avenging of a broken oath, the crime of lese-majeste <note>original: "læsæ Majestatis"; a crime against the dignity of the sovereign or the state</note>, forgery, or other exceptional and enormous crimes—Nobility is not a valid defense <note>original: "Patrocinium"</note>. Farinacius, *In Criminal Practice, question 98, throughout*. Consequently, the dignity of Nobles does not at all remove the penalty of the Cornelian Law on Assassins <note>original: "Legis Corneliæ de Sicarijs"; a Roman law originally targeting murderers and poisoners</note>, unless some other support is added. *Law 3, section 'patiatur', Code on Episcopal Audience.* Farinacius, *In Criminal Practice, question 119, number 17.* Even if Nobles, when insulted by commoners or even other Nobles, are perhaps not as strictly bound to flee as the low-born are. *Criminal Constitution of Emperor Charles V, articles 140 and 142.* Tiraqueau, *chapter 20, number 63.* Gail, *book 2, observation 100, number 7 and following.* Farinacius, *Criminal Consultations, book 2, consultation 141, number 20.* Pruckman, *consultation 22, number 79.* For among such men, flight is considered more shameful and disgraceful than for others. Vasquez, *Controversies, book 1, chapter 11, number 14.* Those who are provoked to a Duel, and who stain themselves with the blood of an adversary, are also exempted from the ordinary penalty for murderers. Josua Nolden, *On Nobility, number 121.* See, however, the disagreement of Lord Harpprecht, *section 'item Lex Cornelia de Sicar', number 124, Institutes on Public Judgments.*

Even in those crimes for which—setting Nobility aside—death is dictated as the punishment, a penalty that is too shameful or servile ought not to be easily applied. Hence the foulness of hanging is avoided (unless the disgrace of treason is charged). Farinacius, *question 98, number 15.* Schrader, *On Fiefs, part 9, chapter 3, number 61.* Rods and whips <note>original: "Virgæ & flagella"</note> are to be kept away from a Noble body. *Based on law 'moris', 9, section 'ista', Digest on Penalties; Law 5, Code on the causes of infamy; chapter 'pastoralis', at the end, on the office of the delegate.* Farinacius, *question 98, number 100.* Giuseppe Ludovici, *Decisions of Lucca 1, number 23.* Nolden, *Nobility, number 110.*

Furthermore, that Nobles are not to be subjected to torture <note>original: "Tormentis"</note>—except for the "excepted crimes"—is argued from the text in *Law 3, section 1, Digest on Military Affairs; law 'milites'; law 'divo', Code on Investigations; Law 'omnes', 33, Code on Decurions; law 'severam', 10, Code on Dignities.* Farinacius, *question 41, number 74.* Zanger, *On Torture, chapter 5, number 30 and following.* Indeed, any custom of a contrary practice <note>original: "contrariæ usurpationis consuetudo"</note> is of no significance. *Based on the cited law 'omnes Iudices', 33, Code on Decurions; law 'nullus', 4, Code on the Julian Law of Treason.* Lord Bocer, *On Torture, chapter 4, number 78.*

Finally, when commoners bring an action for injuries against them, Nobles are not held to undergo the risk of a public recantation <note>original: "famosæ recantationis"; a humiliating public apology meant to restore the victim's reputation</note>. *Based on law 'non debet', 11, verse 'sed nec', Digest on Fraud.* Covarruvias, *Varied Resolutions, book 2, chapter 9, number 4.* Nolden, *On Nobility, number 111.* In all these matters, however, the wisdom and fitting judgment <note>original: "condecens arbitrium"</note> of a discerning Judge must always carry great weight.

<summary>This page outlines the legal privileges and heavier responsibilities of the nobility in criminal law, specifically their exemption from shameful punishments like hanging or whipping and their protection from torture except in cases of high treason.</summary>

### On Nobility

<meta>catchword: alone</meta>

...proofs are admitted in these matters. Menochius, *On Presumptions, book 6, presumption 60, number 8*. There are, in fact, many ways of proving Nobility. Among these, a legal document—specifically an Imperial diploma <note>A formal charter or deed issued by the Emperor</note> granting Nobility—presents itself. Deciani, *Responses, number 57, volume 3*. Similarly, the registry <term>matricula</term> <note>A formal list or roll of individuals belonging to a specific group, in this case, the official register of the noble class</note> of Nobles provides no small measure of proof. Deciani, *Alleged Responses, response 66, number 5, volume 3*. So too do history books, monuments, genealogical records, birth records, and other documents drawn from public archives, concerning which Josias Nolden writes in his *Treatise, number 126*. Furthermore, a third method—a deposition <note>a formal witness statement</note> supported by sound reasoning for the opinion—is highly regarded. Mascardi, *Alleged Conclusions, number 12*. To these are added notoriety and evidence, which make a matter manifest and require no further proof. Gutierrez, *Practices, questions 14 and 15, throughout*. Of no small weight also is the possession of an estate to which <term>Regalia</term> <note>Sovereign rights or privileges, such as the right to hunt, mine, or hold a court, which were attached to specific noble lands</note> are annexed. Mascardi, *at the same location, number 5*. Likewise, the common estimation of men, Mascardi, *numbers 8 and 9*, and public reputation, Cassaneus, in *Catalog of the Glory of the World, part 8, consideration 16*. Or the fact that one’s ancestors were treated as Nobles and were called to and admitted into the assemblies of Nobles. Deciani, *at response 66, number 50*. Each of these factors either provides full proof on its own, or if some of these methods are deemed insufficiently firm, they nevertheless shift the burden of proof <note>original: "onus probandi"</note> onto the opponent. Therefore, I believe it is not indifferent to burden someone with this perilous demonstration <note>i.e., a difficult legal requirement to prove status</note> to show: (1) that his Nobility descends directly from his ancestors; (2) that his ancestors were held to be Nobles and conducted themselves as such, never pursuing a way of life that dishonors Nobility; and (3) that he himself is treated as a Noble by everyone and lives in no other way than becomes a Noble. All these things together are required by Friedrich Pruckmann, *Consultations, number 140, book 1*.

Next follows the loss of Nobility, which proceeds chiefly from three causes: (1) when someone embraces a humble way of life; (2) when he changes his family; (3) when he commits a crime.

A "humble way of life" includes vile trades and manual crafts; likewise base and abject offices and deformed services, in which the very wages are, as it were, a sign of servitude <note>original: "authoramentum quasi servitutis"</note>. *Code of Justinian, Law 6, 'let no one', on Dignities*; Cicero, *On Duties, book 1*; Josias Nolden, *On Nobility, chapter 17, number 160 and following*. Into this class the Emperors cast moneyers, bankers, money-changers, and all others fed by base profits, whose gains (according to Cicero, *book 1, On Duties*) especially incur the hatred of men. *Code of Justinian, Law 6, 'let no one', on Dignities; Law 'if a member of the cohort', section 1; and the Law 'we decree', on Purple-fish Gatherers.* <note>The "Murileguli" or purple-fish gatherers were a hereditary guild in the late Roman Empire whose work was considered essential but socially low-status.</note>

I wish to investigate whether trade <term>mercatura</term> stands in the way of Nobility, or whether it is permitted for Nobles to engage in business dealings without harming the privilege of their Nobility. Indeed, I know that the exercise of trade is forbidden to Nobles, lest commerce with them be difficult for the common people, and the profits of trade...

<summary>This page details the legal methods for proving noble status—such as Imperial diplomas, registries, and social reputation—and begins an analysis of how Nobility is lost, specifically through "vile" manual labor and base trades.</summary>
<keywords>proofs of nobility, imperial diploma, matricula, loss of nobility, base trades, manual labor, commerce, Roman law, legal evidence</keywords>

<meta>This page continues the legal analysis of how noble status is maintained or lost, specifically addressing whether engaging in commerce or manual labor diminishes one's standing. It follows a discussion on how nobility is proven through documents and reputation.</meta>

# Dissertation II

...snatch the leaves to themselves. <note>original: "foli ad se rapiant"—a metaphorical reference to legal citations regarding merchants</note> *Book 3 of the Code on Commerce and Merchants.* It is well established that Nobility is somewhat cheapened by the distribution of goods—unless the custom of the region and the necessity of the place stand in the way, where almost no other income can be scraped together except from trade (see Andreas Knichen, *On Investiture Agreements, chapter 4, number 95*). Indeed, Nobility does not sit well with the pursuit of profit and gain. Mynsinger, *6th Century of Observations, observation 54*; Knichen, *allegation 4, number 98*. Consequently, trade should not easily be permitted to Nobles, even if the business is conducted through agents. Jean Bodin, *On the Republic, book 3, page 560*, argues this is to ensure the distinction is not lost between those distinguished by their high rank and private citizens. The former ought to defend the Commonwealth <term>Respublica</term> <note>The state or public interest</note> and handle public affairs, while the latter should manage "economic" matters, importing and exporting necessities. Franciscus Pfeil, *Counsel 202, century 2*; Keckermann, *On Politics, book 1, page 243*.

From this, however, you would not correctly conclude that trade destroys the privileges of Nobility. Master Bocer, *On Regalia, chapter 2, number 52*; Camman, *On the Rights of Majesty, dispute 4, thesis 38*. This applies unless the trade is "petty" <note>original: "tenuis," referring to small-scale retail</note>—or as the French say, *retail* <note>original: "en detail"</note>—where one buys something only to sell it immediately in the same place. Cicero, *On Duties, book 1*; Mynsinger, *observation 54, number 11 and following*. This is how I understand the laws <note>Citing various sections of the Justinian Code regarding dignity and status</note>.

In the same way, it does not harm one's Nobility to have cultivated one’s own fields. Antonius Faber, *On the Code, book 9, title 28, definition 3*. Hadrianus Junius says in his *History of Batavia, chapter 9*, that it is characteristic of the French Nobility to cultivate their estates and the countryside, and to flee from city commerce as if it were a primary stain upon them. Thus, the reputation of Nobility there mostly depends on a rural life and the possession of an estate—a custom from which the modern German Nobility is not far removed. So too the Nobles of England, enclosed in forests and various parks, spend their lives in the country and increase their wealth from the wool trade <note>original: "laniceo"</note> and the offspring of the flocks they graze.

Furthermore, the office of Procurators <note>Legal representatives</note> and Notaries does not extinguish Nobility. Nolden, *discourse number 87 and following*; Antonius Faber, *title 3, definitions 4 and 5*. Nor do the hardships of poverty hinder it. Tiraquellus, *On Nobility, chapter 35, number 5 and following*. Neither the entry into a Religious Order nor the attainment of academic honors <note>original: "literarij honoris gradus"</note> overshadows it. Baptist de Villalobos, *Common Opinions, letter N, number 14*; Matthias Stephanus, *On Jurisdiction, book 3, part 2, chapter 14, at the end*. Finally, the passage of time or the discontinued use of privileges does not destroy Nobility. Guillaume de Montferrat, *On the Succession of the Kings of France, doubt 4, numbers 6 and 15*; Gutierrez, *Practical Questions, book 3 and 4, question 13, number 90*; Deciani, *Responses 66, number 70, volume 3*. However, Guido Papa says in *Decision 387* that the long-term payment of taxes <note>original: "collectarum solutionem," usually a burden from which nobles were exempt</note> can prejudice a noble's exemption, as noted by Antonius Ranchin and Antonius Faber.

I have said, moreover, that the loss of Nobility can result from a change in the family; for a Noble woman who chooses a non-noble husband [loses] her Nobility...

<summary>This page discusses the activities that might cause a person to lose their noble status, specifically contrasting the "noble" life of land ownership and public service with the "vile" nature of retail trade. It highlights regional differences, noting that while commerce is generally frowned upon, rural land management and sheep farming (as in England) are considered acceptable for the upper class.</summary>
<keywords>nobility, trade, commerce, agriculture, social status, England, France, Germany, legal privileges, retail, poverty, notaries, tax exemption</keywords>

--- Page 157 ---
<meta>This page concludes the discussion on the loss of nobility, continuing from the previous page's analysis of how marriage to a commoner or engagement in trade affects a woman's or a man's status. It addresses the legal impact of adoption, criminal activity, and how status may be recovered.</meta>

### Dissertation II. on Nobility

...she suffers its loss and loses the splendor of her lineage. <note>The text here continues from the previous page regarding a noblewoman who marries a commoner.</note> *Law "Fœminæ" 8, Digest, on the Senate; Law 1, Code, on Dignities; Tiraqueau, On Nobility, chapter 18, number 12.* This loss is so absolute that she does not even regain her Nobility upon the death of her husband. *Guido Papa, question 380; Costalius on Law "Fœminæ," Digest, on the Senate.* Adoption, however, is not a means of losing one's rank; a Noble adopted by a commoner <term>plebeius</term> <note>A person of the non-noble class</note> retains the prior honor of his family, according to the text *in Law "per adoptionem" 35, Digest, on Adoptions; and Law "Senatoris" 6, paragraph 1, along with the following law at the beginning, Digest, on the Senate; Master Bocer, On Regalia, chapter 2, number 43 and following.*

The final way of losing Nobility is the commission of a Crime, *Law "Senatoris filio," Digest, on the Senate; Law 1, Code, "Where the Senate or the most illustrious"; Law "Divo Marco," Code, on [the same]; Law 2, Law "Judices," and Law "quoties," Code, on Dignities; Georg Everhard, counsel 2, number 8, book 1; Matthaeus Stephanus, treatise On Nobility, chapter 7, number 2.* Yet the foulness of a parent's life or crime does not extinguish the ancestral Nobility of the children. *Law 3, Digest, on Interdicts and Relegation; Tiraqueau, On Nobility, chapters 15 and 35.* Hence, if a Father—either through menial trades <note>original: "sordidis artibus"—referring to manual labor or retail commerce considered beneath noble dignity</note> or by some other means—has obscured the Nobility of his lineage, the sons, provided they have committed nothing unworthy themselves, retain the prior splendor of their race. *Antoine Favre, in the Code, book 9, title 28, definition 14.* This holds true even if they were conceived only after the Nobility was lost by the Father, *Antoine Favre, alleged title, definitions 1 and 25,* so long as the Nobility descended from an ancient stock, or the Prince granted the status not only to the Father but also to his children. *Tiraqueau, chapter 35, numbers 5 and 6; Antoine Favre, said title 28, definition 25.* But it is not to be doubted that those whose parents have not stained themselves with the mark of violated shame are looked upon with greater favor. *Antoine Favre, alleged definition 14.*

These aforementioned rules must be understood with moderation: a person loses their Privileges only for as long as they are involved in menial trades and offices. For if a Noble of ancient lineage ceases these activities, they say he recovers his status by right of law <term>ipso jure</term>, *through the last chapter, on the life and honor of clerics; and in the Clementines 1 of the same; Law "ingenuam" with the gloss, Code, on freeborn manumission; Guido Papa, decisions 196, 217, and 391; Tiraqueau, On Nobility, chapter 27, number 5; Antoine Favre, alleged definition 1.* In such a case, however, a new grant from the Prince is required for a person who was the first in their line to acquire Nobility for themselves.

And so much for Nobility, to which I add a prayer: that no one might aspire to Nobility except through virtue, that no one might defend it except through virtue, and that everyone might persuade themselves that it is better to have given birth to Nobility <note>original: "Nobilitatem peperisse"—referring to earning one's status through merit rather than merely inheriting it</note> than to have corrupted it with vices.

->**GLORY BE TO GOD.**<-

<vocab>Nobility, Commission of a crime, Adoption, Privileges, Virtue, Tiraqueau, Antoine Favre</vocab>

<summary>This page concludes the dissertation by explaining that while nobility can be lost through "lowly" trades or crime, ancient noble status is often hereditary and can be recovered if the dishonorable activity ceases. The author ends with a moral exhortation that true nobility is rooted in virtue.</summary>
<keywords>loss of nobility, legal status of children, adoption, criminal forfeiture, recovery of status, virtue and honor, Tiraqueau, Antoine Favre</keywords>
